10.6 A Gauduchon variational volume

Definitions, results, and proofs on this subject.

26 A Gauduchon variational volume

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Let $X$ be a connected compact complex manifold of dimension $n\geq 1$, let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form, which we call the background, and let $\mu $ be a smooth strictly positive probability form on $X$. If $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v>0$ for some smooth real function $v$, there is exactly one constant $c>0$ for which the Monge–Ampère equation $(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=c\mu $ has a smooth solution $u$ with $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u>0$. When $\theta $ is closed, Stokes' theorem gives $c=\int _ X\theta ^ n$, independently of $\mu $. For a nonclosed background the total mass $\int _ X(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n$ can depend on the potential, and the constant $c$ is the one number that the equation attaches to the pair $(\theta ,\mu )$. This chapter studies it as a function of the background.

The starting point is a dual formula. Integrating the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality against the $(n-1)$st power of a Gauduchon metric $\gamma $, that is, of a Hermitian form with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\gamma ^{n-1})=0$, shows that $c^{1/n}$ is the infimum of a quotient whose numerator $\int _ X\theta \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}$ is linear in $\theta $ and unchanged by smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ changes of $\theta $. Taking this infimum for every pseudoeffective background defines the root of the Gauduchon variational volume. The root volume is concave. On the positive cone, a minimizing Gauduchon metric computes its derivative, and the Hessian of its negative logarithm defines a Riemannian metric on background classes in each finite-dimensional family. Invariant forms on compact quotients of complex Lie groups and warped products with a curve show that the unit-volume hypersurface of this metric may be complete or may acquire a boundary at finite distance.

For $n\geq 2$, the same quotient computes the subsolution threshold $b_\omega (f)$ for a Hermitian background $\omega $ and an integrable density $f$. Allowing positive $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-closed currents as tests makes this dual problem compact. For continuous densities the maximizing currents represent exactly the supporting probabilities of the normalization functional; for integrable densities, a uniform tail condition passes the determinant integral to weak limits and again gives a maximizing current. A maximizing test that is a smooth strictly positive form gives more: equality in the trace inequality determines the solution current and forces an exact smooth equation. The exact equation persists when the maximizing form degenerates along a proper analytic set, whereas a threshold solution with a point pole can admit no smooth maximizing test at all.

The last part of the chapter concerns the boundary of the positive cone and the dependence on $\mu $. At a nef background the variational volume is the limit of the smooth equation constants of nearby positive backgrounds. A projective radial model gives positive variational volume without bigness, even at the finite maximal time of a Chern–Ricci flow. Varying $\mu $ recovers the upper and lower Monge–Ampère masses. A threefold with zero lower mass shows that positivity for each smooth density need not be uniform over densities, whereas on an elliptic threefold a nonnegative sum of curvature squares gives a bounded cubic energy identity and hence a positive lower bound for every potential, even when the manifold admits no pluriclosed metric. Finally, nef degeneration gives a further contrast with closed Kähler classes: intersection polynomials are replaced by fractional, logarithmic or oscillating decay, and a single blowup can change its order.

Throughout the chapter, a smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\theta $ is called pseudoeffective if $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\ne \varnothing $, that is, if $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0$ for some quasi-plurisubharmonic $u$; by theorem 4.3.1 this holds exactly when $\int _ X\theta \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}\geq 0$ for every Gauduchon metric $\gamma $. Bigness is defined in section 1.19, and $\theta $ is nef if, for a Hermitian form $h$ and every $\epsilon >0$, there is a smooth real $f_\epsilon $ with $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f_\epsilon \geq -\epsilon h$; this is the condition of section 23.6 for the affine class of $\theta $ in section 24.1. None of these notions requires $\theta $ to be closed, and each is unchanged by a smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ change of $\theta $.

26.1 The quotient and the smooth equation

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

For a Gauduchon metric $\gamma $, Stokes' theorem shows that the pairing $\int _ X\theta \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}$ is unchanged by a smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ change of $\theta $; this pairing will be the numerator of our quotient. For the denominator, put

\[ D_\mu (\gamma )=\int _ X \left(\frac{\mu }{\gamma ^ n}\right)^{1/n}\gamma ^ n. \]

This number is positive. Like the numerator, it is multiplied by $r^{n-1}$ when $\gamma $ is multiplied by a positive constant $r$, so the quotient below depends only on the ray of $\gamma $.

Definition 26.1.1. For a pseudoeffective smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\theta $ on $X$, define

\[ \lambda _\mu (\theta )= \inf _{\gamma \text{ Gauduchon}} \frac{\displaystyle \int _ X\theta \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}}{D_\mu (\gamma )}, \qquad \mathcal V_\mu (\theta )=\lambda _\mu (\theta )^ n. \]

We call $\mathcal V_\mu (\theta )$ the Gauduchon variational volume of $\theta $ with respect to $\mu $, and $\lambda _\mu (\theta )$ its root volume. In dimension one every Hermitian metric is Gauduchon, and every quotient equals $\int _ X\theta $.

Proposition 26.1.2. For a pseudoeffective $\theta $, the number $\lambda _\mu (\theta )$ is finite and nonnegative. It is unchanged by a smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ change of $\theta $, positively homogeneous of degree one, and concave on the cone of pseudoeffective forms. If $\beta $ is a smooth real $(1,1)$-form and $\beta -\theta $ is pseudoeffective, then

\[ \lambda _\mu (\beta )\geq \lambda _\mu (\theta ). \]

Finally, $\lambda _\mu $ is upper semicontinuous under smooth convergence within this cone.

Proof. Choose $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. For every Gauduchon metric $\gamma $, Stokes' theorem for currents and the Gauduchon condition give

\[ \int _ X\theta \wedge \gamma ^{n-1} =\int _ X(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}\geq 0. \]

Thus every quotient is nonnegative, and testing one fixed Gauduchon metric gives a finite upper bound. The same identity shows that each quotient is unchanged by a smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ change of the background, and, applied to $\beta -\theta $, it proves the monotonicity.

Each quotient is a linear function of the background, so their infimum is concave and positively homogeneous. Each individual quotient is also continuous in the smooth topology. Given a smoothly converging sequence, we bound the infimum by any one quotient, take the upper limit, and finally take the infimum over the test metric. This proves upper semicontinuity. $\square$

The smooth equation identifies both the value of the quotient and every minimizing metric. Let $\mathscr P$ be the set of backgrounds $\theta $ admitting a smooth real function $u$ with $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u>0$. Choosing such a Hermitian representative of $\theta $ and writing $\mu $ as a smooth positive density relative to its volume form, we may apply theorem 9.1.1. It gives, for each $\theta \in \mathscr P$, a unique positive form $\Omega _\theta $, which we call the solution form, and a unique constant $c_\theta (\mu )>0$ such that

\[ \Omega _\theta =\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\theta , \qquad \Omega _\theta ^ n=c_\theta (\mu )\mu . \]

The potential $u_\theta $ is unique after any fixed additive normalization.

Theorem 26.1.3. For every $\theta \in \mathscr P$,

\[ \lambda _\mu (\theta )=c_\theta (\mu )^{1/n}, \qquad \mathcal V_\mu (\theta )=c_\theta (\mu ). \]

The infimum in definition 26.1.1 is attained. When $n\geq 2$, its minimizing Gauduchon metrics are precisely the constant multiples of the Gauduchon metric conformal to $\Omega _\theta $.

Proof. For any Hermitian metric $\gamma $, the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality for the eigenvalues of $\Omega _\theta $ relative to $\gamma $ gives the pointwise inequality

\[ \Omega _\theta \wedge \gamma ^{n-1} \geq \left(\frac{\Omega _\theta ^ n}{\gamma ^ n}\right)^{1/n}\gamma ^ n =c_\theta (\mu )^{1/n} \left(\frac{\mu }{\gamma ^ n}\right)^{1/n}\gamma ^ n. \]

If $\gamma $ is Gauduchon, integration and Stokes' theorem replace $\Omega _\theta $ in the numerator by $\theta $. Every test quotient is therefore at least $c_\theta (\mu )^{1/n}$.

For $n\geq 2$, take the Gauduchon metric $\gamma =\mathrm{e}^ g\Omega _\theta $ conformal to $\Omega _\theta $, given by theorem 4.1.2, where $g$ is a smooth real function. Since all eigenvalues of $\Omega _\theta $ relative to $\gamma $ are equal at every point, equality holds pointwise and hence in the quotient. This proves the formula and attainment. Conversely, if a Gauduchon metric attains the infimum, the nonnegative smooth difference of the two sides of the pointwise inequality has zero integral. It therefore vanishes everywhere, and the equality case of the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality makes $\gamma $ conformal to $\Omega _\theta $. Since the Gauduchon metric in a conformal class is unique up to a constant factor, this proves the last assertion.

For $n=1$, we integrate the equation and use $\int _ X\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\theta =0$. This gives $c_\theta (\mu )=\int _ X\theta $, which equals every quotient in the definition. $\square$

For a Hermitian background $\omega $, a smooth positive probability volume $\mathrm{d}V$ and a nonzero nonnegative density $f\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$, the analogous quotient, with $\mu $ replaced by $f\, \mathrm{d}V$, computes the subsolution threshold $b_\omega (f)$ of section 14.27; see theorem 14.28.1. Attainment in this extremal problem, and the regularity of the extremal tests, are studied from section 26.6 on.

Corollary 26.1.4. If $\theta $ is big, then $\lambda _\mu (\theta )>0$.

Proof. By the definition of bigness, there are a Hermitian form $h$ and $\delta >0$ such that $\theta -\delta h$ is pseudoeffective. Monotonicity, homogeneity and the theorem, applied to $h\in \mathscr P$, give $\lambda _\mu (\theta )\geq \delta \lambda _\mu (h)>0$. $\square$

The converse fails for nef nonclosed backgrounds: theorem 26.13.2 gives, on a projective manifold, a nef background of positive volume that is not big.

26.2 The equality case of concavity and the first variation

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Since $\lambda _\mu $ is an infimum of linear functions, each minimizing Gauduchon metric at $\theta $ defines a linear functional that supports $\lambda _\mu $ at $\theta $. If equality holds in the concavity inequality along a segment, a metric minimizing at an interior point must minimize at both endpoints, and theorem 26.1.3 then makes the two endpoint solution forms proportional. Differentiating the smooth equation shows that the supporting functional is the derivative of $\lambda _\mu $. For $\theta \in \mathscr P$ we write $\gamma _\theta $ for a minimizing Gauduchon metric, which exists by theorem 26.1.3; variation directions are smooth real $(1,1)$-forms.

Theorem 26.2.1. Let $\theta _0,\theta _1\in \mathscr P$ and $0<s<1$. Then

\[ \lambda _\mu ((1-s)\theta _0+s\theta _1) \geq (1-s)\lambda _\mu (\theta _0)+s\lambda _\mu (\theta _1). \]

Equality holds if and only if, for some $r>0$ and some smooth real $f$,

\[ \theta _1=r\theta _0+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f. \]

Proof. Concavity was proved in proposition 26.1.2. Suppose first that $n\geq 2$ and that equality holds. Take a minimizing Gauduchon metric for the middle background $(1-s)\theta _0+s\theta _1$. Its quotient at the middle background is the same convex combination of its quotients at $\theta _0$ and $\theta _1$, and each of these is at least the corresponding infimum. Equality therefore forces this metric to minimize both endpoint quotients.

By theorem 26.1.3, the solution forms $\Omega _ i=\Omega _{\theta _ i}$, with constants $c_ i=c_{\theta _ i}(\mu )$ for $i=0,1$, are then both conformal to this metric. Their volume forms are $c_0\mu $ and $c_1\mu $, so their pointwise proportionality factor is the constant $r=(c_1/c_0)^{1/n}$. Hence $\Omega _1=r\Omega _0$, which gives the stated smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ relation. Conversely, if that relation holds, invariance under smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ changes and homogeneity give equality.

When $n=1$, the function is $\theta \mapsto \int _ X\theta $. Take $r=\int _ X\theta _1/\int _ X\theta _0$. The difference $\theta _1-r\theta _0$ has integral zero, so the Green operator of the compact curve writes it as $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f$ with $f$ smooth. Thus the same equivalence holds. $\square$

Proposition 26.2.2. The function $\lambda _\mu $ is smooth along smooth finite-dimensional families in $\mathscr P$. For a smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\chi $ and $n\geq 2$, its derivative at $\theta $ is

\[ (\mathrm{d}\lambda _\mu )_\theta (\chi ) =\frac{\displaystyle \int _ X\chi \wedge \gamma _\theta ^{n-1}}{D_\mu (\gamma _\theta )}, \]

where $\gamma _\theta $ is any minimizing Gauduchon metric. In dimension one the derivative is $\int _ X\chi $.

Proof. Choose a smooth potential $u_*$ with $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_*>0$. Backgrounds $\theta _ t$ near $\theta $ then have the positive representatives $\theta _ t+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_*$. The implicit-function argument in the proof of proposition 15.8.3 applies to this family and shows that the solution and its constant depend smoothly on the parameters. Adding $u_*$ to these solution potentials and fixing the mean gives the normalized solutions for $\theta _ t$, so $\lambda _\mu (\theta _ t)=c_{\theta _ t}(\mu )^{1/n}$ is smooth.

To compute the derivative, write $\lambda =\lambda _\mu (\theta )$ and $\Omega =\Omega _\theta $, and write the minimizing metric as $\gamma _\theta =\mathrm{e}^ g\Omega $ with $g$ smooth and real, as theorem 26.1.3 permits. Along $\theta _ t=\theta +t\chi $ for small real $t$, put $\Omega _ t=\theta _ t+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ t$ and $\lambda _ t=\lambda _\mu (\theta _ t)$, and let dots denote derivatives at $t=0$. Differentiating $\Omega _ t^ n=\lambda _ t^ n\mu $ gives

\[ (\chi +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\dot u)\wedge \Omega ^{n-1} =\lambda ^{n-1}\dot\lambda \, \mu . \]

We multiply by $\mathrm{e}^{(n-1)g}$, so that $\Omega ^{n-1}$ becomes $\gamma _\theta ^{n-1}$, and integrate. The term with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\dot u$ vanishes by the Gauduchon condition, and $\int _ X\chi \wedge \gamma _\theta ^{n-1}=\lambda ^{n-1}\dot\lambda \int _ X\mathrm{e}^{(n-1)g}\mu $. On the other hand, $\gamma _\theta ^ n=\mathrm{e}^{ng}\lambda ^ n\mu $, so that

\[ D_\mu (\gamma _\theta ) =\lambda ^{n-1}\int _ X \mathrm{e}^{(n-1)g}\mu . \]

Dividing the two identities proves the derivative formula. In dimension one the formula follows directly from the definition. $\square$

26.3 Second variation on background classes

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Differentiating the smooth equation twice along an affine family of backgrounds computes the second derivative of the root volume. After integration against the adjoint probability of the solution form, the second derivative of the potential disappears, and what remains is the integrated squared norm of the linearized solution form. Consequently the Hessian of $-\log \lambda _\mu $ is positive definite once smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ directions are factored out. On the cone of closed Kähler classes, where $\lambda _\mu (\theta )=(\int _ X\theta ^ n)^{1/n}$, it is $1/n$ times the Hessian of $-\log \int _ X\theta ^ n$.

Fix $\theta \in \mathscr P$ and write $\Omega =\Omega _\theta $, $u=u_\theta $ and $\lambda =\lambda _\mu (\theta )$. Let $\nu _\Omega $ be the adjoint probability of $\Omega $ from proposition 4.8.1, that is, the smooth positive probability measure, given by the Gauduchon multiplier of $\Omega $, which satisfies $\int _ X\operatorname {tr}_\Omega (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)\, \mathrm{d}\nu _\Omega =0$ for every smooth real $v$. In dimension one this probability is $\Omega /\int _ X\Omega =\mu $.

Let $\theta _ t=\theta +t\chi $, where $t$ is real and $\chi $ is a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $X$. For sufficiently small $|t|$, the background $\theta _ t$ stays in $\mathscr P$. We normalize the solution potentials $u_ t$ by one fixed condition on their mean, and dots denote derivatives of $u_ t$ and of $\lambda _\mu (\theta _ t)$ at $t=0$. Define the Hermitian endomorphism

\[ A_\chi =\Omega ^{-1}(\chi +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\dot u) \]

of $T^{1,0}X$, where $\Omega ^{-1}$ means raising an index with the positive form $\Omega $; traces of powers of $A_\chi $ are independent of coordinates. We write $I$ for the identity endomorphism of $T^{1,0}X$ and $\| \cdot \| _\Omega $ for the Hilbert–Schmidt norm on its endomorphisms induced by $\Omega $.

Proposition 26.3.1. Along this affine variation,

26.1
\begin{align} \frac{\ddot\lambda }{\lambda } & =-\frac1n\int _ X \left\| A_\chi -\frac{\dot\lambda }{\lambda }I\right\| _\Omega ^2 \, \mathrm{d}\nu _\Omega ,\\ -\frac{\mathrm{d}^2}{\mathrm{d}t^2}\log \lambda _\mu (\theta _ t) & =\frac1n\int _ X\operatorname {tr}(A_\chi ^2)\, \mathrm{d}\nu _\Omega . \end{align}

The first expression vanishes exactly when $\chi =r\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f$ for a real constant $r$ and a smooth real $f$. The second vanishes exactly when $\chi =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f$ for a smooth real $f$.

Proof. Smooth dependence on $t$ was proved in proposition 26.2.2. We differentiate the logarithmic form $\log (\Omega _ t^ n/\mu )=n\log \lambda _\mu (\theta _ t)$ of the equation. Since $\mu $ is fixed,

\[ \operatorname {tr}A_\chi =n\frac{\dot\lambda }{\lambda }. \]

In particular this trace is constant on $X$. Differentiating once more along the affine path $\theta _ t$ gives

\[ \operatorname {tr}_\Omega (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\ddot u) -\operatorname {tr}(A_\chi ^2) =n\left(\frac{\ddot\lambda }{\lambda } -\frac{\dot\lambda ^2}{\lambda ^2}\right). \]

Integration against the probability $\nu _\Omega $, which is fixed since everything is evaluated at $t=0$, removes the first term. This proves (26.2).

Put $r=\dot\lambda /\lambda $. Since $A_\chi $ is Hermitian and $\operatorname {tr}A_\chi =nr$,

\[ \| A_\chi -rI\| _\Omega ^2 =\operatorname {tr}(A_\chi ^2)-nr^2. \]

Substituting this into (26.2) gives (26.1). Since the adjoint measure has a strictly positive smooth density, the right side of (26.1) vanishes exactly when $A_\chi =rI$ everywhere, that is, when

\[ \chi +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\dot u=r(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u). \]

This is the stated relation with $f=ru-\dot u$. Conversely, if $\chi =r\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f$, then $\theta _ t$ differs from $(1+rt)\theta $ by a smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ term for small $t$. Homogeneity gives $\lambda _\mu (\theta _ t)=(1+rt)\lambda $, whose second derivative is zero.

The integrand in (26.2) is the squared norm of the Hermitian endomorphism $A_\chi $. Its integral therefore vanishes exactly when $A_\chi =0$, that is, when $\chi =-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\dot u$. Conversely, $\lambda _\mu (\theta +t\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f)=\lambda $ for all $t$. This proves both equality statements. $\square$

The linearized solution and the derivative of the constant depend linearly on $\chi $. For smooth real $(1,1)$-forms $\chi _1,\chi _2$ on $X$, polarization therefore gives a symmetric bilinear form

26.3
\begin{equation} \mathfrak g_\theta (\chi _1,\chi _2) =-D^2\log \lambda _\mu |_\theta (\chi _1,\chi _2) =\frac1n\int _ X\operatorname {tr}(A_{\chi _1}A_{\chi _2})\, \mathrm{d}\nu _\Omega . \end{equation}

We call the classes of smooth real $(1,1)$-forms modulo $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}C^\infty (X,\mathbb R)$ background classes. By proposition 26.3.1, the form $\mathfrak g_\theta $ descends to background classes of directions and is positive definite there. A change of representative of $\theta $ leaves its solution form $\Omega $ unchanged, and a change of representative of a direction is absorbed by the derivative of the solution potential. Thus $\mathfrak g$ is intrinsically defined on the background classes in $\mathscr P$. On every finite-dimensional affine family whose directions are linearly independent modulo smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ terms, it is a smooth Riemannian metric.

These variations change the background class. Directions obtained by varying a potential inside one fixed class are exactly the directions annihilated by (26.3). Thus this construction concerns a different space from the potential-space geometry of chapter 3.

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory
26.2
\[\begin{aligned}\begin{align} -\frac{\mathrm{d}^2}{\mathrm{d}t^2}\log \lambda _\mu (\theta _ t) & =\frac1n\int _ X\operatorname {tr}(A_\chi ^2)\, \mathrm{d}\nu _\Omega . \end{align}\end{aligned}\]

26.4 Recovering a positive class from its Gauduchon derivative

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

By proposition 26.2.2, the derivative of $\lambda _\mu $ at $\theta \in \mathscr P$ is represented by a minimizing Gauduchon metric, which is determined up to a constant factor. The solution form is conformal to this metric and has a prescribed volume form, so the value and the derivative of $\lambda _\mu $ together reconstruct the solution form, and hence the background class. Fix $\theta \in \mathscr P$ and a minimizing metric $\gamma _\theta $.

For $n\geq 2$ define the smooth positive $(n-1,n-1)$-form

\[ \Xi _\theta =\frac{\gamma _\theta ^{n-1}}{D_\mu (\gamma _\theta )}. \]

Since numerator and denominator are both homogeneous of degree $n-1$ in $\gamma _\theta $, this form does not depend on the choice of the minimizing metric. It is $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-closed, and by proposition 26.2.2 it represents the derivative in every smooth real $(1,1)$-direction $\chi $:

\[ (\mathrm{d}\lambda _\mu )_\theta (\chi )=\int _ X\chi \wedge \Xi _\theta . \]

Proposition 26.4.1. Let $n\geq 2$ and $\theta \in \mathscr P$. The value $\lambda _\mu (\theta )$ and the derivative of $\lambda _\mu $ at $\theta $ on all smooth real $(1,1)$-forms together determine the background class of $\theta $, and the derivative alone determines this class up to multiplication by a positive constant. More precisely, if $\widehat\gamma $ is the positive $(1,1)$-form with $\widehat\gamma ^{n-1}=\Xi _\theta $, then

\[ D_\mu (\widehat\gamma )=1, \qquad \Omega _\theta = \lambda _\mu (\theta ) \left(\frac{\mu }{\widehat\gamma ^ n}\right)^{1/n} \widehat\gamma . \]

Moreover, assigning to a background class in $\mathscr P$ with $\lambda _\mu =1$ the ray of its minimizing Gauduchon metrics is a bijection onto the set of rays of Gauduchon metrics.

Proof. The values of the derivative on all smooth $(1,1)$-forms determine the smooth form $\Xi _\theta $. Its positive root exists and is unique by the positive-root construction preceding the proof of theorem 4.3.1. If $D=D_\mu (\gamma _\theta )$, this root is $\widehat\gamma =D^{-1/(n-1)}\gamma _\theta $, and the homogeneity of $D_\mu $ gives $D_\mu (\widehat\gamma )=1$.

By theorem 26.1.3, the solution form $\Omega _\theta $ is conformal to $\widehat\gamma $, and its volume form is $\lambda _\mu (\theta )^ n\mu $. These two requirements determine the conformal factor pointwise and give the formula for $\Omega _\theta $. Thus the value and the derivative recover $\Omega _\theta $, a representative of the class of $\theta $. Without the value, the same formula recovers $\Omega _\theta $ up to a positive constant factor. Conversely, multiplying the class by a positive constant multiplies $\Omega _\theta $ by the same constant and leaves its minimizing Gauduchon ray, and hence $\Xi _\theta $, unchanged; so the derivative cannot determine more than the ray of the class.

For the surjectivity in the last assertion, take any Gauduchon metric $\gamma $ and set

\[ \Omega _\gamma =\left(\frac\mu {\gamma ^ n}\right)^{1/n}\gamma . \]

This Hermitian form is unchanged by constant rescaling of $\gamma $ and satisfies $\Omega _\gamma ^ n=\mu $. The zero potential therefore solves the equation for the background $\Omega _\gamma $ with constant one, so its class has $\lambda _\mu =1$, and $\gamma $ is a minimizing Gauduchon metric by theorem 26.1.3. Injectivity follows from the recovery formula: two classes with $\lambda _\mu =1$ and the same Gauduchon ray have the same solution form. This proves the bijection. $\square$

In dimension one, a background class is determined by its integral, by the Green operator of the curve. The derivative is then the fixed functional $\chi \mapsto \int _ X\chi $, and the value alone determines the class.

26.5 The Hessian metric and its completion

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now study the metric $\mathfrak g$ of (26.3) on finite-dimensional cones of backgrounds, where it defines an ordinary Riemannian path distance. Homogeneity of $\lambda _\mu $ separates the scale of a positive class from its position on a level set of $\lambda _\mu $, and the logarithm of this scale turns out to be a Euclidean coordinate orthogonal to the level sets. The metric is therefore a product, and its completeness reduces to that of the unit-volume hypersurface.

Let $V$ be a finite-dimensional real vector space of smooth real $(1,1)$-forms such that

\[ V\cap \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}C^\infty (X,\mathbb R)=\{ 0\} , \qquad \mathscr C=V\cap \mathscr P\ne \varnothing . \]

Then $\mathscr C$ is an open convex cone in $V$. Write $\lambda =\lambda _\mu |_{\mathscr C}$, and let $g=-D^2\log \lambda $ be the restriction to $\mathscr C$ of the metric (26.3); it is a smooth Riemannian metric on $\mathscr C$ because $V$ meets $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}C^\infty (X,\mathbb R)$ only in zero. Put

\[ \mathscr S=\{ \theta \in \mathscr C:\lambda (\theta )=1\} , \]

and equip this smooth hypersurface with the induced metric $g_{\mathscr S}$.

Proposition 26.5.1. The map

\[ \Phi :\mathbb R\times \mathscr S\longrightarrow \mathscr C, \qquad (r,q)\longmapsto \mathrm{e}^ r q \]

is an isometry from the product metric $\mathrm{d}r^2+g_{\mathscr S}$ onto $g$. In particular, if $d_ g$ and $d_{\mathscr S}$ are the intrinsic distances, then

\[ d_ g(\mathrm{e}^{r_0}q_0,\mathrm{e}^{r_1}q_1)^2 =|r_1-r_0|^2+d_{\mathscr S}(q_0,q_1)^2. \]

The metric on $\mathscr C$ is complete if and only if the induced metric on $\mathscr S$ is complete. Every path for which $\lambda $ tends to zero or to infinity has infinite length.

Proof. Homogeneity gives $\lambda (s\theta )=s\lambda (\theta )$ for $s>0$. Hence $r=\log \lambda (\theta )$ and $q=\theta /\lambda (\theta )$ define the smooth inverse of $\Phi $. The level set is smooth because the Euler identity gives $D\lambda _\theta (\theta )=\lambda (\theta )>0$.

Differentiating the Euler identity $D\log \lambda _\theta (\theta )=1$ in a direction $\chi \in V$ gives

\[ g_\theta (\theta ,\chi )=D\log \lambda _\theta (\chi ), \qquad g_\theta (\theta ,\theta )=1. \]

For directions $\chi ,\eta \in V$, differentiating homogeneity twice also gives

\[ g_{s\theta }(s\chi ,s\eta )=g_\theta (\chi ,\eta ). \]

Let $q\in \mathscr S$, let $\xi ,\eta \in T_ q\mathscr S$, and let $a,b,r$ be real numbers. Since $\xi $ and $\eta $ are tangent to a level set of $\lambda $, the first of these identities shows that both are $g_ q$-orthogonal to the radial vector $q$. Using also the second identity and the scaling rule, we obtain

\[ g_{\mathrm{e}^ rq}\bigl (\mathrm{e}^ r(aq+\xi ),\mathrm{e}^ r(bq+\eta )\bigr ) =ab+g_ q(\xi ,\eta ), \]

which proves the metric identity.

It remains to prove the distance formula and its consequences. A path $(r(t),q(t))$ in the product has length $\int \sqrt{|r'|^2+|q'|_{g_{\mathscr S}}^2}\, \mathrm{d}t$. The triangle inequality in $\mathbb R^2$ (applied to the integral of the vector $(|r'|,|q'|_{g_{\mathscr S}})$) bounds this below by the square root of the sum of the squared endpoint difference in $r$ and the squared length of the projected path in $\mathscr S$. For the opposite bound, choose a path in $\mathscr S$ of length within $\epsilon $ of the distance of its endpoints, parametrize it with constant speed, and let $r$ vary linearly; then let $\epsilon $ decrease to zero. This proves the distance formula. It identifies Cauchy sequences in $\mathscr C$ with pairs of Cauchy sequences in the two factors; since $\mathbb R$ is complete, the completeness assertion follows. Finally, the length of any path is at least the total change of $\log \lambda $ along it, and this change is infinite when $\lambda $ tends to zero or to infinity. $\square$

26.5.1 Product and blowup examples

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The following two examples of closed classes show that the unit-volume hypersurface may be complete or may have a boundary at finite distance. The first also shows why the derivative is needed in proposition 26.4.1: knowing the volume for every smooth probability measure need not identify the class.

For the first example take $X=\mathbb P^1\times \mathbb P^1$, and let $\alpha ,\beta $ be the pulled-back Fubini–Study forms of integral one on their respective factors. They satisfy

\[ \alpha ^2=\beta ^2=0,\qquad \int _ X\alpha \wedge \beta =1. \]

For $a,b>0$ the form $\theta _{a,b}=a\alpha +b\beta $ is Kähler. Since it is closed, integrating the smooth equation gives, for every smooth positive probability measure $\mu $,

\[ \mathcal V_\mu (\theta _{a,b})=2ab, \qquad \lambda _\mu (\theta _{a,b})=\sqrt{2ab}. \]

Consequently, for real $t$, all the forms $\theta _{\mathrm{e}^ t,\mathrm{e}^{-t}}$ have volume $2$ for every $\mu $. Their background classes are nevertheless distinct: for any fixed point $p$ of the second factor, integration over $\mathbb P^1\times \{ p\} $ gives $\mathrm{e}^ t$, and this integral is unchanged by the addition of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f$.

By direct differentiation, the restriction of (26.3) to this two-dimensional cone is

\[ \mathfrak g=\frac12\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}a^2}{a^2}+\frac{\mathrm{d}b^2}{b^2}\right). \]

The coordinates $((\log a)/\sqrt2,(\log b)/\sqrt2)$ identify this metric with the Euclidean metric on $\mathbb R^2$, so the metric on this family is complete. The curve $a=\mathrm{e}^ t$, $b=\mathrm{e}^{-t}$ has unit speed. All distances here are intrinsic to this two-dimensional family.

In the second example, the blowup of $\mathbb P^2$ at a point, the cone is incomplete, and the boundary added by the completion consists of classes of positive volume.

Example 26.5.2. Let $\pi :X\to \mathbb P^2$ be the blowup at one point. Write $H$ for the pullback of the hyperplane class and $E$ for the exceptional divisor class. In the two-dimensional space they span, the cone of classes with a positive smooth representative is

\[ \mathscr C=\{ aH-bE:a>b>0\} . \]

These are closed classes, and we represent them by fixed smooth forms in order to apply proposition 26.5.1. Their intersection numbers are $H^2=1$, $H\cdot E=0$, $E^2=-1$. Consequently, for every smooth positive probability measure $\mu $,

\[ \lambda _\mu (aH-bE)=\sqrt{a^2-b^2}. \]

In the coordinates

\[ a=\mathrm{e}^ r\cosh s,\qquad b=\mathrm{e}^ r\sinh s, \qquad r\in \mathbb R,\quad s>0, \]

the Hessian metric is $\mathrm{d}r^2+\mathrm{d}s^2$. Its completion is therefore the Euclidean closed half-plane. The added boundary $s=0$ consists of the nef classes $\mathrm{e}^ rH$, each of positive volume $\mathrm{e}^{2r}$.

Proof. The projection from the blown-up point extends to a morphism $f:X\to \mathbb P^1$ with class $L=H-E$. The incidence map $(\pi ,f):X\to \mathbb P^2\times \mathbb P^1$ is an embedding. If $h$ and $\ell $ are pullbacks of Fubini–Study forms of integral one, then

\[ (a-b)h+b\ell \]

is positive for $a>b>0$, by the product embedding. Conversely, a positive representative has positive integrals over the exceptional curve and the strict transform of a line through the blown-up point. These integrals are respectively $b$ and $a-b$, proving the cone description. The same curves show that no nonzero linear combination in this two-dimensional space is smoothly $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-exact.

The intersection numbers follow from the hyperplane pullback and the normal bundle $\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^1}(-1)$ of the exceptional curve; equivalently $H^2=H\cdot L=1$ and $L^2=0$. Since the classes are closed, integrating the smooth equation gives $\mathcal V_\mu =a^2-b^2$, independently of $\mu $. Direct differentiation of $-\log \sqrt{a^2-b^2}$ gives

\[ g=\frac{(a^2+b^2)(\mathrm{d}a^2+\mathrm{d}b^2)-4ab\, \mathrm{d}a\, \mathrm{d}b}{(a^2-b^2)^2}. \]

Substituting the stated coordinates gives $\mathrm{d}r^2+\mathrm{d}s^2$. The coordinate domain is exactly $\mathbb R\times (0,\infty )$, so the intrinsic distance and completion are those of that Euclidean half-plane. Finally, $H$ has a semipositive pullback representative and is a limit of positive classes, hence is nef. Its square is one, but its integral over $E$ is zero, so it has no positive representative. This proves all assertions about the added boundary. $\square$

26.5.2 Invariant backgrounds on compact Lie-group quotients

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

On a compact quotient of a complex Lie group, the backgrounds with constant coefficients in an invariant coframe form a cone of nonclosed backgrounds on which everything can be computed explicitly. With respect to the normalized invariant volume, the root volume is the $n$th root of a determinant, the minimizing metric is the background itself, and the Hessian metric is the trace metric on positive Hermitian matrices. Its unit-volume hypersurface is complete, so the boundary of the cone of coefficient matrices lies at infinite distance after normalization.

Let $G$ be a connected complex Lie group of dimension $n\geq 2$, and let $X=\Gamma \backslash G$ be compact, where the discrete subgroup $\Gamma $ acts on the left. Choose a left-invariant holomorphic coframe $\alpha _1,\ldots ,\alpha _ n$, which descends to $X$. For a Hermitian matrix $A$ put

\[ \begin{gathered} \theta _ A=\frac{\mathrm i}{2\pi }\sum _{j,k}A_{jk}\alpha _ j\wedge \bar\alpha _ k, \qquad h_0=\theta _ I,\\ H=\int _ Xh_0^ n,\qquad \mu =\frac{h_0^ n}{H}. \end{gathered} \]

Here $\operatorname {Herm}(n)$ is the real vector space of Hermitian $n\times n$ matrices, and $A>0$ (respectively $A\geq 0$) means that $A$ is positive definite (respectively semidefinite). Fix a real linear subspace $W\subset \operatorname {Herm}(n)$ containing a positive matrix, and write $V_ W=\{ \theta _ A:A\in W\} $. Throughout this subsection, the probability form is the normalized invariant volume $\mu $ just defined.

Proposition 26.5.3. The backgrounds in $V_ W\cap \mathscr P$ are exactly the forms $\theta _ A$ with $A\in W$ and $A>0$. Moreover,

\[ V_ W\cap \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}C^\infty (X,\mathbb R)=\{ 0\} . \]

Every positive $\theta _ A$ is balanced, and its unique minimizing Gauduchon metric normalized by $D_\mu =1$ is

26.4
\begin{equation} \lambda _\mu (\theta _ A)=(H\det A)^{1/n},\qquad \gamma _ A=\frac{\theta _ A}{\lambda _\mu (\theta _ A)},\qquad \gamma _ A^ n=\mu . \end{equation}

On this coefficient cone the Hessian metric is

26.5
\begin{equation} g_ A(U,Z)=\frac1n\operatorname {tr}(A^{-1}UA^{-1}Z), \qquad U,Z\in W. \end{equation}

For $A\in W$, there is a real distribution $u$ with $\theta _ A+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0$ exactly when $A\geq 0$. The closure of $\{ A\in W:A>0\} $ is $W\cap \{ A\geq 0\} $, and each of its points $A$ is represented by the smooth semipositive form $\theta _ A$. For $A=0$, the only positive current $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ is zero. If $G$ is nonabelian, every positive background in this cone is nonclosed, and adding a smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ term never makes it closed.

Proof. Invariant averages and balancedness. Let $Z_1,\ldots ,Z_ n$ be the invariant holomorphic vector fields dual to $\alpha _1,\ldots ,\alpha _ n$, and set $\Upsilon =\alpha _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \alpha _ n$. This holomorphic top-degree form and its conjugate are closed. For an invariant holomorphic vector field $Z$, evaluation on the invariant frame gives

\[ \mathcal L_ Z\Upsilon =-\operatorname {tr}(\operatorname {ad}Z)\Upsilon , \qquad \mathcal L_ Z\bar\Upsilon =0. \]

The second identity also follows from Cartan's formula. Since $h_0^ n$ is a positive constant multiple of the appropriately oriented form $\Upsilon \wedge \bar\Upsilon $, Stokes' theorem on compact $X$ gives

\[ 0=\int _ X\mathcal L_ Zh_0^ n=-H\operatorname {tr}(\operatorname {ad}Z). \]

Thus the fields $Z_ j$ and their conjugates have zero divergence with respect to $\mu $. In particular,

26.6
\begin{equation} \int _ XZ_ jf\, \mu =\int _ X\bar Z_ jf\, \mu =0 \qquad (f\in C^\infty (X,\mathbb C)). \end{equation}

Cartan's formula also gives $\mathrm d(\iota _{Z_ j}\Upsilon )=0$. These contractions span the invariant $(n-1,0)$-forms. The form $\theta _ A^{n-1}$ is a constant linear combination of their wedges with conjugates, so $\mathrm d\theta _ A^{n-1}=0$.

The holomorphic fields commute with the antiholomorphic fields, and $\mathrm d\alpha _ j$ has type $(2,0)$. The coefficient matrix of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ is therefore $(Z_ j\bar Z_ k u)$. For smooth $u$ its entries have $\mu $-mean zero by (26.6), so the matrix of $\mu $-means of the coefficients of $\theta _ A+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ is $A$. If $\theta _ A+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u>0$, averaging the positive coefficient matrix gives $A>0$. The converse follows by taking $u=0$. The same average shows that $V_ W$ contains no nonzero smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ direction.

For a distribution $u$, the adjoints of $Z_ j$ and $\bar Z_ k$ with respect to $\mu $ annihilate constants, so the mean of each $Z_ j\bar Z_ k u$ is still zero. The coefficients of a positive current in this global frame form a positive matrix of measures, whose matrix of total masses is semipositive; for $\theta _ A+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0$ this gives $A\geq 0$. Conversely, $\theta _ A$ itself is a positive current when $A\geq 0$. If $A=0$, the positive trace measure of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ has total mass zero, and polarization then makes the entire current zero. Finally, for a fixed positive $A_+\in W$, the matrices $A+\varepsilon A_+$ with $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ approximate every semipositive $A\in W$ from the positive cone. This proves the closure assertion.

The quotient and its equality case. Put $Q=H\det A$, so that $\theta _ A^ n=Q\mu $. For every smooth positive Hermitian form $\gamma $, the matrix arithmetic–geometric mean inequality gives

\[ \frac{\theta _ A\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}}{\gamma ^ n} =\frac1n\operatorname {tr}_\gamma \theta _ A \geq \left(\frac{\theta _ A^ n}{\gamma ^ n}\right)^{1/n}. \]

Integration yields $\int _ X\theta _ A\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}\geq Q^{1/n}D_\mu (\gamma )$, which bounds every Gauduchon quotient in definition 26.1.1 from below. The balanced, hence Gauduchon, metric $\gamma =\theta _ A$ has $D_\mu (\theta _ A)=Q^{(n-1)/n}$ and attains equality. This proves the formula for $\lambda _\mu (\theta _ A)$.

For a minimizing Gauduchon metric $\gamma $, the continuous nonnegative difference of the two sides of the pointwise inequality has integral zero. Pointwise equality then forces $\gamma =f\theta _ A$ for a smooth positive function $f$. Set $q=f^{n-1}$ and $T=\theta _ A^{n-1}$. Since $\mathrm dT=0$, the Gauduchon equation becomes $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q\wedge T=0$, and

\[ 0=\int _ Xq\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q\wedge T=-\int _ X\mathrm dq\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}q\wedge T. \]

The last integrand is nonnegative and vanishes exactly where $\mathrm dq=0$. Thus $f$ is constant. As $n\geq 2$ and

\[ D_\mu (c\theta _ A)=c^{n-1}\lambda _\mu (\theta _ A)^{n-1}, \]

normalization determines $c=\lambda _\mu (\theta _ A)^{-1}$. This proves the unique minimizer and its volume identity.

Differentiating $-\log \lambda _\mu (\theta _ A)=-(\log H+\log \det A)/n$ twice gives (26.5). The corresponding squared norm of $U$ is $\| A^{-1/2}UA^{-1/2}\| _{\mathrm F}^2/n$, which is positive for $U\ne 0$; here $\| B\| _{\mathrm F}^2=\operatorname {tr}(B^*B)$ denotes the squared Frobenius norm.

Finally,

\[ \partial \theta _ A=\frac{\mathrm i}{2\pi }\sum _{j,k}A_{jk}\, \mathrm d\alpha _ j\wedge \bar\alpha _ k. \]

For invertible $A$, the vanishing of this form forces every $\mathrm d\alpha _ j=0$, by independence of the conjugate coframe, and the Lie algebra would then be abelian. Since adding $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ does not change $\mathrm d\theta _ A$, this proves the last assertion. $\square$

Lemma 26.5.4. On the cone of all positive definite Hermitian $n\times n$ matrices, the Riemannian distance of the metric (26.5) is

26.7
\begin{equation} d_{\mathrm{full}}(A,B)=\frac1{\sqrt n} \| \log (A^{-1/2}BA^{-1/2})\| _{\mathrm F}. \end{equation}

For endpoints with equal determinant, this is also the intrinsic distance in the hypersurface of all positive definite matrices with that determinant.

Proof. By the trace formula, the congruence $M\mapsto P^*MP$ by a fixed invertible matrix $P$ is an isometry. Taking $P=A^{-1/2}$, we may assume that the endpoints are $I$ and $C=A^{-1/2}BA^{-1/2}>0$. The curve $\exp (t\log C)$, $0\leq t\leq 1$, has constant speed $\| \log C\| _{\mathrm F}/\sqrt n$, which gives the upper bound for the distance.

For the reverse inequality, diagonalization gives the convergent resolvent formula for the matrix logarithm and its derivative

\begin{align*} \log M& =\int _0^\infty \bigl ((1+s)^{-1}I-(M+sI)^{-1}\bigr )\, \mathrm ds,\\ D\log _ M[U]& =\int _0^\infty (M+sI)^{-1}U(M+sI)^{-1}\, \mathrm ds. \end{align*}

On a neighborhood of $M$ where the spectrum is bounded below by a positive constant, differentiation under the integral sign is justified by an integrable bound. In a unitary basis diagonalizing $M$, with eigenvalues $d_ i>0$, the derivative has entries $c_{ij}U_{ij}$, where

\[ c_{ij}=\frac{\log d_ i-\log d_ j}{d_ i-d_ j}\quad (d_ i\ne d_ j), \qquad c_{ij}=\frac1{d_ i}\quad (d_ i=d_ j). \]

Writing $d_ i/d_ j=\mathrm e^{2s}$ gives $\sqrt{d_ id_ j}|c_{ij}|=|s/\sinh s|\leq 1$, including the limiting value at $s=0$. Hence

\[ \| D\log _ M[U]\| _{\mathrm F} \leq \| M^{-1/2}UM^{-1/2}\| _{\mathrm F}. \]

Applying this along a piecewise $C^1$ path of positive matrices from $I$ to $C$ and integrating, we bound its length below by $\| \log C\| _{\mathrm F}/\sqrt n$. This proves the distance formula. In terms of the original endpoints, the length-minimizing curve is

26.8
\begin{equation} M(t)=A^{1/2}(A^{-1/2}BA^{-1/2})^ tA^{1/2}. \end{equation}

Its determinant is $(\det A)^{1-t}(\det B)^ t$, so for endpoints with equal determinant it stays in the fixed-determinant hypersurface. $\square$

Write $\lambda (A)=\lambda _\mu (\theta _ A)$ and

\[ S_ W=\{ A\in W:A>0,\ H\det A=1\} . \]

The radial derivative of $\lambda $ is positive, so $S_ W$ is a smooth hypersurface of the cone $\{ A\in W:A>0\} $. We write $d_ W$ for the intrinsic path distance of $S_ W$ with the metric induced by (26.5).

Theorem 26.5.5. The metric space $(S_ W,d_ W)$ is complete and proper, and a sequence in $S_ W$ is Cauchy exactly when its coefficient matrices converge to a positive matrix in $S_ W$. For $A,B\in S_ W$,

26.9
\begin{align} d_ W(A,B)& \geq \frac1{\sqrt n} \| \log (A^{-1/2}BA^{-1/2})\| _{\mathrm F},\\ d_ W(A,B)& \leq \frac{\| A-B\| _{\mathrm F}}{\sqrt n\min \{ \lambda _{\min }(A),\lambda _{\min }(B)\} }. \end{align}

The normalized minimizing metrics of (26.4) satisfy, pointwise on $X$,

26.11
\begin{equation} \mathrm e^{-\sqrt n d_ W(A,B)}\gamma _ A \leq \gamma _ B\leq \mathrm e^{\sqrt n d_ W(A,B)}\gamma _ A. \end{equation}

In particular, the completion of $S_ W$ adds no point, and no point of the boundary of the coefficient cone is a limit of a Cauchy sequence.

If $A_ j>0$ in $W$ converges to a nonzero singular semipositive $A_0$, then $\widehat A_ j=A_ j/\lambda (A_ j)$ lies in $S_ W$ and, for fixed $A_\star \in S_ W$,

26.12
\begin{equation} d_ W(A_\star ,\widehat A_ j) \geq \frac{|\log \det A_ j|}{n\sqrt n}+O(1) \longrightarrow \infty . \end{equation}

By contrast, a sequence of coefficient matrices tending to zero can normalize to a point of $S_ W$.

Proof. Paths in $W$. Every path in $S_ W$ is a path in the hypersurface of all positive matrices with $H\det A=1$, so lemma 26.5.4 gives (26.9). To obtain an upper bound by paths inside $W$, put

\[ U=B-A,\qquad C(t)=A+tU,\qquad N(t)=\frac{C(t)}{\lambda (C(t))}. \]

Since the positive cone is convex and $W$ is linear, $N(t)$ stays in $S_ W$ and joins $A$ to $B$. With $K=C^{-1/2}UC^{-1/2}$, differentiation gives

\[ \begin{gathered} g_ N(N',N')=\frac1n\operatorname {tr}(K^2) -\frac1{n^2}(\operatorname {tr}K)^2 \leq \frac{\| U\| _{\mathrm F}^2}{nm^2},\\ m=\min \{ \lambda _{\min }(A),\lambda _{\min }(B)\} . \end{gathered} \]

Indeed $C(t)\geq mI$ and $(\log \lambda (C))'=\operatorname {tr}(C^{-1}U)/n$. Integrating proves (26.10), and shows in particular that $S_ W$ is connected.

Put $d=d_ W(A,B)$. Every eigenvalue of $A^{-1/2}BA^{-1/2}$ has a logarithm of absolute value at most $\| \log (A^{-1/2}BA^{-1/2})\| _{\mathrm F}\leq \sqrt n\, d$, by (26.9). Hence

26.13
\begin{equation} \mathrm e^{-\sqrt n d}A\leq B\leq \mathrm e^{\sqrt n d}A. \end{equation}

Since $\gamma _ A=\theta _ A$ on $S_ W$, this proves (26.11).

Cauchy sequences and bounded balls. For a Cauchy sequence $(A_ j)$ in $(S_ W,d_ W)$, comparison with one fixed element of the sequence, using (26.13), gives $mI\leq A_ j\leq MI$ with fixed positive $m,M$. Comparing two elements then gives

\[ \| A_ j-A_ k\| _{\mathrm{op}} \leq M\bigl (\mathrm e^{\sqrt n d_ W(A_ j,A_ k)}-1\bigr ), \]

where $\| \cdot \| _{\mathrm{op}}$ is the operator norm. The coefficient matrices therefore form a Cauchy sequence. Their limit lies in the closed linear space $W$, is positive, and satisfies $H\det A=1$, and the upper bound (26.10) proves convergence to that point in $d_ W$. Conversely, coefficient convergence to a positive point gives convergence in $d_ W$ by the same bound. This proves completeness and the Cauchy criterion. The minimizing forms then converge in $C^\infty $, because their coefficients multiply a fixed smooth coframe.

By these estimates, $d_ W$ induces the topology of coefficient convergence. A closed ball of radius $R$ around $A_\star $ is contained in the compact matrix interval

\[ \begin{gathered} \mathrm e^{-\sqrt nR}A_\star \leq A\leq \mathrm e^{\sqrt nR}A_\star ,\\ A\in W,\qquad H\det A=1. \end{gathered} \]

The ball is closed in that interval, hence compact. This proves properness. In particular, distances from a fixed reference point are bounded exactly when the eigenvalues relative to that point are bounded above and away from zero.

The coefficient boundary. Write $\lambda _{\max }$ for the largest eigenvalue. If $A_ j\to A_0$ with $A_0\geq 0$ singular and nonzero, then $\lambda (A_ j)\to 0$ while $\lambda _{\max }(A_ j)$ has a positive limit, so the largest eigenvalue of $\widehat A_ j$ tends to infinity. Applying (26.13) to $A_\star ,\widehat A_ j$ gives

\[ \begin{aligned} d_ W(A_\star ,\widehat A_ j) & \geq \frac1{\sqrt n}\log \frac{\lambda _{\max }(A_ j)}{\lambda (A_ j)\lambda _{\max }(A_\star )}\\ & =\frac{|\log \det A_ j|}{n\sqrt n}+O(1). \end{aligned} \]

The error term is bounded because $A_0\ne 0$. This proves (26.12). For comparison, $A_ j=t_ jA_\star $ with $t_ j\downarrow 0$ normalizes to $A_\star $ for every $j$; its coefficient limit, the zero matrix, gives no new point of the completion. $\square$

By proposition 26.5.1, the whole cone $\{ \theta _ A:A\in W,\ A>0\} $ is then complete as well. The argument uses the invariant probability and the linearity of $W$; in other geometries the unit-volume hypersurface can have a boundary at finite distance, as in example 26.5.2.

The length-minimizing path (26.8) need not stay in a proper subspace $W$. For $n=3$, take

\[ W=\operatorname {span}_{\mathbb R}\{ I,\operatorname {diag}(0,1,2)\} , \qquad a=H^{-1/3}. \]

The points $aI$ and $a6^{-1/3}\operatorname {diag}(1,2,3)$ of $S_ W$ have, on the path (26.8), the midpoint $a6^{-1/6}\operatorname {diag}(1,\sqrt2,\sqrt3)$. The diagonal entries fail the relation $x_1-2x_2+x_3=0$, since $1-2\sqrt2+\sqrt3\ne 0$. Thus this path leaves $W$, whereas the normalized segment $N(t)$ used in the proof remains in it.

Example 26.5.6. On $G=\mathbb C^3$ take the multiplication

\[ z\cdot w=(z_1+w_1,z_2+w_2,z_3+w_3+z_1w_2), \]

the Gaussian lattice $\Gamma =\mathbb Z[\mathrm i]^3$, and $X=\Gamma \backslash G$. This is a compact non-Kähler manifold of dimension three, with invariant coframe

\[ \alpha _1=\mathrm dz_1,\qquad \alpha _2=\mathrm dz_2,\qquad \alpha _3=\mathrm dz_3-z_1\mathrm dz_2. \]

For its full nine-dimensional cone $W=\operatorname {Herm}(3)$ and the normalized invariant probability,

\[ H=\frac6{\pi ^3},\qquad \mathcal V_\mu (\theta _ A)=\frac6{\pi ^3}\det A,\qquad S=\{ A>0:\det A=\pi ^3/6\} . \]

Every positive background is nonclosed. The eight-dimensional unit-volume hypersurface is complete and proper, and its intrinsic distance is

26.16
\begin{equation} d_ S(A,B)=\frac1{\sqrt3}\| \log (A^{-1/2}BA^{-1/2})\| _{\mathrm F}. \end{equation}

Proof. The bilinear cross term makes the multiplication associative, with identity zero and inverse $(-z_1,-z_2,-z_3+z_1z_2)$. These operations preserve the Gaussian lattice. Its action by left multiplication is free and properly discontinuous: for a compact set $K\subset G$, elements $\gamma $ with $\gamma K\cap K\ne \varnothing $ belong to the compact set $KK^{-1}$, containing only finitely many lattice elements.

Choose Gaussian integers $\gamma _1,\gamma _2$ that move $z_1,z_2$ into the half-open unit square, and then $\gamma _3$ that moves $z_3+\gamma _1z_2$ into it. This gives a representative in the real six-dimensional unit cube. If two points of the half-open cube are equivalent, then $\gamma _1=\gamma _2=0$ and then $\gamma _3=0$. The cube is therefore a measurable fundamental domain, and $X$ is compact, being the image of its closure.

This coframe is left-invariant, descends to $X$, and has

\[ \mathrm d\alpha _3=-\alpha _1\wedge \alpha _2,\qquad \alpha _1\wedge \alpha _2\wedge \alpha _3 =\mathrm dz_1\wedge \mathrm dz_2\wedge \mathrm dz_3. \]

The density of $h_0^3$ is $6/\pi ^3$ times real Lebesgue volume on the fundamental cube, giving the stated $H$. The coefficient of $\alpha _1\wedge \alpha _2\wedge \bar\alpha _3$ in $\partial \theta _ A$ is $-\mathrm iA_{33}/(2\pi )$, so positivity makes every such background nonclosed.

To see that $X$ admits no Kähler metric at all, suppose that a positive closed $(1,1)$-form $\kappa $ existed, and average its coefficient functions in this coframe against $\mu $. Their mean is a positive matrix $A$. In the coefficients of $\partial \kappa $, derivative terms have mean zero by (26.6); the remaining means are exactly the coefficients of $\partial \theta _ A$. Closedness would force them to vanish, contradicting $A_{33}>0$. Thus $X$ is non-Kähler.

The assertions about the volume, the minimizers and completeness follow from proposition 26.5.3, theorem 26.5.5. As $W$ is the full matrix space, the equal-determinant exponential path stays in $S$, and lemma 26.5.4 gives (26.16). For $A_\star =aI$, $a=(\pi ^3/6)^{1/3}$, this becomes

\[ d_ S(A_\star ,A)^2=\frac13\sum _{j=1}^3 \left(\log \frac{\lambda _ j(A)}a\right)^2. \]

The three logarithms sum to zero.

For a concrete degeneration, take $A_\varepsilon =\operatorname {diag}(1,1,\varepsilon )$, $0<\varepsilon \leq 1$. As $\varepsilon \to 0$, the coefficients converge to the nonzero smooth semipositive form $\theta _{\operatorname {diag}(1,1,0)}$. The normalized matrices and their distances, on the other hand, are

26.17
\begin{equation} \begin{gathered} \widehat A_\varepsilon =a\operatorname {diag}(\varepsilon ^{-1/3},\varepsilon ^{-1/3},\varepsilon ^{2/3}),\\ d_ S(A_\star ,\widehat A_\varepsilon )=\frac{\sqrt2}{3}|\log \varepsilon |. \end{gathered} \end{equation}

Thus, after normalization, the boundary form $\theta _{\operatorname {diag}(1,1,0)}$ lies at infinite distance. $\square$

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory
26.10
\[\begin{aligned}\begin{align} d_ W(A,B)& \leq \frac{\| A-B\| _{\mathrm F}}{\sqrt n\min \{ \lambda _{\min }(A),\lambda _{\min }(B)\} }. \end{align}\end{aligned}\]

Masses and reference densities in dimension three

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

In dimension three, an invariant positive background has a nonnegative complex Hessian. Together with the mass identities for Gauduchon backgrounds, this determines its upper Monge–Ampère mass and shows that the singular semipositive coefficient limits have zero top mass. On the Iwasawa quotient, the same calculation identifies exactly which smooth reference probabilities keep the determinant formula for the volume.

We keep the invariant coframe, $h_0$, $H$ and $\theta _ A$ of section 26.5.2, and now assume $n=3$. Write $\mu _0=h_0^3/H$ to distinguish this invariant probability from other reference measures, and let $\mathscr M$ be the set of smooth strictly positive probability forms on $X$. Recall that $v_+$ denotes the upper Monge–Ampère mass of definition 6.5.1.

Proposition 26.5.7. For $A>0$ on any such compact complex Lie-group quotient,

26.18
\begin{equation} v_+(\theta _ A)=H\det A =\max _{\nu \in \mathscr M}\mathcal V_\nu (\theta _ A). \end{equation}

The invariant probability $\mu _0$ attains this maximum. In particular the quotient has the bounded mass property.

Proof. Since the forms $\mathrm d\alpha _ j$ are holomorphic, we have

26.19
\begin{equation} \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\theta _ A=\frac1{4\pi ^2} \sum _{j,k}A_{jk}\, \mathrm d\alpha _ j\wedge \mathrm d\bar\alpha _ k\geq 0. \end{equation}

Indeed $\bar\partial (\alpha _ j\wedge \bar\alpha _ k) =-\alpha _ j\wedge \mathrm d\bar\alpha _ k$, and applying $\partial $ and multiplying by $(\mathrm i/(2\pi ))^2$ gives the identity in (26.19). Diagonalizing the positive coefficient matrix expresses the result as a sum of nonnegative multiples of $\beta \wedge \bar\beta $ for invariant $(2,0)$-forms $\beta $. In dimension three these are nonnegative $(2,2)$-forms: the wedge product with $\mathrm i\xi \wedge \bar\xi $, for a $(1,0)$-form $\xi $, has the squared modulus of $\xi \wedge \beta $ as coefficient.

By proposition 26.5.3, $\theta _ A$ is balanced, hence Gauduchon. The maximum case of theorem 26.15.3 therefore gives $v_+(\theta _ A)=\int _ X\theta _ A^3=H\det A$. The supremum of $\mathcal V_\nu (\theta _ A)$ over $\nu \in \mathscr M$ equals this upper mass by theorem 26.14.3, and (26.4) shows that it is attained at $\mu _0$. Finally, proposition 6.5.2 makes the finiteness of the upper mass a property of the manifold. $\square$

Corollary 26.5.8. If $A\geq 0$ is singular, then every bounded $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ A)$ satisfies

\[ \int _ X(\theta _ A+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^3=0. \]

For every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta _ A)$, bounded or not, the non-pluripolar top power of $\theta _ A+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ is also zero. Thus the nonzero semipositive boundary forms $\theta _ A$ of proposition 26.5.3 have zero non-pluripolar top mass.

Proof. For $\varepsilon >0$, the same bounded $u$ is $\theta _{A+\varepsilon I}$-plurisubharmonic, so the preceding proposition bounds its mass by $H\det (A+\varepsilon I)$, which tends to zero with $\varepsilon $. By the multilinear construction of bounded products in proposition 1.7.1,

\[ (\theta _{A+\varepsilon I}+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^3 =\bigl (\theta _ A+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u+\varepsilon h_0\bigr )^3 \]

is a polynomial in $\varepsilon $ whose coefficients are mixed products of finite mass. Its total mass therefore converges to $\int _ X(\theta _ A+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^3$ as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, so this mass is zero, and positivity makes the measure zero.

For unbounded $u$, we subtract a constant so that $u\leq 0$. Since $\theta _ A\geq 0$, the truncations $u_ k=\max (u,-k)$ are bounded and $\theta _ A$-plurisubharmonic, so their top products vanish by the bounded case. Restricting them to $\{ u>-k\} $ and passing to the limit that defines the non-pluripolar product gives zero as well. $\square$

We now specialize to the Iwasawa quotient of example 26.5.6. Put

\[ E=\mathbb C/\mathbb Z[\mathrm i],\qquad B=E^2,\qquad p:X\longrightarrow B,\quad [z]\longmapsto ([z_1],[z_2]). \]

This is a holomorphic submersion with connected elliptic fibers, and the coordinate description of the fundamental cube gives $p_*\mu _0=\mu _ B$, where $\mu _ B$ is the Haar probability on $B$. A smooth probability is called fiber invariant if it has the form

\[ \mu _ f=(f\circ p)\mu _0,\qquad f\in C^\infty (B),\quad f>0,\quad \int _ B f\, \mu _ B=1. \]

Thus a smooth probability is fiber invariant when its density relative to $\mu _0$ is constant along the elliptic fibers of $p$; no invariance in the base directions is required.

Theorem 26.5.9. For every $A>0$ and fiber-invariant $\mu _ f$,

26.20
\begin{equation} \mathcal V_{\mu _ f}(\theta _ A)=H\det A, \qquad H=\frac6{\pi ^3}. \end{equation}

Its unique minimizing Gauduchon metric normalized by $D_{\mu _ f}=1$ is

26.21
\begin{equation} \gamma _{A,f}=\frac{\theta _ A+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(p^*v_{A,f})}{(H\det A)^{1/3}}, \qquad \gamma _{A,f}^3=\mu _ f, \end{equation}

where $v_{A,f}$ is the solution of the Monge–Ampère equation (26.22) on the torus $B$, with a closed background, constructed in the proof. Conversely, for a fixed $A>0$, the probabilities $\nu \in \mathscr M$ maximizing $\mathcal V_\nu (\theta _ A)$ are exactly the fiber-invariant ones.

For each fixed real linear $W\subset \operatorname {Herm}(3)$ containing a positive matrix, the Hessian metric for $\mu _ f$ is

\[ g_ A(U,Z)=\frac13\operatorname {tr}(A^{-1}UA^{-1}Z). \]

Its unit-volume hypersurface $S_ W=\{ A\in W:A>0,\ H\det A=1\} $ is complete and proper, and the Cauchy criterion and the distance bounds of theorem 26.5.5 hold with $n=3$.

Proof. The map $p$ is well defined because multiplication by lattice elements adds Gaussian integers to the first two coordinates. It is a submersion, and fixing representatives of those coordinates identifies each fiber with the central quotient $E$. Integrating first in the third coordinate over the fundamental cube proves the pushforward identity.

The closed equation on the base. Write the positive Hermitian matrix in block form and take its Schur complement:

\[ \begin{gathered} A=\begin{pmatrix} C & b \\ b^* & a \end{pmatrix},\\ a=A_{33}>0,\qquad S=C-a^{-1}bb^*>0. \end{gathered} \]

On $B$, let

\[ \omega _ S=\frac{\mathrm i}{2\pi }\sum _{j,k=1}^2 S_{jk}\, \mathrm dz_ j\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ k, \qquad Q_ B=\int _ B\omega _ S^2=\frac2{\pi ^2}\det S. \]

We apply theorem 9.1.1 to this closed background with the density $Q_ Bf\mu _ B$. Since $\omega _ S$ is closed and the density has total mass $Q_ B$, Stokes' theorem forces the constant in the equation to be one. We obtain a smooth real $v=v_{A,f}$, unique up to an additive constant, such that

26.22
\begin{equation} (\omega _ S+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^2=Q_ Bf\mu _ B, \qquad \omega _ S+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v>0. \end{equation}

If $K$ is the coefficient matrix of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ in the base coframe, this says $\det (S+K)=(\det S)f$. The form

\[ \Omega =\theta _ A+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(p^*v) \]

has coefficient matrix $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C+K& b\\ b^*& a\end{smallmatrix}\right)$ in the invariant coframe. Since $a>0$ and the Schur complement $S+K$ is positive, $\Omega >0$; its determinant is $a\det (S+K)=(\det A)(f\circ p)$. Consequently $\Omega ^3=(H\det A)\mu _ f$.

This solution form is also balanced. Put $\beta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(p^*v)$, which is closed and uses only $\alpha _1,\alpha _2$ and their conjugates. Since $\mathrm d\alpha _3=-\alpha _1\wedge \alpha _2$, every term of $\partial \theta _ A$ contains $\alpha _1\wedge \alpha _2$, and every conjugate term contains $\bar\alpha _1\wedge \bar\alpha _2$. Thus $\mathrm d\theta _ A\wedge \beta =0$. Together with $\mathrm d\theta _ A^2=0$, this proves

\[ \mathrm d\Omega ^2=\mathrm d\theta _ A^2+2\mathrm d\theta _ A\wedge \beta =0. \]

Theorem 26.1.3 now gives (26.20). Its characterization of the minimizers, the balancedness of $\Omega $ and the homogeneity of degree two of $D_{\mu _ f}$ give the unique normalized minimizer (26.21).

All maximizing densities. Let $Z_3=\partial /\partial z_3$ be dual to $\alpha _3$, and set $\tau _ j=(\mathrm i/(2\pi ))\alpha _ j\wedge \bar\alpha _ j$. Directly from (26.19),

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\theta _ A=A_{33}\tau _1\wedge \tau _2, \qquad h_0^3=6\tau _1\wedge \tau _2\wedge \tau _3. \]

Since $\theta _ A$ is Gauduchon, the identity (26.92) therefore becomes, for every smooth real $u$,

26.23
\begin{equation} \int _ X(\theta _ A+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^3 =H\det A-\frac{A_{33}H}{2}\int _ X|Z_3u|^2\mu _0. \end{equation}

Here $\mathrm du\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}u=(\mathrm i/(2\pi ))\partial u\wedge \bar\partial u$, and wedging with $\tau _1\wedge \tau _2$ selects its coefficient on $\alpha _3\wedge \bar\alpha _3$; this also accounts for the factor $1/2$.

For an arbitrary $\nu \in \mathscr M$, let $u_\nu $ be the smooth solution of the equation for $\theta _ A$ and $\nu $. Its mass equals $\mathcal V_\nu (\theta _ A)$ by theorem 26.1.3. By (26.23), this mass equals $H\det A$ exactly when $Z_3u_\nu =0$. As $u_\nu $ is real, its derivatives in both real directions of each elliptic fiber then vanish, and connectedness of the fibers gives $u_\nu =p^*v$ for a smooth $v$ on $B$. The density of $(\theta _ A+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\nu )^3$ relative to $\mu _0$ is then a function on $B$, so the equation makes $\nu $ fiber invariant. The converse was proved by the construction on the base.

Completion in the background parameters. For fixed $f$, formula (26.20) holds on the entire cone of positive matrices, and differentiating the logarithm of its cube root twice gives the stated Hessian metric. Since this is the same metric as in theorem 26.5.5, the matrix-path and coefficient-limit arguments of that proof apply unchanged on each fixed $W$. They give the distance bounds, completeness, properness and the Cauchy criterion; they do not use that the minimizing forms have constant coefficients. $\square$

For the minimizing metrics $\gamma _{A,f}$, the pointwise estimate (26.11) is replaced by an estimate for coefficient means. For a form $\eta $ whose coefficient matrix in the invariant coframe is $(\eta _{jk})$, write $\overline\eta $ for the matrix of means $\int _ X\eta _{jk}\, \mu _0$. The mean-zero identity (26.6) gives

\[ \overline{\gamma }_{A,f}=\frac{A}{(H\det A)^{1/3}}. \]

Thus on $S_ W$ the distance bounds control these means, and along a normalized sequence whose coefficients converge to a nonzero singular semipositive matrix the largest eigenvalue of the means diverges. The corresponding minimizing metrics then cannot be bounded above by a fixed multiple of $h_0$. For $f\ne 1$, however, we do not obtain the pointwise bound (26.11) for the nonconstant forms $\gamma _{A,f}$.

Reference densities that vary along the fibers give a strictly smaller volume. Choose a smooth real $\chi $ supported in a coordinate ball with $Z_3\chi \not\equiv 0$, and let $t\ne 0$ be sufficiently small that $\theta _ A+t\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi >0$. Put

\[ M(t)=\int _ X(\theta _ A+t\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi )^3, \qquad \nu _ t=\frac{(\theta _ A+t\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi )^3}{M(t)}. \]

Then $t\chi $ solves the equation for $\theta _ A$ and $\nu _ t$ with constant $M(t)$, so theorem 26.1.3 and (26.23) give

\[ \mathcal V_{\nu _ t}(\theta _ A) =H\det A-\frac{A_{33}H}{2}t^2\int _ X|Z_3\chi |^2\mu _0 <H\det A. \]

Here $\nu _ t$ is constructed from the fixed background $\theta _ A$. The example therefore shows that the volume depends on the reference measure, but it does not determine the Hessian completion for a fixed density that varies along the fibers.

26.5.3 Warped product cones and flat completion

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The invariant cones above have complete unit-volume hypersurfaces. On the product of a compact Kähler manifold with a curve, a nonconstant coefficient in the fiber direction gives cones of nonclosed backgrounds whose unit-volume hypersurfaces are flat and complete instead to a strip or a half-plane. The volume is the reciprocal of an integral of the reciprocal fiber coefficient, and the variance of that reciprocal coefficient measures the distance to the boundary. The probability measure in this variance is a trace of a minimizing balanced metric.

Let $(B,\kappa )$ be a connected compact Kähler manifold of dimension $m\geq 2$, with $\int _ B\kappa ^ m=1$. Let $C$ be a connected compact complex curve with a smooth positive $(1,1)$-form $w$ of integral one. Fix a smooth strictly positive probability form $\sigma $ on $B$ and a smooth nonconstant real function $a$ on $B$ with $\min a=0$ and $\max a=M>0$. On $X=B\times C$, of dimension $n=m+1$, put $\mu =\sigma \wedge w$; we omit pullbacks from the notation. For real parameters $b,c,e$, the backgrounds in the real span of $\kappa ,aw,w$ are

\[ \theta _{b,c,e}=b\kappa +Lw,\qquad L=ca+e. \]

Theorem 26.5.10. This span contains no nonzero smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ direction, and its intersection with $\mathscr P$ is the cone

\[ \mathscr C=\{ b>0,\ e>0,\ e+cM>0\} . \]

For $\theta =\theta _{b,c,e}\in \mathscr C$, let $H(c,e)=\int _ B\sigma /L$. Then

26.24
\begin{equation} \mathcal V_\mu (\theta )=\frac{nb^ m}{H(c,e)}. \end{equation}

A minimizing Gauduchon metric is

\[ \gamma =L^{-1/m}(A+Lw),\qquad A=b\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi >0,\qquad A^ m=\frac{b^ m}{H}\frac{\sigma }{L}, \]

where $\phi $ is a smooth real function on $B$. This metric is balanced. Backgrounds with $c\ne 0$ are nonclosed.

Proof. Pairing a smooth positive representative $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ with the closed semipositive form $\kappa ^{m-1}\wedge w$ gives $b>0$, and its integral over the compact fiber $\{ y\} \times C$ is $L(y)>0$, by Stokes' theorem. These are exactly the stated inequalities; conversely, they make $\theta $ itself positive. The same tests applied to a smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ direction give $b=0$ and $ca+e=0$ everywhere, and since $a$ is nonconstant, $c=e=0$. Also $\mathrm{d}\theta =c\, \mathrm{d}a\wedge w$, which is not identically zero when $c\ne 0$.

Next we apply theorem 9.1.1 on $B$ to the closed positive background $b\kappa $ and the density $(b^ m/H)\sigma /L$. Since this density has integral $b^ m=\int _ B(b\kappa )^ m$, Stokes' theorem forces the constant in the equation to be one, and we obtain $A$. Put $\Omega =A+Lw$. Since $A^{m+1}=0$ and $(Lw)^2=0$, the binomial expansion gives

26.25
\begin{equation} \Omega ^ n=\frac{nb^ m}{H}\mu ,\qquad \gamma ^ m=L^{-1}A^ m+mA^{m-1}\wedge w. \end{equation}

The first summand is the pullback of a top-degree form on $B$, and the second is closed. Hence $\gamma $ is balanced, and in particular Gauduchon. For every Gauduchon metric $\eta $ on $X$, Stokes' theorem and the pointwise arithmetic–geometric mean inequality give

\[ \int _ X\theta \wedge \eta ^ m =\int _ X\Omega \wedge \eta ^ m \geq \left(\frac{nb^ m}{H}\right)^{1/n}D_\mu (\eta ). \]

For $\eta =\gamma $ the numerator is $nb^ m$, while $D_\mu (\gamma )=(nb^ m/H)^{m/n}H$. The quotient of $\gamma $ therefore attains the lower bound, which proves (26.24) and the minimizing property of $\gamma $. $\square$

The minimizing metric gives the probability identity

26.26
\begin{equation} \frac{Lw\wedge \gamma ^ m}{b^ m} =\frac{\sigma \wedge w}{HL}. \end{equation}

Its marginal $\sigma /(HL)$ on $B$ is the probability measure that governs the Hessian metric. To compute that metric we use the ratio $v=c/e$ and set

\begin{align*} v& =c/e\in (-1/M,\infty ), & Q(v)& =\int _ B\frac{\sigma }{1+va},\\ \mathrm{d}\nu _ v& =\frac{\sigma }{Q(v)(1+va)}, & \ell _ v& =\frac{a}{1+va}. \end{align*}

Proposition 26.5.11. On the unit-volume hypersurface $\mathscr S=\{ \theta \in \mathscr C:\mathcal V_\mu (\theta )=1\} $, the functions $U=\log b$ and $v$ are global coordinates, in which

26.27
\begin{equation} \begin{aligned} g_{\mathscr S}& =m\, \mathrm{d}U^2+\rho (v)\, \mathrm{d}v^2,\\ \rho (v)& =\frac{Q''/Q-2(Q'/Q)^2}{n} =\frac2n\operatorname {Var}_{\nu _ v}(\ell _ v)>0. \end{aligned} \end{equation}

For any $v_*\in (-1/M,\infty )$, the coordinates

\[ Y=\sqrt m\, U,\qquad R(v)=\int _{v_*}^ v\sqrt{\rho (t)}\, \mathrm{d}t \]

define a global isometry onto $\mathbb R\times I$, where $I$ is an open interval with the Euclidean metric. The intrinsic distance is the Euclidean distance in $(Y,R)$.

Proof. Consider an affine variation $\delta =(\delta b,\delta c,\delta e)$ of the coefficients, write $F=\delta L/L$, and note that $\sigma /(HL)=\mathrm{d}\nu _ v$ because $HL=Q(v)(1+va)$. Differentiating under the integral sign gives

\[ D^2\log H(\delta ,\delta ) =2\mathbb E_{\nu _ v} F^2-(\mathbb E_{\nu _ v} F)^2, \]

where $\mathbb E_{\nu _ v}$ denotes expectation with respect to $\nu _ v$. This is positive unless $\delta L=0$ everywhere, which forces $\delta c=\delta e=0$. Since $\log \lambda _\mu =(\log n+m\log b-\log H)/n$,

\[ g=-D^2\log \lambda _\mu =\frac mn\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}b}{b}\right)^2 +\frac1nD^2\log H \]

is positive definite. On the unit-volume hypersurface, the relation $H(c,e)=Q(v)/e=nb^ m$ gives

\[ e=\frac{Q(v)}{n\exp (mU)},\qquad c=ve. \]

To pull back the Hessian, write $t=\mathrm{d}e/e$ and $z=Q'/Q$. Along an affine path of coefficients one has $v''=-2tv'$, so differentiating $\log H=-\log e+\log Q(v)$ twice gives

\[ D^2\log H=t^2-2zt\, \mathrm{d}v +(Q''/Q-z^2)\, \mathrm{d}v^2. \]

On $\mathscr S$ we have $t=z\, \mathrm{d}v-m\, \mathrm{d}U$; substituting this and using $n=m+1$ gives (26.27). Since $Q'/Q=-\mathbb E_{\nu _ v}\ell _ v$ and $Q''/Q=2\mathbb E_{\nu _ v}\ell _ v^2$, the variance formula follows, and nonconstancy of $a$ makes this variance positive. In the coordinates $(Y,R)$ the metric is Euclidean on the convex set $\mathbb R\times I$, so straight segments prove the distance assertion. $\square$

Lengths of the two ends

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The interval $I$ has two ends, corresponding to $v\to \infty $ and $v\to -1/M$. Whether they lie at finite distance depends on how $a$ attains its extreme values. Assume that each of the extreme sets $\{ a=0\} $ and $\{ a=M\} $ is a finite disjoint union of smooth compact real submanifolds $Z_ i$ of positive codimension, and that near each component, respectively,

\[ a\asymp \operatorname {dist}(Z_ i)^{k_ i},\qquad M-a\asymp \operatorname {dist}(Z_ i)^{k_ i},\qquad k_ i>0, \]

where $\asymp $ means comparability up to positive constant factors and $\operatorname {dist}(Z_ i)$ is the distance to $Z_ i$ for a fixed Riemannian metric on $B$. The function $a$, respectively $M-a$, is then bounded below by a positive constant outside small disjoint tubes around these components. Define, with minima over the components $Z_ i$ of the respective extreme set,

\[ r_0=\min _{\{ a=0\} } \frac{\operatorname {codim}_{\mathbb R}Z_ i}{k_ i},\qquad r_ M=\min _{\{ a=M\} } \frac{\operatorname {codim}_{\mathbb R}Z_ i}{k_ i}. \]

Lemma 26.5.12. The end $v\to \infty $ of $I$ has finite length exactly when $r_0>1$, and the end $v\to -1/M$ exactly when $r_ M>1$. More precisely, at the end $v\to \infty $ put $f=a$, $\epsilon =1/v$ and $r=r_0$; at the end $v\to -1/M$ put $f=M-a$, $\epsilon =-1/v-M$ and $r=r_ M$. Let

\[ H_ f=\int _ B\frac{\sigma }{f+\epsilon },\quad J_ f=\int _ B\frac{\sigma }{(f+\epsilon )^2},\quad K_ f=\int _ B\frac{\sigma }{(f+\epsilon )^3}. \]

Then $\epsilon \downarrow 0$ at the end, and

26.30
\begin{equation} \rho (v)\, \mathrm{d}v^2=G_ f(\epsilon )\, \mathrm{d}\epsilon ^2, \quad G_ f=\frac2n\left[\frac{K_ f}{H_ f} -\left(\frac{J_ f}{H_ f}\right)^2\right]. \end{equation}

Moreover, $G_ f\asymp \epsilon ^{-2}$ for $r<1$ and $G_ f\asymp \epsilon ^{-2}/\log (1/\epsilon )$ for $r=1$, while for $r>1$ the function $\sqrt{G_ f}$ is integrable near $\epsilon =0$.

Proof. At the end $v\to \infty $, where $\epsilon =1/v$, we have

\[ \ell _ v=\epsilon -\frac{\epsilon ^2}{a+\epsilon },\qquad \mathrm{d}v=-\epsilon ^{-2}\mathrm{d}\epsilon . \]

At the end $v\to -1/M$, where $\epsilon =-1/v-M$, we obtain

\[ \ell _ v=\frac{(M+\epsilon )^2}{M-a+\epsilon }-(M+\epsilon ), \qquad \mathrm{d}v=(M+\epsilon )^{-2}\mathrm{d}\epsilon . \]

In both cases $\ell _ v$ is an affine function of $1/(f+\epsilon )$ with coefficients depending only on $\epsilon $, and (26.30) expresses $\rho (v)\, \mathrm{d}v^2$ through the variance of $1/(f+\epsilon )$ under the probability $\sigma /[H_ f(f+\epsilon )]$. This probability is $\nu _ v$, the marginal in (26.26), because $L$ is a positive multiple of $f+\epsilon $ and positive scaling of $L$ does not change that marginal.

Integration over tubes around the components $Z_ i$, splitting the normal radius at $\epsilon ^{1/k_ i}$, gives

\[ H_ f\asymp \begin{cases} \epsilon ^{r-1}& r<1,\\ \log (1/\epsilon )& r=1,\\ 1& r>1, \end{cases} \qquad K_ f\asymp \begin{cases} \epsilon ^{r-3}& r<3,\\ \log (1/\epsilon )& r=3,\\ 1& r>3. \end{cases} \]

The analogous threshold for $J_ f$ is two. For a finite sum of tube integrals, the smallest ratio governs.

If $r<1$, choose a component attaining $r$, and two normal annuli at fixed multiples of $\epsilon ^{1/k_ i}$, so far apart that the two-sided bounds separate their ranges of $f/\epsilon $. Each annulus has probability bounded below, since its weighted mass and $H_ f$ both have order $\epsilon ^{r-1}$, and the values of $1/(f+\epsilon )$ on the two annuli differ by at least $c\epsilon ^{-1}$. The identity

\[ \operatorname {Var}_\nu T =\frac12\iint (T(x)-T(y))^2\, \mathrm{d}\nu (x)\mathrm{d}\nu (y) \]

applied to $T=1/(f+\epsilon )$ gives the lower bound, and $T\leq \epsilon ^{-1}$ gives the upper bound.

If $r=1$, the near annulus has probability at least $c/\log (1/\epsilon )$. In the same tube, the normal radii between $\epsilon ^{1/(2k_ i)}$ and a fixed small radius carry probability bounded below, by the logarithmic integral. There $1/(f+\epsilon )\leq C\epsilon ^{-1/2}$, while $1/(f+\epsilon )\geq c\epsilon ^{-1}$ on the near annulus. The variance identity and the bound $G_ f\leq 2K_ f/(nH_ f)$ prove the claimed logarithmic estimate. In both cases $r<1$ and $r=1$, the integral of $\sqrt{G_ f}$ diverges at $\epsilon =0$.

For $r>1$, $H_ f$ tends to a finite positive integral and $G_ f\leq C K_ f$. For $1<r<3$, $\sqrt{G_ f}$ is therefore bounded by $C\epsilon ^{(r-3)/2}$, whose exponent exceeds $-1$. At $r=3$ the bound is $C\sqrt{\log (1/\epsilon )}$, and for $r>3$ it is a constant. All these bounds are integrable. $\square$

Theorem 26.5.13. Under the hypotheses of lemma 26.5.12 on the extreme sets, the metric completion of $\mathscr S$ is a closed Euclidean strip if $r_0,r_ M>1$, a closed Euclidean half-plane if exactly one of them exceeds one, and the whole Euclidean plane otherwise. Each boundary line of the completion is parametrized by $b>0$, and its points are represented by the forms

\begin{align*} \theta _+(b)& =b\kappa +\frac{H_ a}{nb^ m}a w, & H_ a& =\int _ B\frac{\sigma }{a}\quad (r_0>1),\\ \theta _-(b)& =b\kappa +\frac{H_-}{nb^ m}(1-a/M)w, & H_-& =\int _ B\frac{\sigma }{1-a/M}\quad (r_ M>1). \end{align*}

These forms are smooth, semipositive, nef, nonclosed and not big, and their variational volume is one. The completion has no other boundary points.

Proof. The completion of the convex product $\mathbb R\times I$ of proposition 26.5.11 is its Euclidean closure. Along a Cauchy sequence, $Y$ converges, so $b$ has a finite positive limit, and $R$ converges to a point of $I$ or to a finite endpoint of $I$. Lemma 26.5.12 decides which endpoints are finite and gives the three alternatives.

On $\mathscr S$ we have $e=Q(v)/(nb^ m)$ and $c=ve$. At the end $v\to \infty $, $vQ(v)\to H_ a$ by monotone convergence, which gives the coefficient limit $\theta _+(b)$. At the end $v\to -1/M$, write $1+va=1-a/M+(v+1/M)a$; dominated convergence gives $Q(v)\to H_-$, and hence the limit $\theta _-(b)$. These are the coefficient limits at all finite ends. The forms are semipositive, hence nef, and they are nonclosed. Increasing their fiber coefficient by $\epsilon >0$ makes them positive, and (26.24) shows that the resulting volumes tend to one as $\epsilon \downarrow 0$. Monotonicity bounds the root volume of the limit form by this limit. Conversely, each fixed Gauduchon quotient is continuous in $\epsilon $, so the limit of the root volumes is at most every quotient of the limit form, hence at most its root volume. Thus the variational volume of $\theta _\pm (b)$ is one.

Suppose that some quasi-plurisubharmonic $u$ satisfied

\[ \theta _\pm +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq \delta (\kappa +w),\qquad \delta >0. \]

For every smooth nonnegative top-degree form $\eta $ on $B$, its pullback is $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-closed for degree reasons, and $\kappa \wedge \eta =0$. Stokes' theorem for currents therefore gives

\[ \int _ B(L_\pm -\delta )\eta \geq 0. \]

where $L_\pm $ is the fiber coefficient of $\theta _\pm $. But $L_\pm $ vanishes somewhere, and a nonzero $\eta $ supported where $L_\pm <\delta $ contradicts this inequality. Thus $\theta _\pm $ is not big. $\square$

Current cones and endpoint equations

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We keep the product $X=B\times C$, the backgrounds $\theta =b\kappa +(ca+e)w$ with real parameters $b,c,e$, and the probability $\mu =\sigma \wedge w$ of section 26.5.3, and write $h=\kappa +w$ for the product Hermitian form. Pullbacks of nonnegative top-degree forms on $B$ are closed, and testing against them determines which backgrounds have positive current representatives, not only which have smooth positive ones. At a finite end of the unit-volume hypersurface, the reciprocal of the fiber coefficient is integrable with an exponent greater than one, so the equation on the base still has a continuous solution.

Proposition 26.5.14. In this real span, the pseudoeffective and nef cones both equal

\[ \{ b\geq 0,\ e\geq 0,\ e+cM\geq 0\} . \]

A background in this span is big, that is, admits a representative $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq \delta h$ with $\delta >0$, if and only if $b>0$, $e>0$ and $e+cM>0$. Thus the big backgrounds in this span form exactly the cone $\mathscr C$ of theorem 26.5.10.

Proof. Put $L=ca+e$, and suppose that a quasi-plurisubharmonic $u$ satisfies $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq \delta h$ for some $\delta \geq 0$. Pairing with the closed semipositive form $\kappa ^{m-1}\wedge w$ and using Stokes' theorem for currents gives $b\geq \delta $. For every smooth nonnegative top-degree form $\eta $ on $B$, its pullback to $X$ is closed for degree reasons, and

\[ \int _ X(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u-\delta h)\wedge \eta =\int _ B(L-\delta )\eta \geq 0. \]

If $L<\delta $ at some point, a nonzero form $\eta $ supported in the open set where $L<\delta $ contradicts this inequality. Hence $L\geq \delta $ everywhere.
