For $\delta =0$ these conditions are exactly $b\geq 0$, $e\geq 0$ and $e+cM\geq 0$; conversely, they make $\theta $ semipositive, and such forms are nef because $\theta +\varepsilon h>0$ for every $\varepsilon >0$. Conversely, if $\theta $ is nef, applying the same tests to smooth positive representatives of $\theta +\varepsilon h$ and letting $\varepsilon $ tend to zero gives these inequalities. For $\delta >0$ the conditions force the three strict inequalities, and conversely these make $\theta $ itself strictly positive. $\square$

Proposition 26.5.15. Under the hypotheses of lemma 26.5.12 on the extreme sets, let $\theta =b\kappa +L_0w$, with $b>0$, be one of the boundary forms $\theta _+(b)$ or $\theta _-(b)$ of theorem 26.5.13 at a finite end. There is a continuous $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function $u$, pulled back from $B$, such that

26.37
\begin{equation} (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=\mu . \end{equation}

Here the product is the usual product of bounded potentials. No solution of this equation is of class $C^{1,1}$ on $X$.

Proof. Let $f=a$ for $\theta _+(b)$ and $f=1-a/M$ for $\theta _-(b)$, and let $r>1$ be the corresponding ratio $r_0$ or $r_ M$. The coefficient $L_0$ is a positive constant multiple of $f$. In each tube around an extreme component, the integral of $f^{-p}$ converges exactly when

\[ \int _0^{s_0}s^{\operatorname {codim}_{\mathbb R}Z_ i-1-k_ ip}\, \mathrm{d}s \]

converges, where $s$ is the normal radius and $s_0>0$ is small. Thus $1/L_0\in L^ p(B,\sigma )$ for every $1<p<r$. The zero set of $L_0$ has volume zero, so the value assigned to the density there is irrelevant. The formulas for $\theta _\pm (b)$ give

\[ H_0:=\int _ B\frac{\sigma }{L_0}=nb^ m. \]

We apply theorem 10.1.1 on $B$ to the positive closed background $b\kappa $ and obtain a continuous $\phi $ with $A=b\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi \geq 0$ and $A^ m=c_0\sigma /L_0$ for a constant $c_0>0$. Since $b\kappa $ is closed, bounded potentials do not change its mass, by theorem 2.2.2; hence $\int _ BA^ m=b^ m$ and $c_0=b^ m/H_0$. Set $u=\phi \circ p_ B$, where $p_ B:X\to B$ is the projection. Then $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=A+L_0w\geq 0$.

The pullback identity of lemma 1.7.3 gives $A^{m+1}=0$ on $X$, and $(L_0w)^2=0$. The multilinear expansion of proposition 1.7.1 therefore gives

\[ (A+L_0w)^ n=nL_0 A^ m\wedge w =\frac{nb^ m}{H_0}\sigma \wedge w=\mu . \]

The cancellation $L_0(\sigma /L_0)=\sigma $ holds as measures, since the zero set of $L_0$ has volume zero and $A^ m$ is absolutely continuous with density $c_0/L_0$ relative to $\sigma $.

Suppose that some solution $q$, not necessarily pulled back from $B$, were of class $C^{1,1}$ on $X$. Since $\mu /h^ n$ is smooth and strictly positive on compact $X$, proposition 1.7.5 would give $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q\geq \delta h$ for some $\delta >0$. This contradicts proposition 26.5.14, because $L_0$ vanishes somewhere. $\square$

Limits of the base potentials

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The base potentials used in theorem 26.5.10 to construct the minimizing balanced metrics converge uniformly at each finite end, and the limit solves the endpoint equation (26.37). Both the compactness and the uniqueness needed here concern only the equation on $B$, whose background is closed.

Proposition 26.5.16. Under the hypotheses of theorem 26.5.13, let $\theta _ j=b_ j\kappa +L_ jw\in \mathscr S$ converge in coefficients to a boundary form $\theta _0=b\kappa +L_0w$ at a finite end. Normalize the smooth base potentials $\phi _ j$ of theorem 26.5.10 by $\sup _ B\phi _ j=0$ and put $u_ j=\phi _ j\circ p_ B$. Then the $u_ j$ converge uniformly on $X$ to a continuous solution $u_0$ of (26.37) pulled back from $B$. For every $1\leq k\leq n$,

\[ (\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ k\rightharpoonup (\theta _0+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_0)^ k. \]

Moreover, $u_0$ is the unique bounded solution of (26.37) that is pulled back from $B$ and has supremum zero.

Proof. Write $v_ j=c_ j/e_ j$ and $\psi _ j=\phi _ j/b_ j$. On $\mathscr S$ the equation for $\phi _ j$ on $B$ becomes

\[ (\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi _ j)^ m=F_{v_ j}\sigma , \qquad F_ v=\frac1{Q(v)(1+va)},\qquad \sup _ B\psi _ j=0. \]

At the end where $\theta _0=\theta _+(b)$, $v_ j\to \infty $. Set $\epsilon =1/v$ and $H_\epsilon =\int _ B\sigma /(a+\epsilon )$. Then $F_ v=[H_\epsilon (a+\epsilon )]^{-1}$ and $H_\epsilon \to H_ a$. At the end where $\theta _0=\theta _-(b)$, $v_ j\to -1/M$. With $f=1-a/M$ and $t=v+1/M>0$, the denominator of $F_ v$ is $Q(v)(f+ta)$, and $Q(v)\to H_-$. Thus the respective limits of $F_{v_ j}$ are

\[ F_+=\frac1{H_ a a},\qquad F_-=\frac1{H_-(1-a/M)}. \]

Choose $1<p<r$, where $r>1$ is the ratio $r_0$ or $r_ M$ of that end. Integration over tubes as in the proof of proposition 26.5.15 gives $a^{-1}\in L^ p(B,\sigma )$, respectively $f^{-1}\in L^ p(B,\sigma )$. Near the first end $F_ v\leq C/a$, and near the second $F_ v\leq C/f$. Since the extreme sets have volume zero, dominated convergence gives $F_{v_ j}\to F_\pm $ in $L^ p(B,\sigma )$, and each limiting density has integral one. Since $\sigma /\kappa ^ m$ is smooth and strictly positive on compact $B$, the densities of the equations also have a common bound in $L^ p(B,\kappa ^ m)$.

We apply theorem 10.3.4 to the fixed background $\kappa $: every subsequence of $(\psi _ j)$ has a uniformly convergent further subsequence. Continuity of bounded products under uniform convergence identifies its limit with a normalized solution of $(\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi _0)^ m=F_\pm \sigma $. Since $\kappa $ is closed, it satisfies the Guan–Li condition, so 11.5.1 makes this solution unique. Every cluster limit is consequently the same, which proves uniform convergence of the whole sequence. Since $b_ j\to b>0$, we obtain $u_ j\to u_0=(b\psi _0)\circ p_ B$ uniformly.

To prove convergence of the products, note that on each coordinate ball the convergence of the background coefficients provides a common smooth function $q$, a quadratic polynomial, such that all $u_ j+q$ are plurisubharmonic. These corrected potentials are uniformly bounded and converge uniformly. The continuity of bounded products under convergence in capacity (section 1.6) gives weak convergence of all their products, with uniform local mass bounds. Expanding the products as in (1.21), and using smooth convergence of the remaining background factors, we obtain the asserted convergence of currents. In top degree, $(\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ n=\mu $ for all $j$, so the limit solves the endpoint equation.

For uniqueness, let $v=\chi \circ p_ B$ be a bounded solution pulled back from $B$, with $\sup _ B\chi =0$. The current $A_\chi =b\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi $ on $B$ is positive, as one sees by pairing the pulled-back current $\theta _0+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ with $w$ and with pullbacks of positive forms on $B$. The product expansion gives $nL_0A_\chi ^ m\wedge w=\sigma \wedge w$, which determines $A_\chi ^ m=\sigma /(nL_0)$ off the extreme set. That density already has total mass $H_0/n=b^ m$, whereas mass invariance for the closed background $b\kappa $ gives $\int _ BA_\chi ^ m=b^ m$. Positivity therefore excludes any mass of $A_\chi ^ m$ on the extreme set. Thus $\chi /b$ satisfies the same normalized equation on $B$ as $\psi _0$, and 11.5.1 proves the asserted uniqueness. $\square$

The proposition concerns the limit of the base potentials constructed above. It does not describe solutions of the endpoint equation that are not pulled back from $B$, nor does it give a pointwise limit of the balanced metrics $\gamma _ j=L_ j^{-1/m}(b_ j\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi _ j+L_ jw)$.

Traces of minimizing metrics

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Uniform convergence of the base potentials does not control the minimizing balanced metrics themselves: by the cofactor formula (26.25), their $(n-1)$st powers contain the square of the reciprocal fiber coefficient. Depending on the ratio at the end, the traces of these powers stay bounded or blow up, and in the second case the trace-normalized powers concentrate on fibers over the extreme set.

Keep the sequence $\theta _ j\to \theta _0$ of proposition 26.5.16, and let $\phi _0$ be the uniform limit of its base potentials $\phi _ j$. Put $A_ j=b_ j\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi _ j$, and write $G_ j=\gamma _ j^ m$, where $\gamma _ j$ is the minimizing metric of theorem 26.5.10 for $\theta _ j$. Traces are taken with respect to $h=\kappa +w$.

Proposition 26.5.17. Let $r>1$ be the ratio $r_0$ or $r_ M$ of the end. After discarding finitely many terms, write $L_ j=s_ j(f+\epsilon _ j)$ with $s_ j>0$, $s_ j\to s_0>0$ and $\epsilon _ j\to 0^+$. Here $f=a$, $s_ j=c_ j$ at the first face, and $f=M-a$, $s_ j=-c_ j$ at the second. Set $J_ j=\int _ B\sigma /L_ j^2$. Then

26.38
\begin{equation} G_ j=\frac{\sigma }{nL_ j^2}+mA_ j^{m-1}\wedge w, \qquad t_ j:=\int _ Xh\wedge G_ j=\frac{J_ j}{n}+mb_ j^{m-1}. \end{equation}

For $1<r<2$, $t_ j\asymp \epsilon _ j^{r-2}$; for $r=2$, $t_ j\asymp \log (1/\epsilon _ j)$. For $r>2$, the entire sequence converges weakly to the finite positive closed current

\[ G_0=\frac{\sigma }{nL_0^2}+mA_0^{m-1}\wedge w, \qquad A_0=b\kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi _0, \]

and $t_ j\to t_0=\int _ Xh\wedge G_0>0$.

Proof. On $\mathscr S$, $H(c_ j,e_ j)=nb_ j^ m$, so $A_ j^ m=\sigma /(nL_ j)$. Substituting this in (26.25) gives the formula for $G_ j$. Stokes' theorem on $B$, for the closed form $\kappa $, gives $\int _ B\kappa \wedge A_ j^{m-1}=b_ j^{m-1}$, which proves the trace identity. The factorization of $L_ j$ uses $\epsilon _ j=e_ j/c_ j$ at the first end and $\epsilon _ j=e_ j/(-c_ j)-M$ at the second, and the coefficient limits give $s_ j\to s_0>0$.

On each tube around an extreme component, $f\asymp \operatorname {dist}(Z_ i)^{k_ i}$, so the integral of $f^{-2}$ is comparable to

\[ \int _0^{\varrho _0}\frac{\varrho ^{\operatorname {codim}_{\mathbb R}Z_ i-1}}{(\varrho ^{k_ i}+\epsilon _ j)^2}\, \mathrm{d}\varrho , \]

where $\varrho $ is normal radius, $\varrho _0>0$ is small and $r_ i=\operatorname {codim}_{\mathbb R}Z_ i/k_ i$. Splitting the integral at $\varrho =\epsilon _ j^{1/k_ i}$ gives order $\epsilon _ j^{r_ i-2}$ for $r_ i<2$, order $\log (1/\epsilon _ j)$ for $r_ i=2$, and a finite limit for $r_ i>2$. The finite sum over components is governed by $r=\min _ i r_ i$. Since $b_ j$ and $s_ j$ have positive finite limits, these estimates prove the three alternatives for $t_ j$.

If $r>2$, then $f^{-2}$ is integrable and dominates $L_ j^{-2}$ up to a fixed constant factor, so $\sigma /L_ j^2\to \sigma /L_0^2$ in total variation. Since $w\wedge w=0$, we have $A_ j^{m-1}\wedge w=(\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^{m-1}\wedge w$, which converges weakly by proposition 26.5.16. This proves $G_ j\rightharpoonup G_0$, and $G_0$ is positive and closed as a weak limit of positive closed forms. Pairing with $h$ gives the limit of the traces, which is positive because of the term $\sigma /(nL_0^2)$. $\square$

Proposition 26.5.18. Suppose $1<r\leq 2$, and put $T_ j=G_ j/t_ j$. Every weak cluster limit of $(T_ j)$ has the form $p_ B^*\nu $, where $\nu $ is a probability measure on $B$ supported on the extreme components with ratio $r$. Such a limit is positive and closed, has trace one relative to $h$, and satisfies $\theta _0\wedge p_ B^*\nu =0$. If those components reduce to a single point $p$, the whole sequence converges to the current of integration $[\{ p\} \times C]$.

The trace-normalized smooth metrics $\eta _ j=t_ j^{-1/m}\gamma _ j$ satisfy

\[ \int _ X\theta _ j\wedge \eta _ j^ m=D_\mu (\eta _ j) =\frac{nb_ j^ m}{t_ j}\longrightarrow 0. \]

Both quantities have order $\epsilon _ j^{2-r}$ when $1<r<2$, and order $1/\log (1/\epsilon _ j)$ when $r=2$. Their quotient remains one.

Proof. Let $\nu _ j=\sigma /(J_ jL_ j^2)$. These are probability measures on $B$, and

\[ T_ j=\frac{J_ j}{nt_ j}p_ B^*\nu _ j +\frac m{t_ j}A_ j^{m-1}\wedge w. \]

The first coefficient tends to one. The second current is positive with trace $mb_ j^{m-1}/t_ j\to 0$, hence converges weakly to zero. Compactness of probability measures on $B$ and weak continuity of pullback under the submersion $p_ B$ therefore show that every cluster limit is $p_ B^*\nu $ for a cluster limit $\nu $ of $(\nu _ j)$. Away from small tubes around the extreme set, the density $1/L_ j^2$ is uniformly bounded, while $J_ j\to \infty $; that region receives no mass in the limit. The tube integrals for components with ratio greater than $r$ are $o(J_ j)$ by the preceding estimates, so only components with ratio $r$ keep mass.

For a limit $T=p_ B^*\nu $, $\kappa \wedge T=0$ for degree reasons on $B$, while $L_0w\wedge T=0$ because $L_0$ vanishes on the support of $\nu $. Its trace is one because $\int _ Cw=1$. A top-degree measure on $B$ is closed, so its pullback is closed as well. If the support is the single point $p$, every cluster limit of $(\nu _ j)$ is $\delta _ p$; compactness then gives convergence of the whole sequence, and $p_ B^*\delta _ p$ is the current of integration over the fiber $\{ p\} \times C$.

The minimizing quotient is one on $\mathscr S$, and before trace normalization both its numerator and its denominator equal $nb_ j^ m$. Each is multiplied by $t_ j^{-1}$ under the normalization, and the trace estimates give their limits and rates. $\square$

In the projective strip of example 26.5.19, both ratios are two and each extreme set is a single point. At each of the two ends, the normalized minimizing tests therefore converge to the current of integration over the corresponding fiber $\{ p\} \times \mathbb P^1$, and the numerator and the denominator of the quotient both decay like $1/\log (1/\epsilon _ j)$. This is convergence of currents; it does not give a pointwise limit of the metrics.

Projective and torus examples

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Example 26.5.19. On $B=\mathbb P^2$, take normalized Fubini–Study $\kappa $, $\sigma =\kappa ^2$, $C=\mathbb P^1$, and

\[ a([Z])=\frac{|Z_1|^2/2+|Z_2|^2}{|Z_0|^2+|Z_1|^2+|Z_2|^2}. \]

Then $M=1$, $r_0=r_ M=2$, and the completion is a closed strip with finite faces

\[ b\kappa +\frac{4\log 2}{3b^2}a w,\qquad b\kappa +\frac{4\log 2}{3b^2}(1-a)w. \]

For $v\ne 0$,

\[ Q(v)=\frac4{v^2}\bigl [(v+1)\log (1+v) -(v+2)\log (1+v/2)\bigr ], \qquad Q(0)=1. \]

The width of the strip is the finite positive integral

\[ \int _{-1}^{\infty } \sqrt{\frac{Q''/Q-2(Q'/Q)^2}{3}}\, \mathrm{d}v. \]

Proof. The extreme sets are the points $[1:0:0]$ and $[0:0:1]$. In affine coordinates, $a$ and $1-a$ vanish there to exactly second order, so both real codimensions are four and both orders are two. To compute the law of $a$ under $\sigma $, polar integration in the chart $Z_0=1$ gives $2\, \mathrm{d}s_1\mathrm{d}s_2/(1+s_1+s_2)^3$, where $s_ j=|z_ j|^2$. The transformation $u_ j=s_ j/(1+s_1+s_2)$ gives the uniform density two on the triangle $u_1,u_2\geq 0$, $u_1+u_2\leq 1$, and $a=u_1/2+u_2$. For $0\leq s\leq 1/2$, the triangle $u_1/2+u_2\leq s$ has area $s^2$, so the law of $a$ has density $4s$ on $[0,1/2]$. With $u_0=1-u_1-u_2$, exchanging $u_0$ and $u_2$ preserves the uniform measure and replaces $a$ by $1-a$; hence the law has density $4(1-s)$ on $[1/2,1]$. Integrating against these two densities gives $H_ a=H_-=4\log 2$ and the formula for $Q$, and the integral definition of $Q$ shows that $Q(0)=1$ is its continuous extension. Theorem 26.5.13 gives the boundary lines and the finite width, with Euclidean coordinates $Y=\sqrt2\log b$ and $R(v)$. $\square$

Example 26.5.20. Let $E=\mathbb C/(\mathbb Z+i\mathbb Z)$, $B=E^ m$, and write $z_ j=u_ j+i\tau _ j$. Put

\[ \eta _ j=\frac i2\mathrm{d}z_ j\wedge \mathrm{d}\bar z_ j, \qquad s=(m!)^{-1/m},\qquad \kappa =s\sum _ j\eta _ j,\qquad \sigma =\kappa ^ m. \]

For $a=\sin ^2(\pi u_1)$, the unit-volume hypersurface is itself a complete Euclidean plane, with Euclidean coordinates

\[ Y=\sqrt m\log b,\qquad R=\frac{\log (1+v)}{2\sqrt n},\qquad v>-1. \]

Its minimizing balanced metrics can be written down by integrating a periodic function twice.

Proof. The forms descend to $B$, and $\sigma =\eta _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \eta _ m$ is a probability form. Both extreme sets are real hypersurfaces along which $a$, respectively $1-a$, vanishes to second order, so $r_0=r_ M=1/2$. Splitting the period and substituting $t=\tan (\pi u)$ gives

\begin{align*} Q(v)& =\int _0^1\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{1+v\sin ^2(\pi u)}\\ & =\frac2\pi \int _0^\infty \frac{\mathrm{d}t}{1+(1+v)t^2} =\frac1{\sqrt{1+v}}. \end{align*}

Let $F_ v(u)=[Q(v)(1+v\sin ^2(\pi u))]^{-1}$, which has mean one over a period. Integrating the equation

\[ \phi _ v''=4\pi bs(F_ v-1) \]

twice, whose right side has mean zero, gives a smooth periodic potential $\phi _ v$ of $u=u_1$; the first integration constant is chosen so that $\phi _ v'$ has mean zero. In our normalization $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi _ v=\phi _ v''\eta _1/(4\pi )$, hence

\[ A=bs\left(F_ v\eta _1+\sum _{j=2}^ m\eta _ j\right)>0, \qquad A^ m=b^ mF_ v\sigma =\frac{b^ m}{H}\frac{\sigma }{L}. \]

Thus the minimizer of theorem 26.5.10, with its cofactor formula (26.25), is explicit in this example, without an appeal to theorem 9.1.1. Differentiating $Q$ gives

\[ \rho (v)=\frac1{4n(1+v)^2},\qquad g_{\mathscr S}=m\, \mathrm{d}U^2+ \frac1{4n}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}v}{1+v}\right)^2. \]

As $U$ and $v$ range over $\mathbb R$ and $(-1,\infty )$, the coordinates $(Y,R)$ range over all of $\mathbb R^2$. Both boundary faces of the coefficient cone are therefore at infinite distance on the unit-volume hypersurface. $\square$

These distances are those of the fixed three-parameter cone with the product probability $\mu =\sigma \wedge w$. The endpoint criterion of lemma 26.5.12 uses the hypothesis that the extreme sets are disjoint unions of submanifolds along which $a$ vanishes to finite power order; intersecting extreme strata or vanishing of infinite order would require further estimates. The variances are computed from the minimizing metrics, but we do not assert that these metrics have a pointwise limit at a finite end.

26.6 A compact dual problem over Gauduchon currents

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now fix a Hermitian background and vary the density. For a smooth positive density, theorem 26.1.3 provides a minimizing Gauduchon metric; for a density that is only continuous or integrable, there is no smooth equation to produce one. The extremal problem becomes compact when positive $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-closed $(n-1,n-1)$-currents are allowed as tests, provided the denominator $D_\mu $ is extended to currents. For this we take the determinant of the matrix density of a current relative to its trace measure. The integral of its $n$th root is an infimum of continuous linear functionals, hence upper semicontinuous under weak convergence, and its maximum over normalized currents is attained. The smooth equation identifies this maximum with the supremum over smooth tests.

From here until section 26.10, we assume $n\geq 2$, and we fix a Hermitian form $\omega $ and a smooth positive probability volume form $\mathrm{d}V$ on $X$; integrability and convergence in measure refer to $\mathrm{d}V$. As in chapter 6, $\mathrm{MA}_\omega (u)=(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n$ for bounded $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$, and $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (u)$ is the extended non-pluripolar Monge–Ampère measure of theorem 6.1.2. Let $\mathscr G_\omega $ be the compact convex set of normalized Gauduchon currents of section 4.4, that is, of positive $(n-1,n-1)$-currents $T$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}T=0$ and $\int _ X\omega \wedge T=1$. Finally, let $F\in C(X,\mathbb R)$.

For $T\in \mathscr G_\omega $, the trace measure $\sigma _ T=\omega \wedge T$ is a probability measure, and all coefficient measures of $T$ are absolutely continuous with respect to it. In a unitary frame for $\omega $, let $B$ be the matrix of a smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\beta $ relative to $\omega $. The coefficient densities of $T$ form a measurable positive semidefinite Hermitian matrix $P_ T$, determined $\sigma _ T$-almost everywhere by

\[ \beta \wedge T=\frac1n\operatorname {tr}(B P_ T)\, \mathrm{d}\sigma _ T, \qquad \operatorname {tr}P_ T=n\quad \sigma _ T\text{-almost everywhere}. \]

The matrix $P_ T$ transforms by unitary conjugation when the frame changes, so its determinant is well defined. Define

26.39
\begin{equation} f_ F=\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^ F\mathrm{d}V}{\omega ^ n}\right)^{1/n},\qquad \Gamma _ F(T)=f_ F(\det P_ T)^{1/n}\sigma _ T,\qquad \mathfrak D_ F(T)=\Gamma _ F(T)(X). \end{equation}

These quantities are finite, since the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality gives $(\det P_ T)^{1/n}\leq 1$; the measure $\Gamma _ F(T)$ may vanish for some currents. The same definitions apply to every positive $(n-1,n-1)$-current $T$ of finite mass, with the finite measure $\sigma _ T=\omega \wedge T$ and $\operatorname {tr}P_ T=n$, and $\Gamma _ F$ is positively homogeneous of degree one in $T$. If the coefficient measures of $T$ are written instead as a matrix density $P'$ relative to another positive measure $\sigma '$ with respect to which they are absolutely continuous, then $(\det P_ T)^{1/n}\sigma _ T=(\det P')^{1/n}\sigma '$, by homogeneity of the determinant.

For a smooth positive $T=\gamma ^{n-1}$, we may therefore use the reference measure $\omega ^ n$. The coefficient matrix of $T$ relative to $\omega ^ n$ is the cofactor matrix of the matrix of $\gamma $, and its determinant is $(\gamma ^ n/\omega ^ n)^{n-1}$. The definition therefore gives

\[ \mathfrak D_ F(\gamma ^{n-1}) =\int _ X\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^ F\mathrm{d}V}{\gamma ^ n}\right)^{1/n}\gamma ^ n. \]

Thus $\mathfrak D_ F$ extends the denominator $D_\mu $ of definition 26.1.1 to currents, with $\mu $ replaced by the possibly unnormalized density $\mathrm{e}^ F\mathrm{d}V$.

Lemma 26.6.1. For every positive $(n-1,n-1)$-current $T$ of finite mass on $X$,

\[ \mathfrak D_ F(T) =\inf _{\substack {\beta >0\ \mathrm{continuous}\\ \beta ^ n=\mathrm{e}^ F\mathrm{d}V}} \int _ X\beta \wedge T. \]

In particular, $\mathfrak D_ F$ is concave, positively homogeneous and weakly upper semicontinuous on $\mathscr G_\omega $, and its value does not depend on the auxiliary frame or on the metric used to express the matrix density.

Proof. Let $I$ be the identity matrix of size $n$. For any positive semidefinite Hermitian matrix $P$ of that size,

\[ (\det P)^{1/n} =\inf _{Q>0,\ \det Q=1}\frac1n\operatorname {tr}(QP). \]

The inequality $\leq $ is the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality. For the reverse inequality, take, for real $\varepsilon >0$,

\[ Q_\varepsilon = \det (P+\varepsilon I)^{1/n}(P+\varepsilon I)^{-1}. \]

Its determinant is one. If $P$ is positive definite, the trace expression $\frac1n\operatorname {tr}(Q_\varepsilon P)$ converges to $(\det P)^{1/n}$ as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$. If $P$ is singular, it converges to zero: each ratio of an eigenvalue of $P$ to that eigenvalue plus $\varepsilon $ is bounded, while the prefactor tends to zero. When $\operatorname {tr}P=n$, the trace expression is at most $1+\varepsilon $.

We apply this formula to $P_ T$ and multiply by $f_ F$. Every continuous positive $\beta $ in the asserted infimum has matrix $f_ FQ$ with $\det Q=1$, so it gives an upper bound for $\mathfrak D_ F(T)$. Conversely, for fixed $\varepsilon >0$ the measurable field $Q_\varepsilon $ formed with $P=P_ T$ is bounded above and below by positive constants. It can be approximated in $L^1(\sigma _ T)$ by continuous positive definite Hermitian fields with bounds of the same type. One way to construct them is to apply Lusin's theorem in finitely many bundle charts, extend the continuous fields from the resulting compact sets, and glue with a partition of unity; spectral truncation to a fixed positive interval preserves the bounds. Finally we divide each approximating field by the $n$th root of its determinant. This normalization is uniformly continuous on fields with spectrum in a fixed compact positive interval, so the normalized fields still converge in $L^1(\sigma _ T)$ and have determinant one. Their trace integrals converge to that of $Q_\varepsilon $.

Now let $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$. The bound $1+\varepsilon $ permits dominated convergence, and we obtain equality of the infimum and the integral. For a current of arbitrary positive finite trace mass we use homogeneity, and the zero current is immediate. The infimum is taken over functionals $T\mapsto \int _ X\beta \wedge T$ that are linear and weakly continuous, which proves concavity and upper semicontinuity. It also expresses $\mathfrak D_ F(T)$ without a matrix frame or an auxiliary metric, which proves the final assertion. $\square$

Let $B_0(F)$ be the normalization scalar of section 15.1 for the background $\omega $ and the reference potential zero. Thus the equation $\mathrm{MA}_\omega (u)=\mathrm{e}^{B_0(F)+F}\mathrm{d}V$ has a bounded solution $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$, and $B_0(F)$ is the only constant with this property.

Theorem 26.6.2. For every $F\in C(X,\mathbb R)$,

26.40
\begin{equation} M(F):=\max _{T\in \mathscr G_\omega }\mathfrak D_ F(T) =\mathrm{e}^{-B_0(F)/n}>0. \end{equation}

The maximum equals the supremum over the smooth strictly positive members of $\mathscr G_\omega $. The set $\mathscr M(F)$ of maximizing currents is nonempty, compact and convex.

If $F$ is smooth, there is exactly one maximizing current, namely the normalized $(n-1)$st power of the Gauduchon metric conformal to the smooth solution form $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ of $\mathrm{MA}_\omega (u)=\mathrm{e}^{B_0(F)+F}\mathrm{d}V$.

Proof. Upper semicontinuity of $\mathfrak D_ F$ on the compact set $\mathscr G_\omega $ gives attainment, and testing a smooth positive member gives a positive value. Concavity makes the set of maximizers convex, and upper semicontinuity makes it closed.

Suppose first that $F$ is smooth, and let $\Omega =\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u>0$ be the smooth solution form with $\Omega ^ n=\mathrm{e}^{B_0(F)+F}\mathrm{d}V$. The form $\beta =\mathrm{e}^{-B_0(F)/n}\Omega $ is an admissible test in the preceding lemma, so Stokes' theorem for currents gives, for every $T\in \mathscr G_\omega $,

\[ \mathfrak D_ F(T)\leq \int _ X\beta \wedge T =\mathrm{e}^{-B_0(F)/n}\int _ X\omega \wedge T=\mathrm{e}^{-B_0(F)/n}. \]

If $\gamma $ is the Gauduchon metric conformal to $\Omega $, equality holds in the pointwise matrix inequality for $T$ proportional to $\gamma ^{n-1}$; normalizing $T$ so that $\int _ X\omega \wedge T=1$, we obtain the formula and its attainment by a smooth test.

For uniqueness, let $T$ be a maximizer. Equality in the integrated inequalities forces equality in the matrix arithmetic–geometric mean inequality $\sigma _ T$-almost everywhere. Since the matrix of $\beta $ is positive definite and $\operatorname {tr}P_ T=n$, this means that the coefficient measures of $T$ are a nonnegative scalar measure times the smooth coefficients of $\gamma ^{n-1}$. Thus $T=a\gamma ^{n-1}$, where $a$ is a positive measure, regarded as a distribution. The equation $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(a\gamma ^{n-1})=0$ is the adjoint of a scalar elliptic equation, so elliptic regularity makes $a$ smooth. Since $\gamma $ is Gauduchon, proposition 4.8.1 identifies the kernel of this adjoint operator with the constants: the trace operator $v\mapsto n\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}/\gamma ^ n$ has the constants as kernel and index zero, and integration against $\gamma ^ n$ annihilates its range. Thus $a$ is constant, and the normalization fixes the constant. This proves uniqueness.

For continuous $F,G$, the pointwise definition gives

\[ \mathrm{e}^{-\| F-G\| _\infty /n}\mathfrak D_ G(T) \leq \mathfrak D_ F(T) \leq \mathrm{e}^{\| F-G\| _\infty /n}\mathfrak D_ G(T). \]

The same bounds hold for the maxima, and for the suprema over smooth positive members. We approximate $F$ uniformly by smooth functions. Since $B_0$ is $1$-Lipschitz for the uniform norm by proposition 15.1.1, the formula just proved for smooth data gives (26.40) in the limit, including the equality with the smooth supremum. Note that only $F$ is approximated here: some normalized Gauduchon currents are not limits of smooth positive ones, as example 28.8.2 shows. $\square$

26.7 Supporting probabilities represented by maximizing currents

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

By theorem 26.6.2, $B_0=-n\log M$, and $M(F)$ is the maximum of the functionals $T\mapsto \mathfrak D_ F(T)=\int _ X\mathrm{e}^{F/n}\, \mathrm{d}\Gamma _0(T)$, each of which is linear in $\mathrm{e}^{F/n}$. The supporting probabilities of $B_0$ should therefore be the normalized determinant measures of maximizing currents, and we show that they are exactly these. The point that needs proof is the continuity of the determinant measures: on the set of maximizers, upper semicontinuity of the total determinant integral becomes equality. Recall from section 15.2 that the set $\mathcal A_0(F)$ of supporting probabilities of $B_0$ at $F\in C(X)$ consists of the Borel probability measures $\nu $ with $B_0(G)\leq B_0(F)-\int _ X(G-F)\, \mathrm{d}\nu $ for all $G\in C(X)$; by theorem 15.2.1 it is nonempty, convex and weakly compact.

For $T\in \mathscr M(F)$ put

\[ \nu _{T,F}=\Gamma _ F(T)/M(F). \]

This is a probability measure.

Lemma 26.7.1. Suppose $T_ j\to T$ weakly in $\mathscr G_\omega $ and $\mathfrak D_ F(T_ j)\to M(F)$. Then $T\in \mathscr M(F)$ and $\Gamma _ F(T_ j)\rightharpoonup \Gamma _ F(T)$. On $\mathscr M(F)$ the map $T\mapsto \Gamma _ F(T)$ is affine and continuous.

Proof. Upper semicontinuity gives $\mathfrak D_ F(T)\geq M(F)$, hence equality. The measures $\Gamma _ F(T_ j)$ have bounded mass; let $\Gamma '$ be a weak cluster point, whose mass is $M(F)$. For a continuous real function $h$, choose $\varepsilon >0$ with $1\pm \varepsilon h>0$. Since $\Gamma _{F+n\log (1\pm \varepsilon h)}=(1\pm \varepsilon h)\Gamma _ F$, upper semicontinuity of $\mathfrak D$ for these two continuous logarithmic densities gives

\[ \int _ X(1\pm \varepsilon h)\, \mathrm{d}\Gamma ' \leq \int _ X(1\pm \varepsilon h)\, \mathrm{d}\Gamma _ F(T). \]

Since both measures have mass $M(F)$, the two signs give equality of the integrals of $h$. Thus every cluster point equals $\Gamma _ F(T)$, which proves the convergence.

For affineness, take $T_1,T_2\in \mathscr G_\omega $ and $t\in [0,1]$, and express the coefficient measures of both currents through densities with respect to the sum of their trace measures. Concavity and homogeneity of the $n$th root of the determinant, applied to these matrix densities, give

\[ \Gamma _ F(tT_1+(1-t)T_2) \geq t\Gamma _ F(T_1)+(1-t)\Gamma _ F(T_2),\qquad 0\leq t\leq 1. \]

If $T_1$ and $T_2$ maximize, so does the current on the left, by concavity of $\mathfrak D_ F$. Both measures in the inequality then have mass $M(F)$, so equality holds. This proves affineness, and continuity is the special case of the first assertion in which every $T_ j$ is a maximizer. $\square$

Theorem 26.7.2. For every $F\in C(X,\mathbb R)$,

\[ \mathcal A_0(F)=\{ \nu _{T,F}:T\in \mathscr M(F)\} . \]

Consequently the determinant measures $\Gamma _ F(T)$ of all maximizing currents are absolutely continuous with respect to $\mathrm{d}V$. (This concerns the measures $\Gamma _ F(T)$, not the currents $T$ themselves.)

Proof. For a maximizing $T$ and any $G\in C(X)$,

\[ M(G)\geq \mathfrak D_ G(T) =M(F)\int _ X\mathrm{e}^{(G-F)/n}\, \mathrm{d}\nu _{T,F}. \]

Since $B_0=-n\log M$, taking $-n$ times the logarithm and applying Jensen's inequality gives

\[ B_0(G)\leq B_0(F)-n\log \int _ X\mathrm{e}^{(G-F)/n}\, \mathrm{d}\nu _{T,F} \leq B_0(F)-\int _ X(G-F)\, \mathrm{d}\nu _{T,F}. \]

Hence every probability $\nu _{T,F}$ with $T\in \mathscr M(F)$ belongs to $\mathcal A_0(F)$.

Denote the set of these probabilities by $\mathscr N(F)$; by the preceding lemma it is compact and convex. To prove the reverse inclusion we compute the right derivative $(B_0)'_+(F;h)=\lim _{t\downarrow 0}(B_0(F+th)-B_0(F))/t$ for $h\in C(X)$. Since $\mathfrak D_{F+th}(T)=\int _ X\mathrm{e}^{th/n}\, \mathrm{d}\Gamma _ F(T)$ and the masses of the measures $\Gamma _ F(T)$ are bounded on $\mathscr G_\omega $, a Taylor expansion of the exponential gives, as $t\downarrow 0$,

\[ \mathfrak D_{F+th}(T) =\mathfrak D_ F(T)+\frac tn\int _ Xh\, \mathrm{d}\Gamma _ F(T)+O(t^2), \]

with the error bounded uniformly in $T$. Testing maximizers at $F$ gives

\[ \liminf _{t\downarrow 0}\frac{M(F+th)-M(F)}t \geq \frac{M(F)}n\max _{\nu \in \mathscr N(F)}\int _ Xh\, \mathrm{d}\nu . \]

For the reverse inequality, choose a maximizer $T_ t$ at $F+th$. The multiplicative bounds in the previous proof imply $\mathfrak D_ F(T_ t)\to M(F)$. Every sequence $t\downarrow 0$ has a subsequence along which $T_ t$ converges, and the preceding lemma identifies the limit of $\Gamma _ F(T_ t)$ with the determinant measure of a maximizer at $F$. Since $\mathfrak D_ F(T_ t)\leq M(F)$, the Taylor expansion gives the matching upper bound for the difference quotients of $M$. Since $B_0=-n\log M$, therefore

\[ (B_0)'_+(F;h) =-\max _{\nu \in \mathscr N(F)}\int _ Xh\, \mathrm{d}\nu . \]

If $\nu \in \mathcal A_0(F)$, its supporting inequality in direction $th$ implies

\[ \int _ Xh\, \mathrm{d}\nu \leq \max _{\widetilde\nu \in \mathscr N(F)} \int _ Xh\, \mathrm{d}\widetilde\nu \quad \text{for every }h\in C(X). \]

If $\nu $ were not in the weakly compact convex set $\mathscr N(F)$, the Hahn–Banach separation theorem in the dual of $C(X)$ would give some $h\in C(X)$ violating this inequality. Hence $\nu \in \mathscr N(F)$, and the two sets are equal. The absolute continuity of their members was proved in theorem 15.2.1. $\square$

For continuous $F$, the supporting probability is therefore unique exactly when all maximizing currents have the same normalized determinant measure, which is weaker than uniqueness of the maximizing current. Whether a maximizing current can be written as a conformal multiple of a power $(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^{n-1}$ of a weak solution form is a further question, discussed in section 34.15; the construction above works directly with the coefficient measures of the currents.

26.8 Integrability of Gauduchon determinant measures

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

A lower bound for the constants of smooth equations in terms of an $L^ p$ norm of the density gives, by duality, a uniform $L^ q$ bound for the determinant measures of all normalized Gauduchon currents, for every exponent $q<n/(n-1)$. At the endpoint $q=n/(n-1)$, the optimal constant is exactly $v_-(\omega )^{-1/n}$, where $v_-(\omega )$ is the lower Monge–Ampère mass of definition 6.5.1. Thus integrability of the dual tests at the critical exponent is equivalent to positive lower mass of the background.

Write $\Gamma _0(T)$ for the determinant measure $\Gamma _ F(T)$ of (26.39) with $F=0$.

Theorem 26.8.1. For every $T\in \mathscr G_\omega $, the measure $\Gamma _0(T)$ is absolutely continuous with respect to $\mathrm{d}V$; we write $\Gamma _0(T)=k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V$ and call $k_ T\geq 0$ the determinant density of $T$. For every $1<q<n/(n-1)$ there is $C_ q>0$ such that

\[ \| k_ T\| _{L^ q(\mathrm{d}V)}\leq C_ q \qquad (T\in \mathscr G_\omega ). \]

Proof. Fix $p>1$ and a smooth strictly positive function $h$. By theorem 9.1.1 there are a smooth form $\Omega _ h=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ h>0$ and a constant $c_ h>0$ such that

\[ \Omega _ h^ n=c_ hh^ n\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

The lower-density estimate of theorem 10.3.3 gives a constant $c_ p>0$, independent of $h$, such that

\[ c_ h\| h^ n\| _{L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)}\geq c_ p. \]

The pointwise matrix arithmetic–geometric mean inequality, applied to $\Omega _ h$ and the coefficient density of $T$, gives the inequality of measures

\[ c_ h^{1/n}h\, \Gamma _0(T)\leq \Omega _ h\wedge T. \]

Integrating and using $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}T=0$ yields

\[ \int _ Xh\, \mathrm{d}\Gamma _0(T) \leq c_ h^{-1/n}\int _ X\Omega _ h\wedge T =c_ h^{-1/n} \leq c_ p^{-1/n}\| h\| _{L^{np}(\mathrm{d}V)}. \]

Approximation by smooth strictly positive functions extends this bound to every continuous nonnegative $h$, and applied to $|h|$ it bounds the integral of every continuous function against $\Gamma _0(T)$ by its $L^{np}$ norm. Since continuous functions are dense in $L^{np}$, the duality between $L^{np}$ and $L^{np/(np-1)}$ shows that $\Gamma _0(T)$ has a density in $L^{np/(np-1)}$, with norm at most $c_ p^{-1/n}$. As $p$ ranges over $(1,+\infty )$, the exponent $np/(np-1)$ ranges over $(1,n/(n-1))$. This proves the theorem. $\square$

In particular, the determinant of the matrix density vanishes on the singular part of the trace measure: if $\sigma _ T=\sigma _{T,\mathrm{ac}}+\sigma _{T,\mathrm{s}}$ is its Lebesgue decomposition with respect to $\mathrm{d}V$, then

\[ \det P_ T=0\quad \sigma _{T,\mathrm{s}}\text{-almost everywhere}. \]

Indeed, $f_0=(\mathrm{d}V/\omega ^ n)^{1/n}$ is everywhere positive and $\Gamma _0(T)=f_0(\det P_ T)^{1/n}\sigma _ T$ is absolutely continuous. This says nothing about a possible singular part of the current $T$ itself.

Theorem 26.8.2. With the extended value $+\infty $ allowed, one has

\[ \sup _{T\in \mathscr G_\omega } \| k_ T\| _{L^{n/(n-1)}(\mathrm{d}V)}=v_-(\omega )^{-1/n}, \]

where the right side is interpreted as $+\infty $ when $v_-(\omega )=0$. The supremum does not change if $T$ is restricted to the smooth strictly positive members of $\mathscr G_\omega $. Thus a uniform estimate at the endpoint exponent holds if and only if $v_-(\omega )>0$.

Proof. Suppose first that $v_-(\omega )>0$. For the smooth equation in the preceding proof, the definition of $v_-$ gives

\[ c_ h\int _ Xh^ n\, \mathrm{d}V=\int _ X\Omega _ h^ n\geq v_-(\omega ). \]

The same pointwise inequality and Stokes' theorem therefore give

\[ \int _ Xh\, \mathrm{d}\Gamma _0(T) \leq v_-(\omega )^{-1/n}\| h\| _{L^ n(\mathrm{d}V)}. \]

By duality, $\| k_ T\| _{L^{n/(n-1)}}\leq v_-(\omega )^{-1/n}$ for every $T\in \mathscr G_\omega $.

For the reverse bound, let $g$ be any smooth positive probability density relative to $\mathrm{d}V$, and solve

\[ \Omega _ g^ n=c(g)g\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Let $T_ g$ be the trace-normalized $(n-1)$st power of the Gauduchon metric conformal to $\Omega _ g$. Equality in the pointwise matrix inequality gives

\[ \int _ Xg^{1/n}\, \mathrm{d}\Gamma _0(T_ g)=c(g)^{-1/n}. \]

Since $\| g^{1/n}\| _{L^ n}=1$, Hölder's inequality gives $\| k_{T_ g}\| _{L^{n/(n-1)}}\geq c(g)^{-1/n}$. By theorem 26.14.3, theorem 26.1.3,

\[ \inf _{g>0\ \mathrm{smooth},\ \int _ Xg\, \mathrm{d}V=1}c(g)=v_-(\omega ). \]

Taking the supremum over these smooth currents $T_ g$ proves the matching lower bound; if $v_-(\omega )=0$, the same argument makes this supremum infinite. Since the currents $T_ g$ are smooth, this also proves that allowing nonsmooth currents does not change the supremum. $\square$

The formula shows that the optimal endpoint constant does not depend on the choice of $\mathrm{d}V$, whereas the subcritical constants $C_ q$ may depend on it.

Corollary 26.8.3. For every $R>0$ and $1<q<n/(n-1)$ there is a constant $C_{R,q}$ with the following property. If $F\in C(X)$ and $\| F\| _\infty \leq R$, every supporting probability $\nu \in \mathcal A_0(F)$ of theorem 26.7.2 has a density $a$ satisfying

\[ \nu =a\, \mathrm{d}V,\qquad \| a\| _{L^ q(\mathrm{d}V)}\leq C_{R,q}. \]

If $v_-(\omega )>0$, this holds also for $q=n/(n-1)$.

Proof. Recall that $M(F)=\exp (-B_0(F)/n)$, and represent $\nu $ by a maximizing current $T\in \mathscr M(F)$, using theorem 26.7.2. Since $\Gamma _ F(T)=\mathrm{e}^{F/n}\Gamma _0(T)$ and $B_0$ is $1$-Lipschitz,

\[ \nu =\frac{\mathrm{e}^{F/n}k_ T}{M(F)}\, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad M(F)\geq \mathrm{e}^{-R/n}M(0)>0. \]

Theorem 26.8.1 therefore gives the subcritical bound with constant $\mathrm{e}^{2R/n}M(0)^{-1}C_ q$. When $v_-(\omega )>0$, the same argument with the endpoint constant $v_-(\omega )^{-1/n}$ of theorem 26.8.2 gives the case $q=n/(n-1)$. $\square$

On the threefold of theorem 26.18.1, every Hermitian form has zero lower mass, so the endpoint estimate fails for the whole family $\mathscr G_\omega $, while all the subcritical estimates above remain valid. For a fixed smooth density, theorem 26.6.2 gives a unique smooth maximizing current, and its determinant density is bounded. Sequences of currents whose values approach the maximum need not, however, have uniformly bounded higher moments, as proposition 26.17.2 shows.

On the other hand, the endpoint estimate can hold on a manifold with infinite upper mass. On the Hopf threefold of chapter 8, take the Gauduchon metric $\omega $ of that chapter, for which $v_-(\omega )=6$ and $v_+(\omega )=+\infty $, and set $\mathrm{d}V=\omega ^3/6$. The normalized Gauduchon form $T_0=\omega ^2/6$ has $\sigma _{T_0}=\mathrm{d}V$ and $P_{T_0}=I$. Hence $k_{T_0}=6^{-1/3}$, and

\[ \sup _{T\in \mathscr G_\omega }\| k_ T\| _{L^{3/2}(\mathrm{d}V)}=6^{-1/3}. \]

Thus an endpoint determinant estimate, with its optimal constant attained, can coexist with infinite upper Monge–Ampère mass.

26.8.1 Uniform bounds near a smooth density

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The endpoint estimate can fail for the family of all tests while the maximizing tests for a family of densities remain uniformly controlled. For smooth densities close in $C^{2,\alpha }$ to the normalized volume form $\omega ^ n/\int _ X\omega ^ n$ of the background, a fixed-point argument keeps the solution forms close to $\omega $ and their Gauduchon factors close to that of $\omega $. This gives uniform bounds for both the determinant density of the maximizing current and the density of the supporting probability.

In this subsection we assume $n\geq 3$ and fix $0<\alpha <1$. All Hölder norms are defined using a fixed finite atlas and fixed smooth connections. Put

\[ H=\frac{\omega ^ n}{\mathrm{d}V},\qquad V=\int _ X\omega ^ n,\qquad F_*=\log (H/V). \]

Thus $\mathrm{e}^{F_*}\mathrm{d}V=\omega ^ n/V$ is a probability measure. By theorem 4.1.2, there is a unique smooth positive function $\chi _0$, the normalized Gauduchon factor of $\omega $, such that

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\chi _0\omega ^{n-1})=0,\qquad \int _ X\chi _0\omega ^ n=1. \]

Write $m=\min _ X\chi _0$, $a=\max _ X\chi _0$ and $C_0=\| \chi _0\| _{C^{2,\alpha }}$.

We first specify the constants that determine the size of the neighborhood. For a smooth positive $(1,1)$-form $\Omega $, set

\[ L_\omega v=\frac{n\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\wedge \omega ^{n-1}}{\omega ^ n}, \qquad A_\Omega \eta =\frac{\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\eta \Omega ^{n-1})}{\mathrm{d}V}. \]

The compatibility argument of proposition 4.8.1, proposition 9.3.1 shows that

\[ \mathcal L(v,c)=L_\omega v-c, \qquad \int _ Xv\, \mathrm{d}V=0, \]

is an isomorphism from the space of pairs $(v,c)\in C^{4,\alpha }\times \mathbb R$ with this normalization onto $C^{2,\alpha }$. Let $K\geq 1$ bound its inverse for the norm $\| v\| _{C^{4,\alpha }}+|c|$. The formal adjoint of $A_\omega $ with respect to $\mathrm{d}V$ is $(H/n)L_\omega $, whose kernel consists of the constants and whose index is zero. Consequently $A_\omega $ has kernel $\mathbb R\chi _0$ and range

\[ Z_0=\left\{ g\in C^{0,\alpha }:\int _ Xg\, \mathrm{d}V=0\right\} . \]

Its restriction to

\[ E_\chi =\left\{ \eta \in C^{2,\alpha }:\int _ X\eta \omega ^ n=0\right\} \]

has a bounded inverse $\mathcal R:Z_0\to E_\chi $. Fix $G\geq 1$ bounding this inverse.

We normalize the Hölder norms of forms and of endomorphism fields so that they dominate pointwise operator norms and are submultiplicative under composition of endomorphisms; the subscript $\mathrm{alg}$ below refers to this norm on endomorphism fields. Choose $D\geq 1$ so that

\[ \| \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\| _{C^{2,\alpha }}\leq D\| v\| _{C^{4,\alpha }},\qquad \| \omega ^{-1}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\| _{C^{2,\alpha },\mathrm{alg}} \leq D\| v\| _{C^{4,\alpha }}. \]

Let $C_{\mathrm{tr}}\geq 1$ be a bound for the trace map on endomorphism fields, and put $Q=2C_{\mathrm{tr}}D^2$. Expanding $\Omega ^{n-1}-\omega ^{n-1}$ gives a constant $C_ A\geq 1$ such that

\[ \| A_\Omega -A_\omega \| _{C^{2,\alpha }\to C^{0,\alpha }} \leq C_ A\| \Omega -\omega \| _{C^{2,\alpha }} \quad \text{if }\| \Omega -\omega \| _{C^{2,\alpha }}\leq 1. \]

Only two derivatives of $\Omega $ enter this polynomial estimate. Define

26.41
\begin{equation} r=\min \left\{ \frac1{2D},\frac1{4KQ}, \frac{m}{4GC_ ADC_0}\right\} , \qquad \varepsilon =\frac r{2K}. \end{equation}

These constants depend only on $\omega $, $\mathrm{d}V$, $\alpha $ and the chosen norms.

Lemma 26.8.4. Let $F$ be smooth with $\| F-F_*\| _{C^{2,\alpha }}\leq \varepsilon $. The solution form $\Omega _ F=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ F$ of $\mathrm{MA}_\omega (u_ F)=\mathrm{e}^{B_0(F)+F}\mathrm{d}V$ and its Gauduchon factor $\chi _ F$, normalized as below, satisfy

\begin{gather*} \tfrac 12\omega \leq \Omega _ F\leq \tfrac 32\omega , \qquad \frac m2\leq \chi _ F\leq \frac{3a}2,\\ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\chi _ F\Omega _ F^{n-1})=0, \qquad \int _ X\chi _ F\omega ^ n=1. \end{gather*}

The potential can be normalized by $\int _ Xu_ F\, \mathrm{d}V=0$ with $\| u_ F\| _{C^{4,\alpha }}+|B_0(F)-\log V|\leq r$.

Proof. For $\| v\| _{C^{4,\alpha }}\leq 1/(2D)$ put

\[ N(v)=\log \det (I+\omega ^{-1}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)-L_\omega v. \]

The logarithm series starts at degree two. If the endomorphism norms of $\omega ^{-1}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ and $\omega ^{-1}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w$ are at most $\rho \leq 1/2$, telescoping their powers bounds the difference series by $C_{\mathrm{tr}}\rho /(1-\rho )$ times the endomorphism difference. Hence

\begin{align*} \| N(v)\| _{C^{2,\alpha }}& \leq Q\| v\| _{C^{4,\alpha }}^2,\\ \| N(v)-N(w)\| _{C^{2,\alpha }} & \leq Q(\| v\| _{C^{4,\alpha }}+\| w\| _{C^{4,\alpha }}) \| v-w\| _{C^{4,\alpha }}. \end{align*}

On the closed radius-$r$ ball of normalized pairs $(v,c)$, the map

\[ (v,c)\longmapsto \mathcal L^{-1}(F-F_*-N(v)) \]

has image norm at most $K\varepsilon +KQr^2\leq 3r/4$ and Lipschitz constant at most $2KQr\leq 1/2$. Its fixed point $(u,c)$ satisfies

\[ \log \frac{(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n}{\omega ^ n}-c=F-F_*. \]

The estimate $Dr\leq 1/2$ gives the form comparison. The scalar is $b=\log V+c$. Differentiating the local equation gives a uniformly elliptic equation for each first derivative of $u$, with coefficients initially in $C^{2,\alpha }$. Interior Schauder estimates improve the regularity one order at a time, so $u$ is smooth. Uniqueness in theorem 9.1.1 identifies it with $u_ F$ and gives $b=B_0(F)$. No uniform higher-derivative bounds on $F$ enter the estimates above.

Put $\Delta A=A_{\Omega _ F}-A_\omega $. Stokes' theorem makes its image lie in $Z_0$. On the complete space $E_\chi $, the affine map

\[ \eta \longmapsto -\mathcal R\Delta A(\chi _0+\eta ) \]

has Lipschitz constant $\kappa =GC_ ADr\leq m/(4C_0)\leq 1/4$. Its fixed point obeys

\[ \| \eta \| _{C^{2,\alpha }} \leq \frac{\kappa C_0}{1-\kappa } \leq 2\kappa C_0\leq m/2. \]

Since $A_\omega \chi _0=0$ and $A_\omega \mathcal R$ is the identity on $Z_0$, the function $\chi _ F=\chi _0+\eta $ satisfies $A_{\Omega _ F}\chi _ F=0$, and it has the stated normalization and bounds. Elliptic regularity makes it smooth. Every Gauduchon factor of $\Omega _ F$ with the same normalization satisfies the same affine fixed-point equation, so this factor is the one given by theorem 4.1.2. $\square$

Theorem 26.8.5. For every smooth $F$ in the ball of lemma 26.8.4, let $T_ F$ be the maximizing current of theorem 26.6.2 and $\nu _ F$ the supporting probability of $B_0$ at $F$; both are unique. With

\[ H_{\max }=\max _ XH,\qquad C_\nu =\frac{3^{n+1}}4aH_{\max },\qquad C_ k=\frac{3^ n}2aH_{\max }^{(n-1)/n}, \]

their densities satisfy

\[ \nu _ F=q_ F\, \mathrm{d}V,\qquad \| q_ F\| _{L^\infty }\leq C_\nu ,\qquad \| k_{T_ F}\| _{L^\infty }\leq C_ k. \]

In particular, for $p=n/(n-1)$,

\[ \| q_ F\| _{L^ p}\leq C_\nu ^{1/n},\qquad \| k_{T_ F}\| _{L^ p}\leq C_ k. \]

The constants do not involve $v_-(\omega )$; in particular, these bounds hold also when $v_-(\omega )=0$.

Proof. Set $\Omega =\Omega _ F$, $\chi =\chi _ F$ and

\[ Z=\int _ X\chi \omega \wedge \Omega ^{n-1}. \]

The form comparison and $\int _ X\chi \omega ^ n=1$ give

26.42
\begin{equation} 2^{-(n-1)}\leq Z\leq (3/2)^{n-1}. \end{equation}

Since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\chi \Omega ^{n-1})=0$, Stokes' theorem also gives $Z=\int _ X\chi \Omega ^ n$. The form $T=(\chi /Z)\Omega ^{n-1}$ is normalized and is a constant multiple of the $(n-1)$st power of the Gauduchon metric conformal to $\Omega $. By theorem 26.6.2, it is the unique maximizing current, so $T=T_ F$. The equality case in the proof of that theorem gives $\nu _ F=\Omega \wedge T_ F$, and hence

26.43
\begin{equation} \begin{aligned} T_ F& =\frac\chi Z\Omega ^{n-1},\\ \nu _ F& =\Omega \wedge T_ F=\frac\chi Z\Omega ^ n,\\ q_ F& =\frac\chi Z\frac{\Omega ^ n}{\mathrm{d}V},\\ k_{T_ F}& =\frac\chi Z \left(\frac{\Omega ^ n}{\mathrm{d}V}\right)^{(n-1)/n}. \end{aligned} \end{equation}

The last identity uses the positive root $\gamma =(\chi /Z)^{1/(n-1)}\Omega $, for which $T_ F=\gamma ^{n-1}$. Uniqueness of the supporting probability follows from theorem 26.7.2.

Now $\chi /Z\leq (3a/2)2^{n-1}$ and $\Omega ^ n/\mathrm{d}V\leq (3/2)^ nH_{\max }$, and multiplying gives exactly $q_ F\leq C_\nu $ and $k_{T_ F}\leq C_ k$. Since $\int _ Xq_ F\, \mathrm{d}V=1$, we have $\int _ Xq_ F^ p\, \mathrm{d}V\leq C_\nu ^{p-1}$, which gives the sharper $L^ p$ estimate for $q_ F$. The $L^ p$ estimate for $k_{T_ F}$ follows from $\int _ X\mathrm{d}V=1$. $\square$

Corollary 26.8.6. The ball of lemma 26.8.4 contains smooth injectively parametrized families, of arbitrarily large finite dimension, of data $F$ normalized by $\int _ X\mathrm{e}^ F\mathrm{d}V=1$. It also contains a smooth path of such data whose maximizing currents are pairwise distinct.

Proof. For a small smooth function $f$, put

\[ F(f)=F_*+f-\log \int _ X\mathrm{e}^{F_*+f}\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

If $F(f)=F(g)$, then $f-g$ is constant, so the map $f\mapsto F(f)$ is injective on functions of zero $\mathrm{d}V$-mean. By its continuity in $C^{2,\alpha }$, it maps a sufficiently small neighborhood of zero in any fixed finite-dimensional space of such smooth functions into the ball, and these spaces can have arbitrary dimension.

For the path, choose a smooth real $\psi $ of zero $\mathrm{d}V$-mean whose complex Hessian at some point $x_0$ has, in an $\omega $-unitary frame, a matrix $Q_0$ that is not a multiple of the identity. A cutoff of $|z_1|^2-|z_2|^2$ in local coordinates, minus its mean, is such a function. For small real $t$ put

\[ \Omega _ t=\omega +t\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi ,\qquad V_ t=\int _ X\Omega _ t^ n,\qquad F_ t=\log \frac{\Omega _ t^ n}{V_ t\, \mathrm{d}V}. \]

These normalized data tend to $F_*$ in $C^{2,\alpha }$ as $t\to 0$, and by uniqueness in theorem 9.1.1 their solution forms are the forms $\Omega _ t$. If $F_ s=F_ t$, then $\log (\Omega _ s^ n/\Omega _ t^ n)$ is the constant $\log (V_ s/V_ t)$. The signs of the complex Hessian of $(s-t)\psi $ at a maximum and at a minimum force this constant to be zero. Integrating the derivative of $\log \det $ along the segment between $\Omega _ t$ and $\Omega _ s$ then gives a uniformly elliptic equation for $(s-t)\psi $ without zeroth-order term, and the maximum principle forces $(s-t)\psi $ to be constant. Since $\psi $ is not constant, $s=t$.

If the maximizing currents for $F_ s$ and $F_ t$ agree, (26.43) makes $\Omega _ s^{n-1}$ and $\Omega _ t^{n-1}$ proportional at $x_0$. Positive cofactor matrices determine positive forms up to scale, so $I+sQ_0$ and $I+tQ_0$ are proportional. Since $I$ and $Q_0$ are linearly independent, comparing coefficients gives $s=t$. $\square$

The small $C^{2,\alpha }$ neighborhood gives a condition directly on the prescribed density. The next estimate instead assumes uniform bounds for the solution forms and covers a larger smooth family without derivative bounds on its densities. The question for arbitrary bounded families of continuous densities remains open when $v_-(\omega )=0$. The concentrating sequences of proposition 26.17.2 consist of near-maximizers, so these estimates do not apply to them.

26.8.2 Uniformly controlled complex Hessians

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The preceding construction bounds the solution form and its Gauduchon factor by controlling derivatives of the logarithmic density. A bound on the solution form itself leads to a different estimate. If $\Omega =\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$, then $d\Omega =d\omega $, so the divergence of its cofactor depends only on the fixed torsion and the size of $\Omega $. This permits uniform bounds for the factor even when derivatives of the varying complex Hessian are unbounded.

We continue to assume $n\geq 3$ and that $\mathrm{d}V$ is a probability volume form. We first give the real elliptic estimate used below. A cube denoted by $tQ$ has the same center as $Q$ and $t$ times its side length, and $\fint _ Q$ denotes its Euclidean average.

Lemma 26.8.7. Let $Q\subset \mathbb R^ m$, $m>2$, be a cube. Suppose that $u$ is a smooth strictly positive solution on a neighborhood of $3Q$ of

\[ \operatorname {div}(A\nabla u+Bu)=0, \qquad \lambda I\leq A\leq \Lambda I,\qquad |B|\leq K, \]

where $A$ is a smooth symmetric matrix field and $B$ is a smooth vector field. Then

\[ \sup _{Q/2}u\leq C\inf _{Q/2}u, \]

where $C$ depends only on $m,\lambda ,\Lambda ,K$ and the side length of $Q$. Derivatives of $A$ and $B$ do not enter $C$.

Proof. We first estimate positive and negative powers, then compare their small moments by an estimate for $\log u$.

For $p\ne 0,1$, test the weak equation by $\eta ^2u^{p-1}$, where $\eta $ is a smooth cutoff. The principal term is

\[ (p-1)\int \eta ^2u^{p-2}\langle A\nabla u,\nabla u\rangle . \]

The other terms contain respectively $2\eta u^{p-1}A\nabla u\cdot \nabla \eta $, $(p-1)\eta ^2u^{p-1}B\cdot \nabla u$, and $2\eta u^ pB\cdot \nabla \eta $. Taking absolute values after moving the principal term to one side and applying Young's inequality absorbs half of its energy. Thus, for either sign of $p$,

26.48
\begin{equation} \int \eta ^2|\nabla u^{p/2}|^2 \leq C_1p^2(1+|p-1|^{-2}) \int (|\nabla \eta |^2+K^2\eta ^2)u^ p, \end{equation}

with $C_1$ depending only on the ellipticity bounds. Each test is legitimate since this individual smooth positive solution is bounded above and away from zero on the cutoff support.

Put $\beta =m/(m-2)>1$ and choose $p_0=\beta ^{-k-1/2}$, with $k$ to be fixed later. Apply the Euclidean Sobolev inequality to $\eta u^{p/2}$ on nested cubes shrinking from $Q$ to $Q/2$. Choose the cutoff gaps proportional to $2^{-j}$ and the powers $p_ j=p_0\beta ^ j$. The positive powers never equal one: the closest two are $\beta ^{-1/2}$ and $\beta ^{1/2}$. Consequently the constants in (26.48), together with the cutoff terms from $\nabla (\eta u^{p/2})$, grow at most polynomially in $p_ j$ and exponentially in $j$. Since $\sum _ j(j+1)\beta ^{-j}<\infty $, their iteration product converges. Negative powers give the same calculation for $u^{-1}$. We obtain

26.49
\begin{equation} \sup _{Q/2}u\leq C_2\left(\fint _ Q u^{p_0}\right)^{1/p_0}, \qquad \inf _{Q/2}u\geq C_2^{-1} \left(\fint _ Q u^{-p_0}\right)^{-1/p_0}. \end{equation}

Here $C_2$ also depends on $p_0$ and the fixed cube size.

Testing instead by $\eta ^2/u$ gives

\[ \int \eta ^2|\nabla \log u|^2 \leq C_3\int (|\nabla \eta |^2+K^2\eta ^2). \]

For every subcube $S\subset Q$ of side length $\ell $, a cutoff supported in $2S\subset 3Q$, equal to one on $S$, has derivative bounded by $C/\ell $. Poincaré's inequality and Cauchy–Schwarz therefore give

\[ \fint _ S|\log u-(\log u)_ S| \leq C_4(1+K\ell )\leq K_0, \]

where $K_0\geq 1$ is uniform and $(\log u)_ S$ denotes the mean. We spell out the exponential consequence of this mean oscillation bound.

Write $f=\log u$ and fix $a>2$. Select the maximal dyadic subcubes $S$ of $Q$ on which $\fint _ S|f-f_ Q|>aK_0$. Their total measure is at most $|Q|/a$. The parent of each selected cube has average at most $aK_0$, hence $|f_ S-f_ Q|\leq 2^ maK_0$. Outside their union, differentiation of dyadic averages gives $|f-f_ Q|\leq aK_0$ almost everywhere. Repeat this selection inside each chosen cube, subtracting its own mean. The union at generation $j$ has measure at most $a^{-j}|Q|$, and outside generation $j+1$ the accumulated mean changes and last oscillation give

\[ |f-f_ Q|\leq (j2^ ma+a)K_0. \]

The intersection of all generations is null. Choosing $c>0$ so that $\exp (c2^ ma)<a$ and summing over the generations yields

\[ \fint _ Q\exp \! \left(\frac{c|f-f_ Q|}{K_0}\right)\leq C_5, \]

with dimensional constants. Now fix $k$ large enough that $p_0<c/K_0$. Multiplying the estimates for the positive and negative exponentials cancels $f_ Q$ and gives

\[ \left(\fint _ Q u^{p_0}\right) \left(\fint _ Q u^{-p_0}\right)\leq C_5^2. \]

Combining this with (26.49) proves the result. Every constant comes from the stated coefficient bounds and the fixed cube. $\square$

Proposition 26.8.8. Fix $0<\delta \leq \Lambda $. For every smooth real $v$ with

\[ \delta \omega \leq \Omega _ v:=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\leq \Lambda \omega , \]

let $\rho _ v$ be its Gauduchon factor normalized by

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\rho _ v\Omega _ v^{n-1})=0, \qquad \int _ X\rho _ v\, \mathrm{d}V=1. \]

There is $C_ H\geq 1$, depending only on $X,\omega ,\mathrm{d}V,\delta ,\Lambda $, such that $C_ H^{-1}\leq \rho _ v\leq C_ H$ on $X$ for every such $v$.

Proof. Existence and uniqueness of $\rho _ v$ follow from theorem 4.1.2. Fix a real reference metric on $X$, and put $Q_ v=\Omega _ v^{n-1}$ and $J_ v=\Omega _ v^ n/\mathrm{d}V$. Since $d\Omega _ v=d\omega $,

26.50
\begin{equation} dQ_ v=(n-1)d\omega \wedge \Omega _ v^{n-2}. \end{equation}

Both $Q_ v$ and $dQ_ v$ have uniformly bounded coefficients in fixed charts.

Define a real tensor $A_ v$ and vector field $b_ v$ by

\[ \langle A_ vd\phi ,d\psi \rangle \, \mathrm{d}V =n\, d\psi \wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\phi \wedge Q_ v, \qquad (b_ v\cdot d\phi )\, \mathrm{d}V =-n\, \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\phi \wedge dQ_ v. \]

Only the $(1,1)$ part of $d\psi \wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\phi $ contributes to the first identity; it is

\[ \frac{\mathrm i}{4\pi } (\partial \psi \wedge \bar\partial \phi +\partial \phi \wedge \bar\partial \psi ). \]

Thus $A_ v$ is symmetric and uniformly positive definite, with a uniform upper bound. The second identity and (26.50) give a uniform bound for $b_ v$.

For $L_{\Omega _ v}\phi =n\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi \wedge Q_ v/\Omega _ v^ n$, Stokes' theorem gives

\begin{align*} \int _ X\psi J_ vL_{\Omega _ v}\phi \, \mathrm{d}V & =-n\int _ Xd\psi \wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\phi \wedge Q_ v +n\int _ X\psi \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\phi \wedge dQ_ v\\ & =-\int _ X\langle A_ vd\phi ,d\psi \rangle \, \mathrm{d}V -\int _ X\psi b_ v\cdot d\phi \, \mathrm{d}V. \end{align*}

In particular, the adjoint equation for $\rho _ v$ is

\[ \operatorname {div}_{\mathrm{d}V}(A_ vd\rho _ v+b_ v\rho _ v)=0. \]

In each coordinate chart, multiplying the coefficients by the fixed smooth volume density converts this to Euclidean divergence form while preserving uniform ellipticity and a uniform bound for the vector coefficient. Lemma 26.8.7 applies. A fixed finite cover by inner cubes has a connected overlap graph, since $X$ is connected. Chaining its local estimates gives $\sup _ X\rho _ v\leq C_ H\inf _ X\rho _ v$. The normalization and $\mathrm{d}V(X)=1$ imply the claimed two-sided bound. $\square$

For a fixed $0<\varepsilon <1$, consider the full family

26.51
\begin{equation} \mathcal V_\varepsilon = \{ v\in C^\infty (X,\mathbb R):\sup _ Xv=0, -\varepsilon \omega \leq \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\leq \varepsilon \omega \} . \end{equation}

Set $c_ v=\int _ X\Omega _ v^ n$ and $F_ v=\log (J_ v/c_ v)$, so that $\int _ X\mathrm e^{F_ v}\mathrm{d}V=1$. The density $F_ v$ is defined by an actual solution form, rather than prescribed before solving an equation.

Theorem 26.8.9. Let

\[ j_-=(1-\varepsilon )^ n\min _ X\frac{\omega ^ n}{\mathrm{d}V}, \qquad j_+=(1+\varepsilon )^ n\max _ X\frac{\omega ^ n}{\mathrm{d}V}, \]

and take $C_ H$ from proposition 26.8.8 with $\delta =1-\varepsilon $ and $\Lambda =1+\varepsilon $. For every $v\in \mathcal V_\varepsilon $, the exact maximizing current $T_ v\in \mathscr G_\omega $ for $F_ v$ is unique. Its determinant density $k_{T_ v}$ and the probability density $r_ v=\mathrm d\nu _{T_ v,F_ v}/\mathrm{d}V$ satisfy

26.52
\begin{equation} \frac{j_-}{C_ Hj_+}\leq r_ v\leq \frac{C_ Hj_+}{j_-}, \qquad j_+^{-1/n}\frac{j_-}{C_ Hj_+} \leq k_{T_ v}\leq j_-^{-1/n}\frac{C_ Hj_+}{j_-}. \end{equation}

In particular both densities are uniformly bounded at the critical exponent $n/(n-1)$, without an assumption on the lower mass. Moreover,

\[ \| F_ v-F_0\| _\infty \leq n\log \frac{1+\varepsilon }{1-\varepsilon }, \]

and this normalized family contains infinitely many distinct nonconstant densities on the fixed manifold $X$.

Proof. Use the normalization of proposition 26.8.8 and put

\[ N_ v=\int _ XJ_ v\rho _ v\, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad T_ v=\frac{\rho _ v}{N_ v}\Omega _ v^{n-1}. \]

The factor equation and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-Stokes give

\[ \int _ X\omega \wedge \rho _ v\Omega _ v^{n-1} =\int _ X\Omega _ v\wedge \rho _ v\Omega _ v^{n-1}=N_ v. \]

Hence $T_ v\in \mathscr G_\omega $. It is the $(n-1)$st power of the Gauduchon metric $\gamma _ v=(\rho _ v/N_ v)^{1/(n-1)}\Omega _ v$. The equation $\Omega _ v^ n=c_ v\mathrm e^{F_ v}\mathrm{d}V$ gives $B_0(F_ v)=\log c_ v$. Thus theorem 26.6.2 identifies $T_ v$ as the unique exact maximizer, and $M(F_ v)=c_ v^{-1/n}$. The determinant and probability normalizations are therefore

26.53
\begin{equation} k_{T_ v}=\frac{\rho _ v}{N_ v}J_ v^{(n-1)/n}, \qquad \Gamma _{F_ v}(T_ v)=c_ v^{-1/n}\frac{J_ v\rho _ v}{N_ v}\, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad r_ v=\frac{J_ v\rho _ v}{N_ v}. \end{equation}

Here $j_-\leq J_ v\leq j_+$ and, since $\int _ X\rho _ v\, \mathrm{d}V=1$, also $j_-\leq N_ v\leq j_+$. The factor bound gives the first pair of inequalities in (26.52); the identity $k_{T_ v}=J_ v^{-1/n}r_ v$ gives the second. The critical norms are bounded by the respective upper constants because $\mathrm{d}V$ is a probability volume.

The inequalities

\[ (1-\varepsilon )^ n\leq \frac{J_ v}{J_0}\leq (1+\varepsilon )^ n, \qquad (1-\varepsilon )^ n\leq \frac{c_ v}{c_0}\leq (1+\varepsilon )^ n \]

give the bound for $F_ v-F_0$ by subtracting logarithms. To prove nontriviality, choose a nonconstant smooth real $\phi $. For small $t$, the potential $t\phi -\sup _ X(t\phi )$ belongs to $\mathcal V_\varepsilon $ and gives the same density as $t\phi $. Differentiation at zero gives

\[ \left.\frac{d}{dt}F_{t\phi }\right|_{t=0} =L_\omega \phi - \frac{\int _ XJ_0L_\omega \phi \, \mathrm{d}V}{\int _ XJ_0\, \mathrm{d}V}. \]

This function is nonconstant: otherwise $L_\omega \phi $ would be constant, its values at a maximum and a minimum would force that constant to be zero, and the strong maximum principle would make $\phi $ constant. Choose two points where the displayed derivative differs. The difference of the densities at these points is strictly monotone for $t$ in a small interval. These give infinitely many distinct densities, with at most one parameter having equal values at the chosen points. Discarding that parameter gives the nonconstant family. $\square$

Uniform bounds for the solution forms need not give derivative bounds, or even equicontinuity, for their normalized logarithmic densities.

Example 26.8.10. For every fixed $0<\varepsilon <1$, there are $v_ j\in \mathcal V_\varepsilon $ with $\| v_ j\| _{C^0}\to 0$ such that their normalized densities $F_{v_ j}$ are pairwise distinct, bounded in $C^0$, and unbounded in $C^1$. This family is not equicontinuous. Nevertheless, both densities of its exact maximizers satisfy the uniform bounds of theorem 26.8.9.

Proof. Choose holomorphic coordinates centered at a point $p$, put $x=\operatorname {Re}z_1$, and take a smooth real cutoff $\zeta $ supported in the chart and equal to one near $p$. We may assume $0\leq \zeta \leq 1$. On the chart set

\[ \beta =dx\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}x=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(x^2/2) =\frac{\mathrm i}{8\pi }dz_1\wedge d\bar z_1. \]

This is a nonzero semipositive form of rank one. For a constant $a>0$ to be chosen, extend

\[ w_ j=\frac a{j^2}\zeta \sin (jx) \]

by zero outside the chart, and put $v_ j=w_ j-\sup _ Xw_ j$. The product rule gives

\begin{align*} \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_ j={}& -a\zeta \sin (jx)\beta +\frac aj\cos (jx) (d\zeta \wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}x+dx\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\zeta )\\ & +\frac a{j^2}\sin (jx)\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\zeta . \end{align*}

All fixed forms in this expression have compact support after multiplication by the indicated cutoff or its derivatives. Thus a fixed $C>0$ bounds the operator norm of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_ j$ relative to $\omega $ by $aC$ for every $j\geq 1$. Choose $a$ so small that $aC\leq \varepsilon /2$. Then $v_ j\in \mathcal V_\varepsilon $, and $\| v_ j\| _{C^0}\leq 2a/j^2$.

Write $s=\operatorname {tr}_{\omega (p)}\beta >0$. Since $x(p)=0$ and $\zeta =1$ near $p$, we have $\Omega _{v_ j}(p)=\omega (p)$ and

\[ \partial _ x\Omega _{v_ j}(p)=\partial _ x\omega (p)-aj\beta . \]

Differentiating the logarithmic determinant, and observing that its normalizing integral is constant in the spatial variable, gives the exact identity

\[ \partial _ xF_{v_ j}(p) =\partial _ x\log \frac{\omega ^ n}{\mathrm{d}V}(p)-ajs. \]

These derivatives are pairwise different and their absolute values tend to infinity. Hence the densities are pairwise distinct and their $C^1$ norms are unbounded. Their uniform $C^0$ bound is part of theorem 26.8.9.

To check failure of equicontinuity, take points $p_ j^\pm $ with $x=\pm \pi /(2j)$ and all other real coordinates zero. For large $j$ they lie where $\zeta =1$, and $\Omega _{v_ j}(p_ j^\pm )=\omega (p_ j^\pm )\mp a\beta $. The integral normalizing $F_{v_ j}$ cancels from the difference, so

\[ F_{v_ j}(p_ j^-)-F_{v_ j}(p_ j^+) \longrightarrow \log \frac{1+as}{1-as}>0. \]

Indeed, $\beta ^2=0$ gives $(\omega (p)\pm a\beta )^ n=(1\pm as)\omega (p)^ n$, and $as<1$ by the chosen form comparison. Both points tend to $p$, which proves the failure of equicontinuity. The exact-adjoint bounds still apply because the entire sequence lies in $\mathcal V_\varepsilon $. $\square$

The same estimate applies to the family with $\delta \omega \leq \Omega _ v\leq \Lambda \omega $ by replacing $j_-$ and $j_+$ with $\delta ^ n\min _ X(\omega ^ n/\mathrm{d}V)$ and $\Lambda ^ n\max _ X(\omega ^ n/\mathrm{d}V)$. Applying the estimate to a prescribed family of densities requires uniformly comparable solution forms. Thus this construction provides critical estimates when $v_-(\omega )=0$ for a larger smooth family, but leaves the question for arbitrary bounded families of continuous densities open.

26.9 Integrable densities in the compact Gauduchon problem

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We keep the Hermitian background $\omega $ and replace the continuous logarithmic density by an integrable density. For an unbounded density, weak compactness of the test currents alone does not pass the determinant integral to the limit; uniform control of the weighted tails provides the missing upper semicontinuity.

We use the following notation from section 14.27, section 14.28, with $\omega $ in place of $h$. For a nonzero nonnegative $f\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$, the subsolution threshold $b_\omega (f)\geq 0$ is, by theorem 14.27.2, the largest $b\geq 0$ for which some $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ satisfies $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (u)\geq bf\, \mathrm{d}V$. For $p\in L^ n$, the root norm is $\rho _\omega (p)=b_\omega (|p|^ n)^{-1/n}$ if $p\ne 0$, and $\rho _\omega (0)=0$. The space $\mathcal D_\omega $ of proposition 14.31.3 consists of the $p\in L^ n$ with $\rho _\omega (p)<+\infty $, and $\mathcal D_\omega ^0$ is the closure of the bounded functions in $\mathcal D_\omega $, characterized by vanishing tails in proposition 14.31.5. For a Gauduchon metric $\gamma $, $L_{\omega ,\gamma }(p)$ denotes the test quotient of section 14.28, and theorem 14.28.1 gives $\rho _\omega (p)=\sup _\gamma L_{\omega ,\gamma }(|p|)$. Finally, $k_ T$ is the determinant density of $T\in \mathscr G_\omega $ from theorem 26.8.1.

If $p\geq 0$ is measurable, define

\[ D_ p(T)=\int _ Xp k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V\qquad (T\in \mathscr G_\omega ), \]

allowing the value $+\infty $. If $T=\gamma ^{n-1}/\int _ X\omega \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}$ for a Gauduchon metric $\gamma $, then $D_ p(T)=L_{\omega ,\gamma }(p)$.

Theorem 26.9.1. For every nonnegative $p\in L^ n$,

26.54
\begin{equation} \sup _{T\in \mathscr G_\omega }D_ p(T)=\rho _\omega (p), \end{equation}

with the value $+\infty $ allowed. If $p\in \mathcal D_\omega ^0$, the functional $D_ p$ is finite, concave and weakly upper semicontinuous on $\mathscr G_\omega $, its maximum is attained, and the densities $p k_ T$, for $T\in \mathscr G_\omega $, are uniformly integrable.

Proof. For a smooth strictly positive $p$, we have $D_ p=\mathfrak D_{n\log p}$ and $\mathrm{e}^{-B_0(n\log p)/n}=\rho _\omega (p)$, so theorem 26.6.2, applied to $F=n\log p$, proves (26.54). By Hölder's inequality and theorem 26.8.1, for every real $r>n$ there is a constant $C_ r$ such that, for every $q\in L^ r$,

26.55
\begin{equation} \sup _{T\in \mathscr G_\omega }\int _ X|q| k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V \leq C_ r\| q\| _{L^ r}. \end{equation}

Next, approximate a bounded nonnegative $p$ in $L^{2n}$ by smooth strictly positive functions. This estimate and (14.114) give uniform convergence on $\mathscr G_\omega $ of the corresponding functionals $D$ and convergence of the root norms, so the supremum formula holds for bounded $p$. For such $p$, the functional $D_ p$ is also upper semicontinuous and concave, by lemma 26.6.1 and uniform convergence. For general $p\in L^ n$, we use the truncations $\min (p,K)$ at positive integer levels $K$. Monotone convergence and the definition of $b_\omega $ through truncations give

\[ \sup _ T D_ p(T)=\sup _ T\sup _ K D_{\min (p,K)}(T) =\sup _ K\rho _\omega (\min (p,K))=\rho _\omega (p). \]

This step only interchanges two suprema and gives no attainment.

Now suppose $p\in \mathcal D_\omega ^0$. The supremum formula applied to the tail gives

\[ 0\leq D_ p(T)-D_{\min (p,K)}(T) \leq \rho _\omega (p\mathbf1_{\{ p>K\} })\longrightarrow 0 \]

uniformly in $T$, by proposition 14.31.5. Hence $D_ p$ is finite and is a uniform limit of upper semicontinuous concave functionals, and compactness of $\mathscr G_\omega $ gives a maximizer. Finally, fix $1<q<n/(n-1)$. By Hölder's inequality and theorem 26.8.1, for every measurable set $E$,

\[ \sup _ T\int _ Ep k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V \leq \rho _\omega (p\mathbf1_{\{ p>K\} }) +K C_ q\, \mathrm{d}V(E)^{1-1/q}. \]

Choosing first $K$ large and then the volume of $E$ small proves uniform absolute continuity. Since the total masses are bounded by $\rho _\omega (p)$, the densities are uniformly integrable. $\square$

For a nonzero density, a maximizing current gives a supporting probability for the threshold, as we show below. Whether the threshold is attained by an exact equation depends on the regularity of the maximizing current, which enters through the trace inequalities of 26.9.3.

26.9.1 The tail condition and perturbations of the density

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Vanishing tails are equivalent to continuity of the threshold under uniformly bounded multiplicative perturbations of the density that converge in measure. This explains the role of the tail condition: if it fails, a perturbation supported on sets of shrinking volume can lower the threshold by a fixed factor.

Proposition 26.9.2. Let $0\leq p\in \mathcal D_\omega $ be nonzero. The following are equivalent:

  1. $p\in \mathcal D_\omega ^0$;

  2. the densities $p k_ T$, for $T\in \mathscr G_\omega $, are uniformly integrable;

  3. whenever $F_ j$ are smooth real functions, uniformly bounded and tending to zero in measure, one has

    \[ b_\omega (\mathrm{e}^{F_ j}p^ n)\longrightarrow b_\omega (p^ n). \]

If the first condition holds, the convergence in the third also holds for bounded measurable $F_ j$ with the same uniform bound and convergence in measure. If it fails, there are smooth $F_ j\geq 0$, uniformly bounded and tending to zero in measure, such that

\[ \mathrm{e}^{F_ j}p^ n\longrightarrow p^ n\quad \text{in }L^1, \qquad b_\omega (\mathrm{e}^{F_ j}p^ n)\leq 2^{-n}b_\omega (p^ n). \]

Proof. The first condition implies the second by theorem 26.9.1. Conversely, $\mathrm{d}V(\{ p>K\} )\to 0$ as $K\to \infty $, so uniform integrability and (26.54) give

\[ \rho _\omega (p\mathbf1_{\{ p>K\} }) =\sup _{T\in \mathscr G_\omega }\int _{\{ p>K\} }p k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V \longrightarrow 0. \]

This is the first condition by proposition 14.31.5.

If $p\in \mathcal D_\omega ^0$ and $|F_ j|\leq A$, then $p(\mathrm{e}^{F_ j/n}-1)$ tends to zero in measure and its absolute value is at most $(\mathrm{e}^{A/n}+1)p$. The dominated-convergence criterion of proposition 14.31.5 makes its root norm tend to zero, and the triangle inequality gives $\rho _\omega (p\mathrm{e}^{F_ j/n})\to \rho _\omega (p)>0$. Taking negative $n$th powers proves the third condition, also for bounded measurable $F_ j$.

Suppose now that the tails do not tend to zero, and put $R=\rho _\omega (p)>0$. The formula of theorem 14.28.1, which uses only smooth tests, yields $\delta >0$, numbers $K_ j\to \infty $ and smooth strictly positive $T_ j\in \mathscr G_\omega $ such that

\[ \int _{\{ p>K_ j\} }p k_{T_ j}\, \mathrm{d}V>\delta . \]

Since the finite measure $p k_{T_ j}\, \mathrm{d}V$ is regular, we can choose a compact set $C_ j\subset \{ p>K_ j\} $ of mass greater than $\delta /2$ for this measure. By outer regularity of $\mathrm{d}V$, choose an open set $O_ j\supset \{ p>K_ j\} $ with $\mathrm{d}V(O_ j)\leq \mathrm{d}V(\{ p>K_ j\} )+1/j$, and a smooth cutoff $0\leq \chi _ j\leq 1$ that equals one on $C_ j$ and has support in $O_ j$. Fix $A>0$ with $\mathrm{e}^{A/n}\delta /2>2R$, and set $F_ j=A\chi _ j$. Then $F_ j\to 0$ in measure, while

\[ \rho _\omega (p\mathrm{e}^{F_ j/n}) \geq \int _ Xp\mathrm{e}^{F_ j/n}k_{T_ j}\, \mathrm{d}V >2R. \]

Since $b_\omega (\mathrm{e}^{F_ j}p^ n)=\rho _\omega (p\mathrm{e}^{F_ j/n})^{-n}$, this gives the asserted uniform gap. Finally, $|\mathrm{e}^{F_ j}-1|p^ n\leq (\mathrm{e}^ A-1)p^ n\mathbf1_{O_ j}$, whose integral tends to zero by absolute continuity of the integral. This proves the last assertion and the converse to the third condition. $\square$

For $f=p^ n$, the perturbed densities satisfy $\mathrm{e}^{-A}f\leq \mathrm{e}^{F_ j}f\leq \mathrm{e}^ Af$ with a fixed $A$, so the continuity statement concerns only such order intervals. The instability under general $L^1$ approximation in section 14.31 involves approximations without these bounds.

For attainment at one density, uniform integrability along a single maximizing sequence is enough, and this weaker condition also identifies the limit of its weighted measures.

Proposition 26.9.3. Let $0\leq p\in \mathcal D_\omega $ be nonzero and put $R=\rho _\omega (p)$. Suppose $T_ j\in \mathscr G_\omega $ converge weakly to $T$ and satisfy $D_ p(T_ j)\to R$. If the densities $p k_{T_ j}$ are uniformly integrable, then

\[ D_ p(T)=R,\qquad p k_{T_ j}\, \mathrm{d}V\rightharpoonup p k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Consequently, a maximizing sequence for $D_ p$ with uniformly integrable densities $pk_{T_ j}$ has a maximizing current among its weak cluster limits.

Proof. For positive integers $K$, put $p_ K=\min (p,K)$. Since $p$ is finite almost everywhere, uniform integrability gives

\[ \varepsilon _ K:=\sup _ j\int _{\{ p>K\} }p k_{T_ j}\, \mathrm{d}V \longrightarrow 0. \]

For every nonnegative continuous function $h$, the weight $hp_ K$ is bounded and measurable, so the upper semicontinuity of $D_{hp_ K}$ proved in theorem 26.9.1 gives

\[ \limsup _ j\int _ Xhp k_{T_ j}\, \mathrm{d}V \leq \int _ Xhp_ K k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V +\| h\| _\infty \varepsilon _ K. \]

Now let $K\to \infty $. For $h=1$ this yields $R\leq D_ p(T)$, and the supremum formula (26.54) gives the reverse inequality. If $\Lambda $ is a weak cluster limit of the measures $p k_{T_ j}\, \mathrm{d}V$, the same estimate for all nonnegative continuous $h$ gives $\Lambda \leq p k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V$. Both measures have mass $R$, so they agree. Since these measures have bounded masses, they form a weakly relatively compact family, and every cluster limit has now been identified. The final assertion follows from the compactness of $\mathscr G_\omega $. $\square$

26.9.2 Supporting probabilities for an integrable density

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Under the vanishing-tail condition, we can also perturb the density by continuous multiplicative factors $\mathrm{e}^ F$. The logarithm of the threshold is then a concave function of $F$, and its supporting probabilities are given by the maximizing currents, just as for the continuous densities in section 26.7.

Let $f\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$ be nonzero and nonnegative, and assume $p=f^{1/n}\in \mathcal D_\omega ^0$. For a continuous real $F$, set

\[ B_ f(F)=\log b_\omega (\mathrm{e}^ Ff) =-n\log \rho _\omega (p \mathrm{e}^{F/n}),\qquad M_ f(F)=\rho _\omega (p \mathrm{e}^{F/n}). \]

Both quantities are finite and $M_ f(F)>0$, since $\mathcal D_\omega ^0$ is an order ideal. Let $\mathscr M_ f(F)$ be the set of currents in $\mathscr G_\omega $ maximizing $D_{p \mathrm{e}^{F/n}}$, which is nonempty by theorem 26.9.1, and put

\[ \nu _{T,F}=\frac{p \mathrm{e}^{F/n} k_ T}{M_ f(F)}\, \mathrm{d}V \quad (T\in \mathscr M_ f(F)). \]

These are probability measures. Let $\mathcal A_ f(F)$, the set of supporting probabilities of $B_ f$ at $F$, consist of all Borel probability measures $\nu $ satisfying

26.56
\begin{equation} B_ f(G)\leq B_ f(F)-\int _ X(G-F)\, \mathrm{d}\nu \qquad (G\in C(X,\mathbb R)). \end{equation}

Proposition 26.9.4. The functional $B_ f$ is concave, decreasing and $1$-Lipschitz for the uniform norm, and $B_ f(F+a)=B_ f(F)-a$ for constants $a$. Moreover,

\[ \mathcal A_ f(F)=\{ \nu _{T,F}:T\in \mathscr M_ f(F)\} . \]

This set is nonempty, convex and weakly compact, and its measures are absolutely continuous. For each $R>0$, the densities of all these measures, for all $F$ with $\| F\| _\infty \leq R$, are uniformly integrable. For $H\in C(X,\mathbb R)$, the one-sided directional derivatives of $B_ f$ at $F$ are

\[ (B_ f)'_+(F;H)=-\max _{\nu \in \mathcal A_ f(F)}\int _ XH\, \mathrm{d}\nu , \qquad (B_ f)'_-(F;H)=-\min _{\nu \in \mathcal A_ f(F)}\int _ XH\, \mathrm{d}\nu . \]

Proof. For each $T$, Hölder's inequality makes $F\mapsto \int _ Xp \mathrm{e}^{F/n}k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V$ logarithmically convex, and taking suprema preserves the inequality defining logarithmic convexity. Thus $M_ f$ is logarithmically convex and $B_ f=-n\log M_ f$ is concave. Monotonicity and homogeneity of $\rho _\omega $ give the translation rule and, by comparison with constant translates, the Lipschitz estimate.
