For fixed $F$, the weight $p\mathrm{e}^{F/n}$ belongs to $\mathcal D_\omega ^0$, so the densities $p\mathrm{e}^{F/n}k_ T$, $T\in \mathscr G_\omega $, are uniformly integrable by theorem 26.9.1. If $T_ j\to T$ and $D_{p\mathrm{e}^{F/n}}(T_ j)\to M_ f(F)$, proposition 26.9.3 makes $T$ a maximizer and gives convergence of the weighted measures. For $T_1,T_2\in \mathscr G_\omega $ and real $0\leq t\leq 1$, matrix determinant concavity gives

\[ p \mathrm{e}^{F/n}k_{tT_1+(1-t)T_2}\, \mathrm{d}V \geq t p \mathrm{e}^{F/n}k_{T_1}\, \mathrm{d}V +(1-t)p \mathrm{e}^{F/n}k_{T_2}\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

For two maximizing currents, the current on the left also maximizes, and equal total masses turn this inequality into an equality. The set $\mathscr N_ f(F)=\{ \nu _{T,F}:T\in \mathscr M_ f(F)\} $ is therefore nonempty, compact and convex.

A maximizing $T$ gives

\[ M_ f(G)\geq M_ f(F)\int _ X\mathrm{e}^{(G-F)/n}\, \mathrm{d}\nu _{T,F}. \]

Taking $-n$ times the logarithm and applying Jensen's inequality proves (26.56), so $\mathscr N_ f(F)\subset \mathcal A_ f(F)$. For the reverse inclusion, a Taylor expansion of the exponential gives

\[ D_{p \mathrm{e}^{(F+tH)/n}}(T) =D_{p \mathrm{e}^{F/n}}(T)+\frac tn\int _ X H p \mathrm{e}^{F/n} k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V+O(t^2), \]

where the error is uniform in $T$ since $D_{p \mathrm{e}^{F/n}}(T)\leq M_ f(F)$. Maximizers at $F+tH$ are near-maximizers at $F$, by the multiplicative bounds $\mathrm{e}^{-t\| H\| _\infty /n}\leq M_ f(F+tH)/M_ f(F)\leq \mathrm{e}^{t\| H\| _\infty /n}$. The convergence of their weighted measures proved above and this uniform expansion are exactly the two inputs of the one-sided differentiation argument in the proof of theorem 26.7.2, which gives

\[ (B_ f)'_+(F;H)=-\max _{\nu \in \mathscr N_ f(F)}\int _ XH\, \mathrm{d}\nu . \]

The separation argument of that proof then identifies all supporting probabilities with $\mathscr N_ f(F)$, and replacing $H$ by $-H$ gives the left derivative.

Finally, $M_ f(F)\geq \mathrm{e}^{-R/n}\rho _\omega (p)$ when $\| F\| _\infty \leq R$, so the densities of the probabilities $\nu _{T,F}$ are at most $\mathrm{e}^{2R/n}\rho _\omega (p)^{-1}p k_ T$. The uniform integrability of the densities $pk_ T$ in theorem 26.9.1 proves the remaining assertions. $\square$

When $f=1$, we have $B_ f=B_0$, and the proposition reduces to theorem 26.7.2. For general $f$, the functional $B_ f$ is defined through the subsolution threshold and does not presuppose that the corresponding equation has a solution. If $v_-(\omega )>0$, every nonzero nonnegative $f\in L^1$ satisfies $f^{1/n}\in \mathcal D_\omega ^0$ by proposition 14.31.5, and corollary 31.7.1 identifies $b_\omega (f)$ as the unique constant for which the equation $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (v)=b\, f\, \mathrm{d}V$ has a solution $v$ in the envelope class $\mathcal E_{\mathrm{env}}(X,\omega ,0)$ of that corollary. Without positive lower mass, the uniform tail condition is an explicit sufficient hypothesis for this compact dual description.

26.9.3 Trace inequalities at the subsolution threshold

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Let $0\leq f\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$ be nonzero with $b=b_\omega (f)>0$. Recall from section 14.27 that a threshold subsolution is a function $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ with $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (u)\geq bf\, \mathrm{d}V$, and a threshold solution is one for which equality holds; threshold subsolutions exist by theorem 14.27.2. A smooth Gauduchon test can be paired with the full current $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ of a threshold subsolution, and by lemma 6.4.2 the resulting trace measure is bounded below by the determinant-weighted density. The nonnegative difference has a mass that depends only on the test. Along a maximizing sequence this mass tends to zero, uniformly over all threshold subsolutions. We write $\| \cdot \| _{\mathrm{TV}}$ for the total variation of a finite signed measure.

Proposition 26.9.5. Let $0\leq f\in L^1$ be nonzero and $b=b_\omega (f)>0$, and write $p=f^{1/n}$. Suppose $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ satisfies $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (u)\geq bf\, \mathrm{d}V$. For every smooth strictly positive $T\in \mathscr G_\omega $, the measure

\[ R_{u,T}=(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)\wedge T-b^{1/n}p k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V \]

is positive and has mass

\[ R_{u,T}(X)=1-b^{1/n}D_ p(T). \]

In particular, if $(T_ j)$ is a sequence of smooth strictly positive members of $\mathscr G_\omega $ with $D_ p(T_ j)\to \rho _\omega (p)$, then $\| R_{u,T_ j}\| _{\mathrm{TV}}\to 0$, uniformly over all threshold subsolutions $u$.

If also $p\in \mathcal D_\omega ^0$ and $T_ j\to T$ as currents, then $T$ maximizes $D_ p$ and

\[ (\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)\wedge T_ j \rightharpoonup b^{1/n}p k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

In particular, for a fixed sequence $(T_ j)$ with limit $T$, this limiting probability is the same for all threshold subsolutions $u$.

Proof. A smooth strictly positive $T\in \mathscr G_\omega $ is $\gamma ^{n-1}$ for a Gauduchon metric $\gamma $ normalized by $\int _ X\omega \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}=1$. Lemma 6.4.2 proves positivity of $R_{u,T}$, and Stokes' theorem for currents gives $((\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)\wedge T)(X)=\int _ X\omega \wedge T=1$. This proves the mass formula. Since $\rho _\omega (p)=b^{-1/n}$, the nonnegative mass tends to zero along a maximizing sequence; it equals the total variation norm and does not depend on $u$.

Under the tail hypothesis, theorem 26.9.1 gives uniform integrability of the densities $p k_{T_ j}$. Thus proposition 26.9.3 makes $T$ a maximizer and gives $p k_{T_ j}\, \mathrm{d}V\rightharpoonup p k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V$. Adding the error measures $R_{u,T_ j}$, which tend to zero in total variation, proves the limit formula. $\square$

The limit formula concerns smooth maximizing sequences. It does not define the product $(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)\wedge T$ for a singular current $T$, nor does it show that the limit is the same for different maximizing currents.

If a maximizing test is itself a smooth strictly positive form, the trace inequality becomes an equality of currents, and since $n\geq 2$ this forces an exact smooth equation. The local regularity needed for this comes from lemma 1.1.4.

Corollary 26.9.6. Let $0\leq f\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$ be nonzero with $b=b_\omega (f)>0$. If $D_{f^{1/n}}$ has a smooth strictly positive maximizer $T\in \mathscr G_\omega $, then $f^{1/n}$ agrees almost everywhere with a smooth nonnegative function, and every threshold subsolution $u$ is smooth and satisfies

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\omega (u)=bf\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

In particular a smooth threshold solution exists, and any two threshold subsolutions differ by a constant.

Proof. Let $u$ be any threshold subsolution; one exists by theorem 14.27.2. Write $T=\gamma ^{n-1}$, where $\gamma $ is the smooth Gauduchon metric given by the positive $(n-1)$st root of $T$, and set

\[ a=\left(\frac{bf\, \mathrm{d}V}{\gamma ^ n}\right)^{1/n}. \]

Put $A=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0$. Since $T$ maximizes, the positive measure $R_{u,T}$ of proposition 26.9.5 has mass zero, which gives the equality

\[ A\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}=a\gamma ^ n. \]

The coefficient measures of the positive current $A$ are controlled by this trace measure, which is absolutely continuous. Thus $A$ has a matrix density that is positive semidefinite almost everywhere, with coefficients in $L^ n$ because $a\in L^ n$.

For every smooth Hermitian form $\beta $, lemma 6.4.2 also gives

\[ A\wedge \beta ^{n-1} \geq \left(\frac{bf\, \mathrm{d}V}{\beta ^ n}\right)^{1/n}\beta ^ n. \]

The resulting pointwise matrix inequalities hold almost everywhere, simultaneously for all positive definite matrices: on each coordinate chart, first use a countable dense family of constant positive matrices, realized on smaller charts by global smooth Hermitian forms, and then use continuity in the matrix. The variational formula for $(\det P)^{1/n}$ in the proof of lemma 26.6.1 therefore yields

\[ \left(\frac{A^ n}{\gamma ^ n}\right)^{1/n}\geq a \quad \text{almost everywhere}. \]

Here $A^ n$ denotes only the determinant of the measurable coefficient matrix of $A$; no nonlinear product of currents is used. The trace equality gives the reverse inequality, by the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality. Equality then forces all eigenvalues of $A$ relative to $\gamma $ to equal $a$, also where $a=0$. Consequently $A=a\gamma $ as currents.

By lemma 1.1.4, applied with $\theta =\omega $, both $a$ and $u$ are smooth, and $f^{1/n}=b^{-1/n}a(\gamma ^ n/\mathrm{d}V)^{1/n}$ has a smooth nonnegative representative. Since $u$ is smooth, we may now take the top power: $(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n=a^ n\gamma ^ n=bf\, \mathrm{d}V$. Finally, the function $a$ was defined from $f$, $b$ and $\gamma $ alone, so two threshold subsolutions $u_1,u_2$ satisfy $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1=a\gamma =\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_2$. Their smooth difference is therefore pluriharmonic, hence constant on the connected compact manifold $X$. $\square$

If $f$ is smooth and strictly positive, theorem 26.1.3 provides a smooth strictly positive maximizing Gauduchon form, obtained from the smooth equation. The corollary therefore applies, and shows in particular that all threshold subsolutions, bounded or not, differ from the smooth solution by constants.

Corollary 26.9.7. Under the hypotheses of corollary 26.9.6, let $u$ be the threshold solution with $\sup _ Xu=0$. If $u_\lambda $ is the unique bounded solution of

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\omega (u_\lambda )=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u_\lambda }f\, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad \lambda >0, \]

then $u_\lambda -\sup _ Xu_\lambda \to u$ uniformly as $\lambda \downarrow 0$.

Proof. By corollary 26.9.6, $f$ agrees almost everywhere with a smooth function, so it belongs to $L^ p$ for every finite $p$. By proposition 14.27.4, the constant $c$ for which $\mathrm{MA}_\omega (v)=cf\, \mathrm{d}V$ has a bounded solution is $b_\omega (f)$. Thus proposition 14.19.1, with the trivial symmetry group, shows that the normalized family $u_\lambda -\sup _ Xu_\lambda $ is uniformly precompact as $\lambda \downarrow 0$ and that every cluster limit is a normalized solution with this constant. Each cluster limit is therefore a normalized threshold subsolution and equals $u$ by corollary 26.9.6. If the whole family did not converge uniformly, a sequence staying at a fixed positive uniform distance from $u$ would have a uniformly convergent subsequence, a contradiction. $\square$

Corollary 26.9.8. Suppose $\omega $ is Kähler. For a nonzero nonnegative $f\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$, the functional $D_{f^{1/n}}$ has a smooth strictly positive maximizer in $\mathscr G_\omega $ if and only if $f$ agrees almost everywhere with a smooth strictly positive function.

Proof. The Kähler formula in proposition 14.27.4 gives $b_\omega (f)>0$. If a smooth strictly positive maximizer exists, the proof of corollary 26.9.6 gives

\[ \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=a\gamma , \qquad a\geq 0, \]

with $a,u$ smooth and $a\not\equiv 0$. Since $\mathrm{d}\omega =0$, the first-order equation in lemma 1.1.4 becomes $\mathrm{d}a=-aC$ for a smooth one-form $C$. Along any smooth path $\sigma $, this is the linear scalar equation

\[ (a\circ \sigma )'=-(a\circ \sigma )C(\dot\sigma ). \]

whose solution is the initial value multiplied by a positive exponential. Since $X$ is connected and $a\not\equiv 0$, it follows that $a>0$ everywhere, and $f=b_\omega (f)^{-1}a^ n\gamma ^ n/\mathrm{d}V$ is smooth and strictly positive. Conversely, if $f$ is smooth and strictly positive, the smooth equation and theorem 26.1.3 provide the maximizing Gauduchon form. $\square$

For a nonclosed background, the strict positivity of $f$ in corollary 26.9.8 fails, on every compact complex manifold of dimension at least two, including Kähler manifolds.

Example 26.9.9. On every connected compact complex manifold $X$ of dimension $n\geq 2$, with a fixed smooth strictly positive probability volume $\mathrm{d}V$, there is a nonclosed Hermitian form $\omega $ and a nonzero smooth nonnegative density $f$ vanishing on an open set such that $b_\omega (f)=1$ and $D_{f^{1/n}}$ has a smooth strictly positive maximizer.

Proof. Fix a Gauduchon metric $\gamma $ and a point $x$. Choose a smooth real function $\psi $ such that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi \leq -\gamma $ on a coordinate neighborhood $U$ of $x$; in holomorphic coordinates $z=(z_1,\ldots ,z_ n)$ centered at $x$, such a function is obtained by multiplying $-M|z|^2$, with $M$ large enough, by a cutoff that equals one near $x$, and then shrinking $U$.

Choose a smooth function $\chi $ with $0\leq \chi \leq 1$ that vanishes on a smaller neighborhood of $x$ and equals one outside $U$. Put

\[ a=L\chi ,\qquad \omega =a\gamma -\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi . \]

Since $X$ is compact, $\omega $ is strictly positive for sufficiently large $L>0$: on $U$ the term $-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi $ is at least $\gamma $, and outside $U$ the term $L\gamma $ dominates $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi $. Set $f=a^ n\gamma ^ n/\mathrm{d}V$. The smooth function $\psi $ satisfies $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi =a\gamma \geq 0$ and $\mathrm{MA}_\omega (\psi )=f\, \mathrm{d}V$, so $b_\omega (f)\geq 1$.

Stokes' theorem and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\gamma ^{n-1}=0$ give

\[ \int _ X\omega \wedge \gamma ^{n-1} =\int _ Xa\gamma ^ n =\int _ X(f\, \mathrm{d}V/\gamma ^ n)^{1/n}\gamma ^ n. \]

Testing (14.104) with $\gamma $ therefore gives the opposite inequality $b_\omega (f)\leq 1$. Hence $b_\omega (f)=1$, the metric $\gamma $ attains the infimum, and the normalized current $\gamma ^{n-1}/\int _ X\omega \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}$ is a smooth strictly positive maximizer of $D_{f^{1/n}}$. If $\omega $ were closed, corollary 26.9.8 would force $f$ to be strictly positive, contrary to its vanishing on an open set. Thus $\omega $ is nonclosed. $\square$

In example 26.9.9, corollary 26.9.6, corollary 26.9.7 show that every threshold subsolution is a constant translate of $\psi $ and that the normalized solutions of the exponential equations converge uniformly to $\psi -\sup _ X\psi $. Thus vanishing of the density on an open set does not by itself force nonuniqueness or the failure of this approximation.

The restriction $n\geq 2$ in corollary 26.9.6 is necessary. On a compact connected Riemann surface $X$ with a Hermitian form $\omega $, a normalized Gauduchon current has bidegree $(0,0)$, and the equation $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}T=0$ makes it a harmonic function, hence the constant $(\int _ X\omega )^{-1}$. This single smooth strictly positive current maximizes for every nonzero nonnegative $L^1$ density, which need not have a smooth representative.

26.9.4 A smooth maximizing form with an analytic degeneracy set

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now allow the smooth maximizing form to degenerate. Where it is strictly positive, the argument of corollary 26.9.6 still determines the solution current and gives the equation. If the degeneracy set is a proper analytic subset, neither side of the equation charges it, so the equation holds on all of $X$. Uniqueness follows by passing to the maximum of two threshold subsolutions and applying lemma 1.17.2.

Theorem 26.9.10. Let $0\leq f\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$ be nonzero, and assume $b=b_\omega (f)>0$. Suppose that $D_{f^{1/n}}$ has a smooth semipositive maximizer $T\in \mathscr G_\omega $ that is strictly positive on $X\setminus Z$, where $Z$ is a proper analytic subset of $X$. Then every threshold subsolution $u$ is smooth on $X\setminus Z$ and satisfies

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (u)=bf\, \mathrm{d}V \]

on $X$. In particular a threshold solution exists, and it has finite mass. The function $f^{1/n}$ has a smooth nonnegative representative on $X\setminus Z$.

Any two threshold subsolutions differ by a constant.

Proof. Put $p=f^{1/n}$ and $A=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$, where $u$ is any threshold subsolution; one exists by theorem 14.27.2. Fix a smooth strictly positive $T_0\in \mathscr G_\omega $, and for real $\varepsilon >0$ set $T_\varepsilon =(T+\varepsilon T_0)/(1+\varepsilon )$. Each $T_\varepsilon $ is a smooth strictly positive member of $\mathscr G_\omega $, so proposition 26.9.5 gives

\[ A\wedge T_\varepsilon \geq b^{1/n}p k_{T_\varepsilon }\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

For a smooth semipositive form $T$ with coefficient matrix $P'$ relative to $\omega ^ n$, (26.39) gives $k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V=f_0(\det P')^{1/n}\omega ^ n$, so that $k_ T=f_0^{1-n}(\det P')^{1/n}$ is a continuous function of that matrix. Thus $k_{T_\varepsilon }\to k_ T$ uniformly on the compact manifold $X$. Since $p\in L^1$, passing to the limit gives

\[ A\wedge T\geq b^{1/n}p k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

The left side has mass one by Stokes' theorem and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}T=0$, and the right side has mass $b^{1/n}D_ p(T)=1$ because $T$ maximizes. Their difference is positive and has mass zero, hence

26.57
\begin{equation} A\wedge T=b^{1/n}p k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V. \end{equation}

On $U=X\setminus Z$ write $T=\gamma ^{n-1}$, where $\gamma $ is smooth and strictly positive, and set $a=(bf\, \mathrm{d}V/\gamma ^ n)^{1/n}$. The trace equality becomes $A\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}=a\gamma ^ n$ on $U$. On relatively compact subsets of $U$, it controls every coefficient measure of $A$ by an $L^ n$ density. The trace inequalities for all Hermitian forms and the matrix arithmetic–geometric mean inequality, used exactly as in the proof of corollary 26.9.6, force $A=a\gamma $ on $U$. This step uses only the positivity of $\gamma $ on $U$, since the Hermitian test forms needed there can be taken to be global forms obtained by extension from smaller coordinate charts.

The local regularity lemma lemma 1.1.4 makes $u$ and $a$ smooth on $U$. Taking the top power proves the equation on $U$, and $f^{1/n}=b^{-1/n}a(\gamma ^ n/\mathrm{d}V)^{1/n}$ is smooth and nonnegative there. Both $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (u)$ and $f\, \mathrm{d}V$ put no mass on the pluripolar set $Z$. Hence the equality holds on $X$, and its total mass is $b\int _ Xf\, \mathrm{d}V<+\infty $.

It remains to prove uniqueness. Let $v$ be another threshold subsolution and put $w=\max (u,v)$. By proposition 6.2.1,

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (w)\geq \mathbf1_{\{ u\geq v\} }\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (u) +\mathbf1_{\{ u<v\} }\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (v) \geq bf\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Here the pole sets carry no mass for any of the measures involved. Thus $w$ is also a threshold subsolution, and the preceding argument gives the same smooth current $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w=a\gamma $ on $U$.

We apply lemma 1.17.2 to $u\leq w$ and to $v\leq w$: their complex Hessians agree on $X\setminus Z$, so $w-u$ and $w-v$ are constant. Hence $u-v$ is constant. $\square$

26.9.5 The limit of the exponential equations

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The uniqueness just proved identifies the limit, as $\lambda \downarrow 0$, of the solutions of the exponential equations $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (u)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}f\, \mathrm{d}V$. To control their normalization, we use the probability measure obtained from the maximizing form and the density. Normalized potentials have bounded moments with respect to this measure, and Jensen's inequality converts this bound into an estimate for the constants and a common lower barrier for the potentials.

Proposition 26.9.11. Assume the hypotheses of theorem 26.9.10, and let $u_0$ be its unique threshold solution with $\sup _ Xu_0=0$. For real $\lambda >0$, let $u_\lambda $ be the greatest solution of the exponential equation (14.123), with $\omega $ in place of $h$, given by corollary 14.34.2, and put

\[ v_\lambda =u_\lambda -\sup _ Xu_\lambda ,\qquad c_\lambda =\mathrm{e}^{\lambda \sup _ Xu_\lambda }. \]

There is a constant $C$ independent of $\lambda >0$ such that

\[ b\leq c_\lambda \leq b\, \mathrm{e}^{C\lambda },\qquad u_0-C\leq v_\lambda \leq 0. \]

Moreover $v_\lambda \to u_0$ in $L^1(X)$ as $\lambda \downarrow 0$.

Proof. Write $p=f^{1/n}$ and introduce the probability measure

\[ \mathrm{d}\nu =b^{1/n}p k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

It has mass one because $T$ maximizes. Its density belongs to $L^ n$, since $p\in L^ n$ and the smooth semipositive form $T$ has bounded determinant density $k_ T$. By lemma 1.5.1, $\omega $-plurisubharmonic functions with supremum zero have uniformly bounded moments of every finite order. Hölder's inequality with the exponents $n$ and $n/(n-1)$, which is finite because $n\geq 2$, therefore gives

\[ \int _ X(-v_\lambda )\, \mathrm{d}\nu \leq C. \]

Since $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (v_\lambda )=c_\lambda \mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\lambda }f\, \mathrm{d}V$, the trace inequality of lemma 6.4.2, applied with the positive regularizations of $T$ and passed to the limit as in the preceding proof, gives $(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\lambda )\wedge T\geq (c_\lambda /b)^{1/n}\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\lambda /n}\, \nu $. Integrating, we obtain

\[ 1\geq (c_\lambda /b)^{1/n} \int _ X\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\lambda /n}\, \mathrm{d}\nu \geq (c_\lambda /b)^{1/n}\mathrm{e}^{-C\lambda /n}, \]

where the last step is Jensen's inequality. This proves the upper bound for $c_\lambda $. The lower bound and the comparison

\[ v_\lambda \geq u_0-\lambda ^{-1}\log (c_\lambda /b) \]

follow from corollary 14.34.2 applied with $v=u_0$, and together with the upper bound they give the common lower barrier $u_0-C$.

Take any sequence $\lambda _ j\downarrow 0$. By theorem 1.4.2, a subsequence of $(v_{\lambda _ j})$ converges in $L^1$ to some $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ with supremum zero, and proposition 14.27.3 makes $v$ a threshold subsolution. Uniqueness in theorem 26.9.10 therefore gives $v=u_0$. Since every sequence tending to zero has a subsequence with this same $L^1$ limit, the whole family converges. $\square$

Under the additional envelope condition of the next corollary, the lower barrier upgrades this to convergence in capacity. If the maximizing form is strictly positive everywhere, corollary 26.9.7 already gives uniform convergence.

Corollary 26.9.12. Under the hypotheses of proposition 26.9.11, suppose in addition that the obstacle envelope $P_{\delta \omega }(u_0)$ of section 1.19 is not identically $-\infty $ for some real $0<\delta <1$. Then $v_\lambda \to u_0$ in capacity as $\lambda \downarrow 0$. In particular this holds if the threshold solution $u_0$ is bounded.

Proof. Fix a sequence $\lambda _ j\downarrow 0$. Proposition 26.9.11 gives $u_0-C\leq v_{\lambda _ j}\leq 0$ and $v_{\lambda _ j}\to u_0$ in $L^1$. For all $j$ with $\lambda _ j\leq 1$,

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (v_{\lambda _ j}) =c_{\lambda _ j}\mathrm{e}^{\lambda _ jv_{\lambda _ j}}f\, \mathrm{d}V \leq b\mathrm{e}^{C}f\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

The bounded functions $|\mathrm{e}^{v_{\lambda _ j}}-\mathrm{e}^{u_0}|$ tend to zero in $\mathrm{d}V$-measure. Since $f\in L^1$, their integrals against $f\, \mathrm{d}V$ tend to zero, so the error term in (14.10) tends to zero. We apply proposition 14.8.2 with $\theta =\omega $ and $\varphi =u_0$; its additional hypothesis that the $L^1$ limit $u$ satisfies $u\preceq \varphi $, in the notation of definition 1.12.1, holds trivially, since $u=u_0$. This proves convergence in capacity along every sequence, and hence for the family. Finally, if $u_0$ is bounded, a constant below it is a competitor for $P_{\delta \omega }(u_0)$, so this envelope is not identically $-\infty $. $\square$

In general, the condition on $P_{\delta \omega }(u_0)$ is an additional hypothesis. The next subsection shows that it holds when the Monge–Ampère mass of $\omega $ is invariant, or when the singularity of $u_0$ satisfies a quantitative bound.

26.9.6 Envelope criteria and extension across the degeneracy set

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The capacity criterion above requires the obstacle envelope of $u_0$ in a smaller background $\delta \omega $ not to be identically $-\infty $. We give two sufficient conditions of different origin: invariance of the Monge–Ampère mass controls the contact measures of envelopes of stretched truncations, while a sublinear bound on the singularity provides explicit competitors. Both conditions also give zero Lelong numbers. In the setting of theorem 26.9.10, if the maximizing form has a globally smooth root of rank at least two, zero Lelong numbers then allow the threshold solution to be extended smoothly across the analytic degeneracy set.

The setting is unchanged: $X$ has dimension $n\geq 2$, $\omega $ is a Hermitian form and $\mathrm{d}V$ a smooth positive probability volume form. Inequalities between quasi-plurisubharmonic functions refer to their canonical representatives, and $\nu (u,x)$ denotes the Lelong number of $u$ at $x$. We will use the following consequence of lemma 1.17.7, where $C_\omega $ is the bound for the Lelong numbers of $\omega $-plurisubharmonic functions given there: if $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ and, for every $0<\delta <1$, some $v_\delta \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\delta \omega )$ satisfies $v_\delta \leq u$, then

\[ 0\leq \nu (u,x)\leq \nu (v_\delta ,x) =\delta \nu (v_\delta /\delta ,x)\leq \delta C_\omega . \]

Thus $u$ has zero Lelong numbers; the bound is uniform in $\delta $ because $v_\delta /\delta \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$.

Theorem 26.9.13. Suppose $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\omega ^ j)=0$ for $1\leq j<n$. Put $V=\int _ X\omega ^ n$. For every nonzero $0\leq f\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$, with $M=\int _ Xf\, \mathrm{d}V$, one has

\[ b_\omega (f)=\frac VM. \]

Every threshold subsolution $u$ satisfies $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (u)=(V/M)f\, \mathrm{d}V$ and has mass $V$. Its obstacle envelope $P_{\delta \omega }(u)$ is not identically $-\infty $ for any $0<\delta <1$, and $u$ has zero Lelong numbers. More generally, these last two conclusions hold for every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ with $\int _ X\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (u)=V$.

Proof. By theorem 2.2.2, every bounded $\omega $-plurisubharmonic function has mass $V$, and truncation therefore bounds every extended mass by $V$. For $f_ k=\min (f,k)$, integration of the bounded equation gives $c_\omega (f_ k)=V/\int _ Xf_ k\, \mathrm{d}V$, so the definition (14.101) gives $b_\omega (f)=V/M$. A threshold subsolution has mass at least $b_\omega (f)M=V$. Its mass is consequently $V$, and the nonnegative difference between its measure and $b_\omega (f)f\, \mathrm{d}V$ has mass zero.

Now let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ have extended mass $V$. Normalize $u\leq 0$, put $\mu _ u=\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (u)$, and fix $a>1$. For $u_ j=\max (u,-j)$, let $w_ j=P_\omega (au_ j)$. Comparison with constants gives $-aj\leq w_ j\leq 0$. The bounded contact theorem theorem 13.1.3 shows that $\mathrm{MA}_\omega (w_ j)$ is concentrated on the contact set $C_ j=\{ w_ j=au_ j\} $. Since the two functions are canonical representatives and $w_ j\leq au_ j$ almost everywhere, this inequality holds everywhere. Both functions are $a\omega $-plurisubharmonic, so positivity and proposition 6.2.1 give

\[ \mathrm{MA}_\omega (w_ j) \leq \mathbf1_{C_ j}\mathrm{MA}_{a\omega }(w_ j) \leq a^ n\mathbf1_{C_ j}\mathrm{MA}_\omega (u_ j). \]

For integers $j\geq N$, locality of the products on $\{ u>-N\} $ and the mass identity for bounded functions yield

\[ \int _{\{ u_ j\leq -N\} }\mathrm{MA}_\omega (u_ j) =V-\mu _ u(\{ u>-N\} )=\mu _ u(\{ u\leq -N\} ). \]

Choose $N$ with $a^ n\mu _ u(\{ u\leq -N\} )<V$; this is possible because $\mu _ u$ is finite and puts no mass on the pole set of $u$. If $\sup _ Xw_ j<-aN$, the contact set $C_ j$ lies in $\{ u_ j<-N\} $, and the preceding inequalities contradict $\int _ X\mathrm{MA}_\omega (w_ j)=V$. Hence $\sup _ Xw_ j\geq -aN$ for $j\geq N$, and for smaller $j$ the bound $\sup _ Xw_ j\geq -aj$ suffices.

The envelopes $w_ j$ decrease with $j$, and by compactness of normalized quasi-plurisubharmonic functions this uniform lower bound for their suprema prevents their limit from being identically $-\infty $. Their limit $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ satisfies $w\leq au$, also at the poles of $u$. With $\delta =1/a$, the function $\delta w\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\delta \omega )$ is therefore a competitor for $P_{\delta \omega }(u)$, so this envelope is not identically $-\infty $. Since $a>1$ was arbitrary, this holds for every $0<\delta <1$, and the Lelong estimate preceding the theorem completes the proof. The normalization $u\leq 0$ does not affect any conclusion. $\square$

Proposition 26.9.14. Let $\psi \leq 0$ be a quasi-plurisubharmonic function with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi \geq -C\omega $ for some $C>0$, and let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ satisfy, everywhere on $X$,

\[ u\geq -A(1-\psi )^\alpha -B, \qquad A>0,\quad B\in \mathbb R,\quad 0<\alpha <1. \]

Then $P_{\delta \omega }(u)$ is not identically $-\infty $ for every $0<\delta <1$, and $u$ has zero Lelong numbers.

Proof. Choose $\alpha <\beta <1$ and $\varepsilon >0$ with $\varepsilon \beta C\leq \delta $. The number

\[ K=B+\sup _{t\geq 1}(At^\alpha -\varepsilon t^\beta ) \]

is finite, and $v_\delta =-\varepsilon (1-\psi )^\beta -K\leq u$. The function $t\mapsto -\varepsilon (1-t)^\beta $ is increasing and convex on $t\leq 0$, with derivative at most $\varepsilon \beta $. Applying the chain inequality for quasi-plurisubharmonic functions to $\max (\psi ,-j)$ and passing to the decreasing limit as $j\to \infty $, we obtain $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\delta \geq -\varepsilon \beta C\omega \geq -\delta \omega $. The limit is not identically $-\infty $ because $\psi $ is finite almost everywhere; at the poles of $\psi $ it takes the value $-\infty $, where the comparison with $u$ still holds. Thus $v_\delta \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\delta \omega )$ is a competitor for $P_{\delta \omega }(u)$ for each $\delta $, and the Lelong estimate preceding theorem 26.9.13 proves the last assertion. $\square$

A globally smooth root of rank at least two

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Theorem 26.9.15. Assume the hypotheses of theorem 26.9.10, with analytic degeneracy set $Z$, and let $\Phi _ f$ be the greatest threshold subsolution with supremum zero, from proposition 14.27.3; by that theorem it is the threshold solution with supremum zero. Suppose that the maximizing form is $T=\gamma ^{n-1}$ for a globally smooth semipositive $(1,1)$-form $\gamma $ whose rank is at least two everywhere. If $\Phi _ f$ has zero Lelong numbers, then it is smooth on $X$; by theorem 26.9.13, proposition 26.9.14, this applies under either of the conditions above. Every threshold subsolution is then smooth, since these subsolutions differ by constants.

Proof. Put $U=X\setminus Z$ and $A=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Phi _ f$. By theorem 26.9.10, $A$ is smooth on $U$ and $\Phi _ f$ satisfies the equation on $X$; the root $\gamma $ is positive on $U$. Stokes' theorem gives $\int _ XA\wedge T=1$, while attainment gives

\[ \int _ U(A^ n)^{1/n}(\gamma ^ n)^{(n-1)/n} =b_\omega (f)^{1/n}D_{f^{1/n}}(T)=1. \]

The difference $A\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}-(A^ n/\gamma ^ n)^{1/n}\gamma ^ n$ is nonnegative and continuous on $U$ by the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality, and its integral vanishes. The equality case gives $A=r\gamma $ on $U$ with the smooth function $r=\operatorname {tr}_\gamma A/n\geq 0$; where $A^ n=0$, equality forces $A=0$ and $r=0$.

Since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Phi _ f$ is closed,

\[ \mathrm dr\wedge \gamma +r\, \mathrm d\gamma =\mathrm d\omega \quad \hbox{on }U. \]

For a semipositive form $\gamma $ of rank at least two, the real-linear map $W_\gamma :\xi \mapsto \xi \wedge \gamma $ from real one-forms to three-forms is injective at every point. Indeed, write $\gamma =\sum _ k\lambda _ k\, \mathrm dx_ k\wedge \mathrm dy_ k$ at the point in suitable real coordinates $z_ k=x_ k+\mathrm iy_ k$, with $\lambda _ k\geq 0$ and at least two $\lambda _ k$ positive. For each index $j$ choose $k\neq j$ with $\lambda _ k>0$. The coefficient of $\mathrm dx_ j\wedge \mathrm dx_ k\wedge \mathrm dy_ k$ in $\xi \wedge \gamma $ is $\lambda _ k$ times the coefficient of $\mathrm dx_ j$ in $\xi $, and similarly for $\mathrm dy_ j$. Hence $\xi \wedge \gamma =0$ forces $\xi =0$.

Choose an auxiliary Riemannian metric. The left inverse $L_\gamma =(W_\gamma ^*W_\gamma )^{-1}W_\gamma ^*$ is smooth and uniformly bounded on compact $X$. Thus

26.58
\begin{equation} \mathrm dr=\alpha -r\beta ,\qquad \alpha =L_\gamma (\mathrm d\omega ),\quad \beta =L_\gamma (\mathrm d\gamma ), \end{equation}

with smooth one-forms on $X$. In particular $|\mathrm d\log (1+r)|\leq C$ on $U$.

Points of $U$ can be joined by short paths avoiding $Z$. In a coordinate ball, stratify $Z$ into countably many smooth strata of real dimension at most $2n-2$. For a fixed point outside $Z$, the endpoints of segments from it that meet one stratum lie in a cone of dimension at most $2n-1$, which is Lebesgue-null. Given two nearby points outside $Z$, choose a generic intermediate point in a ball of radius comparable to their distance; both connecting segments then avoid $Z$, and their combined length is bounded by a fixed multiple of that distance. Using a finite connected cover by coordinate balls, with intermediate points chosen in the overlaps, we also obtain a uniform bound for the lengths of connecting paths in $U$.

The bound on $\mathrm d\log (1+r)$ now bounds $r$ on $U$, and (26.58) bounds its derivative. The short paths therefore give a unique locally Lipschitz extension of $r$ across $Z$. Since $Z$ is Lebesgue-null, the weak derivative of the extension satisfies (26.58) on $X$. The right side is continuous, so $r$ is $C^1$, and iterating makes it smooth.

Set $S=r\gamma $. It is smooth and semipositive, and $\mathrm dS=\mathrm d\omega $ on $X$ by density of $U$. The current $R=A-S$ is therefore closed, and it is supported on $Z$. It is also positive: since $A=S$ off $Z$ and $S$ puts no mass on $Z$, the current $R$ is the restriction of the positive current $A$ to $Z$. The support theorem recalled in section 1.17 expresses $R$ as a nonnegative combination of currents of integration along the components of $Z$ of codimension one. Locally $S-\omega $ is smooth and closed, so it has a smooth local $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-potential, and the coefficients of $R$ are the generic Lelong numbers of $\Phi _ f$ along these components. All of them vanish, so $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Phi _ f=S-\omega $ is smooth. Taking a Euclidean trace and applying elliptic regularity gives a smooth function equal to $\Phi _ f$ almost everywhere, and hence everywhere, since $\Phi _ f$ is its canonical representative. $\square$

The smooth root and its rank bound are hypotheses of the theorem: a smooth semipositive $(n-1,n-1)$-form need not have a smooth $(1,1)$-root across its degeneracy set. On a surface the rank condition already forces $\gamma >0$ everywhere.

Two attained instances

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Example 26.9.16. On the Hopf surface $X=(\mathbb C^2\setminus \{ 0\} )/\langle z\mapsto 2z\rangle $, the background $\omega =|z|^{-2}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2$ satisfies $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\omega =0$, so its Monge–Ampère mass is invariant. There are smooth densities whose threshold solutions are smooth and nonconstant and whose maximizing tests are smooth strictly positive forms.

Proof. The form is invariant under $z\mapsto 2z$, so it descends to $X$, and it is positive. For $s=|z|^2$ and $F(s)=s^{-1}$, the identity $sF''+2F'=0$ gives $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\omega =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(s^{-1})\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}s=0$, while $\omega $ is not closed. Choose a nonconstant smooth real $q$ and small nonzero $\varepsilon $ such that $A=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\varepsilon q)>0$. Put $V=\int _ X\omega ^2$, $\mathrm{d}V=\omega ^2/V$, $f=A^2/\mathrm{d}V$ and $u=\varepsilon q-\sup _ X\varepsilon q$. Invariance of the mass gives $\int _ Xf\, \mathrm{d}V=\int _ XA^2=V$, hence $b_\omega (f)=1$ by theorem 26.9.13.

The form $T=A/V$ satisfies $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}T=0$, $\int _ X\omega \wedge T=1$ and $D_{\sqrt f}(T)=1$. For every smooth strictly positive $Q\in \mathscr G_\omega $, the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality and Stokes' theorem give $D_{\sqrt f}(Q)\leq \int _ XA\wedge Q=1$. Thus $T$ is a smooth strictly positive maximizer, and since $u$ is a threshold solution with supremum zero, corollary 26.9.6 gives $\Phi _ f=u$. This is a bounded nonconstant example. $\square$

Example 26.9.17. Let $n\geq 3$, and view the smooth quadric of dimension $n$ as a double cover of $\mathbb P^ n$. For the restricted Fubini–Study form and a suitable smooth density, the maximizing test is a smooth semipositive form whose smooth root has rank at least two everywhere and degenerates along the ramification divisor.

Proof. Let

\[ X=\{ [w:z_0:\cdots :z_ n]:w^2=\textstyle \sum _{i=0}^ nz_ i^2\} \subset \mathbb P^{n+1},\qquad F([w:z])=[z]. \]

The quadratic form is nonsingular, and $F$ is finite of degree two, with smooth ramification divisor $Z=\{ w=0\} $. Local branch coordinates show that $\mathrm dF$ has rank $n-1$ on $Z$ and $n$ elsewhere. Let $\omega $ be the restriction to $X$ of the normalized Fubini–Study form of $\mathbb P^{n+1}$, and let $A=F^*\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$, where $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$ is the normalized Fubini–Study form of $\mathbb P^ n$. Then

\[ u=\log \frac{\sum _ i|z_ i|^2}{|w|^2+\sum _ i|z_ i|^2} \]

is a smooth function on $X$ with $-\log 2\leq u\leq 0$, $\sup _ Xu=0$ and $A=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$. The change of variables for the branched cover gives $\int _ XA^ n=2$, and Stokes' theorem gives $V=\int _ X\omega ^ n=2$.

Set $\mathrm{d}V=\omega ^ n/V$, $f=A^ n/\mathrm{d}V$, $T=A^{n-1}/V$ and $\gamma =A/V^{1/(n-1)}$. Since $\omega $ is closed, theorem 26.9.13 gives $b_\omega (f)=V/\int _ Xf\, \mathrm{d}V=1$. Stokes' theorem gives $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}T=0$ and $\int _ X\omega \wedge T=1$, and computing the determinant density gives $D_{f^{1/n}}(T)=1$. The arithmetic–geometric mean inequality, which remains valid for semipositive $A$, bounds $D_{f^{1/n}}(Q)$ by $\int _ XA\wedge Q=1$ for every smooth strictly positive $Q\in \mathscr G_\omega $. Thus $T$ is a maximizer, strictly positive off the proper analytic set $Z$, and theorem 26.9.10 gives $\Phi _ f=u$. Its root $\gamma $ has rank $n-1\geq 2$ on $Z$ and is positive elsewhere, as required. $\square$

The invariant-mass and sublinear conditions settle the envelope condition only in special situations. We do not know whether general moment or entropy bounds imply either of them, or the hypothesis on the smooth root.

Corollary 26.9.18. Under the hypotheses of theorem 26.9.10, suppose in addition that $f\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$ for some $p>1$. Then the normalized threshold solution $u_0$ is continuous, and the normalized greatest exponential solutions converge uniformly to $u_0$ as the exponent tends to zero.

Proof. By proposition 14.27.4, the constant $c$ for which $\mathrm{MA}_\omega (v)=cf\, \mathrm{d}V$ has a continuous solution is $b_\omega (f)$. The continuous normalized solution is therefore a threshold solution, and it equals $u_0$ by the uniqueness in theorem 26.9.10. For $L^ p$ data the greatest exponential solution coincides with the bounded solution, as explained after theorem 14.34.1. By proposition 14.19.1, applied with the trivial group, the normalized family is uniformly precompact, and every cluster limit solves the threshold equation. Uniqueness identifies every cluster limit with $u_0$, which proves uniform convergence of the whole family. $\square$

26.9.7 A full-mass point pole without a smooth maximizing test

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The envelope criteria above do not require $L^ p$ integrability of the density. In the following example on projective space, with a closed background, the threshold solution is unbounded, while its envelopes $P_{\delta \omega }(u)$ in smaller backgrounds are not identically $-\infty $. The dual supremum is approached by smooth tests but attained by no smooth semipositive test.

Proposition 26.9.19. Let $X=\mathbb P^ n$, $n\geq 2$, and let $\omega $ be the normalized Fubini–Study form, with $\int _ X\omega ^ n=1$ and $\mathrm{d}V=\omega ^ n$. For $0<\alpha <1$ put

\[ \psi =\log \frac{\sum _{i=1}^ n|z_ i|^2}{\sum _{i=0}^ n|z_ i|^2}, \qquad u=1-(1-\psi )^\alpha , \qquad o=[1:0:\cdots :0]. \]

The function $u$ belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$, satisfies $\sup _ Xu=0$, has $o$ as its only pole, and has extended mass one. For every $0<\delta <1$, the envelope $P_{\delta \omega }(u)$ is not identically $-\infty $. The measure $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (u)$ is $f\, \mathrm{d}V$ with $f\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$ and $f\notin L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$ for every $p>1$.

Moreover, $b_\omega (f)=1$ and $\rho _\omega (f^{1/n})=1$, so $u$ is a threshold solution for $f$. No smooth semipositive $T\in \mathscr G_\omega $ attains $D_{f^{1/n}}(T)=1$.

Proof. The numerator in the definition of $\psi $ vanishes only at $o$, and locally $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log \sum _{i=1}^ n|z_ i|^2\geq 0$. The function $\chi (t)=1-(1-t)^\alpha $ is increasing and convex on $t\leq 0$, with $0\leq \chi '\leq \alpha $. Applying the chain inequality to bounded truncations of $\psi $ and taking the decreasing limit, we obtain $u=\chi \circ \psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$, with

\[ A=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq (1-\alpha )\omega . \]

The function $u$ is smooth off $o$, and it attains its supremum zero on $\{ z_0=0\} $. Since $u=-(1-\psi )^\alpha +1$, proposition 26.9.14 shows that all the envelopes $P_{\delta \omega }(u)$ are not identically $-\infty $.

To compute the mass, use the chart $z_0=1$ and write $s=\sum _{i=1}^ n|z_ i|^2$. A local potential $F$ for $A$, as a function of $s$, and the function $q=sF'$ are

26.59
\begin{equation} \begin{aligned} F(s)& =\log (1+s)+1-\left(1-\log \frac{s}{1+s}\right)^\alpha ,\\ q(s)& =sF'(s)=\frac{s+\alpha (1-\log (s/(1+s)))^{\alpha -1}}{1+s}. \end{aligned} \end{equation}

The eigenvalues of $A$ are $q/s$ in the tangential directions and $q'$ in the radial direction. Polar integration gives the radial top-degree measure $nq^{n-1}q'\, \mathrm{d}s$, where the angular normalization follows from the Fubini–Study case $q=s/(1+s)$, whose radial density is $ns^{n-1}(1+s)^{-n-1}\, \mathrm{d}s$ and whose total mass is one. Consequently, for $0<s_1<s_2$,

\[ \int _{\{ s_1<|z|^2<s_2\} }A^ n=q(s_2)^ n-q(s_1)^ n. \]

Here $q(s)\to 0$ as $s\downarrow 0$ and $q(s)\to 1$ as $s\to \infty $. On each compact annulus the extended measure equals the smooth top power, and it puts no mass on $o$ or on the hyperplane at infinity, since both are pluripolar. Exhausting the affine chart by annuli therefore gives extended mass exactly one. (This computation does not use the vanishing of the Lelong numbers.)

Near $o$ write $L=1-\log s$. Differentiating (26.59) gives

\[ \begin{gathered} \frac qs\sim \alpha s^{-1}L^{\alpha -1},\qquad q'\sim \alpha (1-\alpha )s^{-1}L^{\alpha -2},\\ f(s)\asymp s^{-n}L^{-1-n(1-\alpha )}. \end{gathered} \]

The smooth volume form, written in terms of $s$, is comparable to $s^{n-1}\, \mathrm{d}s$, so the contribution of a neighborhood of $o$ to $\int _ Xf\, \mathrm{d}V$ is comparable to $\int ^\infty L^{-1-n(1-\alpha )}\, \mathrm{d}L$, which is finite. For every $p>1$, the corresponding integral of $f^ p$ contains an exponentially growing factor $\exp (n(p-1)L)$ and diverges. The measure is smooth off $o$ and puts no mass on $o$, so it is $f\, \mathrm{d}V$ on all of $X$. Since its total mass is one and $\omega $ is closed, theorem 26.9.13 gives $b_\omega (f)=1$, and hence $\rho _\omega (f^{1/n})=b_\omega (f)^{-1/n}=1$.

Suppose that a smooth semipositive $T\in \mathscr G_\omega $ attained this value. The positive current $A$ satisfies $\int _ XA\wedge T=1$ by Stokes' theorem for currents. On $X\setminus \{ o\} $, the matrix arithmetic–geometric mean inequality gives

\[ A\wedge T\geq f^{1/n}k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

The right side has total mass one by attainment, so the nonnegative difference of the two sides vanishes off $o$. Since $A$ is smooth and strictly positive there, the equality case gives $T=tA^{n-1}$ on $X\setminus \{ o\} $, also at points where $T=0$: applying the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality to the positive semidefinite coefficient matrix of $T$ conjugated by $A^{1/2}$ shows that all its eigenvalues agree, possibly at zero. The function $t=(T\wedge A)/A^ n$ is smooth and nonnegative on $X\setminus \{ o\} $.

Since $A$ is closed, the equation $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}T=0$ becomes $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}t\wedge A^{n-1}=0$, a locally strictly elliptic scalar equation on $X\setminus \{ o\} $. All eigenvalues of $A$ tend to infinity at $o$, and since $n\geq 2$, so do all eigenvalues of $A^{n-1}$, which are products of $n-1$ eigenvalues of $A$. Since the coefficient matrix of the smooth form $T$ is bounded, it follows that $t\to 0$ at $o$.

Set $t(o)=0$. The resulting continuous nonnegative function on compact $X$ is not identically zero, by the normalization of $T$, so it attains a positive maximum away from $o$. The strong maximum principle on the connected manifold $X\setminus \{ o\} $ forces it to be constant there, contrary to its zero limit at $o$. This excludes every smooth semipositive maximizing test; no assumption on the degeneracy set of $T$ was needed. $\square$

Corollary 26.9.20. For the density and potential of proposition 26.9.19,

\[ \int _ X f\log f\, \mathrm{d}V<+\infty \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad \alpha <1-\frac1n. \]

For every real $r>0$,

\[ \int _ X(-u)^ r f\, \mathrm{d}V<+\infty \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad \alpha r<n(1-\alpha ). \]

In particular, finite entropy of the density can coexist with an unbounded threshold solution of full mass and with the absence of a smooth semipositive maximizing test.

Proof. Only a neighborhood of $o$ can contribute a divergence. There the preceding estimates give $f\, \mathrm{d}V\asymp L^{-1-n(1-\alpha )}\, \mathrm{d}L$, $\log f\sim nL$ and $-u\asymp L^\alpha $. Hence the entropy integral converges exactly when $n(1-\alpha )>1$, and the weighted integral exactly when $\alpha r<n(1-\alpha )$. In the equality cases both integrals are comparable to $\int ^\infty L^{-1}\, \mathrm{d}L$ and diverge. The negative part of $f\log f$ is bounded, hence integrable for the probability volume, so the entropy criterion is unambiguous. $\square$

These are integrals of the single density $f$. They should be distinguished from uniform moment or entropy bounds for the determinant-weighted probabilities of a maximizing sequence, as in theorem 26.9.24; since the present example has no smooth semipositive maximizer, it lies outside the setting of theorem 26.9.10. The proposition concerns the explicit threshold solution $u$; identifying it with the greatest normalized threshold subsolution $\Phi _ f$ would require a separate uniqueness argument.

26.9.8 Quantitative convergence of the currents

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The bound for the constants $c_\lambda $ in proposition 26.9.11 also bounds the trace error linearly in $\lambda $. Away from the degeneracy set, the deficit in the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality converts this bound into a rate of convergence for all coefficients of the currents. We keep the notation $u_0$, $v_\lambda $, $c_\lambda $ of that proposition. The total variation of a current of order zero is measured with respect to a fixed smooth Hermitian metric; on a relatively compact chart it is equivalent to the sum of the total variations of its coefficients.

Proposition 26.9.21. Under the hypotheses and notation of proposition 26.9.11, put

\[ A_\lambda =\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\lambda ,\qquad A_0=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_0. \]

There is a constant $C$ such that

\[ \| A_\lambda \wedge T-A_0\wedge T\| _{\mathrm{TV}} \leq C\lambda \qquad (\lambda >0). \]

For each compact set $K\Subset X\setminus Z$, there is a constant $C_ K$ such that

\[ \| A_\lambda -A_0\| _{\mathrm{TV}(K)} \leq C_ K\sqrt\lambda \qquad (0<\lambda \leq 1). \]

Proof. Put $p=f^{1/n}$, use the probability measure $\nu =A_0\wedge T=b^{1/n}p k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V$ from the proof of proposition 26.9.11, where the equality is (26.57), and set

\[ q_\lambda =(c_\lambda /b)^{1/n}\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\lambda /n}, \qquad R_\lambda =A_\lambda \wedge T-q_\lambda \nu \geq 0. \]

Here $R_\lambda \geq 0$ by the trace inequality used in that proof, which also gives $c_\lambda \geq b$ and $\int _ X(-v_\lambda )\, \mathrm{d}\nu \leq C_0$ for a constant $C_0$. Write $s_+=\max (s,0)$ for the positive part of a real number $s$. Since $q_\lambda \geq \mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\lambda /n}$ and $1-\mathrm{e}^{x}\leq -x$, we obtain

\[ (1-q_\lambda )_+\leq 1-\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\lambda /n}, \qquad \int _ X(1-q_\lambda )_+\, \mathrm{d}\nu \leq C_0\lambda /n. \]

Since $A_\lambda \wedge T$ and $\nu $ both have mass one,

\[ \| A_\lambda \wedge T-\nu \| _{\mathrm{TV}} \leq R_\lambda (X)+\int _ X|q_\lambda -1|\, \mathrm{d}\nu =2\int _ X(1-q_\lambda )_+\, \mathrm{d}\nu . \]

This proves the first estimate, with $C=2C_0/n$, and also gives $R_\lambda (X)\leq C_0\lambda /n$ and $\int _ X|q_\lambda -1|\, \mathrm{d}\nu \leq 2C_0\lambda /n$.

On $U=X\setminus Z$, write $T=\gamma ^{n-1}$ and $A_0=a\gamma $ as in the proof of theorem 26.9.10, and put $\alpha _\lambda =q_\lambda a$. Decompose the positive current $A_\lambda $ into its absolutely continuous part, with matrix density $B_\lambda $ relative to $\gamma $, and its positive singular part. Since $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (v_\lambda )=c_\lambda \mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\lambda }f\, \mathrm{d}V$, the trace inequalities for all Hermitian forms imply, as in the proof of corollary 26.9.6,

\[ (\det B_\lambda )^{1/n}\geq \alpha _\lambda \quad \text{almost everywhere on }U. \]

This is a statement about determinants of matrices; no nonlinear product of coefficient measures is involved. Put $s_\lambda =\operatorname {tr}B_\lambda /n$ and $\delta _\lambda =s_\lambda -\alpha _\lambda \geq 0$. On $U$, the absolutely continuous part of the trace error $R_\lambda $ is $\delta _\lambda \gamma ^ n$, and its singular part is the trace measure of the singular part of $A_\lambda $. Thus

\[ \int _ U s_\lambda \gamma ^ n\leq 1,\qquad \int _ U\delta _\lambda \gamma ^ n\leq C_0\lambda /n, \]

and the singular trace mass on $U$ has the same upper bound.

For nonnegative numbers $\ell _1,\ldots ,\ell _ n$ with geometric mean at least $\alpha \geq 0$ and arithmetic mean $s$, the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality give

\[ \sum _ i(\sqrt{\ell _ i}-\sqrt\alpha )^2\leq n(s-\alpha ), \qquad \sum _ i|\ell _ i-\alpha |\leq 2n\sqrt{s(s-\alpha )}. \]

Indeed $\sum _ i\sqrt{\ell _ i}\geq n\sqrt\alpha $, while $\sum _ i(\sqrt{\ell _ i}+\sqrt\alpha )^2\leq 4ns$. Applying this to the eigenvalues of $B_\lambda $ relative to $\gamma $ and integrating, with the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality once more, we see that the absolutely continuous part of $A_\lambda -\alpha _\lambda \gamma $ has integrated trace norm at most $2n\sqrt{C_0\lambda /n}$ on $U$. Its singular part has norm at most $C_0\lambda $ in the same metric. Finally

\[ \int _ U|\alpha _\lambda -a|\gamma ^ n =\int _ U|q_\lambda -1|\, \mathrm{d}\nu \leq 2C_0\lambda /n. \]

Since $\gamma $ is comparable to the fixed metric on the compact set $K\subset U$, these estimates and $\lambda \leq \sqrt\lambda $ for $0<\lambda \leq 1$ give the second conclusion. $\square$

The coefficient estimate is local on $X\setminus Z$. Convergence in capacity across $Z$ requires the additional envelope condition of corollary 26.9.12.

26.9.9 The classes of the divisorial components

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Uniqueness constrains the divisors along which the threshold solution can have positive Lelong numbers. A linear relation between their Bott–Chern classes would produce a global potential that changes the coefficients of these divisors while preserving the equation off the degeneracy set, contradicting uniqueness. We write $\{ D\} \in H^{1,1}_{\mathrm{BC}}(X,\mathbb R)$ for the Bott–Chern class of the current of integration $[D]$ along a prime divisor $D$, and $\nu (u,D)$ for the generic Lelong number of $u$ along $D$.

Corollary 26.9.22. Under the hypotheses of theorem 26.9.10, let $u$ be a threshold solution and let $D_1,\ldots ,D_ k$ be the prime divisors with $\nu (u,D_ j)>0$. Then the classes $\{ D_ j\} $ are linearly independent in $H^{1,1}_{\mathrm{BC}}(X,\mathbb R)$. In particular their number is at most $\dim _{\mathbb R}H^{1,1}_{\mathrm{BC}}(X,\mathbb R)$.

Proof. Since $u$ is smooth off $Z$, these divisors are among the finitely many components of $Z$ of codimension one. Put $a_ j=\nu (u,D_ j)>0$. By lemma 1.17.1,

\[ R=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u-\sum _ j a_ j[D_ j]\geq 0. \]

Suppose that real numbers $c_1,\ldots ,c_ k$ satisfy $\sum _ j c_ j\{ D_ j\} =0$. Fix a Gauduchon metric $\eta $ and apply lemma 23.7.1 to the currents of integration $[D_ j]$, with the integrable potentials $v_\eta ([D_ j])$ given there. Since the smooth representative in that lemma depends linearly on the Bott–Chern class, we obtain

\[ h=\sum _ j c_ j v_\eta ([D_ j])\in L^1(X),\qquad \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h=\sum _ j c_ j[D_ j]. \]

Choose $t\ne 0$ sufficiently small that $a_ j+tc_ j\geq 0$ for every $j$. The distribution $u_ t=u+th$ satisfies

\[ \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ t=R+\sum _ j(a_ j+tc_ j)[D_ j]\geq 0, \]

so it is represented by a function $u_ t\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$. On $X\setminus Z$, the function $h$ is smooth and pluriharmonic, so $u_ t$ has the same Monge–Ampère measure as $u$ there. Since neither extended measure charges the pluripolar set $Z$, $u_ t$ is another threshold solution. Uniqueness makes $th=u_ t-u$ constant. Hence $\sum _ jc_ j[D_ j]=0$, and testing near a generic point of each of the distinct divisors $D_ j$ gives $c_ j=0$. $\square$

The conclusion concerns only the divisors along which the solution has positive generic Lelong number. It imposes no independence condition on the other components of codimension one of the degeneracy set.

26.9.10 A branched projective example

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The squaring map of projective space gives a smooth threshold solution whose maximizing form degenerates along the coordinate hyperplanes. The density is smooth, but its $n$th root is not differentiable along these hyperplanes.

For this example take $X=\mathbb P^ n$, $n\geq 2$, with homogeneous coordinates $[Z_0:\cdots :Z_ n]$, and let $\omega $ be the Fubini–Study form normalized by $\int _ X\omega ^ n=1$. Set $\mathrm{d}V=\omega ^ n$.

Example 26.9.23. For every $n\geq 2$, there is a smooth nonnegative density $f$ on $\mathbb P^ n$, vanishing precisely on the coordinate hyperplanes, such that $b_\omega (f)=1$ and $D_{f^{1/n}}$ has a smooth semipositive maximizer strictly positive off those hyperplanes. The threshold solution is smooth, but $f^{1/n}$ is not differentiable at a general point of any coordinate hyperplane. There is no smooth strictly positive maximizing form. The normalized greatest exponential solutions converge uniformly to the normalized threshold solution.

Proof. Define the holomorphic map $F:\mathbb P^ n\to \mathbb P^ n$ and the semipositive form $A$ by

\[ F[Z_0:\cdots :Z_ n]=[Z_0^2:\cdots :Z_ n^2],\qquad A=\tfrac 12F^*\omega . \]

The homogeneous formula

\[ u=\tfrac 12\log \! \left(\sum _{i=0}^ n|Z_ i|^4\right) -\log \! \left(\sum _{i=0}^ n|Z_ i|^2\right) \]

defines a smooth function on projective space, and $A=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$. The form $A$ is semipositive, and it is strictly positive exactly where every homogeneous coordinate is nonzero. Define

\[ f=A^ n/\omega ^ n,\qquad T=A^{n-1}. \]

Since $\omega $ and $A$ are closed and cohomologous, Stokes' theorem gives $\int _ X f\omega ^ n=\int _ X\omega \wedge T=1$, and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}T=0$; thus $T\in \mathscr G_\omega $. Every $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ has extended mass at most one: each bounded truncation $\max (v,-k)$ has mass one, and restricting its Monge–Ampère measure to $\{ v>-k\} $ can only decrease that mass. Consequently an inequality $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (v)\geq c f\omega ^ n$ forces $c\leq 1$. The smooth solution $u$ gives the reverse bound, so $b_\omega (f)=1$.

The determinant formula for smooth semipositive forms gives

\[ k_ T=f^{(n-1)/n},\qquad D_{f^{1/n}}(T)=\int _{\mathbb P^ n}f\omega ^ n=1. \]

By (26.54) and $\rho _\omega (f^{1/n})=b_\omega (f)^{-1/n}=1$, the form $T$ is a maximizer. It is strictly positive away from the coordinate hyperplanes because $A$ is; on these hyperplanes $A$ has deficient rank, and hence so does the coefficient matrix of $T=A^{n-1}$.

For the density root, use the chart $Z_0=1$ with coordinates $z_1,\ldots ,z_ n$. Pulling back the Fubini–Study volume by $(z_1,\ldots ,z_ n)\mapsto (z_1^2,\ldots ,z_ n^2)$ and including the factor $2^{-n}$ gives

\[ f(z)=2^ n\left(\prod _{i=1}^ n|z_ i|^2\right) \frac{(1+\sum _ i|z_ i|^2)^{n+1}}{(1+\sum _ i|z_ i|^4)^{n+1}}. \]

Near a point of $\{ z_1=0\} $ at which all the other coordinates are nonzero, $f^{1/n}$ is $|z_1|^{2/n}$ times a smooth strictly positive function. Restricting to a real line transverse to that hyperplane proves nondifferentiability, also in the case $n=2$, where $|z_1|^{2/n}=|z_1|$. By symmetry of the coordinates, the same holds on every coordinate hyperplane. The absence of a smooth strictly positive maximizer follows from corollary 26.9.8, since $f$ has zeros. Finally, $f$ is bounded, so corollary 26.9.18 gives the uniform convergence of the normalized exponential solutions. $\square$

26.9.11 A moment condition on maximizing tests

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

For a general Hermitian background, a uniform bound on the moments of normalized potentials with respect to the probabilities of one smooth maximizing sequence can replace the regularity of the maximizing current. Together with the envelope condition in a smaller background, it shows that the cluster limits of the solutions of the exponential equations are exact threshold solutions.

For a nonzero $0\leq f\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$ with $b_\omega (f)>0$, let $\mathcal S_ f$ be the class of threshold subsolutions with supremum zero and $\Phi _ f$ its greatest member, from proposition 14.27.3. As in proposition 26.9.11, $u_\lambda $ denotes the greatest solution of the exponential equation $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (u)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda u}f\, \mathrm{d}V$, and $v_\lambda =u_\lambda -\sup _ Xu_\lambda $, $c_\lambda =\mathrm{e}^{\lambda \sup _ Xu_\lambda }$.

Theorem 26.9.24. Let $0\leq f\in L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$ be nonzero with $b=b_\omega (f)>0$, and put $p=f^{1/n}$ and $R=\rho _\omega (p)=b^{-1/n}$. Suppose that there are smooth strictly positive $T_ j\in \mathscr G_\omega $, with associated probability measures $\nu _ j$, such that

\[ d_ j=D_ p(T_ j)\longrightarrow R,\qquad \mathrm{d}\nu _ j=d_ j^{-1}p k_{T_ j}\, \mathrm{d}V, \]

and a constant $C$ satisfying

26.62
\begin{equation} \int _ X(-v)\, \mathrm{d}\nu _ j\leq C \quad \text{for all }j\text{ and all }v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega ) \text{ with }\sup _ Xv=0. \end{equation}

Then, for every $\lambda >0$,

\[ b\leq c_\lambda \leq b\mathrm{e}^{C\lambda },\qquad \Phi _ f-C\leq v_\lambda \leq 0. \]

Suppose in addition that $P_{\delta \omega }(\Phi _ f)$ is not identically $-\infty $ for some $0<\delta <1$. Whenever $\lambda _ k\downarrow 0$ and $v_{\lambda _ k}\to v$ in $L^1(X)$, the convergence holds in capacity and

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (v)=bf\, \mathrm{d}V, \qquad \Phi _ f-C\leq v\leq \Phi _ f. \]

In particular an exact threshold solution exists.

Proof. Since $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (v_\lambda )=c_\lambda \mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\lambda }f\, \mathrm{d}V$, the trace inequality of lemma 6.4.2 and Stokes' theorem give, for each $j$ and $\lambda >0$,

\[ 1\geq c_\lambda ^{1/n}d_ j \int _ X\mathrm{e}^{\lambda v_\lambda /n}\, \mathrm{d}\nu _ j \geq c_\lambda ^{1/n}d_ j\mathrm{e}^{-C\lambda /n}. \]

The second inequality follows from Jensen's inequality and (26.62). Letting $j\to \infty $ and using $R=b^{-1/n}$, we obtain the upper bound for $c_\lambda $. The lower bound and the comparison with every normalized threshold subsolution were proved in corollary 14.34.2; applying that comparison to $\Phi _ f$, we obtain

\[ v_\lambda \geq \Phi _ f-\lambda ^{-1}\log (c_\lambda /b) \geq \Phi _ f-C. \]

Now let $v_{\lambda _ k}\to v$ in $L^1$. By proposition 14.27.3, the limit $v$ lies in $\mathcal S_ f$, hence below $\Phi _ f$. The lower bound passes to the limit, so $\Phi _ f-C\leq v\leq \Phi _ f$; in particular $v\sim \Phi _ f$ in the sense of definition 1.12.1. For all $k$ with $\lambda _ k\leq 1$,

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (v_{\lambda _ k}) =c_{\lambda _ k}\mathrm{e}^{\lambda _ k v_{\lambda _ k}}f\, \mathrm{d}V \leq b\mathrm{e}^{C}f\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

The functions $|\mathrm{e}^{v_{\lambda _ k}}-\mathrm{e}^ v|$ are uniformly bounded and tend to zero in $\mathrm{d}V$-measure, so their integrals against the fixed $L^1$ density $f$ tend to zero. Hence the error term in (14.10) tends to zero, as required by proposition 14.8.2 with $\varphi =\Phi _ f$. The additional hypothesis $v\preceq \Phi _ f$ of that proposition holds since $v\leq \Phi _ f$, and the condition on $P_{\delta \omega }(\Phi _ f)$ is assumed. That proposition therefore gives convergence in capacity.

The continuity of damped measures under convergence in capacity, theorem 7.3.1, now identifies the equation without a mass-conservation argument. Write $g_ k=c_{\lambda _ k}\mathrm{e}^{\lambda _ k v_{\lambda _ k}}f$. We know $g_ k\to bf$ in $L^1$ and $0\leq g_ k\leq b\mathrm{e}^ Cf$ for large $k$. Hence

\[ \mathrm{e}^{v_{\lambda _ k}}\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (v_{\lambda _ k}) =\mathrm{e}^{v_{\lambda _ k}}g_ k\, \mathrm{d}V \longrightarrow \mathrm{e}^ v bf\, \mathrm{d}V \]

in total variation, while theorem 7.3.1 gives the weak limit $\mathrm{e}^ v\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\omega (v)$. Dividing by $\mathrm{e}^ v$ off the pole set of $v$, which neither measure charges, gives the equation and the finiteness of the mass. Finally, compactness of normalized $\omega $-plurisubharmonic functions provides an $L^1$ cluster limit along any sequence $\lambda _ k\downarrow 0$, which proves existence. $\square$

The moment condition holds if the densities of the $\nu _ j$ are uniformly bounded in $L^ q(\mathrm{d}V)$ for some $q>1$, by Hölder's inequality and the uniform bounds for the moments of normalized $\omega $-plurisubharmonic functions. More generally, a uniform entropy bound suffices. Write $a_ j=\mathrm{d}\nu _ j/\mathrm{d}V$ and suppose $\int _ Xa_ j\log a_ j\, \mathrm{d}V\leq H$ for all $j$. (By proposition 15.6.1, a uniform entropy bound is equivalent to a uniform bound for the dual entropy $H_0(\nu _ j)$ with zero reference defined there.) Choose $\alpha >0$ and $A$ from lemma 1.5.1, so that $\int _ X\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha v}\, \mathrm{d}V\leq A$ for every $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ with $\sup _ Xv=0$. The elementary entropy inequality, applied first to the truncations $v_ T=\max (v,-T)$ at levels $T>0$, gives

\[ \alpha \int _ X(-v_ T)a_ j\, \mathrm{d}V \leq \int _ Xa_ j\log a_ j\, \mathrm{d}V +\log \int _ X\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha v_ T}\, \mathrm{d}V \leq H+\log A. \]

To prove this inequality, apply $t\log t-t+1\geq 0$ to $t=a_ j/q$, integrate against $q\, \mathrm{d}V$, where $q=\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha v_ T}/\int _ X\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha v_ T}\, \mathrm{d}V$ is a probability density, and rearrange. Monotone convergence as $T\to \infty $ proves (26.62) with $C=(H+\log A)/\alpha $.

The same entropy bound also gives attainment of the dual supremum along this sequence. Indeed, $t\log t\geq -\mathrm{e}^{-1}$ and, for $K>1$,

\[ \int _{\{ a_ j>K\} }a_ j\, \mathrm{d}V \leq \frac{H+\mathrm{e}^{-1}}{\log K}. \]

This proves uniform integrability of the $a_ j$, and since $d_ j\to R>0$, also of the densities $p k_{T_ j}=d_ ja_ j$. By proposition 26.9.3, every weak cluster limit $T$ of $(T_ j)$ maximizes $D_ p$, and along a subsequence converging to $T$ the probabilities $\nu _ j$ converge to $R^{-1}p k_ T\, \mathrm{d}V$. For each normalized $v$, the function $-v$ is nonnegative and lower semicontinuous, so the lower semicontinuity of its integral under weak convergence of measures passes the moment bound to this limiting probability. These conclusions give no regularity of $T$.

The envelope condition holds if $\Phi _ f$ is bounded. In general, it and the moment bound are additional hypotheses; the tail condition on $f^{1/n}$ alone does not provide them in this argument. The theorem identifies the equation satisfied by each cluster limit. Uniqueness, which gave convergence of the whole family in proposition 26.9.11, is not available here.

26.10 Nef limits and nonattainment

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We return to arbitrary dimension $n\geq 1$. If $\theta $ is nef and $h$ is a Hermitian form, then the backgrounds $\theta +\epsilon h$ lie in $\mathscr P$ for every $\epsilon >0$, directly from the definition of nefness; their volumes are therefore scalars of smooth equations. As $\epsilon $ decreases to zero, the infimum over Gauduchon metrics commutes with the decreasing limit of the background. Thus the boundary value is determined by smooth equations, even when no metric attains it.

Theorem 26.10.1. For every nef background $\theta $ and every Hermitian form $h$,

\[ \lambda _\mu (\theta ) =\lim _{\epsilon \downarrow 0}\lambda _\mu (\theta +\epsilon h), \qquad \mathcal V_\mu (\theta ) =\lim _{\epsilon \downarrow 0}c_{\theta +\epsilon h}(\mu ). \]

Both quantities on the right decrease as $\epsilon \downarrow 0$, and their limits do not depend on $h$.

Proof. For $0<\epsilon '<\epsilon $ the difference of the two backgrounds is the positive form $(\epsilon -\epsilon ')h$, so the monotonicity in proposition 26.1.2 shows that $\lambda _\mu (\theta +\epsilon h)$ decreases as $\epsilon \downarrow 0$. Its limit is therefore the infimum over $\epsilon >0$. Infima over $\epsilon >0$ and over Gauduchon metrics commute, and for a fixed metric the quotient is affine in $\epsilon $. Hence

\[ \begin{split} \inf _{\epsilon >0}\lambda _\mu (\theta +\epsilon h) & =\inf _\gamma \inf _{\epsilon >0} \frac{\int _ X\theta \wedge \gamma ^{n-1} +\epsilon \int _ Xh\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}}{D_\mu (\gamma )}\\ & =\lambda _\mu (\theta ). \end{split} \]

Here the second equality holds because the coefficient of $\epsilon $ is positive, so the inner infimum is the value at $\epsilon =0$. This proves the first identity, and its right side is the definition of $\lambda _\mu (\theta )$, which does not involve $h$. The second identity follows by raising the first to the $n$th power, since $\lambda _\mu (\theta +\epsilon h)^ n=c_{\theta +\epsilon h}(\mu )$ by theorem 26.1.3. $\square$

If $X$ is Kähler and $\theta $ is closed and nef, choose $h$ Kähler. Each perturbed solution form is then closed and cohomologous to $\theta +\epsilon h$, so integrating its equation and applying Stokes' theorem gives

\[ c_{\theta +\epsilon h}(\mu )=\int _ X(\theta +\epsilon h)^ n. \]

Letting $\epsilon \downarrow 0$, we obtain $\mathcal V_\mu (\theta )=\int _ X\theta ^ n$ for every closed nef $\theta $ on a compact Kähler manifold and every $\mu $. We refer to this as the closed nef formula.

Proposition 26.10.2. Suppose $\theta $ is pseudoeffective and $\lambda _\mu (\theta )=0$. The infimum in definition 26.1.1 is attained if and only if $\theta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f$ for a smooth real function $f$.

Proof. Since $D_\mu (\gamma )>0$, a metric $\gamma $ attaining the value zero satisfies $\int _ X\theta \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}=0$. By lemma 4.3.3, including its dimension-one case, $\theta $ is then smoothly $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-exact. Conversely, if $\theta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f$, Stokes' theorem makes every quotient zero, so every admissible metric attains the infimum. $\square$

For example, let $X$ be the ruled surface and $D$ the section of lemma 25.5.1. The class $c_1(\mathcal O_ X(D))$ is nef, has zero square, and contains the nonzero positive current $[D]$. By the closed nef formula its volume is zero. A smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-exact representative would make $[D]$ itself $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-exact, which is impossible because $\int _ D\kappa >0$ for a Kähler form $\kappa $. By proposition 26.10.2, no Gauduchon metric minimizes the defining quotient.

26.11 Subtraction with a closed nef core

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Consider a path of backgrounds that starts in $\mathscr P$ and reaches its boundary at a nef point. If the path contains a closed nef class of positive top intersection, this closed core keeps the root volume positive up to and including the boundary, and adding a semipositive form, closed or not, preserves this lower bound. By proposition 26.2.2, the total decrease of the root volume along the path is the integral of an explicit ratio formed with the minimizing Gauduchon metrics; we call it the subtraction cost. Two test metrics chosen at the initial point bound this cost from above, with explicit dependence on the density and on the derivatives of the added form.

Throughout this section, $X$ is a compact Kähler manifold of dimension $n\geq 2$ and $\Omega $ is a fixed Kähler form on $X$. Let $\alpha $ be a smooth closed nef real $(1,1)$-form, let $B\geq 0$ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form, and let $t>0$. For real $s$, put

\[ a_ s=\alpha +(t-s)\Omega ,\qquad \theta _ s=a_ s+B,\qquad V_ s=\int _ Xa_ s^ n. \]

The class of $a_0$ is Kähler. Choose a Kähler representative $\kappa _0$ of $[a_0]$, and let $u_0$ be the smooth solution of the Calabi–Yau equation $\gamma _0=\kappa _0+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_0>0$, $\gamma _0^ n=V_0\mu $. Define the fixed quantities

26.65
\begin{equation} \begin{gathered} I_ B=\int _ XB\wedge \kappa _0^{n-1},\qquad U_\mu =\frac{V_0+I_ B}{D_\mu (\kappa _0)},\\ \Delta _\mu (B)=V_0^{-(n-1)/n}\int _ XB\wedge \gamma _0^{n-1}. \end{gathered} \end{equation}

Theorem 26.11.1. Suppose that $V:=V_ t>0$ and that there is a smooth compact complex curve $C\subset X$ with $\int _ C\alpha =\int _ CB=0$. Then $\theta _ s$ has a smooth positive representative for $s<t$, $\theta _ t$ is nef but has no smooth positive representative, and $\theta _ s$ is not nef for $s>t$. Moreover,

\[ \lambda _\mu (a_ s)=V_ s^{1/n}\quad (0\leq s\leq t), \qquad \lambda _\mu (\theta _ t)\geq V^{1/n}>0. \]

For every choice of minimizing Gauduchon metrics $g_ s$ for $\theta _ s$, $0\leq s<t$, the ratio $\int _ X\Omega \wedge g_ s^{n-1}/D_\mu (g_ s)$ is a continuous function of $s$, and the subtraction cost satisfies

26.66
\begin{equation} \begin{split} 0\leq \mathcal C & :=\int _0^ t\frac{\int _ X\Omega \wedge g_ s^{n-1}}{D_\mu (g_ s)}\, \mathrm{d}s =\lambda _\mu (\theta _0)-\lambda _\mu (\theta _ t)\\ & \leq \min \{ U_\mu ,V_0^{1/n}+\Delta _\mu (B)\} -V^{1/n}<\infty , \end{split} \end{equation}

where the integral is improper at $t$. If $B\leq M\kappa _0$ for a constant $M\geq 0$, then

\[ \Delta _\mu (B)\leq MV_0^{1/n},\qquad \mathcal C\leq (1+M)V_0^{1/n}-V^{1/n}. \]

Finally, there are a quasi-plurisubharmonic function $\rho $ and a constant $\varepsilon >0$ with $\alpha +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \varepsilon \Omega $ and $\theta _ t+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \varepsilon \Omega $.

Proof. We first locate the boundary of $\mathscr P$ along the path. Let $s<t$. Since $\alpha $ is nef, for every $\delta >0$ there is a smooth function $v_\delta $ with $\alpha +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\delta \geq -\delta \Omega $. Taking $\delta <t-s$ makes $a_ s+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\delta $ Kähler, and adding $B\geq 0$ gives a positive representative of $\theta _ s$. The same approximations show that $\theta _ t$ is nef. On the other hand, the hypotheses on $C$ and Stokes' theorem on the compact curve give

\[ \int _ C(\theta _ s+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)=(t-s)\int _ C\Omega \]

for every smooth $v$. At $s=t$ the right side vanishes, which excludes a positive representative of $\theta _ t$. For $s>t$, a representative with $\theta _ s+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\geq -\delta \Omega $ and $\delta <s-t$ would contradict this identity, so $\theta _ s$ is not nef.

Since every $a_ s$ with $s\leq t$ is closed and nef, the closed nef formula following theorem 26.10.1 gives $\lambda _\mu (a_ s)=V_ s^{1/n}$, including at $s=t$. The difference $\theta _ t-a_ t=B$ is semipositive, so monotonicity gives $\lambda _\mu (\theta _ t)\geq V^{1/n}$, while any fixed Gauduchon test bounds $\lambda _\mu (\theta _ t)$ from above. Since $\theta _ s=\theta _ t+(t-s)\Omega $, theorem 26.10.1 with the Hermitian form $\Omega $ identifies $\lambda _\mu (\theta _ t)$ with the limit of $\lambda _\mu (\theta _ s)$ as $s\uparrow t$.

Write $f(s)=\lambda _\mu (\theta _ s)$ for $s<t$. The path has constant velocity $-\Omega $, so proposition 26.2.2 shows that $f$ is smooth and

\[ H(s):=\frac{\int _ X\Omega \wedge g_ s^{n-1}}{D_\mu (g_ s)}=-f'(s). \]

In particular, the ratio $H$ is continuous and independent of the choice of minimizing metrics. For $r<t$, the fundamental theorem of calculus gives

\[ \int _0^ rH(s)\, \mathrm{d}s=f(0)-f(r). \]

Since $H\geq 0$, letting $r\uparrow t$ and using the limit just established yields the identity in (26.66).

It remains to bound $f(0)=\lambda _\mu (\theta _0)$ from above by two test metrics. The Kähler form $\kappa _0$ is Gauduchon, and since $\kappa _0$ is cohomologous to $a_0$, closed Stokes gives $\int _ X\theta _0\wedge \kappa _0^{n-1}=V_0+I_ B$; hence $\lambda _\mu (\theta _0)\leq U_\mu $. For the second test $\gamma _0$, the equation $\gamma _0^ n=V_0\mu $ and the cohomology class of $\gamma _0$ give

\[ D_\mu (\gamma _0)=V_0^{(n-1)/n},\qquad \int _ Xa_0\wedge \gamma _0^{n-1}=V_0, \]

so its quotient for $\theta _0$ is $V_0^{1/n}+\Delta _\mu (B)$. Combined with the lower bound $\lambda _\mu (\theta _ t)\geq V^{1/n}$, these two tests prove the upper bound in (26.66). If $B\leq M\kappa _0$, closed Stokes yields $\int _ XB\wedge \gamma _0^{n-1}\leq M\int _ X\kappa _0\wedge \gamma _0^{n-1}=MV_0$, which gives the remaining cost bounds.

Finally, $V=\int _ X\alpha ^ n>0$, so the closed nef class of $\alpha $ is big by the nef-and-big criterion on compact Kähler manifolds recalled in [SGPT]. Hence it contains a Kähler current $\alpha +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \varepsilon \Omega $. Adding $B\geq 0$ gives the last assertion. Unlike the first current, the second is not closed when $\mathrm dB\neq 0$, since its exterior derivative is $\mathrm{d}B$. $\square$

Such data arise from a bimeromorphic holomorphic map $p:X\to Y$ onto a compact Kähler manifold $Y$ that contracts a smooth curve $C$ to a point. Take $\alpha =p^*\omega _ Y$, with $\omega _ Y$ Kähler, and any smooth $B\geq 0$ vanishing on $TC$. Since $p$ has degree one, $\int _ X\alpha ^ n=\int _ Y\omega _ Y^ n>0$, while both curve integrals vanish.

The second initial test separates the closed intersection number $I_ B$ from a correction that involves only $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}B$.

Proposition 26.11.2. Set $S=\sum _{j=0}^{n-2}\gamma _0^ j\wedge \kappa _0^{n-2-j}$. Then

26.69
\begin{equation} \Delta _\mu (B)=V_0^{-(n-1)/n} \left(I_ B+\int _ Xu_0\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}B\wedge S\right). \end{equation}

If $-C_ B\kappa _0^2\leq \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}B\leq C_ B\kappa _0^2$ in the sense of weak positivity of $(2,2)$-forms, then

\[ \Delta _\mu (B)\leq V_0^{-(n-1)/n}I_ B +(n-1)C_ B\operatorname {osc}_ X(u_0)V_0^{1/n}. \]

Such a finite constant $C_ B$ exists for every fixed smooth $B$.

Proof. The factors of $S$ are closed, and $\gamma _0^{n-1}-\kappa _0^{n-1}=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_0\wedge S$. Integration by parts therefore gives (26.69). Since $\int _ X\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}B\wedge S=0$, the right side does not change when a constant is added to $u_0$, so we may assume $\sup _ Xu_0=0$. Then $-\operatorname {osc}_ X(u_0)\leq u_0\leq 0$, and the lower bound for $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}B$ together with the positivity of the summands of $S$ bounds the correction term above by

\[ C_ B\operatorname {osc}_ X(u_0)\int _ X\kappa _0^2\wedge S =(n-1)C_ B\operatorname {osc}_ X(u_0)V_0. \]

The equality holds because the cohomologous Kähler forms $\gamma _0,\kappa _0$ have all mixed top intersections equal to $V_0$. Finally, restrict $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}B$ to the complex two-planes of $TX$ and normalize by $\kappa _0$; compactness of the bundle of these planes bounds the restrictions and gives $C_ B$. $\square$

The potential $u_0$ solves a single complex Monge–Ampère equation, with density $V_0\mu /\kappa _0^ n$ relative to $\kappa _0^ n$. Its oscillation therefore carries the dependence on $\mu $, whereas the derivatives $\mathrm{d}\theta _ s=\mathrm{d}B$ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\theta _ s=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}B$ do not change along the path.

For the closed core alone the cost is explicit. Put $P_ s=\int _ X\Omega \wedge a_ s^{n-1}$. Since $V_ s'=-nP_ s$,

\[ -\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\lambda _\mu (a_ s) =\frac{P_ s}{V_ s^{(n-1)/n}},\qquad \int _0^ t\frac{P_ s}{V_ s^{(n-1)/n}}\, \mathrm{d}s=V_0^{1/n}-V^{1/n}. \]

Moreover $P_ s'=-(n-1)\int _ X\Omega ^2\wedge a_ s^{n-2}\leq 0$, since all classes on the segment are nef. Integrating $V_ s'=-nP_ s$ and using $P_ s\leq P_0$ gives

26.70
\begin{equation} V\geq V_0-nt\int _ X\Omega \wedge a_0^{n-1}. \end{equation}

This argument does not use $V>0$, so a positive right side guarantees the hypothesis $V>0$ of theorem 26.11.1. The following example shows that this sufficient condition is not necessary.

Example 26.11.3. On the blowup of $\mathbb P^2$ at a point there are data as above with $t=1$, $\mathrm{d}B\neq 0$,

\[ \lambda _\mu (\theta _1)\geq \frac32,\qquad 0\leq \mathcal C\leq \frac{11}{\sqrt8}-\frac32, \]

and an explicit current $\theta _1+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \frac12\Omega $ at the nef endpoint.

Proof. Realize the blowup as $X=\{ (x,[u:v])\in \mathbb P^2\times \mathbb P^1:x_1v=x_2u\} $, with projections $p,q$ and exceptional curve $E=p^{-1}([1:0:0])$. Let $L=p^*\omega _{\mathrm{FS},\mathbb P^2}$, $Q=q^*\omega _{\mathrm{FS},\mathbb P^1}$ and $\Omega =L+Q$, with the Fubini–Study forms normalized to have integral one on projective lines. The incidence embedding makes $\Omega $ Kähler. The degree-one blowdown, a fiber of $q$, and the isomorphism $q|_ E:E\to \mathbb P^1$ give

\[ \int _ XL^2=\int _ XL\wedge Q=1,\quad Q^2=0,\quad L|_{TE}=0,\quad \int _ EQ=1. \]

Take $t=1$, $\alpha =L$, $\kappa _0=2L+Q$ and $\mu =\kappa _0^2/8$. With $r=1-s$, the core volumes are $V_ s=1+4r+3r^2$; in particular $V_0=8$ and $V=V_1=1$. Choose a nonconstant smooth function $0\leq \chi \leq 1$ supported away from $E$, and put

\[ b=\frac13\int _ X\chi L\wedge \kappa _0,\qquad f=\chi -b,\qquad B=(1+f/2)L. \]

Since $\int _ XL\wedge \kappa _0=3$, we have $|f|\leq 1$ and $\int _ XfL\wedge \kappa _0=0$. Thus $B\geq L/2$ and $B|_{TE}=0$, so the curve $C=E$ satisfies the hypotheses of theorem 26.11.1. Where $\mathrm{d}\chi \ne 0$, the form $L$ is symplectic, and wedging with $L$ is injective on real one-forms in real dimension four. Hence $\mathrm{d}B=\tfrac 12\mathrm{d}f\wedge L\ne 0$.
