At the endpoint $\theta _1=(2+f/2)L\geq 3L/2$, and $\lambda _\mu (L)=1$ by the closed nef formula; this gives the lower bound for $\lambda _\mu (\theta _1)$. Since $\kappa _0^2=8\mu $, the initial solution is $\gamma _0=\kappa _0$ with $u_0=0$. Therefore $D_\mu (\kappa _0)=\sqrt8$, $I_ B=3$, $\Delta _\mu (B)=3/\sqrt8$ and $U_\mu =11/\sqrt8$, so $\lambda _\mu (\theta _0)\leq 11/\sqrt8$. The cost identity and the lower bound at the endpoint give the asserted estimate for $\mathcal C$.

The pullback of $\{ x_1=0\} $ is $E$ plus a fiber of $q$, so $\mathcal O(E)=p^*\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^2}(1)\otimes q^*\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^1}(-1)$. Give this bundle its product metric $h_ E$, with curvature $L-Q$, and let $s_ E$ be its canonical section. The Poincaré–Lelong formula gives, for $\rho =\tfrac 12\log |s_ E|_{h_ E}^2$,

\[ \theta _1+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho =B+\tfrac 12\Omega +\tfrac 12[E]\geq \tfrac 12\Omega . \]

Finally, $\int _ X\Omega \wedge a_0=5$, so the right side of (26.70) is $8-10=-2$, although $V_1=1$. $\square$

26.12 Subtraction on warped products

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The closed core in section 26.11 keeps the endpoint big. On a warped product, the subtraction cost can instead be computed all the way to an endpoint that is not big. Its rate is determined by two inverse moments of the fiber coefficient, while its total integral remains finite even when the endpoint volume vanishes.

Let $(B,\alpha )$ be a connected compact Kähler manifold of dimension $m\geq 2$, put $W=\int _ B\alpha ^ m$, and let $C$ be a connected compact complex curve. Fix smooth strictly positive probability forms $\sigma $ on $B$ and $w$ on $C$. On $X=B\times C$, of dimension $n=m+1$, use $\mu =\sigma \wedge w$ and omit pullbacks. For a smooth function $a\geq 0$ on $B$ and a real number $t>0$, set

\[ \theta _ s=\alpha +(a+t-s)w\quad (s<t),\qquad \theta _ t=\alpha +aw. \]

The endpoint is semipositive, hence nef. For $\epsilon >0$, write

\[ H(\epsilon )=\int _ B\frac{\sigma }{a+\epsilon },\qquad J(\epsilon )=\int _ B\frac{\sigma }{(a+\epsilon )^2},\qquad H(0)=\int _ B\frac{\sigma }{a}, \]

allowing $H(0)=+\infty $, with $1/a=+\infty $ at a zero of $a$.

Theorem 26.12.1. For $s<t$, the full Gauduchon infimum is attained by a balanced metric and

\[ \mathcal V_\mu (\theta _ s)=\frac{nW}{H(t-s)},\qquad \mathcal V_\mu (\theta _ t)=\frac{nW}{H(0)}, \]

where the last value is zero if $H(0)=+\infty $. Every minimizing Gauduchon metric $g_ s$ at $s$ has the same subtraction cost

26.71
\begin{equation} \frac{\int _ Xw\wedge g_ s^{n-1}}{D_\mu (g_ s)} =\mathcal C(s) :=n^{-(n-1)/n}W^{1/n} H(t-s)^{-(n+1)/n}J(t-s). \end{equation}

For every selection of these metrics, the improper integral satisfies

26.72
\begin{equation} \int _0^ t\mathcal C(s)\, \mathrm{d}s =\lambda _\mu (\theta _0)-\lambda _\mu (\theta _ t)<\infty . \end{equation}

Proof. Put $\epsilon =t-s$ and $L=a+\epsilon $. Apply theorem 9.1.1 on the closed positive base to obtain

\[ A_\epsilon =\alpha +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\phi _\epsilon >0,\qquad A_\epsilon ^ m=\frac{W}{H(\epsilon )}\frac{\sigma }{L}. \]

The target has total mass $W$, so closed Stokes fixes its scalar. The construction of theorem 26.5.10, now without a normalization of $W$ or a nonconstancy requirement on $a$, gives

\[ \Omega _\epsilon =A_\epsilon +Lw,\qquad \Omega _\epsilon ^ n=\frac{nW}{H(\epsilon )}\mu , \qquad \gamma _ s=L^{-1/m}\Omega _\epsilon . \]

Indeed,

\[ \gamma _ s^ m=L^{-1}A_\epsilon ^ m +mA_\epsilon ^{m-1}\wedge w \]

is closed: its first summand has top degree on the base, and its second has closed factors. Thus $\gamma _ s$ is balanced. Gauduchon Stokes and the matrix arithmetic–geometric mean inequality give

\[ \int _ X\theta _ s\wedge \xi ^ m \geq \left(\frac{nW}{H(\epsilon )}\right)^{1/n}D_\mu (\xi ) \]

for every Gauduchon metric $\xi $. The metric $\gamma _ s$ attains equality, with numerator $nW$ and denominator $(nW/H)^{m/n}H$. This proves the full volume formula.

For every fixed test, its quotient at $\theta _ t+\epsilon w$ decreases to its quotient at $\theta _ t$. The two infima, over $\epsilon >0$ and over the tests, commute, as in theorem 26.10.1. Hence the endpoint value is the limit of the strict values. Monotone convergence of $H(\epsilon )$ gives the asserted value, including when the integral is infinite.

The cost numerator for the displayed balanced metric is $WJ/H$. Dividing by its denominator gives (26.71). Also $\mathrm{d}H(t-s)/\mathrm{d}s=J(t-s)$, so differentiation of the volume formula gives $-\mathrm{d}\lambda _\mu (\theta _ s)/\mathrm{d}s=\mathcal C(s)$. By proposition 26.2.2 this is the cost of every minimizing metric, regardless of the regularity of the selection. On each interval $[0,r]$ with $r<t$, integrate this identity. Since $\mathcal C\geq 0$ and the root volume has the finite endpoint limit just proved, letting $r\uparrow t$ gives (26.72). $\square$

The identity applies to every smooth nonnegative coefficient, including coefficients with flat zeros. Finite-order zeros give more precise rates. Fix a Riemannian metric on $B$ for the following distance comparisons.

Proposition 26.12.2. Suppose that $Z=\{ a=0\} $ is a nonempty smooth compact real submanifold of positive codimension $d$, that $a\asymp \operatorname {dist}(\cdot ,Z)^ k$ in a fixed tube for a real number $k>0$, and that $a$ is bounded below by a positive constant outside that tube. Put $r=d/k$. Then, as $\epsilon \downarrow 0$,

26.73
\begin{equation} \mathcal C(t-\epsilon )\asymp \begin{cases} \epsilon ^{(1-r)/n-1},& r<1,\\ \epsilon ^{-1}(\log (1/\epsilon ))^{-(n+1)/n},& r=1,\\ \epsilon ^{r-2},& 1<r<2,\\ \log (1/\epsilon ),& r=2,\\ 1,& r>2. \end{cases} \end{equation}

Each rate is integrable, and the endpoint volume is positive exactly when $d>k$. For $r>2$, the cost tends to

\[ n^{-(n-1)/n}W^{1/n}H(0)^{-(n+1)/n}J(0)>0, \qquad J(0)=\int _ B\frac{\sigma }{a^2}. \]

For finitely many disjoint zero components satisfying these hypotheses with codimensions $d_ i$ and orders $k_ i$, the same assertions hold with $r=\min _ i d_ i/k_ i$.

Proof. The positive density and the tubular Jacobians are bounded above and below by positive constants. For $p=1,2$, radial integration therefore gives

\[ \int _ B\frac{\sigma }{(a+\epsilon )^ p} \asymp 1+\int _0^ R\frac{u^{d-1}}{(u^ k+\epsilon )^ p}\, \mathrm{d}u =1+\frac{\epsilon ^{r-p}}k \int _0^{R^ k/\epsilon }\frac{v^{r-1}}{(1+v)^ p}\, \mathrm{d}v, \]

with the equality applying to the radial expression. For $r<p$ the last integral tends to a finite positive constant; for $r=p$ it grows logarithmically; for $r>p$ the unregularized moment is finite, and monotone convergence gives its positive limit. Applying this to $H$ and $J$ in (26.71) proves the five rates and the criterion $H(0)<\infty $ exactly when $r>1$.

The power $(1-r)/n-1$ exceeds $-1$ for $r<1$, as does $r-2$ for $1<r<2$. At $r=1$, substitution of $\log (1/\epsilon )$ gives an integrable tail because $(n+1)/n>1$. The logarithmic and bounded rates are also integrable. When $r>2$, both inverse moments have finite positive limits, which gives the displayed limit. With finitely many disjoint components, the moments are finite sums of positive tube contributions and a bounded complement term; their smallest ratio determines the divergence, without cancellation. $\square$

Proposition 26.12.3. The endpoint $\theta _ t$ is big exactly when $a$ is positive everywhere. If $a$ has a zero, there is no decomposition

\[ \theta _ t+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f=\chi +\beta \]

with $f$ smooth and real, $\chi $ smooth and closed with nef class and $\int _ X\chi ^ n>0$, and $\beta \geq 0$ smooth.

Proof. Suppose that $\theta _ t+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq \delta (\alpha +w)$ for a quasi-plurisubharmonic $u$ and $\delta >0$. Pull back an arbitrary smooth nonnegative top-degree form $\zeta $ on $B$. It is $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-closed by dimension, so Stokes' theorem for distributions gives

\[ 0\leq \int _ X(\theta _ t+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u-\delta (\alpha +w))\wedge \zeta =\int _ B(a-\delta )\zeta . \]

A nonzero $\zeta $ supported where $a<\delta $ contradicts this inequality. Thus a zero excludes every strict current representative; no singular potential has been restricted to its zero fiber. Conversely, if $a>0$ everywhere, compactness makes $\theta _ t$ itself Hermitian, so it is big.

For a proposed decomposition, the closed nef-and-big criterion used in theorem 26.11.1 supplies a global quasi-plurisubharmonic $v$ with $\chi +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\geq \delta (\alpha +w)$ for some $\delta >0$. Then

\[ \theta _ t+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(f+v)=\chi +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v+\beta \geq \delta (\alpha +w), \]

contradicting the first part. This excludes any such closed core, including after a smooth change of gauge. $\square$

In the closed case $a\equiv a_0\geq 0$, put $P=\alpha +(a_0+t)w$ and $Q=tw$. These closed nef forms satisfy

26.79
\begin{equation} \int _ XP^ n-n\int _ XP^{n-1}\wedge Q =nWa_0=\mathcal V_\mu (\theta _ t). \end{equation}

This is the expression on the right of the closed Morse inequality discussed in section 34.20; the equality follows from $w^2=0$, $\alpha ^{m+1}=0$ and $\int _ Cw=1$. For nonconstant $a$, the exterior derivative $\mathrm{d}\theta _ s=\mathrm{d}a\wedge w$ is not identically zero, and the endpoint formula retains the specified probability $\sigma $ through $H(0)$. The comparison in (26.79) therefore concerns the constant closed case.

For example, take $B=\mathbb P^ m$, normalized Fubini–Study $\alpha $, $\sigma =\alpha ^ m$, and $a([Z])=\sum _{j=1}^ m|Z_ j|^2/\sum _{j=0}^ m|Z_ j|^2$. Its law under $\sigma $ is $m x^{m-1}\, \mathrm{d}x$ on $[0,1]$, by the radial integration used in theorem 26.13.2. Hence $W=1$, $H(0)=m/(m-1)$ and $\mathcal V_\mu (\theta _ t)=(m+1)(m-1)/m>0$, while the endpoint is not big and admits no closed nef core of positive top intersection. Its sole zero has real codimension $2m$ and order two. The cost is therefore logarithmic when $m=2$ and tends to a positive finite value when $m>2$, by proposition 26.12.2.

26.13 Positive volume without bigness

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

By corollary 26.1.4, every big background has positive volume. For a closed nef class on a compact Kähler manifold the converse also holds: the volume is then $\int _ X\theta ^ n$, and a closed nef class with positive top self-intersection is big. We now show that the converse fails for nonclosed nef backgrounds, even on a projective manifold: such a background can have positive volume without containing a Kähler current. In the example, the coefficient of a fiber direction vanishes at one point of the base. A radial potential on the base compensates for this vanishing, and the determinant of the resulting current bounds every Gauduchon quotient from below. Bigness, on the other hand, is excluded by pairing with a closed semipositive form concentrated near the vanishing point.

Throughout this section fix an integer $m\geq 2$, and put $n=m+1$, $t=(m-1)/m$ and $B=\mathbb P^ m$. Give $B$ and $\mathbb P^1$ their Fubini–Study forms $\omega $ and $w$, normalized by $\int _ B\omega ^ m=\int _{\mathbb P^1}w=1$. On $B$ define

\[ x([Z_0:\cdots :Z_ m])= \frac{\sum _{j=1}^ m|Z_ j|^2}{\sum _{j=0}^ m|Z_ j|^2}, \qquad a=x/t,\qquad p=[1:0:\cdots :0]. \]

Thus $x$ is a smooth function on $B$ with values in $[0,1]$; it vanishes only at $p$ and equals one on the hyperplane $\{ Z_0=0\} $. On $X=B\times \mathbb P^1$, with pullbacks understood, set

\[ h=\omega +w,\qquad \mu =\omega ^ m\wedge w, \qquad \theta =\omega +a w. \]

For $\epsilon >0$ put

26.80
\begin{equation} H_\epsilon =m\int _0^1\frac{y^{m-1}}{y/t+\epsilon }\, \mathrm{d}y. \end{equation}

Lemma 26.13.1. There are smooth real functions $\varphi _\epsilon $ on $B$ such that $A_\epsilon =\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _\epsilon >0$ and

26.81
\begin{equation} A_\epsilon ^ m=\frac{\omega ^ m}{H_\epsilon (a+\epsilon )}. \end{equation}

As $\epsilon \downarrow 0$ they converge uniformly to a bounded continuous function $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(B,\omega )$ that is smooth on $B\setminus \{ p\} $. On $B\setminus \{ p\} $ the current $A=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ is a smooth strictly positive form satisfying

26.82
\begin{equation} A^ m=a^{-1}\omega ^ m. \end{equation}

Moreover $H_\epsilon \to 1$ as $\epsilon \downarrow 0$.

Proof. In the affine chart $Z_0=1$, write $s=|z|^2$. Then $x=s/(1+s)$ and $\omega =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log (1+s)$. Polar integration gives

26.83
\begin{equation} \int _ B b(x)\omega ^ m=m\int _0^1 b(y)y^{m-1}\, \mathrm{d}y \end{equation}

for every integrable function $b$ on $[0,1]$, since the normalized radial density of $\omega ^ m$ in the variable $s$ is $m s^{m-1}(1+s)^{-m-1}\, \mathrm{d}s$. In particular $\int _ B a^{-1}\omega ^ m=mt/(m-1)=1$, and monotone convergence in (26.80) gives $H_\epsilon \to 1$.

Define $q_\epsilon \geq 0$ and $\varphi _\epsilon $ on $[0,1]$ by

\[ q_\epsilon (x)^ m=\frac m{H_\epsilon } \int _0^ x\frac{y^{m-1}}{y/t+\epsilon }\, \mathrm{d}y, \qquad \varphi _\epsilon (x)=\int _0^ x \frac{q_\epsilon (y)-y}{y(1-y)}\, \mathrm{d}y. \]

Near zero,

\[ q_\epsilon (x)=x\left(\frac m{H_\epsilon } \int _0^1\frac{u^{m-1}}{xu/t+\epsilon }\, \mathrm{d}u\right)^{1/m}, \]

so $q_\epsilon $ is $x$ times a smooth positive function; moreover $q_\epsilon (1)=1$. Hence both apparent singularities of $\varphi _\epsilon '$, at $0$ and at $1$, are removable, and $\varphi _\epsilon $ is smooth on $[0,1]$. Composing with the smooth function $x$ gives a smooth function on $B$, again denoted $\varphi _\epsilon $.

To compute $A_\epsilon $, recall that for a local potential of the form $F(s)$, with $q=sF'(s)$, the complex Hessian has the tangential eigenvalue $q/s$, of multiplicity $m-1$, and the radial eigenvalue $\mathrm{d}q/\mathrm{d}s$. For $F_\epsilon =\log (1+s)+\varphi _\epsilon (x)$ one finds $sF_\epsilon '=q_\epsilon (x)$. Differentiating the integral defining $q_\epsilon $ shows that both eigenvalues are positive, also at $s=0$ by the smooth factorization above. Their product relative to the Fubini–Study determinant is

\[ \frac{A_\epsilon ^ m}{\omega ^ m} =\frac{q_\epsilon ^{m-1}q_\epsilon '}{x^{m-1}} =\frac1{H_\epsilon (a+\epsilon )}. \]

At a point of the hyperplane $\{ Z_0=0\} $, the smooth form $A_\epsilon $ is semipositive, as a limit of the positive matrices in the affine chart. Since the determinant just computed extends there with a strictly positive value, every eigenvalue is positive. This proves strict positivity on all of $B$ and (26.81).

We now pass to the limit. For $x>0$, $q_\epsilon (x)$ tends to $x^ t$. To bound $q_\epsilon $ uniformly, note that $q_\epsilon ^ m$ is the distribution function of the probability density $\frac{m}{H_\epsilon }\frac{y^{m-1}}{y/t+\epsilon }$ on $[0,1]$. Relative to the probability density $my^{m-1}$, this density has a decreasing weight; relative to $(m-1)y^{m-2}$, its weight is proportional to $y/(y+t\epsilon )$ and hence increasing. If one probability density on $[0,1]$ has a decreasing weight relative to another, its average weight on $[0,x]$ is at least one and its distribution function is the larger of the two; an increasing weight reverses the inequality. Consequently

\[ x^ m\leq q_\epsilon (x)^ m\leq x^{m-1}, \qquad \text{that is,}\qquad x\leq q_\epsilon (x)\leq x^ t. \]

The function

\[ \varphi (x)=\int _0^ x\frac{y^ t-y}{y(1-y)}\, \mathrm{d}y \]

is bounded and continuous on $[0,1]$, since $t>0$, and smooth on $(0,1]$; its derivative extends smoothly at one. The integrable function $(y^ t-y)/[y(1-y)]$ dominates the derivatives of the functions $\varphi _\epsilon $, so dominated convergence gives uniform convergence $\varphi _\epsilon \to \varphi $ on $B$. Distributional convergence shows that $A$ is a closed positive current. On each coordinate chart, a smooth local potential of $\omega $ plus $\varphi $ is a uniform limit of the corresponding plurisubharmonic functions built with $\varphi _\epsilon $, including at $p$. Hence $\varphi $ is $\omega $-plurisubharmonic. Off $p$, the limit $q=x^ t$ gives

\[ \frac{A^ m}{\omega ^ m} =\frac{(x^ t)^{m-1}t x^{t-1}}{x^{m-1}}=\frac tx=\frac1a. \]

Smoothness of $\varphi $ off $p$ and the same determinant argument at the hyperplane $\{ Z_0=0\} $ prove (26.82). $\square$

Theorem 26.13.2. The background $\theta $ on $X$ is smooth, semipositive, nef and nonclosed. It is not big, but

26.84
\begin{equation} \lambda _\mu (\theta )=n^{1/n},\qquad \mathcal V_\mu (\theta )=n. \end{equation}

For $\theta _\epsilon =\omega +(a+\epsilon )w$, one has

\[ \mathcal V_\mu (\theta _\epsilon )=\frac n{H_\epsilon }, \]

and the smooth positive metrics

26.85
\begin{equation} \gamma _\epsilon =(a+\epsilon )^{-1/m} \bigl (A_\epsilon +(a+\epsilon )w\bigr ) \end{equation}

are balanced and minimize the defining quotient at $\theta _\epsilon $.

Proof. The coefficient $a$ is nonnegative, and $\theta +\eta h>0$ for every $\eta >0$; this proves semipositivity and nefness. Since $a$ is nonconstant, $\mathrm{d}\theta =\mathrm{d}a\wedge w\ne 0$.

Suppose that $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\geq \delta h$ for a quasi-plurisubharmonic function $v$ and some $\delta >0$. Choose a nonzero smooth function $f\geq 0$ on $B$ supported in $\{ a<\delta \} $. Its pullback $S=f\omega ^ m$ is a smooth semipositive $(m,m)$-form, closed because it has top degree on the base. Positivity and distributional Stokes would give

\[ 0\leq \int _ X(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v-\delta h)\wedge S =\int _ B f(a-\delta )\omega ^ m<0. \]

This contradiction shows that $\theta $ is not big, since every Hermitian form on $X$ dominates a positive multiple of $h$.

For the lower bound on the volume, pull back the bounded potential $\varphi $ of lemma 26.13.1. The positive current $\Omega =A+a w=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ is a smooth strictly positive form off $Z=\{ p\} \times \mathbb P^1$, where

\[ \Omega ^ n=n a A^ m\wedge w=n\mu . \]

Let $\gamma $ be a Gauduchon metric. Since $\varphi $ is bounded, distributional Stokes lets us replace $\theta $ by $\Omega $ in the numerator of its quotient. The set $Z$ has zero smooth volume and $\Omega \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}$ is a positive measure, so the pointwise arithmetic–geometric mean inequality off $Z$ gives

\[ \int _ X\theta \wedge \gamma ^{n-1} \geq n^{1/n}\int _ X (\mu /\gamma ^ n)^{1/n}\gamma ^ n =n^{1/n}D_\mu (\gamma ). \]

This lower bound holds for every test in definition 26.1.1; no power of the current $\Omega $ along $Z$ is needed.

For the upper bound, put $c_\epsilon =n/H_\epsilon $ and $\Omega _\epsilon =A_\epsilon +(a+\epsilon )w=\theta _\epsilon +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _\epsilon $. Then $\Omega _\epsilon ^ n=c_\epsilon \mu $. The metrics in (26.85) satisfy

\[ T_\epsilon =\gamma _\epsilon ^ m =(a+\epsilon )^{-1}A_\epsilon ^ m +m A_\epsilon ^{m-1}\wedge w. \]

The first term has top degree on the base and the second is a product of closed forms, so $T_\epsilon $ is closed; that is, $\gamma _\epsilon $ is balanced, and in particular Gauduchon. Smooth Stokes and (26.83) give

\[ \int _ X\theta _\epsilon \wedge T_\epsilon =c_\epsilon \int _ X(a+\epsilon )^{-1}\mu =n, \qquad D_\mu (\gamma _\epsilon )=c_\epsilon ^{m/n}H_\epsilon , \]

so the quotient of $\gamma _\epsilon $ at $\theta _\epsilon $ equals $c_\epsilon ^{1/n}$. The same arithmetic–geometric mean argument, applied to the smooth representative $\Omega _\epsilon $, bounds every Gauduchon quotient at $\theta _\epsilon $ below by $c_\epsilon ^{1/n}$. This proves that $\gamma _\epsilon $ minimizes, and gives the formula for $\mathcal V_\mu (\theta _\epsilon )$. Since $\theta \leq \theta _\epsilon $, the metrics $\gamma _\epsilon $, used as tests for $\theta $, also give $\lambda _\mu (\theta )\leq c_\epsilon ^{1/n}$. Letting $\epsilon \downarrow 0$ proves (26.84). By theorem 26.10.1, this value is also the limit of the volumes of the Hermitian perturbations $\theta +\epsilon h$. $\square$

The value $n$ refers to the probability measure $\mu $ fixed above, but positivity holds for every smooth positive probability measure by proposition 26.14.1. Thus positive volume does not characterize bigness for nonclosed nef backgrounds, even on a projective manifold. The construction needs $m\geq 2$, so that $t>0$ and the endpoint potential is bounded; it says nothing about surfaces, or about closed nef classes on general compact complex manifolds.

26.13.1 Volume tests and threshold tests

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Two quotients are formed from the same pairing $\int _ X\theta \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}$. The volume quotient divides it by the determinant integral $D_\mu (\gamma )$, whereas the linear threshold $\kappa _ h(\theta )$ of corollary 4.3.2 divides it by the trace $\int _ Xh\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}$. This threshold is positive exactly when $\theta $ is big, and we call it the bigness threshold; for the pseudoeffective but nonbig background $\theta $ of theorem 26.13.2 it vanishes. The same metrics can approach both infima, but need not do so. In the radial model, the volume-minimizing metrics approach the bigness threshold only in dimension three, while a second family of smooth balanced metrics approaches it in every dimension by concentrating on the fiber over $p$.

Keep the data of theorem 26.13.2, and put

\[ T_\epsilon =\gamma _\epsilon ^ m,\qquad I_\epsilon =\int _ B(a+\epsilon )^{-2}\omega ^ m, \qquad M_\epsilon =m+I_\epsilon /H_\epsilon , \qquad g_\epsilon =M_\epsilon ^{-1/m}\gamma _\epsilon . \]

Write $Z=\{ p\} \times \mathbb P^1$. Its integration current $[Z]$ satisfies $\int _ Zh=1$ and annihilates $\theta $, since $\theta |_ Z=a(p)w=0$.

Proposition 26.13.3. The metrics $g_\epsilon $ are balanced, satisfy $\int _ Xh\wedge g_\epsilon ^ m=1$, and

26.86
\begin{equation} \int _ X\theta \wedge g_\epsilon ^ m =\frac{n-\epsilon I_\epsilon /H_\epsilon }{M_\epsilon }, \qquad D_\mu (g_\epsilon ) =\frac{(n/H_\epsilon )^{m/n}H_\epsilon }{M_\epsilon }. \end{equation}

Their volume quotients tend to $n^{1/n}$ as $\epsilon \downarrow 0$. If $n=3$, then $g_\epsilon ^2$ converges weakly to $[Z]$, and

26.87
\begin{equation} \int _ X\theta \wedge g_\epsilon ^2 \sim \frac6{\log (1/\epsilon )}, \qquad D_\mu (g_\epsilon ) \sim \frac{2\, 3^{2/3}}{\log (1/\epsilon )}. \end{equation}

If $n\geq 4$, put $M_0=m+(m-1)^2/[m(m-2)]$. Then

\[ \int _ X\theta \wedge g_\epsilon ^ m\longrightarrow \frac n{M_0}>0, \qquad D_\mu (g_\epsilon )\longrightarrow \frac{n^{m/n}}{M_0}>0. \]

Thus this family approaches the bigness threshold, which is zero, exactly when $n=3$.

Proof. By the power formula in the proof of theorem 26.13.2 and (26.81),

\[ T_\epsilon =\frac{\omega ^ m}{H_\epsilon (a+\epsilon )^2} +mA_\epsilon ^{m-1}\wedge w. \]

Since $A_\epsilon $ is cohomologous to $\omega $, closed Stokes gives $\int _ B\omega \wedge A_\epsilon ^{m-1}=1$. Consequently the two terms have $h$-traces $I_\epsilon /H_\epsilon $ and $m$, which proves the trace normalization of $g_\epsilon ^ m=T_\epsilon /M_\epsilon $. Subtracting $\epsilon w$ from $\theta _\epsilon $ subtracts $\epsilon I_\epsilon /H_\epsilon $ from the pairing $\int _ X\theta _\epsilon \wedge T_\epsilon =n$. Rescaling $\gamma _\epsilon $ by $M_\epsilon ^{-1/m}$ divides both the linear pairing and $D_\mu $ by $M_\epsilon $, proving (26.86).

Polar integration gives

\[ I_\epsilon =m t^2\int _0^1 \frac{x^{m-1}}{(x+t\epsilon )^2}\, \mathrm{d}x. \]

When $m=2$, put $q=\epsilon /2$. Direct integration yields

\[ I_\epsilon =\tfrac 12\left(\log \frac{1+q}{q} +\frac q{1+q}-1\right), \qquad H_\epsilon =1-q\log \frac{1+q}{q}. \]

Hence $I_\epsilon \sim \tfrac 12\log (1/\epsilon )$ and $\epsilon I_\epsilon \to 0$. Since $H_\epsilon \to 1$, the asymptotics in (26.87) follow from (26.86).

For the weak limit when $n=3$, note that the mixed positive term $2A_\epsilon \wedge w$ has $h$-trace two; divided by $M_\epsilon \to \infty $, it tends weakly to zero. The horizontal probability measure

\[ I_\epsilon ^{-1}(a+\epsilon )^{-2}\omega ^2 \]

on $B$ concentrates at $p$: outside any fixed neighborhood of $p$ its unnormalized density stays bounded, while $I_\epsilon \to \infty $. For a smooth $(1,1)$-test form on $X$, integration along the fibers $\{ b\} \times \mathbb P^1$ gives a smooth function on $B$. The convergence of these probability measures to the Dirac mass at $p$ therefore shows that the horizontal term of $g_\epsilon ^2$ converges to $[Z]$, because its coefficient $I_\epsilon /(H_\epsilon M_\epsilon )$ tends to one.

For $m\geq 3$, monotone convergence instead gives

\[ I_\epsilon \longrightarrow m t^2\int _0^1x^{m-3}\, \mathrm{d}x =\frac{(m-1)^2}{m(m-2)}. \]

In both cases $\epsilon I_\epsilon \to 0$. Using $H_\epsilon \to 1$ in (26.86) proves the remaining limits, including that of the volume quotients. $\square$

For $n\geq 4$ the metrics $g_\epsilon $ still minimize the perturbed volumes, and their quotients converge to $\lambda _\mu (\theta )$, but their normalized pairings with $\theta $ stay bounded away from zero. This does not contradict nonbigness: the bigness threshold is approached by a different family.

Proposition 26.13.4. For every $n\geq 3$ there are smooth balanced metrics $b_\rho $, $0<\rho <1$, with

\[ \int _ Xh\wedge b_\rho ^ m=1,\qquad b_\rho ^ m\rightharpoonup [Z],\qquad \int _ X\theta \wedge b_\rho ^ m\longrightarrow 0, \qquad D_\mu (b_\rho )\longrightarrow 0 \]

as $\rho \downarrow 0$.

Proof. Choose a nonzero smooth function $f_\rho \geq 0$ on $B$, supported in $\{ x<\rho \} $ and normalized by $\int _ Bf_\rho \omega ^ m=1$. The form

26.88
\begin{equation} \Gamma _\rho =\frac{f_\rho \omega ^ m+\rho h^ m}{1+n\rho } \end{equation}

is smooth, strictly positive and closed: the first summand has top degree on the base, and $h$ is Kähler. The positive-root construction in the proof of theorem 4.3.1 gives a unique smooth metric $b_\rho $ with $b_\rho ^ m=\Gamma _\rho $, and $b_\rho $ is balanced because $\Gamma _\rho $ is closed. Since $\int _ Xh^{m+1}=n$, its $h$-trace is one.

Put $C_\theta =\int _ X\theta \wedge h^ m$. Since $a<\rho /t$ on the support of $f_\rho $,

\[ 0\leq \int _ X\theta \wedge \Gamma _\rho \leq \frac{\rho /t+\rho C_\theta }{1+n\rho }\longrightarrow 0. \]

The lower bound in the proof of theorem 26.13.2 gives $D_\mu (b_\rho )\leq n^{-1/n}\int _ X\theta \wedge \Gamma _\rho $, so the determinant integrals also tend to zero. The probability measures $f_\rho \omega ^ m$ converge to the Dirac mass at $p$ because their supports shrink to that point. Fiber integration, as in the preceding proof, gives the current limit $[Z]$, while the term $\rho h^ m$ tends to zero. $\square$

Thus the normalized current $[Z]$ is a weak limit of smooth balanced metrics normalized by their $h$-traces. It annihilates $\theta $, and its determinant-weighted measure vanishes by proposition 26.20.2. The two families give explicit instances of the separation between the volume and threshold denominators described in proposition 33.2.1, without any general smoothing result for Gauduchon currents.

26.14 Varying the probability measure

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

From now on $X$ is again an arbitrary connected compact complex manifold of dimension $n\geq 1$. Changing the smooth positive probability measure $\mu $ multiplies each denominator $D_\mu (\gamma )$ by a factor bounded above and below independently of $\gamma $. The value of the volume may therefore change, but its vanishing and positivity do not. Moreover, every smooth strictly positive potential solves the normalized equation for a suitable measure. Consequently, on $\mathscr P$ the volume is independent of the measure exactly when the total Monge–Ampère mass is independent of the potential.

Proposition 26.14.1. For smooth positive probability measures $\mu ,\nu $, choose constants $m,M>0$ such that $m\mu \leq \nu \leq M\mu $. Then for every pseudoeffective $\theta $,

\[ M^{-1/n}\lambda _\mu (\theta ) \leq \lambda _\nu (\theta ) \leq m^{-1/n}\lambda _\mu (\theta ). \]

In particular, whether $\lambda _\mu (\theta )$ vanishes does not depend on the smooth positive probability measure $\mu $.

Proof. Since $D_\nu (\gamma )=\int _ X(\nu /\mu )^{1/n}(\mu /\gamma ^ n)^{1/n}\gamma ^ n$, the inequalities $m\mu \leq \nu \leq M\mu $ give

\[ m^{1/n}D_\mu (\gamma )\leq D_\nu (\gamma )\leq M^{1/n}D_\mu (\gamma ) \]

for every admissible metric $\gamma $. The numerators of the quotients are nonnegative and do not involve the measure. Dividing and taking infima proves the assertion. $\square$

Proposition 26.14.2. Let $\omega $ be a Hermitian form on $X$. The value $\mathcal V_\mu (\omega )$ is the same for all smooth positive probability measures $\mu $ if and only if

\[ \int _ X(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n \]

is independent of the smooth potential $v$ with $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v>0$.

Proof. For a smooth potential $v$ with $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v>0$, put

\[ M_ v=\int _ X(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n, \qquad \mu _ v=\frac{(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n}{M_ v}. \]

Then $\mu _ v$ is a smooth positive probability measure, and $v$ solves the normalized equation for $\mu _ v$ with scalar $M_ v$. Uniqueness of the scalar and theorem 26.1.3 give $\mathcal V_{\mu _ v}(\omega )=M_ v$. Hence, if the volume does not depend on the measure, neither does the mass. Conversely, suppose that all these masses equal one constant $M$. For an arbitrary smooth positive probability measure $\mu $, integrating the normalized equation gives $c_\omega (\mu )=M$, so $\mathcal V_\mu (\omega )=M$ for every $\mu $. $\square$

For instance, the nonclosed torus metric of example 2.3.1 has volumes that depend on the measure. If $n=2$ and $\omega $ is Gauduchon, Stokes' theorem shows that the smooth masses are all equal to $\int _ X\omega ^2$, so $\mathcal V_\mu (\omega )=\int _ X\omega ^2$ for every $\mu $. On the Hopf threefold of chapter 8, each $\mathcal V_\mu (\omega )$ is finite and positive, although the upper mass $v_+(\omega )$ is infinite.

26.14.1 Upper and lower masses

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

As the probability measure varies, the smooth equation realizes the mass of every smooth strictly positive potential, by the proof of proposition 26.14.2. The extrema of the volume over all measures are therefore the extrema of these masses, and regularization of bounded potentials identifies them with the lower and upper masses $v_-(\omega )$ and $v_+(\omega )$ of definition 6.5.1. The infimum simplifies further: at each Gauduchon metric an explicit measure maximizes the denominator, and this removes the density from the formula entirely.

Throughout this subsection $\omega $ is a Hermitian form on $X$, and $\mathscr M$ denotes the set of smooth strictly positive probability measures on $X$.

Theorem 26.14.3. One has

\[ \inf _{\mu \in \mathscr M}\mathcal V_\mu (\omega )=v_-(\omega ), \qquad \sup _{\mu \in \mathscr M}\mathcal V_\mu (\omega )=v_+(\omega ), \]

where the supremum may be infinite. Moreover,

26.89
\begin{equation} v_-(\omega )^{1/n} =\inf _{\gamma \text{ Gauduchon}} \frac{\displaystyle \int _ X\omega \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}}{\displaystyle \left(\int _ X\gamma ^ n\right)^{(n-1)/n}}. \end{equation}

Proof. For each $\mu \in \mathscr M$, the smooth normalized equation realizes $\mathcal V_\mu (\omega )$ as the mass $\int _ X(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n$ of a smooth potential $v$ with $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v>0$. Conversely, every such potential realizes its mass as $\mathcal V_{\mu _ v}(\omega )$ for the measure $\mu _ v$ in the proof of proposition 26.14.2. Thus the set of volumes $\mathcal V_\mu (\omega )$, $\mu \in \mathscr M$, coincides with the set of these smooth masses.

It remains to show that the smooth masses have the same extrema as the masses of bounded potentials, which define $v_\pm (\omega )$. If $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )\cap L^\infty $, theorem 1.11.1 gives uniformly bounded smooth strictly $\omega $-plurisubharmonic functions $u_ j$ decreasing to $u$. Their Monge–Ampère measures converge by the monotone continuity of bounded products, and so do their total masses, since $X$ is compact. Every bounded mass is therefore a limit of smooth strictly positive masses. Conversely, smooth potentials are bounded. This proves the first two identities; no uniform bound on the masses is needed.

For the last formula, take $n$th roots and interchange the infima over $\mu $ and over $\gamma $ in theorem 26.1.3. For a fixed Gauduchon metric $\gamma $, Hölder's inequality and $\int _ X\mu =1$ give

\[ D_\mu (\gamma ) \leq \left(\int _ X\gamma ^ n\right)^{(n-1)/n}, \]

with equality for the smooth probability measure $\mu =\gamma ^ n/\int _ X\gamma ^ n$. The numerator is positive and does not depend on $\mu $, so the infimum of the quotient over $\mu $ is obtained by maximizing the denominator. This gives (26.89). For $n=1$ both denominators equal one, and the same argument applies. $\square$

Thus $v_-(\omega )>0$ if and only if $\int _ X\omega \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}$ has a positive lower bound over all Gauduchon metrics $\gamma $ with $\int _ X\gamma ^ n=1$. This lower bound fails on the threefold constructed in theorem 26.18.1. By corollary 14.26.4, positivity of $v_-(\omega )$ is also equivalent to solvability, in the background $\omega $, of the normalized equation for every nonzero finite non-pluripolar measure. In the same way, each individual volume $\mathcal V_\mu (\omega )$ is finite, while their supremum $v_+(\omega )$ can be infinite.

Proposition 26.14.4. The infimum in (26.89) is attained if and only if the infimum of the masses $\int _ X(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n$ over smooth functions $v$ with $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v>0$ is attained. For every such minimizing potential $v$, the metric $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ is Gauduchon.

Proof. Suppose that a Gauduchon metric $\gamma $ attains the infimum in (26.89), and put $m=v_-(\omega )^{1/n}$. The numerator of its quotient is strictly positive, so $m>0$. For the measure $\mu _\gamma =\gamma ^ n/\int _ X\gamma ^ n$, equality holds in the Hölder inequality above, so the test $\gamma $ gives $\lambda _{\mu _\gamma }(\omega )\leq m$. Theorem 26.14.3 gives the opposite inequality. Hence the solution of the smooth equation for $\mu _\gamma $ has mass $m^ n=v_-(\omega )$, which gives a minimizing smooth potential. For $n\geq 2$, $\gamma $ also minimizes the quotient for $\mu _\gamma $, so by theorem 26.1.3 the solution form is conformal to $\gamma $. Since both have volume forms proportional to $\mu _\gamma $, the conformal factor is constant, and the solution metric is Gauduchon.

Conversely, let $v$ be a smooth potential minimizing the mass. For every smooth real $f$, the potentials $v\pm tf$ are admissible for sufficiently small $t>0$. The vanishing of the first variation of the mass therefore gives

\[ \int _ X f\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\bigl ((\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^{n-1}\bigr )=0 \quad \hbox{for every smooth real }f. \]

Thus $\Omega =\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ is Gauduchon. Taking $\gamma =\Omega $ in the quotient and using Stokes' theorem for the Gauduchon metric $\Omega $ gives

\[ \frac{\int _ X\omega \wedge \Omega ^{n-1}}{(\int _ X\Omega ^ n)^{(n-1)/n}} =(\int _ X\Omega ^ n)^{1/n}=v_-(\omega )^{1/n}, \]

so the infimum in (26.89) is attained. In dimension one all metrics are Gauduchon and all masses equal $\int _ X\omega $. $\square$

The infimum over densities also makes sense beyond the positive cone. For a pseudoeffective smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\theta $ on $X$, put

\[ \lambda _*(\theta )= \inf _{\gamma \text{ Gauduchon}} \frac{\int _ X\theta \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}}{(\int _ X\gamma ^ n)^{(n-1)/n}}. \]

The Hölder argument above proves

\[ \lambda _*(\theta )=\inf _{\mu \in \mathscr M}\lambda _\mu (\theta ). \]

Like each $\lambda _\mu $, the function $\lambda _*$ is finite, nonnegative, concave, positively homogeneous, and invariant under smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ changes of $\theta $. Suppose $\theta $ is nef, fix a Hermitian form $h$, and for real $\epsilon >0$ choose a Hermitian representative $\omega _\epsilon $ of $\theta +\epsilon h$. Interchanging the infima over the test metric and over $\epsilon >0$, as in theorem 26.10.1, gives

\[ \lambda _*(\theta ) =\lim _{\epsilon \downarrow 0}\lambda _*(\theta +\epsilon h) =\lim _{\epsilon \downarrow 0}v_-(\omega _\epsilon )^{1/n}. \]

By gauge invariance, the last expression does not depend on the choice of representatives. This extends the lower-mass formula to the nef boundary. We do not compare $\lambda _*$ with other notions of lower volume for singular classes.

26.15 Curvature conditions for mass extrema

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now ask when the lower and upper masses are attained by smooth potentials. At a smooth local extremum of the mass, the first variation forces the metric $\Omega =\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ to be Gauduchon, and the second variation gives a sign for $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Omega ^{n-2})$. On a threefold these necessary conditions are also sufficient, because the mass is then a polynomial of degree two in the variation. Fix a Hermitian form $\omega $, and recall from chapter 2 the set $\mathcal H_\omega $ of smooth real functions $u$ with $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u>0$ and the mass functional $\mathrm M_\omega (u)=\int _ X(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n$.

For $n\geq 2$, theorem 2.2.2, proposition 26.14.2 give the differential conditions under which $\mathcal V_\mu (\omega )$ is independent of the smooth positive probability measure $\mu $. By proposition 15.6.2, the same conditions characterize when the difference $H_0-\operatorname {Ent}_{\mathrm{d}V}$ considered there is constant on probability measures of finite entropy, for a fixed smooth positive probability volume $\mathrm{d}V$; it suffices to test smooth positive probability measures. In dimension one the mass is always $\int _ X\omega $, so both conclusions hold automatically.

Recall that a smooth real $(n-1,n-1)$-form $T$ is nonnegative, written $T\geq 0$, if $\mathrm i\alpha \wedge \bar\alpha \wedge T\geq 0$ for every $(1,0)$-covector $\alpha $; in this bidegree this is the usual notion of positivity. The second variation of the mass produces integrals of the form $\int _ X\mathrm df\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}f\wedge T$, and the following elementary lemma shows that their signs detect the sign of $T$.

Lemma 26.15.1. Suppose a smooth real $(n-1,n-1)$-form $T$ satisfies

\[ \int _ X \mathrm{d}f\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}f\wedge T\geq 0 \quad \hbox{for every smooth real }f. \]

Then $T\geq 0$.

Proof. Suppose instead that $\mathrm i\alpha \wedge \bar\alpha \wedge T<0$ at a point $x_0$ for some $(1,0)$-covector $\alpha $. Every $(1,0)$-covector is the $(1,0)$-part of a real covector, so we can choose a real linear function $\ell $ in local real coordinates around $x_0$ whose differential has $(1,0)$-part $\alpha $ at $x_0$. Then $\mathrm{d}\ell \wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\ell $ is a positive multiple of $\mathrm i\alpha \wedge \bar\alpha $ at $x_0$, and after shrinking the coordinate ball, $\mathrm{d}\ell \wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\ell \wedge T$ is strictly negative on it. Choose a nonzero smooth cutoff function $\chi $ supported in this ball and, for positive integers $k$, set

\[ f_ k=k^{-1}\chi \sin (k\ell ), \]

extended by zero to $X$. Since $\mathrm{d}\ell $ is a constant nonzero covector in these coordinates,

\[ \mathrm{d}f_ k\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}f_ k =\chi ^2\cos ^2(k\ell )\, \mathrm{d}\ell \wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\ell +O(k^{-1}), \]

where $k$ times the remainder has uniformly bounded coefficients. In $\cos ^2(k\ell )=(1+\cos (2k\ell ))/2$, the oscillatory part contributes integrals tending to zero, as one sees by integrating by parts in a coordinate direction in which $\ell $ has nonzero derivative. Hence

\[ \lim _{k\to \infty }\int _ X\mathrm{d}f_ k\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}f_ k\wedge T =\frac12\int _ X\chi ^2\mathrm{d}\ell \wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\ell \wedge T<0, \]

which contradicts the hypothesis. $\square$

Proposition 26.15.2. Let $n\geq 3$, and let $v\in \mathcal H_\omega $ be a local minimum of $\mathrm M_\omega $ for the $C^2$ topology. Then $\Omega =\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ satisfies

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Omega ^{n-1})=0, \qquad \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Omega ^{n-2})\leq 0. \]

At a local maximum the first condition still holds and the second inequality is reversed.

Proof. For every smooth real $f$, the potentials $v+tf$ lie in $\mathcal H_\omega $ for all sufficiently small $|t|$. The vanishing of the first variation therefore gives the Gauduchon condition. The second variation is

\[ \begin{aligned} \left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^2}{\mathrm{d}t^2}\right|_{t=0}\mathrm M_\omega (v+tf) & =n(n-1)\int _ X(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f)^2\wedge \Omega ^{n-2}\\ & =-n(n-1)\int _ X\mathrm{d}f\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}f\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Omega ^{n-2}), \end{aligned} \]

where the last equality follows by integrating by parts twice, using that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Omega ^{n-2})$ is both $\mathrm{d}$-closed and $\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}$-closed. At a local minimum this second variation is nonnegative for every $f$, and lemma 26.15.1, applied to $-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Omega ^{n-2})$, gives $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Omega ^{n-2})\leq 0$. The case of a maximum is identical, with the opposite sign. $\square$

In dimension three these necessary conditions are also sufficient, and every local extremum is global.

Theorem 26.15.3. Assume $n=3$ and $v\in \mathcal H_\omega $. The following are equivalent:

  1. $v$ is a local minimum of $\mathrm M_\omega $;

  2. $v$ is a global minimum of $\mathrm M_\omega $ on $\mathcal H_\omega $;

  3. $\Omega =\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ is Gauduchon and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\omega \leq 0$.

In this case $v_-(\omega )=\int _ X\Omega ^3$, and the infimum in (26.89) is attained. The corresponding statements for maxima hold with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\omega \geq 0$, and then $v_+(\omega )=\int _ X\Omega ^3<+\infty $.

Proof. Since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Omega =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\omega $, the preceding proposition shows that the first condition implies the third. Conversely, if $\Omega $ is Gauduchon, expanding the cube and integrating by parts gives, for every smooth real $f$,

26.92
\begin{equation} \int _ X(\Omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f)^3-\int _ X\Omega ^3 =-3\int _ X\mathrm{d}f\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}f\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\omega . \end{equation}

The form $\mathrm{d}f\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}f$ is nonnegative, so the right side is nonnegative if $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\omega \leq 0$; then $v$ is a global minimum, and in particular a local one. By theorem 26.14.3, regularization identifies the infimum of the masses of smooth strictly $\omega $-plurisubharmonic potentials with $v_-(\omega )$, and proposition 26.14.4 proves attainment in (26.89). Reversing the sign in the same identity proves the statements for maxima, including the equality of $\int _ X\Omega ^3$ with the supremum over bounded potentials. $\square$

These conditions characterize smooth minimizers when they exist; neither argument produces a Gauduchon metric of the form $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ in an arbitrary background.

For the Hopf metric $\omega $ and the pulled-back Fubini–Study form $\alpha $ of chapter 8, one has $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\omega =-\alpha ^2\leq 0$, and $\omega $ is Gauduchon. Thus the minimum of the mass is attained by the constant potential and equals $6$, while the upper mass is infinite. On a surface, by contrast, the mass $\int _ X\omega ^2+2\int _ Xv\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\omega $ is affine in the potential, so a local extremum exists precisely when $\omega $ is Gauduchon, and then all masses are equal. In dimension $n\geq 4$ the sign condition of proposition 26.15.2 does not control the terms of order three and higher in the mass polynomial, and the argument above does not show that the conditions are sufficient.

26.16 Curvature pairs and positive lower mass

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

On a threefold, the lower mass can be bounded below by a curvature condition. Consider a principal elliptic bundle over a Kähler surface, with a connection form $\eta $ whose curvature has real and imaginary parts $\alpha $ and $\beta $. If $\alpha ^2+\beta ^2$ is semipositive, the split metric $g$ built from the base metric and $\eta $ satisfies $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(g^2)=0$ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g\leq 0$. A cubic energy identity for bounded potentials then shows that no bounded potential has mass below $\int _ Xg^3$, and the determinant estimate of theorem 26.8.2 transfers this lower bound to every Hermitian form on the manifold. The same curvature sign excludes pluriclosed metrics on these manifolds.

In this section $X$ is a connected compact complex threefold and $\mathrm dV$ is a smooth positive probability volume form. Products of bounded potentials are those of proposition 1.7.1. For a Hermitian form $h$, $\mathscr G_ h$ denotes the compact convex set of normalized Gauduchon currents of section 4.4, and $k_ T$ is the determinant density of $T\in \mathscr G_ h$ relative to $\mathrm dV$, as in theorem 26.8.1.

26.16.1 The bounded cubic identity

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

When the square of a Hermitian metric $g$ is $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-closed, the mass of a bounded $g$-plurisubharmonic function differs from $\int _ Xg^3$ by a term that is linear in $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g$. Its coefficient is a positive current playing the role of $\mathrm du\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}u$.

Lemma 26.16.1. Let $g$ be a Hermitian form with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(g^2)=0$, and let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,g)$ be bounded. The current

\[ E_ u=\tfrac 12\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u^2)-u\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u \]

is positive, and

26.93
\begin{equation} \int _ X(g+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^3 =\int _ Xg^3-3\int _ XE_ u\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g. \end{equation}

If $-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g\geq 0$, then $v_-(g)=\int _ Xg^3$.

Proof. Locally write $u=p-a$, with $p$ bounded plurisubharmonic and $a$ smooth. Expanding in $p$ and $a$ and using multilinearity, we obtain closed currents $C_ r=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ r$, not necessarily positive, with $C_0=1$. The recursive definition of bounded products, expanded in the same way, gives

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(uC_ r)=C_{r+1},\qquad r=0,1,2. \]

By lemma 1.6.2 and invariance under smooth corrections, the weighted product $u\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ does not depend on the local decomposition. On a smaller ball choose smooth bounded plurisubharmonic $p_ j$ decreasing to $p$, and put $u_ j=p_ j-a$. The same lemma gives $u_ j\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j\to u\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$, while boundedness gives $u_ j^2\to u^2$ in $L^1$. Hence

\[ \mathrm du_ j\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}u_ j =\tfrac 12\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u_ j^2)-u_ j\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j\longrightarrow E_ u. \]

The smooth currents on the left are positive, so their weak limit $E_ u$ is positive and has order zero.

Now expand the cube. Repeated use of distributional Stokes gives

\[ \int _ XC_3=0,\qquad \int _ Xg^2\wedge C_1=\int _ Xu\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(g^2)=0, \qquad \int _ Xg\wedge C_2=\int _ XuC_1\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g. \]

Since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g$ is closed, $\int _ X\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u^2)\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g=0$, so the last integral equals $-\int _ XE_ u\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g$. This proves (26.93). If $-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g\geq 0$, the correction term is nonnegative, and $u=0$ attains the lower bound $\int _ Xg^3$. $\square$

Lemma 26.16.2. Let $g,h$ be Hermitian forms with $v_-(g)=V>0$ and $h\geq c g$ for some $c>0$. Then

26.94
\begin{equation} \sup _{T\in \mathscr G_ h}\| k_ T\| _{L^{3/2}(\mathrm dV)} \leq c^{-1}V^{-1/3},\qquad v_-(h)\geq c^3V. \end{equation}

Proof. We first check that the determinant measure in (26.39) does not depend on the Hermitian form used to express it. Let $T$ be a positive $(2,2)$-current, and let $\sigma _ h=h\wedge T$ and $P=P_ h$ be its trace measure and coefficient matrix relative to $h$, as in section 26.6. In an $h$-unitary frame let $G$ be the matrix of $g$, and set

\[ a=\tfrac 13\operatorname {tr}(GP),\qquad \sigma _ g=a\sigma _ h,\qquad P_ g=a^{-1}G^{1/2}PG^{1/2}. \]

Comparing the defining trace pairings shows that $\sigma _ g=g\wedge T$ and $P_ g$ are the trace measure and coefficient matrix of $T$ relative to $g$, with $\operatorname {tr}P_ g=3$. Since $G>0$ and $\operatorname {tr}P=3$, one has $a>0$ even where $P$ is singular. Moreover $\det P_ g=a^{-3}\det G\det P$ and $g^3=\det G\, h^3$, so the factors $a$ and $\det G$ cancel in $\Gamma _0(T)$. The calculation applies to the matrix density on the whole trace measure, including its singular part.

For $T\in \mathscr G_ h$ put $m=\int _ Xg\wedge T$. Then $0<m\leq c^{-1}$ and $U=T/m\in \mathscr G_ g$. By homogeneity, $k_ T=m k_ U$. Applying theorem 26.8.2 to $g$ gives the norm bound, and applying it again to $h$ gives the lower-mass bound. The supremum here is taken over all of $\mathscr G_ h$, including singular and degenerate currents. $\square$

26.16.2 The curvature criterion

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now describe threefolds on which the hypotheses of lemma 26.16.1 hold. Let $\pi :X\to B$ be a holomorphic principal elliptic bundle over a compact connected Kähler surface $(B,\omega )$, with fiber the elliptic curve $E=\mathbb C/\Lambda $ and fiber coordinate $z$. Assume that there is a smooth $(1,0)$-form $\eta $ on $X$, invariant under the action of $E$ and restricting to $\mathrm dz$ on every fiber, such that

\[ \mathrm d\eta =\pi ^*F,\qquad F=\alpha +\mathrm i\beta , \]

where $\alpha ,\beta $ are smooth closed real $(1,1)$-forms on $B$. Put

\[ Q=\alpha ^2+\beta ^2,\qquad e=\mathrm i\eta \wedge \bar\eta , \qquad g=\pi ^*\omega +s e,\quad s>0, \qquad A_ E=\int _ E\mathrm i\, \mathrm dz\wedge \mathrm d\bar z. \]

We call $g$ the split metric.

Theorem 26.16.3. Suppose $Q\geq 0$ and $Q\not\equiv 0$. The split form $g$ is Hermitian, and its lower mass is

26.95
\begin{equation} v_-(g)=V=3sA_ E\int _ B\omega ^2. \end{equation}

Every bounded $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,g)$ satisfies

\[ \int _ X(g+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^3 =V+\frac{3s}{2\pi }\int _ XE_ u\wedge \pi ^*Q\geq V. \]

Every Hermitian form $h\geq c g$, with $c>0$, satisfies (26.94). Thus the endpoint estimate of theorem 26.8.2 holds for every Hermitian form on $X$, with a constant depending on the form. The manifold $X$ admits no pluriclosed Hermitian metric.

Proof. We suppress pullbacks in the curvature calculation. Comparing types in $\mathrm d\eta =F$ gives $\partial \eta =0$, $\bar\partial \eta =F$, $\partial \bar\eta =\bar F$ and $\bar\partial \bar\eta =0$. Hence

\[ \partial e=-\mathrm i\eta \wedge \bar F,\qquad \bar\partial e=\mathrm iF\wedge \bar\eta , \qquad \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}e=-\frac1{2\pi }F\wedge \bar F=-\frac1{2\pi }Q. \]

Since $e^2=0$ and the base has dimension two,

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g=-\frac{s}{2\pi }\pi ^*Q,\qquad \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(g^2)=-\frac{s}{\pi }\pi ^*(\omega \wedge Q)=0, \qquad g^3=3s\pi ^*(\omega ^2)\wedge e. \]

The form $g$ is positive, because $\pi ^*\omega $ is positive on directions transverse to the fibers and $e$ is positive on the vertical line. Fiber integration of $g^3$ gives $V$, and lemma 26.16.1, lemma 26.16.2 give the mass identity and the endpoint bound. By compactness, every Hermitian form $h$ satisfies $h\geq cg$ for some $c>0$.

Finally, $S=-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g$ is smooth, semipositive and not identically zero. If a Hermitian form $b$ were pluriclosed, Stokes' theorem would give

\[ 0=\int _ Xg\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b =\int _ Xb\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g=-\int _ Xb\wedge S<0, \]

where the last inequality holds because $b$ is strictly positive and $S$ is semipositive and nonzero on an open set. $\square$

Proposition 26.16.4. The split metric $g$ is balanced if and only if $\omega \wedge \alpha =\omega \wedge \beta =0$. For such a primitive pair one has $Q\leq 0$, with strict inequality wherever the pair is nonzero. In particular, a balanced split metric never satisfies the hypothesis $Q\geq 0$, $Q\not\equiv 0$ of theorem 26.16.3.

Proof. One has $\mathrm d(g^2)=2s\pi ^*\omega \wedge \mathrm de$, and $\mathrm de$ has one component containing $\eta $ and one containing $\bar\eta $. These two vertical types are independent, so $\mathrm d(g^2)=0$ if and only if $\omega \wedge F=0$, which is equivalent to the two stated real identities. A primitive real $(1,1)$-form on a surface has eigenvalues $\lambda ,-\lambda $ in an $\omega $-unitary frame, so its square is $-\lambda ^2\omega ^2$. Applying this to $\alpha $ and to $\beta $ gives the sign of $Q$. In this case $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g\geq 0$, so (26.93) bounds the masses of bounded potentials from above rather than from below, and gives no information on the lower mass. $\square$

26.16.3 Negative-line-bundle quotients

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The simplest examples of the curvature criterion come from negative line bundles. Dividing the complement of the zero section of a negative line bundle over $\mathbb P^1\times \mathbb P^1$ by a dilation produces a principal elliptic bundle, and the logarithm of the fiber norm gives a connection form whose curvature is a multiple of a Kähler form on the base.

Take $B=\mathbb P^1\times \mathbb P^1$, let $\omega _1,\omega _2$ be the pullbacks of the Fubini–Study forms of the two factors, normalized to have integral one, and put

\[ \begin{gathered} \Omega =a\omega _1+b\omega _2,\quad a,b\in \mathbb N_{>0},\qquad L=\mathcal O(-a,-b),\\ X=L^\times /\langle v\mapsto \rho v\rangle ,\quad 0<\rho <1, \end{gathered} \]

where $L^\times $ is the total space of $L$ with the zero section removed. Equip $L$ with the Hermitian norm induced by the standard norms on the tautological bundles of the two factors. Let $r=\log \| v\| ^2$ and set $\eta =\partial r$.

Proposition 26.16.5. The quotient $X$ is a compact complex threefold, and the projection to $B$ is a principal elliptic fibration. For $g=\pi ^*\Omega +s\mathrm i\eta \wedge \bar\eta $, $s>0$,

26.96
\begin{equation} v_-(g)=24\pi s ab\log (1/\rho ). \end{equation}

Every Hermitian form on $X$ satisfies the endpoint bound of theorem 26.16.3 over its full set of normalized Gauduchon currents. The manifold has $H^2(X,\mathbb R)\ne 0$ and admits no balanced or pluriclosed metric. In particular, it is not diffeomorphic to a Hopf threefold $(\mathbb C^3\setminus \{ 0\} )/\langle z\mapsto \lambda z\rangle $, $0<|\lambda |<1$.

Proof. In product coordinates, the squared norm of the standard local frame of $L$ is $(1+|z_1|^2)^ a(1+|z_2|^2)^ b$. Therefore

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}r=\pi ^*\Omega ,\qquad \mathrm d\eta =2\pi \mathrm i\pi ^*\Omega . \]

Dilation changes $r$ by a constant, so $\eta $ descends to $X$. The function $r$ changes by the nonzero constant $\log \rho ^2$ under the generator, so the action is free and properly discontinuous; the unit circle bundle, which is compact, times a fundamental interval for $r$ maps onto $X$, which is therefore compact. Each fiber is $\mathbb C^*/\rho ^{\mathbb Z}$. Its logarithmic coordinate has periods $\log \rho $ and $2\pi \mathrm i$, and the curvature $F=2\pi \mathrm i\Omega $ has real part $0$ and imaginary part $2\pi \Omega $; hence

\[ A_ E=4\pi \log (1/\rho ),\qquad Q=4\pi ^2\Omega ^2>0, \qquad \int _ B\Omega ^2=2ab. \]

Theorem 26.16.3 now gives (26.96) and the endpoint bound, and excludes pluriclosed metrics.

To prove $H^2(X,\mathbb R)\ne 0$, put $A=\eta +\bar\eta $ and $B_1=\mathrm i(\eta -\bar\eta )$. Then $\mathrm dA=0$ and $\mathrm dB_1=-4\pi \pi ^*\Omega $. Suppose that $\pi ^*(\omega _1-\omega _2)=\mathrm d\zeta $ for a real one-form $\zeta $. Averaging $\zeta $ over the compact group of elliptic translations, we may assume $\zeta $ invariant, so that

\[ \zeta =\pi ^*\xi +(\pi ^*f)A+(\pi ^*j)B_1 \]

for a one-form $\xi $ and functions $f,j$ on $B$. The components of $\mathrm d\zeta $ with one vertical and one horizontal direction must vanish, which gives $\mathrm df=\mathrm dj=0$. Thus

\[ \omega _1-\omega _2=\mathrm d\xi -4\pi j\Omega . \]

Integrating over the two rulings gives $1=-4\pi ja$ and $-1=-4\pi jb$, which is impossible because $a,b>0$. A Hopf threefold as above is diffeomorphic to $S^5\times S^1$, whose second real cohomology vanishes.

Finally, $\pi ^*\Omega =-\mathrm dB_1/(4\pi )$ is a nonzero semipositive exact form. Its integral against the closed positive square of a balanced metric would vanish by Stokes' theorem and be strictly positive by positivity. Hence no balanced metric exists. $\square$

26.16.4 A Calabi–Eckmann family without LCK metrics

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

A Hermitian metric $\Omega $ is locally conformally Kähler, or LCK, if $\mathrm d\Omega =\vartheta \wedge \Omega $ for a closed real one-form $\vartheta $, its Lee form; such metrics are studied in chapter 29. The quotients of the preceding subsection resemble Hopf manifolds. A second family shows that the curvature criterion also applies on manifolds that carry no LCK metric at all.

Let $\tau =A+\mathrm iB_0$, where $A<0$ and $B_0>0$, and let $\mathbb C$ act holomorphically on $(\mathbb C^2\setminus \{ 0\} )^2$ by

\[ t\cdot (z,w)=(\mathrm e^ t z,\mathrm e^{\tau t}w). \]

Write $X_\tau $ for the quotient and put $r_1=\log |z|^2$, $r_2=\log |w|^2$. The map $(z,w)\mapsto ([z],[w])$ descends to a projection $\pi :X_\tau \to \mathbb P^1\times \mathbb P^1$; let $\omega _1,\omega _2$ be the pullbacks to the base of the Fubini–Study forms of the two factors, normalized to have integral one.

Proposition 26.16.6. The quotient $X_\tau $ is a compact complex threefold diffeomorphic to $S^3\times S^3$, and $\pi $ is a principal elliptic fibration over $\mathbb P^1\times \mathbb P^1$. The form $\eta =\tau \partial r_1-\partial r_2$ descends to $X_\tau $, and for

\[ g=\pi ^*(\omega _1+\omega _2)+s\mathrm i\eta \wedge \bar\eta , \qquad s>0, \]

one has

26.97
\begin{equation} v_-(g)=48\pi ^2sB_0. \end{equation}

Every Hermitian form $h\geq c g$, with $c>0$, satisfies

\[ \sup _{T\in \mathscr G_ h}\| k_ T\| _{L^{3/2}(\mathrm dV)} \leq c^{-1}(48\pi ^2sB_0)^{-1/3}. \]

There is no balanced, pluriclosed or LCK metric on $X_\tau $.

Proof. Every orbit contains a unique point with $|z|=|w|=1$: the required parameter is determined by

\[ \operatorname {Re}t=-r_1/2,\qquad \operatorname {Im}t=(r_2-Ar_1)/(2B_0). \]

These smooth formulas identify $(\mathbb C^2\setminus \{ 0\} )^2$ with $\mathbb C\times (S^3\times S^3)$, so the action is free and proper, and $X_\tau $ is diffeomorphic to $S^3\times S^3$. To obtain holomorphic charts, choose a nonzero coordinate $z_ k$ of $z$ and a branch of its logarithm, and use the action to normalize $z_ k=1$; the functions $z_ l/z_ k$, $l\ne k$, and $w_ jz_ k^{-\tau }$ are then local holomorphic coordinates on the quotient. Changing the branch of the logarithm accounts for the periods along the fibers. Thus $X_\tau $ is a compact complex threefold.

The invariant form $\eta $ vanishes on the generator $z\partial _ z+\tau w\partial _ w$ of the action, so it descends. On an elliptic fiber it equals $\mathrm d\chi $, where $\chi =\tau \log v_1-\log v_2$ in terms of fiber coordinates $(v_1z,v_2w)$; its period lattice is generated by $2\pi \mathrm i\tau $ and $-2\pi \mathrm i$. Hence $A_ E=8\pi ^2B_0$. Direct differentiation gives

\[ \begin{aligned} F& =2\pi \mathrm i(\tau \omega _1-\omega _2),& \alpha & =-2\pi B_0\omega _1,\\ \beta & =2\pi (A\omega _1-\omega _2),& Q& =-8\pi ^2A\, \omega _1\wedge \omega _2>0. \end{aligned} \]

Since $\int _ B(\omega _1+\omega _2)^2=2$, theorem 26.16.3 gives the lower mass, the endpoint bound and the absence of pluriclosed metrics.

Moreover $\mathrm d\operatorname {Re}\eta =-2\pi B_0\pi ^*\omega _1$. This nonzero semipositive exact form excludes every balanced metric by the Stokes argument at the end of the preceding proof, and hence also every Kähler metric. The quotient is simply connected, so the Lee form of a hypothetical LCK metric would be exact, equal to $\mathrm df$; multiplying the metric by $\mathrm e^{-f}$ would then give a Kähler metric, a contradiction. In particular $X_\tau $ carries no Vaisman metric, that is, no LCK metric with parallel Lee form. $\square$

The curvature criterion controls bounded potentials and the full set of normalized Gauduchon currents in these threefolds. Curvature pairs of changing sign, primitive curvature pairs and higher-dimensional analogues are not covered by this argument.

26.17 Geometric bounds and determinant concentration

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

By theorem 26.8.2, the determinant densities of all normalized Gauduchon currents are bounded in the critical space $L^{n/(n-1)}$ exactly when the lower mass is positive. Together with the mass-invariance criterion of theorem 2.2.2, this gives geometric sufficient conditions for the critical bound. The critical exponent cannot be raised, even for currents that are powers of Kähler forms in one fixed class. The same concentrating family shows that currents approaching the maximum of the determinant functional for a fixed smooth density need not satisfy any uniform higher integrability, although the maximizer itself is smooth.

Throughout this section $n\geq 2$ and $\mathrm{d}V$ is a smooth positive probability volume form. For a Hermitian form $h$, $k_ T$ denotes the determinant density relative to $\mathrm{d}V$ of a current $T\in \mathscr G_ h$, as in section 26.8. Put $q=n/(n-1)$.

Corollary 26.17.1. Suppose $g$ is a Hermitian form with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(g^2)=0$, and put $V_ g=\int _ Xg^ n$. For every Hermitian form $h\geq cg$, $c>0$,

\[ v_-(h)\geq c^ nV_ g,\qquad \sup _{T\in \mathscr G_ h}\| k_ T\| _{L^ q(\mathrm{d}V)} \leq c^{-1}V_ g^{-1/n}. \]

Equality holds in the determinant bound when $h=g$ and $c=1$. In particular, on a compact Kähler manifold or a compact complex surface, the critical norm is uniformly bounded over the normalized Gauduchon currents of each fixed Hermitian form.

Proof. The identity $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(g^2)=2g\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g+2\mathrm{d}g\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}g$ shows that $\mathrm{d}g\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}g=0$, so theorem 2.2.2 gives $v_-(g)=V_ g$. The quotient in (26.89) is monotone and homogeneous in its numerator, so $h\geq cg$ implies $v_-(h)^{1/n}\geq cV_ g^{1/n}$. Theorem 26.8.2 turns these lower-mass bounds into the asserted determinant bounds, with equality when $h=g$.

A Kähler form satisfies both differential identities. On a surface, theorem 4.1.2 gives a metric $g$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g=0$, and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(g^2)=0$ for degree reasons. By compactness, each fixed $h$ satisfies $h\geq cg$ for some $c>0$. $\square$

For a fixed smooth $F$, theorem 26.6.2 gives a unique maximizing current for the determinant functional $\mathfrak D_ F$ of (26.39), and this current is smooth and strictly positive, so its determinant density is bounded. The following construction shows that currents approaching this maximum satisfy no uniform bound on higher moments of their determinant densities.

Proposition 26.17.2. Let $X=E^ n$, where $E=\mathbb C/(\mathbb Z+\mathrm i\mathbb Z)$ carries its flat area form of total area one, and write $\omega _ i$ for the pullback of this form from the $i$th factor. Set

\[ h=\sum _{i=1}^ n\omega _ i,\qquad \mathrm{d}V=\omega _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \omega _ n,\qquad V=n!,\qquad \alpha =\frac{n-1}{n}. \]

For $F=0$, the unique maximizing current is $T_*=h^{n-1}/V$, with $k_{T_*}=V^{-1/n}$ and $\mathfrak D_0(T_*)=V^{-1/n}$. There are smooth strictly positive closed forms $S_\varepsilon \in \mathscr G_ h$, $0<\varepsilon <\min \{ 1/4,\mathrm e^{-1}\} $, such that

\[ \| k_{S_\varepsilon }\| _{L^ q}=V^{-1/n},\qquad \mathfrak D_0(S_\varepsilon )\longrightarrow V^{-1/n}, \]

while for every fixed finite $p>q$,

26.100
\begin{equation} \int _ X k_{S_\varepsilon }^{p}\, \mathrm{d}V \geq C_{n,p}[\log (1/\varepsilon )]^{-\alpha p} \varepsilon ^{2-2\alpha p}\longrightarrow +\infty . \end{equation}

Their $L^\infty $ norms also diverge. Each $S_\varepsilon $ is the $(n-1)$st power of a Kähler form in the fixed class $V^{-1/(n-1)}[h]$, and has the same Bott–Chern, de Rham and Aeppli classes as $T_*$. No $S_\varepsilon $ attains the maximum of $\mathfrak D_0$.

Proof. Put $H_ i=\bigwedge _{j\ne i}\omega _ j$. Expanding the wedge powers gives $h^{n-1}=(n-1)!\sum _ iH_ i$ and $h\wedge H_ i=\mathrm{d}V$. Thus $T_*=(1/n)\sum _ iH_ i$ is normalized, and the determinant formula gives $k_{T_*}=V^{-1/n}$. The smooth equation for $F=0$ is solved by the zero potential, since $h^ n=V\, \mathrm{d}V$; theorem 26.6.2 therefore gives the maximal value and the uniqueness of the maximizer.

To construct $S_\varepsilon $, choose a nonnegative smooth function $\beta $ compactly supported in the unit disk in $\mathbb C$, of integral one, with $\beta \geq \beta _0>0$ on a disk of radius $r_0\in (0,1)$. On an embedded disk in the first elliptic factor put $b_\varepsilon (z)=\varepsilon ^{-2}\beta (z/\varepsilon )$, extended by zero; it has integral one. For $0<t<1$, set

\[ \begin{gathered} f_{\varepsilon ,t}=1-t+tb_\varepsilon ,\qquad a=V^{-1/(n-1)},\\ \gamma _{\varepsilon ,t}=a(f_{\varepsilon ,t}\omega _1+\omega _2+\cdots +\omega _ n), \qquad T_{\varepsilon ,t}=\gamma _{\varepsilon ,t}^{n-1}. \end{gathered} \]

The form $\gamma _{\varepsilon ,t}$ is strictly positive, and it is closed because $f_{\varepsilon ,t}$ depends only on the first factor, which has real dimension two. Direct expansion gives

26.101
\begin{equation} T_{\varepsilon ,t}=\frac1n\left(H_1+f_{\varepsilon ,t}\sum _{i=2}^ nH_ i\right), \qquad k_{T_{\varepsilon ,t}}=V^{-1/n}f_{\varepsilon ,t}^{\alpha }. \end{equation}

Since $\int _ Xf_{\varepsilon ,t}\, \mathrm{d}V=1$, the trace integral is one, so $T_{\varepsilon ,t}\in \mathscr G_ h$, and $\| k_{T_{\varepsilon ,t}}\| _{L^ q}=V^{-1/n}$ because $\alpha q=1$.

The mean-zero function $f_{\varepsilon ,t}-1$ has a smooth real potential $\psi $ on $E$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi =(f_{\varepsilon ,t}-1)\omega _1$; for example, the mean-zero Fourier series solving $\Delta \psi =4\pi (f_{\varepsilon ,t}-1)$ in the area-one coordinates. Consequently $\gamma _{\varepsilon ,t}-ah=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(a\psi )$. Since $T_*=(ah)^{n-1}$, the closed form $R=\sum _{j=0}^{n-2}\gamma _{\varepsilon ,t}^{n-2-j}\wedge (ah)^ j$ satisfies

\[ T_{\varepsilon ,t}-T_*=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(a\psi R). \]

This proves all the assertions about classes, including the case $n=2$, where $R=1$.

Jensen's inequality and $f_{\varepsilon ,t}\geq 1-t$ give

\[ V^{-1/n}(1-t)^\alpha \leq \mathfrak D_0(T_{\varepsilon ,t}) =V^{-1/n}\int _ Xf_{\varepsilon ,t}^{\alpha }\, \mathrm{d}V <V^{-1/n}, \]

where the inequality is strict because $f_{\varepsilon ,t}$ is nonconstant and $0<\alpha <1$. On the product of the disk of radius $r_0\varepsilon $ with the other factors, we have $f_{\varepsilon ,t}\geq t\beta _0\varepsilon ^{-2}$, and this set has volume $\pi r_0^2\varepsilon ^2$. Hence

\[ \begin{split} \int _ Xk_{T_{\varepsilon ,t}}^ p\, \mathrm{d}V & \geq V^{-p/n}\pi r_0^2\beta _0^{\alpha p}t^{\alpha p} \varepsilon ^{2-2\alpha p},\\ \| k_{T_{\varepsilon ,t}}\| _\infty & \geq V^{-1/n}\beta _0^\alpha t^\alpha \varepsilon ^{-2\alpha }. \end{split} \]

Now take $t=1/\log (1/\varepsilon )$, which lies in $(0,1)$ by the restriction on $\varepsilon $, and $S_\varepsilon =T_{\varepsilon ,t}$. Then $\mathfrak D_0(S_\varepsilon )$ converges to its maximum, while both lower bounds diverge for $p>q$ because $\alpha p>1$. All constants are independent of $\varepsilon $. $\square$

If instead $t\in (0,1)$ is kept fixed in the same construction, then $\int _ Xk_{T_{\varepsilon ,t}}^ p\, \mathrm{d}V\geq C_{n,p,t}\varepsilon ^{2-2\alpha p}$ and $\| k_{T_{\varepsilon ,t}}\| _\infty \geq C_{n,t}\varepsilon ^{-2\alpha }$. Thus there are no uniform bounds on higher moments, either over the full normalized cone or along a maximizing sequence for one smooth density. Every member of these families attains the supremum $V^{-1/n}$ of the critical norm in corollary 26.17.1, whereas the maximum of $\mathfrak D_0$ is attained only by $T_*$.

26.17.1 Intrinsic determinant measures and the complete profile

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Keep the smooth positive probability volume $\mathrm dV$ fixed. For a positive $(n-1,n-1)$-current $T$ of finite mass, let $\Gamma _{0,h}(T)$ denote the determinant measure of (26.39) computed with a positive auxiliary metric $h$. The current need not be smooth or strictly positive. The following coordinate calculation identifies the measure itself, including on singular coefficient measures.

Lemma 26.17.3. For a fixed positive current $T$ and fixed $\mathrm dV$, the measure $\Gamma _{0,h}(T)$ is independent of the positive auxiliary metric $h$. This statement includes singular and degenerate currents.

Proof. In a holomorphic frame put $\lambda =\bigwedge _{j=1}^ n(i\, dz_ j\wedge d\bar z_ j)$, $h=i\sum H_{ij}\, dz_ i\wedge d\bar z_ j$ and $\mathrm dV=v\lambda $. Positive currents have measure coefficients. Choose a positive Radon measure $\nu $ dominating them. For a smooth real form $\xi =i\sum \Xi _{ij}\, dz_ i\wedge d\bar z_ j$, let $Q$ be the positive semidefinite Hermitian coefficient matrix characterized by $\xi \wedge T=\operatorname {tr}(\Xi Q)\nu $. Then

\[ \sigma _ h=\operatorname {tr}(HQ)\nu ,\qquad P_ h=\frac{nH^{1/2}QH^{1/2}}{\operatorname {tr}(HQ)}. \]

The formula holds on $Q\ne 0$; zero is assigned when $Q=0$. Since $h^ n=n!\det (H)\lambda $, determinant algebra gives

26.104
\begin{equation} \Gamma _{0,h}(T) =n(n!)^{-1/n}v^{1/n}(\det Q)^{1/n}\nu . \end{equation}

This expression has no $H$. Replacing $\nu $ by $L\nu $, where $L>0$ almost everywhere, replaces $Q$ by $Q/L$, leaving the measure unchanged; a common dominating measure compares any two choices. The original geometric definition ensures compatibility under frame changes. Matrix AM–GM bounds it locally by a constant times a reference trace measure, proving local finiteness. Compactness gives a finite Radon measure. $\square$

Write $\Gamma _0(T)$ for this intrinsic measure.

Theorem 26.17.4. Let $\beta $ be smooth positive Hermitian with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\beta ^ j)=0$ for $1\leq j\leq n-1$, $V=\int _ X\beta ^ n$, and let $h=\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u>0$ for a smooth global real function $u$. Then

\[ \mathscr G_ h=\mathscr G_\beta ,\qquad \{ \Gamma _{0,h}(T):T\in \mathscr G_ h\} =\{ \Gamma _{0,\beta }(T):T\in \mathscr G_\beta \} . \]

Every measure in this common family has a density $k_ T$ satisfying

\[ k_ T\geq 0,\qquad \| k_ T\| _{L^{n/(n-1)}(\mathrm dV)}\leq V^{-1/n}. \]

For every real or complex $p\in L^ n(\mathrm dV)$,

26.105
\begin{equation} \sup _{T\in \mathscr G_ h}\int _ X|p|\, \Gamma _0(T) =V^{-1/n}\| p\| _{L^ n(\mathrm dV)}. \end{equation}

All suprema retain singular and degenerate tests.

Proof. Distributional integration by parts gives $\int h\wedge T=\int \beta \wedge T+\int u\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}T =\int \beta \wedge T$. This proves equality of the actual current sets; Lemma 26.17.3 proves equality of their measure sets.

Take a smooth strictly positive real $r$ and put $M=\int r^ n \mathrm dV$. By theorem 9.1.1 there are a smooth potential $w$, a positive form $A=\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w$ and a positive coefficient in its prescribed-volume equation. The mass-invariance hypothesis and theorem 2.2.2 determine that coefficient, giving

26.106
\begin{equation} A^ n=\frac{Vr^ n}{M}\, \mathrm dV. \end{equation}

In particular, the total mass of $A$ is $V$. No estimates uniform in a degenerating smooth density are required.

For every $T\in \mathscr G_\beta $, integration by parts gives $\int A\wedge T=1$. In an $A$-unitary frame, matrix AM–GM gives $\det (P_ A)^{1/n}\leq 1$, including singular coefficient densities. Thus as Radon measures

\[ \Gamma _0(T)\leq (\mathrm dV/A^ n)^{1/n}(A\wedge T). \]

Multiplying by $r$ and using (26.106) yields

26.107
\begin{equation} \int r\, \Gamma _0(T)\leq V^{-1/n}\| r\| _{L^ n(\mathrm dV)}. \end{equation}

Replacing a smooth nonnegative $r$ by $r+\eta $ and letting $\eta \downarrow 0$ extends this bound to nonnegative smooth tests. For smooth real or complex $s$, smooth positive approximations $\sqrt{|s|^2+\eta ^2}$ imply $|\int s\, \Gamma _0(T)|\leq V^{-1/n}\| s\| _ n$. Consequently integration against $\Gamma _0(T)$ extends continuously from smooth functions to $L^ n$. Its positive dual representative is $k_ T\in L^{n/(n-1)}$ with the stated bound. Equality on smooth tests identifies the two Radon measures, proving absolute continuity. The integrals of arbitrary $L^ n$ roots are therefore well-defined.

For the reverse inequality with $r>0$ smooth, let $T_ r=A^{n-1}/V$. It is smooth and positive. Write $E=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w$. For $j\leq n-1$, expansion and closedness of $E$ give

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(A^ j)=\sum _{l=0}^ j\binom jl\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\beta ^{j-l})\wedge E^ l=0. \]

The term with $j=l$ differentiates the constant one. Thus $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}T_ r=0$, and smooth integration by parts gives

\[ \int _ X\beta \wedge A^{n-1} =\int _ X A^ n-\int _ X\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w\wedge A^{n-1}=V. \]

Consequently $T_ r\in \mathscr G_\beta $. Its positive root is $\gamma =V^{-1/(n-1)}A$, so

\[ k_{T_ r}=(\gamma ^ n/\mathrm dV)^{(n-1)/n} =V^{-1/n}\frac{r^{n-1}}{\| r\| _ n^{n-1}}. \]

It attains equality in (26.107). For a nonnegative $p\in L^ n$, take smooth positive $r_ j\to p$ in $L^ n$. The common dual bound controls $\int (p-r_ j)k_{T_{r_ j}}\mathrm dV$, proving the lower bound in (26.105); the upper bound already holds for every current. Apply the same argument to $|p|$ for complex roots. $\square$

26.17.2 Fixed determinant data with degenerating ellipticity

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory
Here $v_-(h)$ and $v_+(h)$ denote the infimum and supremum of $\int _ X(h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n$ over bounded $h$-psh potentials $w$.

Theorem 26.17.5. On $X=(\mathbb C/(\mathbb Z+i\mathbb Z))^ n$, $n\geq 2$, there are positive metrics $\omega _\varepsilon $ in a fixed Kähler class, $0<\varepsilon \leq 1$, with fixed complete current and determinant-measure families and fixed endpoint (26.105), while their least relative eigenvalue equals $\varepsilon $. They have uniformly bounded smooth coefficients and global potentials, and

\[ v_-(\omega _\varepsilon )=v_+(\omega _\varepsilon )=n!>0. \]

The manifold has a global holomorphic volume form and $c_1=0$. Every displayed metric is already balanced and pluriclosed.

Proof. Write $z_ j=x_ j+iy_ j$, $\beta _ j=(i/2)dz_ j\wedge d\bar z_ j=dx_ j\wedge dy_ j$, $\beta =\sum _ j\beta _ j$, $V=n!$, and $\mathrm dV=\beta ^ n/V$. Set

\[ u_\varepsilon =\frac{1-\varepsilon }{4\pi }\cos (4\pi x_1), \qquad a_\varepsilon =1-(1-\varepsilon )\cos (4\pi x_1). \]

For a function of $x_1$, $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=(u''/(4\pi ))\beta _1$. Hence

\[ \omega _\varepsilon =\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\varepsilon =a_\varepsilon \beta _1+\sum _{j=2}^ n\beta _ j. \]

These are globally periodic smooth positive closed forms with $\varepsilon \leq a_\varepsilon \leq 2-\varepsilon $ and $\omega _\varepsilon \leq 2\beta $. Their minimum relative eigenvalue is $\varepsilon $. They converge smoothly to a semipositive form losing rank on $\{ x_1\in \tfrac 12\mathbb Z\} $. All fixed-order smooth norms of the coefficients and potentials are uniformly bounded. Theorem 26.17.4 proves equality of the entire current data and the exact norm at every $\varepsilon $.

Since $\omega _\varepsilon $ is closed, theorem 2.2.2 gives mass $V$ for every bounded $\omega _\varepsilon $-plurisubharmonic potential. The explicit formula also gives $\int _ X\omega _\varepsilon ^ n=V$, since $a_\varepsilon $ has mean one. Thus both extrema over bounded potentials equal $V$. The global form $dz_1\wedge \cdots \wedge dz_ n$ trivializes the canonical bundle; closed positive metrics are balanced and pluriclosed. $\square$

26.17.3 The same obstruction for nonclosed pluriclosed metrics

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Theorem 26.17.6. On the fixed torus above, fix $0<\tau <1/2$. There is a positive nonclosed pluriclosed metric $h_\tau $ and a family $\Omega _\varepsilon =h_\tau +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\varepsilon >0$, $0<\varepsilon \leq 1/2$, with identical actual current sets and intrinsic determinant-measure families. For any fixed smooth positive probability volume their entire-family endpoint is

\[ \sup _{T\in \mathscr G_{\Omega _\varepsilon }} \int _ X|p|\, \Gamma _0(T) =V_\tau ^{-1/n}\| p\| _{L^ n(\mathrm dV)},\qquad V_\tau =n!(1-\tau ^2/2). \]

Both bounded-potential masses equal $V_\tau $. All fixed-order smooth coefficient and potential norms are uniformly bounded, while

\[ \min _ X\lambda _{\min }(\Omega _\varepsilon \mid \beta )=\varepsilon , \qquad d\Omega _\varepsilon =dh_\tau \ne 0. \]

The flat $\beta $ is a fixed balanced metric on this same manifold with $c_1=0$; each $\Omega _\varepsilon $ is pluriclosed.

Proof. With the torus coordinates above set $s=\sin (2\pi x_1)$ and

\[ \xi =\frac i2(dz_1\wedge d\bar z_2+dz_2\wedge d\bar z_1) =dx_1\wedge dy_2+dx_2\wedge dy_1, \qquad h_\tau =\beta +\tau s\xi . \]

The first relative Hermitian block is $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1& \tau s\\ \tau s& 1\end{smallmatrix}\right)$, so positivity follows from $|\tau s|<1$. Moreover

\[ dh_\tau =2\pi \tau \cos (2\pi x_1) dx_1\wedge dx_2\wedge dy_1\ne 0. \]

Because $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}s$ is proportional to $dz_1\wedge d\bar z_1$, $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(s\xi )=0$. The first two-coordinate metric is thus pluriclosed, and its square is top degree on that complex surface. Expanding with the remaining closed curve factors proves

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(h_\tau ^ j)=0\quad (1\leq j\leq n-1),\qquad \int _ X h_\tau ^ n=n!\int _0^1(1-\tau ^2\sin ^2(2\pi x_1))dx_1 =V_\tau . \]

Use the same $u_\varepsilon $ and $a_\varepsilon $ as in Theorem 26.17.5, now with $\varepsilon \leq 1/2$. Then

\[ \Omega _\varepsilon =a_\varepsilon \beta _1+ \sum _{j\geq 2}\beta _ j+\tau s\xi , \qquad a_\varepsilon =\varepsilon +2(1-\varepsilon )s^2. \]

The first block of $\Omega _\varepsilon -\varepsilon \beta $ has diagonal entries $2(1-\varepsilon )s^2$ and $1-\varepsilon $, and determinant

\[ \bigl [2(1-\varepsilon )^2-\tau ^2\bigr ]s^2\geq 0. \]

It is positive semidefinite; the remaining diagonal entries are $1-\varepsilon $. Hence $\Omega _\varepsilon \geq \varepsilon \beta $, with equality in the least eigenvalue at $s=0$. Also $\Omega _\varepsilon \leq 3\beta $, all fixed-order smooth norms are uniformly bounded, and the smooth semipositive limit loses rank at $s=0$. Since $d\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\varepsilon =0$, the exterior derivative is the displayed fixed nonzero $dh_\tau $.

The lower-power expansion in the proof of theorem 26.17.4 applies to $\Omega _\varepsilon =h_\tau +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\varepsilon $; it gives $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Omega _\varepsilon ^ j)=0$ for $j\leq n-1$, in particular pluriclosedness. Its volume is $V_\tau $, either by integrating the displayed determinant or by theorem 2.2.2.
