That theorem also gives mass $V_\tau $ for every bounded $\Omega _\varepsilon $-plurisubharmonic potential, so both mass extrema equal $V_\tau $. Theorem 26.17.4 applied to $h_\tau $ gives equality of the entire current and measure sets and their exact endpoint for every $p\in L^ n$. The balanced trace is fixed as well:

\[ \int _ X\Omega _\varepsilon \wedge \beta ^{n-1}=n!. \]

The off-diagonal term has zero trace and the mean of $a_\varepsilon $ equals one. The global holomorphic volume form gives $c_1=0$. The manifold itself is already Kähler, so these nonclosed metrics show that the complete determinant profile and positive bounded-potential mass extrema can stay fixed while the least relative eigenvalue tends to zero. The coexistence problem of 33.3.12 remains open. $\square$

26.17.4 Concentration at exact maximizers for varying roots

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory
Sharp determinant bounds also need not give uniform integrability of the determinant powers or preserve maximizers under weak current limits.

Theorem 26.17.7. On $X=(\mathbb C/(\mathbb Z+\mathrm i\mathbb Z))^ n$, let $\beta _ j=\mathrm dx_ j\wedge \mathrm dy_ j$, $\beta =\sum _ j\beta _ j$, $V=n!$ and $\mathrm dV=\beta ^ n/V$. There are smooth positive closed $A_\varepsilon =\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_\varepsilon $, $0<\varepsilon <1$, and $T_\varepsilon =A_\varepsilon ^{n-1}/V \in \mathscr G_\beta $ satisfying

\[ \int _ X k_{T_\varepsilon }^{n/(n-1)}\mathrm dV=V^{-1/(n-1)}. \]

Each $T_\varepsilon $ is an actual dual maximizer for a specified unit $L^ n$ root. The potentials $w_\varepsilon $ have uniformly bounded oscillation and converge uniformly to a continuous canonical $\beta $-psh $w_0$. The currents converge weakly to $T_0=(\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_0)^{n-1}/V\in \mathscr G_\beta $, but $\Gamma _0(T_0)=0$. The power densities above are not uniformly integrable. Nevertheless every $w_\varepsilon $, including $w_0$, has bounded-potential Monge–Ampère mass $V$.

Proof. Put $r=1-\varepsilon $ and

\[ P_ r(x)=\frac{1-r^2}{1-2r\cos (2\pi x)+r^2},\qquad f_\varepsilon =\varepsilon +(1-\varepsilon )P_ r. \]

The positive periodic Poisson kernel has mean one and Fourier series

\[ P_ r=1+2\sum _{m\geq 1}r^ m\cos (2\pi mx). \]

Define

\[ w_\varepsilon =-\frac{2(1-\varepsilon )}{\pi } \sum _{m\geq 1}\frac{r^ m\cos (2\pi mx_1)}{m^2}. \]

For fixed $\varepsilon >0$ this is smooth. Differentiation gives $w_\varepsilon ''=4\pi (f_\varepsilon -1)$, and hence

\[ A_\varepsilon =f_\varepsilon (x_1)\beta _1+ \sum _{j=2}^ n\beta _ j=\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_\varepsilon >0. \]

It is closed, and $\int A_\varepsilon ^ n=V$. Theorem 26.17.4 and the smooth-root formula give

\[ k_{T_\varepsilon }=V^{-1/n}f_\varepsilon ^{(n-1)/n}, \qquad k_{T_\varepsilon }^{n/(n-1)} =V^{-1/(n-1)}f_\varepsilon . \]

Thus the displayed power integral attains the upper bound over all currents, not a bound for a selected family. More precisely, $p_\varepsilon =f_\varepsilon ^{1/n}$ has $L^ n$ norm one, and $\int p_\varepsilon k_{T_\varepsilon }\mathrm dV=V^{-1/n}$, so $T_\varepsilon $ attains the original entire-family dual supremum for this varying root. No common fixed density is asserted.

The Fourier series bounds $|w_\varepsilon |\leq \pi /3$ and implies uniform convergence to

\[ w_0=-\frac2\pi \sum _{m\geq 1}\frac{\cos (2\pi mx_1)}{m^2}. \]

This limit is continuous and canonically $\beta $-psh by distributional positivity of $A_0=\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_0$. The probability measures $P_ r(x_1)dx_1$ converge weakly to the periodic Dirac mass at $x_1=0$, as follows from their Fourier coefficients $r^{|m|}$. Thus

\[ A_0=\delta _{x_1=0}\beta _1+\sum _{j=2}^ n\beta _ j, \]

where the Dirac measure is tensored with Lebesgue measure in the remaining real coordinates. Its bounded local products are genuine Bedford–Taylor products. Locally the first-coordinate current $\beta _1+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_0$ is pulled back from one complex coordinate, so its powers of degree at least two vanish in the bounded-product recursion. The remaining factors are smooth and closed. Multilinear expansion therefore contains at most one first-coordinate factor; the same expansion for $A_\varepsilon $ passes directly to the distributional limit. Consequently $T_\varepsilon \to T_0=A_0^{n-1}/V$ weakly. This current is positive, closed and normalized by $\int \beta \wedge T_0=1$.

In its positive coefficient matrix the absolutely continuous part is proportional to the first diagonal matrix below; the singular part on $x_1=0$ is proportional to the second:

\[ \operatorname {diag}(1,0,\ldots ,0),\qquad \operatorname {diag}(0,1,\ldots ,1). \]

Their ranks are respectively one and $n-1$, both less than $n$. With a measure dominating these mutually singular parts, formula (26.104) therefore gives $\Gamma _0(T_0)=0$. This is not the Monge–Ampère measure of $w_0$, which has total mass $V$ by theorem 2.2.2.

Away from $x_1=0$, $f_\varepsilon \to 0$ pointwise; its integral against $\mathrm dV$ remains one. Thus the positive power densities are not uniformly integrable: almost-everywhere convergence to zero and uniform integrability would force their fixed positive integrals to vanish. In particular $T\mapsto \int k_ T^{n/(n-1)}\mathrm dV$ is not continuous under these weak current limits, even with uniformly convergent continuous potentials. The intrinsic measures themselves satisfy $\Gamma _0(T_\varepsilon )\to 0$ in total mass; this follows from their uniform $L^{n/(n-1)}$ bound by splitting off arbitrarily small neighborhoods of $x_1=0$. The discontinuity concerns their power integrals, not that last measure limit. $\square$

The maximizing root varies with $\varepsilon $. This construction concerns the complete dual profile and the critical power integral; it does not give a sequence of exact maximizers for one fixed smooth density.

26.18 Vanishing lower mass

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

By (26.89), the lower mass $v_-(h)$ vanishes as soon as there are Gauduchon metrics $\eta $ whose degrees $\int _ Xh\wedge \eta ^{n-1}$ stay bounded while their volumes $\int _ X\eta ^ n$ tend to infinity. We construct such metrics on a compact threefold. Writing $\Gamma =\eta ^2$, the volume of $\eta $ is a determinant integral of the coefficient matrix of the positive $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-closed form $\Gamma $. The construction first concentrates the trace of a normalized form $\Gamma $ in one direction near a hypersurface, and then adds an average of compact rational curves that fills the two complementary directions. The degree stays bounded, while the determinant, and hence the volume of the positive root, becomes large. The smooth equation then converts these metrics into potentials whose masses tend to zero.

Theorem 26.18.1. There is a connected compact complex threefold $X$ such that, for every Hermitian form $h$ on $X$, there are smooth real functions $u_ j$ satisfying

\[ h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j>0,\qquad \sup _ Xu_ j=0,\qquad \int _ X(h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^3\longrightarrow 0. \]

In particular, $v_-(h)=0$ for every Hermitian form $h$ on this manifold.

26.18.1 The elliptic quotient

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Write points of $\mathbb P^3$ as $[z:w]$, with $z,w\in \mathbb C^2$, and set

\[ \Omega =\{ [z:w]:z\ne 0,\ w\ne 0\} ,\qquad q=\mathrm{e}^{2\pi },\qquad g[z:w]=[z:qw]. \]

Thus $g$ is an automorphism of $\Omega $; let $\langle g\rangle $ be the cyclic group it generates. This group acts freely. Indeed, if $g^ k$ fixed a point, the same scalar would have to equal $1$ on the nonzero block $z$ and $q^ k$ on the nonzero block $w$, which forces $k=0$.

The function

\[ t=\log \frac{\| w\| ^2}{\| z\| ^2} \]

is well defined on $\Omega $ and satisfies $t\circ g=t+4\pi $. Consequently the action is properly discontinuous: $t$ is bounded on compact subsets, so only finitely many translates of one compact set can meet another. Moreover, the strip $\{ 0\leq t\leq 4\pi \} $ is compact. In homogeneous coordinates of total norm one, its defining inequalities bound both blocks away from zero, so it is a closed subset of $\mathbb P^3$ contained in $\Omega $. Since the strip meets every orbit,

\[ X=\Omega /\langle g\rangle \]

is a connected compact complex threefold.

The map $[z:w]\mapsto ([z],[w])$ induces a holomorphic submersion $X\to \mathbb P^1\times \mathbb P^1$. Using a logarithm of the scalar relating two points with the same image, one sees that its fibers are the elliptic curves $E=\mathbb C/(2\pi \mathbb Z+2\pi \mathrm{i}\mathbb Z)$. Let $\alpha ,\beta $ be the pullbacks of the Fubini–Study forms of the two factors, each normalized to have integral one. Although $t$ does not descend to $X$, its differentials $\mathrm dt$ and $\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}t$ do. The form

26.108
\begin{equation} \omega =\alpha +\beta +\mathrm{d}t\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}t \end{equation}

is Hermitian: $\alpha +\beta $ is semipositive with kernel the vertical line, while $\mathrm{d}t\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}t$ is semipositive and strictly positive on the vertical line.

On the open set $\{ z_0w_0\ne 0\} $ we use the coordinates

\[ x=\frac{z_1}{z_0},\qquad y=\frac{w_1}{w_0},\qquad \zeta =\log \frac{w_0}{z_0}\in E. \]

Near $0\in E$, the last coordinate is represented by an ordinary complex number. For any one of these coordinates, denoted by $v$, put $\sigma _ v=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\pi }\mathrm{d}v\wedge \mathrm{d}\bar v$. Choose a smooth probability density $p$ relative to $\sigma _ v$ that is positive on $\{ |v|<1\} $ and vanishes on $\{ |v|\geq 1\} $. Choose also a smooth function $\chi _0$ with values in $[0,1]$, supported in $\{ |v|<1\} $ and equal to one on $\{ |v|\leq 1/2\} $, and write $\chi (x,y)=\chi _0(x)\chi _0(y)$. For $0<\delta <1/100$ define

\[ B_\delta =\chi (x,y)\delta ^{-2}p(\zeta /\delta )\sigma _\zeta , \]

extended by zero. This is a smooth semipositive $(1,1)$-form on $X$, because the chosen functions are smooth with compact support in their coordinate domains. As $\delta \downarrow 0$ it concentrates on the hypersurface $\{ \zeta =0\} $.

The first step shows that normalized positive $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-closed $(2,2)$-forms can pair arbitrarily strongly with $B_\delta $.

Lemma 26.18.2. For $\delta >0$ put

\[ C_\delta =\sup \left\{ \int _ XB_\delta \wedge \Theta : \Theta >0,\ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Theta =0,\ \int _ X\omega \wedge \Theta =1\right\} , \]

the supremum being taken over smooth real $(2,2)$-forms $\Theta $. These numbers are finite and tend to infinity as $\delta \downarrow 0$.

Proof. For fixed $\delta $, choose a constant $M_\delta >0$ with $B_\delta \leq M_\delta \omega $; this shows that $C_\delta \leq M_\delta $. Suppose instead that $C_{\delta _ l}\leq C$ along a sequence $\delta _ l\to 0$. Then $\int _ X(C\omega -B_{\delta _ l})\wedge \Theta \geq 0$ for every admissible $\Theta $, and in particular for the squares of Gauduchon metrics. By theorem 4.3.1, there are quasi-plurisubharmonic functions $v_ l$ such that

\[ C\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ l\geq B_{\delta _ l},\qquad \sup _ Xv_ l=0. \]

By theorem 1.4.2, a subsequence converges in $L^1$ to a $C\omega $-plurisubharmonic function $v$, not identically $-\infty $. Since $\delta ^{-2}p(\zeta /\delta )\sigma _\zeta $ is an approximate identity in the variable $\zeta $,

\[ B_{\delta _ l}\rightharpoonup \chi [\zeta =0],\qquad C\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\geq \chi [\zeta =0]. \]

On a coordinate ball where $\chi =1$, add to $v$ a smooth strictly plurisubharmonic function whose complex Hessian dominates $C\omega $. The resulting plurisubharmonic function has a current that dominates $[\zeta =0]$, so its Lelong number is at least one at every point of an open subset of this hypersurface. Smooth corrections do not change Lelong numbers, so the same holds for $v$. Siu's analyticity theorem, recalled in 1.13.1, shows that $Z=\{ \nu (v,\cdot )\geq 1\} $ is an analytic subset of $X$. It is a proper subset because $v$ is not identically $-\infty $.

Let $\widetilde Z$ be the inverse image of $Z$ in $\Omega $. It contains an open subset of $H_0\cap \Omega $, where $H_0=\{ w_0=z_0\} $. This surface is a projective plane with two points removed, so it is connected and irreducible, and the identity principle gives $H_0\cap \Omega \subset \widetilde Z$. Since $\widetilde Z$ is invariant under $g$, also

\[ H_ k\cap \Omega \subset \widetilde Z,\qquad H_ k=\{ w_0=q^ kz_0\} , \qquad k\in \mathbb Z. \]

Now use the chart $z_1=1$ near the point $[0:1:1:0]\in \Omega $. There $H_ k$ is the graph $z_0=q^{-k}w_0$. For each fixed $(w_0,w_1)$ near $(1,0)$, every local holomorphic defining function of $\widetilde Z$ vanishes at the distinct values $z_0=q^{-k}w_0$, which tend to zero as $k\to +\infty $. The one-variable identity theorem makes each defining function vanish identically on a polydisk. Thus $\widetilde Z$ contains a nonempty open subset of the connected manifold $\Omega $, contradicting properness. Hence no subsequence of $C_\delta $ stays bounded as $\delta \downarrow 0$. $\square$

By the lemma we can choose $\delta _ j\downarrow 0$, each smaller than half its predecessor, and then, by the definition of the supremum, smooth strictly positive $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-closed $(2,2)$-forms $\Theta _ j$ with

26.109
\begin{equation} \int _ X\omega \wedge \Theta _ j=1,\qquad J_ j=\int _ XB_{\delta _ j}\wedge \Theta _ j>j^4. \end{equation}

26.18.2 Averaging the trace and compact curves

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

A large pairing with $B_{\delta _ j}$ means that $\Theta _ j$ has a large coefficient in one direction, but possibly only on a small set. Averaging by translations spreads this coefficient over a whole product region of size comparable to $\delta _ j$ in the vertical direction. A second average, this time of compact rational curves, fills the two complementary directions, which is needed for a lower bound on a determinant. Fix $j$ and write $\delta =\delta _ j$.

For complex parameters $a,b,\tau $, the automorphisms

\[ T_{a,b,\tau }[z_0:z_1:w_0:w_1] =[z_0:z_1+az_0:\mathrm{e}^{\tau }w_0: \mathrm{e}^{\tau }(w_1+bw_0)] \]

of $\Omega $ commute with $g$, and in the product chart they act by $(x,y,\zeta )\mapsto (x+a,y+b,\zeta +\tau )$. Average $T_{a,b,\tau }^*\Theta _ j$ against the probability density

\[ 3^{-4}p(a/3)p(b/3)(3\delta )^{-2}p(\tau /(3\delta )) \sigma _ a\wedge \sigma _ b\wedge \sigma _\tau , \]

and denote the result by $\widehat\Theta _ j$. It is smooth, strictly positive and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-closed. The parameters range in a fixed compact set of automorphisms, so $(T_{a,b,\tau }^{-1})^*\omega \leq C\omega $ with one constant $C$, and a change of variables gives

26.110
\begin{equation} \int _ X\omega \wedge \widehat\Theta _ j\leq C. \end{equation}

Let $F_ j$ be the coefficient of $\sigma _ x\wedge \sigma _ y$ in the restriction of $\Theta _ j$ to the slices $\{ \zeta =\text{constant}\} $, and let $\widehat F_ j$ be the corresponding coefficient of $\widehat\Theta _ j$. Positivity gives $F_ j\geq 0$, and since the translations preserve $\sigma _ x\wedge \sigma _ y$, $\widehat F_ j$ is an average of translates of $F_ j$. At a point of

\[ V_ j=\{ |x|,|y|<1/4,\ |\zeta |<\delta /4\} , \]

every translation carrying it into $\operatorname {supp}B_\delta $ has $|a|,|b|\leq 5/4$ and $|\tau |\leq 5\delta /4$. On this range the first two averaging factors are bounded below by a positive constant, and the third by a positive constant times $\delta ^{-2}$. On the other hand, $\chi \leq 1$ and $p$ is bounded, so the number $J_ j$ of (26.109) is at most a constant times $\delta ^{-2}$ times the integral of $F_ j$ over $\operatorname {supp}B_\delta $. Comparing the two integrals gives

26.111
\begin{equation} \widehat F_ j\geq cJ_ j\quad \hbox{on }V_ j, \end{equation}

with $c>0$ independent of $j$.

The second average fills the two horizontal directions. Put

\[ f(a,b)=p(4(a-1))p(b/2),\qquad \rho _ j(\tau )=4\delta ^{-2}p(2\tau /\delta ). \]

For parameters in the supports of these functions and $|u|\leq 1/16$, consider the holomorphic maps $\iota _{\tau ,u,a,b}:\mathbb P^1\to X$ defined by

26.112
\begin{equation} \iota _{\tau ,u,a,b}[z_0:z_1] =[z_0:z_1:\mathrm{e}^{\tau }(z_0+uz_1): \mathrm{e}^{\tau }(az_1+bz_0)]\in X. \end{equation}

For $0<\varepsilon \leq 1/16$, average their integration currents against

\[ \rho _ j(\tau )f(a,b)\varepsilon ^{-2}p(u/\varepsilon ) \sigma _\tau \wedge \sigma _ a\wedge \sigma _ b\wedge \sigma _ u, \]

and call the result $T_{j,\varepsilon }$. Define $T_{j,0}$ in the same way, but with $u=0$ and without the integral over $u$.

Lemma 26.18.3. For $\varepsilon >0$, the form $T_{j,\varepsilon }$ is smooth on $X$, semipositive and closed, and

\[ \int _ X\omega \wedge T_{j,\varepsilon }\leq C' \]

with $C'$ independent of $j$ and $\varepsilon $. For each fixed $j$, $T_{j,\varepsilon }$ converges smoothly to $T_{j,0}$ on compact subsets of the product chart as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$. In this chart

26.113
\begin{equation} T_{j,0}=\rho _ j(\zeta )\sigma _\zeta \wedge K_ f,\qquad K_ f=\int _\mathbb Cf(a,y-ax)\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\pi } (\mathrm{d}y-a\, \mathrm{d}x)\wedge (\mathrm{d}\bar y-\bar a\, \mathrm{d}\bar x)\, \sigma _ a, \end{equation}

and on $\{ |x|,|y|\leq 1/4\} $ the form $K_ f$ is bounded below by a positive multiple of $\sigma _ x+\sigma _ y$.

Proof. On the parameter supports, $|a-bu|\geq 3/4-2/16=5/8$. Hence the matrix $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1& u\\ b& a\end{smallmatrix}\right)$ is uniformly invertible, and the projective line (26.112) lies in $\Omega $. Distinct deck translates of this line are disjoint: equality at a point fixes $[z_0:z_1]$, and invertibility of the matrix then forces the deck scalar to be one. Thus each map is an embedding onto a compact rational curve in $X$.

The evaluation map from $\mathbb P^1$ times the open parameter domain to $X$ is a holomorphic submersion. Indeed, after fixing a projective gauge, variations of $u$ and $a$ span the two $w$-coordinates at points with $z_1\ne 0$, variations of $\tau $ and $b$ span them at points with $z_1=0$, and the coordinate on $\mathbb P^1$ supplies the remaining direction. For $\varepsilon >0$, the average is the integral along the fibers of this submersion of a smooth compactly supported parameter density, which proves smoothness. As an average of closed positive integration currents, $T_{j,\varepsilon }$ is closed and semipositive.

All curve parameters lie in one compact subset of the domain where the matrix is invertible, so the $\omega $-areas of the curves are uniformly bounded. The weights in $\tau $ and $u$ have mass one, and the weight in $(a,b)$ has a fixed finite mass. This proves the degree bound.

When $u=0$, the curve is given in the product chart by $\zeta =\tau $ and $y=ax+b$. Integrating in $b$ and $\tau $ yields (26.113); the $(1,1)$-factor there is the integration current of the graph of $y=ax+b$. On $\{ |x|,|y|\leq 1/4\} $, the density $f(a,y-ax)$ is bounded below by a positive constant for $a$ in a fixed small disk about $1$. The covectors $\mathrm{d}y-a\, \mathrm{d}x$, for $a$ in that disk, have no common nonzero annihilator, so integrating their squared absolute values and using compactness of the unit sphere in the $(x,y)$-directions gives the asserted lower bound.

For general $u$, the curve is given by

\[ \zeta =\tau +\log (1+ux),\qquad y=\frac{ax+b}{1+ux}. \]

On a fixed compact subset of the chart, $1+ux$ stays away from zero for all sufficiently small $u$. Solving for the parameters gives $\tau =\zeta -\log (1+ux)$ and $b=(1+ux)y-ax$. Regarding $\rho _ j$ as a smooth function on $E$, this change of variables also applies near the boundary of its support. The Jacobians, the density coefficients and any fixed number of their derivatives depend smoothly on $u$. Averaging against the approximate identity in $u$ therefore gives local $C^\infty $ convergence. This convergence is needed only for each fixed $j$; it is not uniform in $j$. $\square$

26.18.3 The volume of the positive root

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The two averages contribute complementary blocks of the coefficient matrix of a $(2,2)$-form: the translated forms give a large vertical entry, and the curves give a positive horizontal block. A Schur-complement estimate combines them into a lower bound for the volume of the positive root. In the coframe $(x,y,\zeta )$ put $\mathrm{d}V_0=\sigma _ x\wedge \sigma _ y\wedge \sigma _\zeta $. For a real $(1,1)$-form $\xi $, let $H_\xi $ be its coefficient matrix in this normalization. A real $(2,2)$-form $\Theta $ corresponds to a Hermitian matrix $Q_\Theta $, which we call its cofactor matrix, by

\[ \xi \wedge \Theta =\operatorname {tr}(H_\xi Q_\Theta )\, \mathrm{d}V_0. \]

Strict positivity of $\Theta $ is equivalent to $Q_\Theta >0$.

If $\eta ^2=\Theta $ and $H=H_\eta $, expanding the wedge products gives

\[ Q_\Theta =2(\det H)H^{-1},\qquad \eta ^3=\frac{3}{\sqrt2}(\det Q_\Theta )^{1/2}\, \mathrm{d}V_0. \]

Conversely, these formulas uniquely recover a smooth positive $H$ from $Q_\Theta >0$; this is the three-dimensional case of the root construction used in the proof of theorem 4.3.1. We write $\Theta ^{3/2}=\eta ^3$.

Lemma 26.18.4. There are smooth strictly positive $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-closed $(2,2)$-forms $\Gamma _ j$ such that

\[ \int _ X\omega \wedge \Gamma _ j\leq C_2,\qquad R_ j=\int _ X\Gamma _ j^{3/2}\longrightarrow +\infty . \]

Proof. Write the cofactor matrix of $\widehat\Theta _ j$ in block form, with a $2\times 2$ Hermitian block $A_ j$ in the $(x,y)$-directions and a column $\kappa _ j\in \mathbb C^2$:

\[ Q_ j=\begin{pmatrix} A_ j & \kappa _ j \\ \kappa _ j^* & \widehat F_ j \end{pmatrix}. \]

The cofactor matrix of $T_{j,0}$ has the form $\operatorname {diag}(B_ j,0)$. Let $I_2$ denote the $2\times 2$ identity matrix. The lower bound for $K_ f$ and the definition of $\rho _ j$ give a constant $c_0>0$ such that $B_ j\geq c_0\delta _ j^{-2}I_2$ on $V_ j$. Since $Q_ j>0$, the Schur complement $A_ j-\kappa _ j\kappa _ j^*/\widehat F_ j$ is positive definite, and

\[ \det \bigl (Q_ j+\operatorname {diag}(B_ j,0)\bigr ) =\widehat F_ j\det \left(B_ j+A_ j- \frac{\kappa _ j\kappa _ j^*}{\widehat F_ j}\right) \geq \widehat F_ j\det B_ j, \]

by monotonicity of the determinant on positive Hermitian matrices. Combining the root formula with (26.111), we obtain

\[ (\widehat\Theta _ j+T_{j,0})^{3/2} \geq c\delta _ j^{-2}\sqrt{J_ j}\, \mathrm{d}V_0 \quad \hbox{on }V_ j. \]

The smaller region $W_ j=\{ |x|,|y|<1/8,\ |\zeta |<\delta _ j/8\} $ has $\mathrm{d}V_0$-volume equal to a fixed positive constant times $\delta _ j^2$. Consequently

\[ I_ j=\int _{W_ j}(\widehat\Theta _ j+T_{j,0})^{3/2} \geq c_1\sqrt{J_ j}>c_1j^2. \]

For this fixed $j$, lemma 26.18.3 gives smooth convergence of $T_{j,\varepsilon }$ to $T_{j,0}$ on the closure of $W_ j$. The cofactor matrix of $\widehat\Theta _ j+T_{j,0}$ is positive definite there, so the integrand of the root volume depends continuously on these smooth coefficients. We can therefore choose $0<\varepsilon _ j\leq 1/16$ so small that

\[ \int _{W_ j}(\widehat\Theta _ j+T_{j,\varepsilon _ j})^{3/2} \geq I_ j/2. \]

Set $\Gamma _ j=\widehat\Theta _ j+T_{j,\varepsilon _ j}$. It is smooth, strictly positive and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-closed. Its degree is bounded by (26.110) and lemma 26.18.3, whereas its root volume is at least $c_1j^2/2$. $\square$

of theorem 26.18.1. Fix a Hermitian form $h$ on $X$, and choose $A>0$ with $h\leq A\omega $. Let $\eta _ j$ be the positive root of $\Gamma _ j$, so that $\eta _ j^2=\Gamma _ j$. These metrics are Gauduchon, and

\[ P_ j=\int _ Xh\wedge \eta _ j^2\leq AC_2,\qquad R_ j=\int _ X\eta _ j^3\longrightarrow +\infty . \]

Set $\mu _ j=\eta _ j^3/R_ j$ and solve the smooth equation in the background $h$:

\[ h_ j=h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j>0,\qquad h_ j^3=c_ h(\mu _ j)\mu _ j, \qquad \sup _ Xu_ j=0. \]

Since $D_{\mu _ j}(\eta _ j)=R_ j^{2/3}$, using $\eta _ j$ as a test in theorem 26.1.3 gives

\[ 0<\int _ Xh_ j^3=c_ h(\mu _ j)=\mathcal V_{\mu _ j}(h) \leq \frac{P_ j^3}{R_ j^2}\longrightarrow 0. \]

The potentials $u_ j$ are smooth, hence bounded, so $v_-(h)=0$. $\square$

26.18.4 Consequences for dimension and equations

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The example occurs in the smallest possible dimension: on a surface, comparison with a Gauduchon metric gives positive lower mass. Products with projective spaces carry the threefold degeneration to every higher dimension, and a product with the Hopf threefold produces, in addition, unbounded upper mass.

Corollary 26.18.5. Every Hermitian form on a compact complex manifold of dimension at most two has positive lower mass. In every dimension $n\geq 3$ there is a connected compact complex manifold on which every Hermitian form has zero lower mass.

Proof. In dimension one the mass is $\int _ Xh>0$, and the surface case is corollary 26.17.1. In higher dimensions we use the monotonicity and the homogeneity of degree $n$ of $v_-$, which follow directly from (26.89), to pass from a split metric to every Hermitian metric.

For $n=3$ this is theorem 26.18.1. For $n>3$, take the product of the threefold $X$ with $\mathbb P^{n-3}$, let $\kappa $ be a Fubini–Study form on the projective factor, and consider the split metric $h_0=\omega +\kappa $, with pullbacks understood. For the potentials $u_ j$ given by the theorem for the background $\omega $,

\[ \int _{X\times \mathbb P^{n-3}}(h_0+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ n =\binom n3\left(\int _ X(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^3\right) \left(\int _{\mathbb P^{n-3}}\kappa ^{n-3}\right) \longrightarrow 0. \]

For another Hermitian form $h$, choose $A$ with $h\leq Ah_0$. Monotonicity and homogeneity give $v_-(h)\leq A^ n v_-(h_0)=0$. $\square$

Corollary 26.18.6. In every dimension $n\geq 6$ there is a connected compact complex manifold with

\[ v_-(h)=0,\qquad v_+(h)=+\infty \]

for every Hermitian form $h$.

Proof. Take the product of the threefold above with the Hopf threefold of chapter 8, with a split metric. Potentials pulled back from the first factor make the masses tend to zero, while the smooth potentials of theorem 8.0.1, pulled back from the second factor, make them tend to infinity. In each case the product integral is $\binom 63$ times the product of the two factor masses. The metric comparison in the preceding proof handles the lower mass for every Hermitian form, and the metric independence in proposition 6.5.2 handles the upper mass. Products with projective spaces give every higher dimension by the same expansion of wedge powers. $\square$

Finally, fix a smooth positive probability measure $\mu $ on the threefold $X$. The smooth equation still gives $\mathcal V_\mu (h)>0$ for each Hermitian form $h$. With $\mathscr M$ and $\lambda _*$ as in section 26.14.1, theorem 26.14.3 gives

\[ \inf _{\nu \in \mathscr M}\mathcal V_\nu (h)=0, \qquad \lambda _*(h)=0. \]

Thus even a smooth strictly positive background can have vanishing infimum over densities. For the metrics $\eta _ j$ and numbers $R_ j,P_ j$ in the proof of theorem 26.18.1, the measures $\mu _ j=\eta _ j^3/R_ j$ satisfy $\mathcal V_{\mu _ j}(h)\leq P_ j^3/R_ j^2\to 0$. Hence the densities $\mu _ j/\mu $ cannot be uniformly bounded above: a bound $\mu _ j\leq M\mu $ would give $\mathcal V_{\mu _ j}(h)\geq M^{-1}\mathcal V_\mu (h)>0$, by the comparison of denominators in the proof of proposition 26.14.1.

The example also shows that the endpoint estimate in theorem 26.8.2 fails for the full family of normalized Gauduchon currents: the supremum of the $L^{3/2}$ norms of their determinant densities is infinite, although every subcritical $L^ q$ norm, $1<q<3/2$, remains uniformly bounded by theorem 26.8.1. For the same fixed background, theorem 26.8.5, corollary 26.8.6 give uniform $L^\infty $ bounds for the exact maximizers and their adjoint probabilities in a small $C^{2,\alpha }$ family of smooth densities. A larger sufficient family is given by theorem 26.8.9: uniformly comparable smooth solution forms yield uniform bounds for both densities, without derivative bounds on the varying Hessian. Endpoint estimates for arbitrary bounded families of continuous densities remain open.

26.18.5 A balanced metric and a finite upper mass

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Although every Hermitian form on the threefold of theorem 26.18.1 has zero lower mass, the metric $\omega $ of (26.108) is balanced and has finite upper mass. A direct calculation expresses the loss of mass under a potential as a vertical Dirichlet energy along the elliptic fibers. The same calculation shows that the degeneration cannot be realized by potentials that are the sum of a periodic function of $t$ and a function on the base: their masses stay above $8$. Put $D=\mathrm{d}t\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}t$, so that $\omega =\alpha +\beta +D$.

Proposition 26.18.7. On the threefold $X$ of theorem 26.18.1, the Hermitian form $\omega =\alpha +\beta +D$ satisfies

\[ \mathrm{d}(\omega ^2)=0,\qquad \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\omega =2\alpha \wedge \beta \geq 0, \qquad \int _ X\omega ^3=24\pi . \]

For every smooth real function $u$,

26.114
\begin{equation} \int _ X(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^3 =24\pi -6\int _ X\mathrm{d}u\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}u\wedge \alpha \wedge \beta . \end{equation}

Consequently $v_+(\omega )=24\pi $. In particular, $X$ has the bounded mass property, while $v_-(h)=0$ for every Hermitian form $h$ on $X$.

Proof. Although $t$ is defined only on the covering space $\Omega $, both $\mathrm{d}t$ and $\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}t$ descend to $X$. The Fubini–Study potentials $\log \| z\| ^2$ and $\log \| w\| ^2$ give $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}t=\beta -\alpha $. The forms $\alpha ,\beta $ are closed, and $\alpha ^2=\beta ^2=0$ since each is pulled back from a curve. As $D^2=0$, we obtain

\[ \mathrm{d}D=-\mathrm{d}t\wedge (\beta -\alpha ),\qquad \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}D=-(\beta -\alpha )^2=2\alpha \wedge \beta , \]
\[ \mathrm{d}(\omega ^2) =-2(\alpha +\beta +D)\wedge \mathrm{d}t\wedge (\beta -\alpha )=0. \]

In the last identity, the term involving $D$ contains $\mathrm{d}t$ twice, and $(\alpha +\beta )\wedge (\beta -\alpha )=0$. Thus $\omega $ is balanced, hence Gauduchon, and the formula for $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\omega $ follows from that for $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}D$.

On a fiber, write $\zeta =a+\mathrm{i}b$, where both real periods are $2\pi $. There $t=2a$ plus a constant, so

\[ D|_ E=\frac1\pi \, \mathrm{d}a\wedge \mathrm{d}b, \qquad \int _ ED=4\pi . \]

Since $\omega ^3=6\alpha \wedge \beta \wedge D$, fiber integration and the normalization of the Fubini–Study forms give $\int _ X\omega ^3=24\pi $.

The identity (26.92) for Gauduchon metrics on threefolds, with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\omega =2\alpha \wedge \beta $, gives (26.114). Its last integral is nonnegative. Thus every smooth strictly $\omega $-plurisubharmonic potential has mass at most $24\pi $, and the constant potential attains this value. Regularization of bounded potentials, as in the proof of theorem 26.14.3, gives the same supremum over bounded potentials, so $v_+(\omega )=24\pi $. The metric independence in proposition 6.5.2 then gives the bounded mass property of $X$. The assertion about the lower mass is theorem 26.18.1. $\square$

For an admissible smooth $u$, equality in the upper-mass bound holds precisely when $u$ is constant along every elliptic fiber. Indeed, $\alpha \wedge \beta $ is a positive volume form in the base directions, so the nonnegative integral in (26.114) vanishes exactly when the derivative of $u$ along the fibers vanishes. The fibers are connected, and smooth local sections then show that $u$ is the pullback of a smooth function on $\mathbb P^1\times \mathbb P^1$. For the potentials with masses tending to zero in theorem 26.18.1, the same integral instead tends to $4\pi $.

A function $f(t)$ descends to $X$ when $f$ is $4\pi $-periodic, since $t\circ g=t+4\pi $. Even after adding an arbitrary potential pulled back from the base, such functions leave a positive mass gap. The sharp gap follows from the bounds on the first two derivatives of $f$ imposed by positivity.

Proposition 26.18.8. Let $\pi :X\to B=\mathbb P^1\times \mathbb P^1$ be the elliptic fibration above, and put $L=4\pi $. Consider the smooth potentials

\[ u=f(t)+\pi ^*v,\qquad \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u>0, \]

where $f:\mathbb R\to \mathbb R$ is smooth and $L$-periodic and $v\in C^\infty (B,\mathbb R)$. The infimum of $\int _ X(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^3$ over these potentials is $8$. Every such mass is strictly greater than $8$, and the infimum is approached already with $v=0$.

Proof. Write $p=f'$, an $L$-periodic function with mean zero. The chain rule gives

\[ \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u =(1-p)\alpha +(1+p)\beta +\pi ^*\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v+(1+p')D. \]

Positivity on the vertical line implies $p'>-1$. On the complex plane $\ker \partial t$, the form $D$ vanishes and the projection to the base is an isomorphism. For each value of $t$ modulo $L$, every base point has a point above it with that value. Hence, writing $\alpha _ B,\beta _ B$ for the two Fubini–Study forms on $B$, the form $(1-p(s))\alpha _ B+(1+p(s))\beta _ B+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ is positive on $B$ for every $s$. Integration over the two rulings gives $1-p>0$ and $1+p>0$. Thus $|p|<1$ and $p'>-1$; when $v=0$, these inequalities also suffice for strict positivity.

In a logarithmic fiber coordinate $x+\mathrm{i}y$, both real periods are $2\pi $, $t=2x$ plus a constant, and $D|_ E=\pi ^{-1}\mathrm{d}x\wedge \mathrm{d}y$. Hence $\int _ E F(t)D=\int _0^ L F(s)\, \mathrm{d}s$ for every $L$-periodic function $F$. Only the derivative of $u$ along the fibers contributes to (26.114), and it equals $p(t)$ times the derivative of $t$. Fiber integration therefore gives

26.115
\begin{equation} \int _ X(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^3 =6\left(L-\int _0^ L p(s)^2\, \mathrm{d}s\right). \end{equation}

We now bound $\int _0^ Lp^2$. Suppose first that $p\not\equiv 0$, and put $m_+=\max p>0$, $m_-=-\min p>0$ and $r=m_++m_-<2$. The function $G(s)=(m_+-s)(s+m_-)$ is nonnegative on $[-m_-,m_+]$. Choose an interval $I$ of length at most $L$ that runs from a maximum point of $p$ forward to the next minimum point. Since $p'\geq -1$, we have $G(p)\geq -G(p)p'$. Using that $p$ has mean zero, we obtain

\[ \begin{split} m_+m_-L-\int _0^ L p^2\, \mathrm{d}s & =\int _0^ L G(p)\, \mathrm{d}s \geq -\int _ I G(p)p'\, \mathrm{d}s\\ & =\int _{-m_-}^{m_+}G(s)\, \mathrm{d}s=\frac{r^3}{6}. \end{split} \]

As $m_+m_-\leq r^2/4$, it follows that

\[ \int _0^ L p^2\, \mathrm{d}s \leq \frac{Lr^2}{4}-\frac{r^3}{6}<L-\frac43, \]

where the last inequality holds because the middle expression is strictly increasing in $r\in (0,2]$, as $L>2$. The inequality $\int _0^ Lp^2<L-4/3$ holds trivially when $p=0$. Equation (26.115) now gives the strict lower bound $8$.

To prove sharpness, put $a=(L-2)/2$ and define an $L$-periodic function $p_0$ by

\[ p_0(s)= \begin{cases} 1,& 0\leq s<a,\\ 1-(s-a),& a\leq s<a+2,\\ -1,& a+2\leq s<L. \end{cases} \]

It has mean zero, $|p_0|\leq 1$ and $\int _0^ L p_0^2\, \mathrm{d}s=L-4/3$. Its distributional derivative is $-1$ on the descending interval and zero on the two constant intervals, and it has a positive atom where the period closes up. Thus $p_0'\geq -1$ as distributions on the circle. Let $\rho _\varepsilon $ be a nonnegative smooth periodic approximate identity of integral one, and set $p_\varepsilon =(1-\varepsilon )(p_0*\rho _\varepsilon )$ for $0<\varepsilon <1$. Then $p_\varepsilon $ is smooth, has mean zero, and

\[ |p_\varepsilon |\leq 1-\varepsilon , \qquad p_\varepsilon '\geq -1+\varepsilon , \qquad p_\varepsilon \longrightarrow p_0\quad \hbox{in }L^2. \]

Its primitive $f_\varepsilon $ is smooth and periodic, so $u_\varepsilon =f_\varepsilon (t)$ is admissible. By (26.115), its mass tends to $8$. $\square$

Proposition 26.18.9. The threefold $X$ admits neither a pluriclosed Hermitian metric nor a closed Kähler current.

Proof. By proposition 26.18.7, the nonzero smooth semipositive $(2,2)$-form $\alpha \wedge \beta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\omega /2)$ is $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-exact. If a Hermitian metric $\eta $ satisfied $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\eta =0$, Stokes' theorem would give

\[ 0<\int _ X\eta \wedge \alpha \wedge \beta =\frac12\int _ X\eta \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\omega =\frac12\int _ X\omega \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\eta =0, \]

a contradiction. The first inequality holds because $\eta $ is positive in the vertical direction at every point.

Similarly, if a closed positive current $T$ satisfied $T\geq \varepsilon \omega $ for some $\varepsilon >0$, positivity and Stokes' theorem for currents would give

\[ 0<\varepsilon \int _ X\omega \wedge \alpha \wedge \beta \leq \int _ XT\wedge \alpha \wedge \beta =\frac12\int _ X\omega \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}T=0. \]

Hence there is no closed Kähler current. $\square$

Since a compact complex manifold carries a closed Kähler current exactly when it belongs to Fujiki's class $\mathcal C$, as recalled in section 33.3, the threefold $X$ lies outside this class. The example shows that a balanced metric does not guarantee positive lower mass, even together with bounded upper mass. The Hopf threefold, in contrast, has positive lower mass and infinite upper mass, so the two properties are logically independent, and the products in corollary 26.18.6 show that both can fail at once. In particular, in a theorem asserting solvability for every nonzero finite non-pluripolar measure, bounded mass alone cannot replace the hypothesis of positive lower mass.

26.19 Orders of degeneration at the nef boundary

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

If $\theta $ is nef and $h$ is Hermitian, the volumes $\mathcal V_\mu (\theta +\epsilon h)$ are positive and, by theorem 26.10.1, decrease to $\mathcal V_\mu (\theta )$ as $\epsilon \downarrow 0$. When this limit is zero, the rate of decay measures how degenerate $\theta $ is at the nef boundary. For a closed nef class on a compact Kähler manifold, choosing $h$ Kähler makes the volume of the perturbation the intersection polynomial $\int _ X(\theta +\epsilon h)^ n$, so it decays like an integral power of $\epsilon $ determined by the numerical dimension. For a nonclosed nef background the logarithmic rate of decay can be fractional, and it need not converge. We therefore define lower and upper orders of degeneration as lower and upper limits of this rate. Changing the probability measure or the perturbing metric only introduces bounded factors, so the orders are invariants of the background.

Let $X$ again be a connected compact complex manifold of dimension $n\geq 1$, and $\mu $ a smooth strictly positive probability form on $X$. Let $\theta $ be nef and let $h$ be Hermitian. For $0<\epsilon <1$, put

\[ a_{\mu ,h}(\theta ;\epsilon ) =\frac{\log \mathcal V_\mu (\theta +\epsilon h)}{\log \epsilon }. \]

The logarithm is finite because $\theta +\epsilon h$ belongs to $\mathscr P$, so that its volume is positive.

Proposition 26.19.1. The limits

\[ \underline a(\theta )=\liminf _{\epsilon \downarrow 0} a_{\mu ,h}(\theta ;\epsilon ), \qquad \overline a(\theta )=\limsup _{\epsilon \downarrow 0} a_{\mu ,h}(\theta ;\epsilon ) \]

are independent of $\mu $ and $h$, and satisfy

\[ 0\leq \underline a(\theta )\leq \overline a(\theta )\leq n. \]

They are unchanged when $\theta $ is replaced by $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f$, with $f$ smooth, or by $r\theta $, with $r>0$. If $\mathcal V_\mu (\theta )>0$, both limits are zero.

Proof. Since $\theta $ is pseudoeffective, monotonicity and homogeneity give

\[ \epsilon \lambda _\mu (h) \leq \lambda _\mu (\theta +\epsilon h) \leq \lambda _\mu (\theta +h), \qquad 0<\epsilon \leq 1. \]

Thus there are constants $c,C>0$ such that

\[ c\epsilon ^ n\leq \mathcal V_\mu (\theta +\epsilon h)\leq C, \qquad 0<\epsilon \leq 1. \]

Taking logarithms and dividing by the negative number $\log \epsilon $ shows that the quotients $a_{\mu ,h}(\theta ;\epsilon )$ stay bounded as $\epsilon \downarrow 0$, and that their lower and upper limits lie in $[0,n]$.

By proposition 26.14.1, changing the smooth probability measure multiplies the volume by a factor between two fixed positive constants. The resulting error in the logarithm is bounded, and after division by $\log \epsilon $ it tends to zero.

For another Hermitian metric $h'$, choose constants $c_2\geq c_1>0$ with $c_1h\leq h'\leq c_2h$. Then

\[ \mathcal V_\mu (\theta +c_1\epsilon h) \leq \mathcal V_\mu (\theta +\epsilon h') \leq \mathcal V_\mu (\theta +c_2\epsilon h). \]

For any fixed $t>0$ and small $\epsilon $,

\[ \frac{\log \mathcal V_\mu (\theta +t\epsilon h)}{\log \epsilon } =a_{\mu ,h}(\theta ;t\epsilon ) \frac{\log (t\epsilon )}{\log \epsilon }. \]

The last factor tends to one and the first is bounded, while the rescaling $\epsilon \mapsto t\epsilon $ does not change lower and upper limits as $\epsilon \downarrow 0$. Hence the left side has the same lower and upper limits as $a_{\mu ,h}(\theta ;\epsilon )$. Dividing the preceding inequalities by $\log \epsilon <0$ reverses them, and taking lower and upper limits proves independence of the metric.

Invariance under smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ changes follows from that of $\mathcal V_\mu $. For scaling, homogeneity gives

\[ \mathcal V_\mu (r\theta +\epsilon h) =r^ n\mathcal V_\mu (\theta +(\epsilon /r)h). \]

The bounded error $n\log r$ and the same rescaling argument prove scale invariance. Finally, if $\mathcal V_\mu (\theta )>0$, then by theorem 26.10.1 the perturbed volumes lie between $\mathcal V_\mu (\theta )$ and $\mathcal V_\mu (\theta +h)$ for $0<\epsilon \leq 1$, so both limits are zero. $\square$

We call $\underline a(\theta )$ and $\overline a(\theta )$ the lower and upper orders of degeneration of the nef background $\theta $. When they agree, their common value is the common order, and we then also say that $\theta $ has this order. The two orders can differ, and a common order can be irrational. Even when both are zero, the volume can tend to zero, at a logarithmic rate. The elliptic examples in section 26.19.1 exhibit each of these behaviors. Closed nef classes on Kähler manifolds, in contrast, degenerate polynomially.

Proposition 26.19.2. Suppose $X$ is Kähler, $\theta $ is closed and nef, and $h$ is Kähler. Set

\[ r=\max \left\{ 0\leq k\leq n: \int _ X\theta ^ k\wedge h^{n-k}>0\right\} . \]

Then

\[ \underline a(\theta )=\overline a(\theta )=n-r. \]

Thus $n$ minus the common order is the numerical dimension of the closed nef class of $\theta $.

Proof. All the intersection numbers in the definition of $r$ are nonnegative. Indeed, the mixed integrals against $h$ of smooth positive representatives of $\theta +\delta h$ are positive, agree with the cohomological integrals by closed Stokes, and converge to the stated numbers as $\delta \downarrow 0$. The number for $k=0$ is $\int _ Xh^ n>0$, so $r$ is well defined.

By the closed nef formula following theorem 26.10.1,

\[ \mathcal V_\mu (\theta +\epsilon h) =\int _ X(\theta +\epsilon h)^ n =\sum _{k=0}^ n\binom nk\epsilon ^{n-k} \int _ X\theta ^ k\wedge h^{n-k}. \]

As $\epsilon \downarrow 0$, the leading nonzero term is a positive constant times $\epsilon ^{n-r}$, and taking the logarithmic quotient proves the assertion. By proposition 26.19.1, the result holds for every Hermitian perturbation, not only for Kähler ones. $\square$

The orders can also be computed on the non-Kähler Hopf threefold $X$ of chapter 8. Keep its forms $\omega =\alpha +\beta $, where $\alpha \geq 0$ is closed of rank two, $\beta \geq 0$ has rank one, $\alpha ^3=\beta ^2=0$, and $\int _ X\omega ^3=6$. Take $\theta =\alpha $, $h=\omega $, and $\mu _0=\omega ^3/6$. The metric

\[ \Omega _\epsilon =\alpha +\epsilon \omega =(1+\epsilon )\alpha +\epsilon \beta \]

is strictly positive. Its volume is

\[ \Omega _\epsilon ^3=\epsilon (1+\epsilon )^2\omega ^3 =6\epsilon (1+\epsilon )^2\mu _0. \]

Hence the zero potential solves the smooth normalized equation, and

\[ \mathcal V_{\mu _0}(\alpha +\epsilon \omega ) =6\epsilon (1+\epsilon )^2, \qquad \underline a(\alpha )=\overline a(\alpha )=1. \]

By proposition 26.19.1, the same orders are obtained for every smooth positive probability measure and every Hermitian perturbation.

Each perturbed metric nevertheless has infinite upper mass. If $V_ j$ are the smooth potentials of theorem 8.0.1, then

\[ \Omega _\epsilon +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\epsilon V_ j) =\alpha +\epsilon (\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V_ j)>0, \]

and monotonicity of the determinant of positive matrices gives

\[ \int _ X\bigl (\Omega _\epsilon +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\epsilon V_ j)\bigr )^3 \geq \epsilon ^3\int _ X(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V_ j)^3\longrightarrow +\infty . \]

Thus a finite order of degeneration at the boundary coexists with infinite upper mass at every positive perturbation.

26.19.1 Elliptic surface volumes and order spectra

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

On a product of two elliptic curves, a semipositive background whose coefficients depend on one factor only has an explicit volume. Solving the smooth equation reduces to a Poisson equation on that factor, and the volume of each Hermitian perturbation is governed by the harmonic mean of the perturbed vertical coefficient. The rate at which this coefficient vanishes therefore determines the order of degeneration. Zeros of finite order give fractional orders or logarithmic decay, while coefficients that vanish to infinite order can make the logarithmic rate oscillate.

Let $E=\mathbb C/(\mathbb Z+\mathrm{i}\mathbb Z)$, write $X=E_ z\times E_ w$, and fix

\[ \omega _1=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\mathrm{d} z\wedge \mathrm{d}\bar z,\qquad \omega _2=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\mathrm{d} w\wedge \mathrm{d}\bar w,\qquad \mu =\omega _1\wedge \omega _2,\qquad h=\omega _1+\omega _2. \]

Both area forms have integral one. Let $a,q,r$ be smooth functions of $z$ alone, with $a,q$ real and nonnegative, $r$ complex-valued, and $|r|^2\leq aq$. Set

\[ \theta =\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\bigl (a\, \mathrm{d} z\wedge \mathrm{d}\bar z+ r\, \mathrm{d} z\wedge \mathrm{d}\bar w+\bar r\, \mathrm{d} w\wedge \mathrm{d}\bar z+ q\, \mathrm{d} w\wedge \mathrm{d}\bar w\bigr ). \]

This is a smooth semipositive form, and in particular it is nef.

Proposition 26.19.3. For $\epsilon >0$, put $g=q+\epsilon $ and $I=\int _ Eg^{-1}\omega _1$. Then

26.121
\begin{equation} \mathcal V_\mu (\theta +\epsilon h) =\frac{2}{I}\left(\int _ E(a+\epsilon )\omega _1- \int _ E\frac{|r|^2}{g}\omega _1\right), \end{equation}

and the infimum defining this volume is attained by a smooth positive Gauduchon metric. In particular, if $a$ is a constant $b>0$ and $r=0$, then $\mathcal V_\mu (\theta +\epsilon h)=2(b+\epsilon )/I$. If $|r|^2=aq$, then

26.122
\begin{equation} \mathcal V_\mu (\theta +\epsilon h)=\frac{2\epsilon (1+K)}{I}, \qquad K=\int _ E\frac{a}{q+\epsilon }\omega _1. \end{equation}

Proof. Define

\[ c=I^{-1}\left(\int _ E(a+\epsilon )\omega _1- \int _ E\frac{|r|^2}{g}\omega _1\right),\qquad A=\frac{c+|r|^2}{g}. \]

Since $(a+\epsilon )g-|r|^2>0$, we have $c>0$, and the definition of $c$ makes $A-a-\epsilon $ have zero mean with respect to $\omega _1$. Dividing its nonconstant Fourier coefficients by the corresponding nonzero eigenvalues of the flat Laplacian produces a smooth real function $u$ on $E$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=(A-a-\epsilon )\omega _1$. Pull it back to $X$. The form $\Omega =\theta +\epsilon h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ has coefficient matrix $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A& r\\ \bar r& g\end{smallmatrix}\right)$, which has determinant $c$ and positive diagonal entries. Hence $\Omega >0$ and $\Omega ^2=2c\mu $, and theorem 26.1.3 gives (26.121).

The metric $\gamma _*=\Omega /g$ has constant coefficient $1$ in the fiber direction, and its remaining coefficients depend only on $z$. Applying $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ to its base term or its mixed terms produces a repeated $\mathrm{d} z$ or $\mathrm{d}\bar z$, so $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\gamma _*=0$. Thus $\gamma _*$ is a smooth Gauduchon metric conformal to $\Omega $, and by theorem 26.1.3 it attains the infimum. The two special cases follow by direct substitution. $\square$

Proposition 26.19.4. For each integer $m>1$ there is a smooth semipositive nonclosed form on $X$ with common order $1-1/m$. For each $b>0$ there is also one with

\[ \mathcal V_\mu (\theta +\epsilon h)\sim \frac{2b}{\pi \log (1/\epsilon )} \quad (\epsilon \downarrow 0); \]

it has volume zero and both orders zero.

Proof. In proposition 26.19.3 take $a=b>0$ and $r=0$. Fix a point $z_0\in E$ and a flat coordinate $\zeta =z-z_0$ on a small disk about $z_0$. Choose $q$ smooth, positive outside $z_0$, and equal to $|\zeta |^{2m}$ on that disk; a positive smooth extension exists away from the disk. For $m=1$, polar coordinates give

\[ I=\int _ E\frac{\omega _1}{q+\epsilon } =\pi \log (1/\epsilon )+O(1), \]

and (26.121) gives the asymptotic formula of the statement. If $m>1$, the substitution $s=|\zeta |^2=\epsilon ^{1/m}t$ gives

\[ I\sim C_ m\epsilon ^{1/m-1},\qquad C_ m=\pi \int _0^\infty \frac{\mathrm{d} t}{1+t^ m}>0, \]

so the common order is $1-1/m$. In the logarithmic case, $\lambda _\mu (\theta )\geq 0$ by semipositivity, while $\lambda _\mu (\theta )\leq \lambda _\mu (\theta +\epsilon h)\to 0$ by monotonicity; hence $\mathcal V_\mu (\theta )=0$. Moreover, the quotient of the volume by $\epsilon ^\delta $ diverges for every $\delta >0$, so both orders vanish. Finally, $\mathrm{d}\theta =\mathrm{d} q\wedge \omega _2\ne 0$, so these backgrounds are not closed. $\square$

Proposition 26.19.5. For every $0<\alpha <\beta <1$ there is a smooth semipositive nonclosed background on $X$, with the same $\mu $ and $h$, such that

\[ \bigl (\underline a(\theta ),\overline a(\theta )\bigr ) =(1+\alpha ,1+\beta ). \]

For every $0<\sigma <1$ there is such a background with common order $1+\sigma $.

Proof. We use the rank-one formula (26.122), with coefficients that vanish to infinite order at one point at a prescribed oscillating rate.

Step 1: the coefficients. For all sufficiently large $t$, define

\[ p(t)=\frac{\alpha +\beta }{2} +\frac{\beta -\alpha }{2}\sin (\log \log t),\qquad P(t)=tp(t). \]

Choose $T$ so large that $0<c_0\leq P'(t)\leq d_0<1$ for $t\geq T$; this is possible because

\[ P'(t)=p(t)+\frac{\beta -\alpha }{2\log t}\cos (\log \log t) \]

and $\alpha \leq p\leq \beta $. Fix $z_0\in E$ and a flat coordinate $\zeta $ centered at $z_0$. On $E\setminus \{ z_0\} $ choose a smooth function $t(z)\geq T$ that equals $|\zeta |^{-2}$ near $z_0$ and is bounded outside every neighborhood of $z_0$. Put

\[ q=\mathrm{e}^{-t},\qquad a=\mathrm{e}^{-P(t)},\qquad r=\mathrm{e}^{-(P(t)+t)/2}, \]

and set all three equal to zero at $z_0$. These functions are smooth and vanish to infinite order at $z_0$: we have $P(t)\geq \alpha t$, and every fixed derivative of $P$ grows at most polynomially in $t$, so by the chain rule each coordinate derivative is bounded by a polynomial in $|\zeta |^{-1}$ times $\mathrm{e}^{-c/|\zeta |^2}$ for some $c>0$. Since $|r|^2=aq$, the resulting $\theta $ is semipositive. It is not closed: the component $\mathrm{d} q\wedge \omega _2$ of $\mathrm{d}\theta $ contains both fiber differentials, and derivatives of the remaining terms cannot cancel it.

Step 2: asymptotics of $I$ and $K$. Write $\epsilon =\mathrm{e}^{-L}$, and let $T_0\geq T$ be such that $t=|\zeta |^{-2}$ on the disk $\{ |\zeta |^2<1/T_0\} $. Outside this disk the integrands of $I$ and $K$ in (26.122) are bounded, so their contributions are $O(1)$, while polar coordinates on the disk give

\[ I_{\mathrm{loc}}=\pi \int _{T_0}^\infty \frac{t^{-2}\, \mathrm{d} t}{\mathrm{e}^{-t}+\mathrm{e}^{-L}},\qquad K_{\mathrm{loc}}=\pi \int _{T_0}^\infty \frac{\mathrm{e}^{-P(t)}t^{-2}\, \mathrm{d} t}{\mathrm{e}^{-t}+\mathrm{e}^{-L}}. \]

The part of $I_{\mathrm{loc}}$ over $t\geq L$ is at least a fixed multiple of $\mathrm{e}^ L/L$. For an upper bound, use $(\mathrm{e}^{-t}+\mathrm{e}^{-L})^{-1}\leq \mathrm{e}^ t$ for $t\leq L$ and $(\mathrm{e}^{-t}+\mathrm{e}^{-L})^{-1}\leq \mathrm{e}^ L$ for $t\geq L$, and split $[T_0,L]$ at $L/2$. Thus

26.123
\begin{equation} \log I=L-\log L+O(1). \end{equation}

The derivatives of $P$ and of $t-P(t)$ are positive and bounded away from zero. Splitting the integral for $K$ at $t=L$ therefore gives $K\leq C\mathrm{e}^{L-P(L)}$. On $[L,L+1]$ the denominator is at most $2\mathrm{e}^{-L}$ and $P(t)\leq P(L)+1$, so

\[ c_1L^{-2}\mathrm{e}^{L-P(L)}\leq K\leq c_2\mathrm{e}^{L-P(L)}; \]

the complementary $O(1)$ term is absorbed because $P(L)\leq \beta L$. Consequently $\log K=L-P(L)+O(\log L)$, and $K\to \infty $.

Step 3: the orders. Combining these estimates with (26.122) and (26.123) yields

26.124
\begin{equation} \log \mathcal V_\mu (\theta +\mathrm{e}^{-L}h)=-L-P(L)+O(\log L), \qquad \frac{\log \mathcal V_\mu (\theta +\epsilon h)}{\log \epsilon } =1+p(L)+o(1). \end{equation}

Since $\sin (\log \log L)$ oscillates between $-1$ and $1$, the lower and upper limits of $p(L)$ are $\alpha $ and $\beta $, which proves the first assertion. If instead $P(t)=\sigma t$, the same smoothness and integral estimates give the common order $1+\sigma $. In particular, $\sigma =\sqrt2-1$ gives an irrational common order. $\square$

26.19.2 Comparison under an arbitrary modification

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The orders of degeneration are defined on a fixed manifold. We now show that pulling a nef background back by a modification can only increase them. Recall that a modification $\pi :Y\to X$ is a proper surjective holomorphic map that restricts to a biholomorphism between dense Zariski open subsets. The proof compares Gauduchon tests on $X$ with tests on $Y$. The pullback of a Gauduchon test is degenerate along the exceptional locus, and adding a small positive test on $Y$ repairs this. To control the effect of the added test uniformly, we first bound the pairing of pulled-back tests with a Hermitian form on $Y$ by their pairing with a Hermitian form on $X$, uniformly over the whole cone of tests.

In this subsection $\pi :Y\to X$ is a modification of connected compact complex manifolds of dimension $n\geq 2$. We use Hironaka's analytic elimination of indeterminacies in the following form: there are a finite composition $p:Z\to X$ of blowups along smooth centers and a modification $F:Z\to Y$ with $p=\pi \circ F$. It is obtained by resolving the indeterminacies of the inverse meromorphic map $\pi ^{-1}\circ p$; the identity $p=\pi \circ F$ holds on the dense set where the maps are biholomorphic, hence everywhere. This classical input is discussed in section 34.15. For an effective real divisor $D$, we write $\eta _ D$ for the curvature form of a smooth Hermitian metric on the corresponding real line bundle, as in section 26.21.

Lemma 26.19.6. Let $p:Z\to X$ be a finite composition of blowups along smooth centers of codimension at least two, and let $h_ X$ be a Hermitian form on $X$. There are a constant $C_0>0$, an effective real divisor $D$ on $Z$ whose components are exceptional for $p$, and a smooth curvature form $\eta _ D$ such that

26.125
\begin{equation} h_ Z=C_0p^*h_ X-\eta _ D>0. \end{equation}

Every component of $D$ is mapped by $p$ into a set of complex codimension at least two in $X$.

Proof. First let $q:M'\to M$ be one blowup along a smooth center, with exceptional divisor $E$, and let $h_ M$ be a Hermitian form on $M$. The restriction of $\mathcal O(-E)$ to each projective fiber of $E$ is $\mathcal O(1)$. A Hermitian metric on the normal bundle of the center therefore induces a metric on $\mathcal O(-E)|_ E$ whose curvature is positive on the tangent spaces of the fibers. Extend this metric to $\mathcal O(-E)$ on $M'$ by extending the smooth logarithm of its ratio to a fixed reference metric from the closed submanifold $E$, and let $\beta $ be its curvature. The semipositive form $q^*h_ M$ is positive off $E$, and at points of $E$ its kernel is exactly the tangent space of the fiber, on which $\beta $ is positive. Hence $Cq^*h_ M+\beta >0$ for every sufficiently large $C$: otherwise, unit tangent vectors violating the inequality for $C\to \infty $ would converge, by compactness, to a vector in the kernel of $q^*h_ M$ on which $\beta $ is nonpositive. With $\eta _ E=-\beta $, the curvature of the induced metric on $\mathcal O(E)$, this reads $Cq^*h_ M-\eta _ E>0$.

For a composition of blowups, apply this step successively, with $h_ M$ the positive form already obtained on the previous blowup. Each step multiplies the previous divisor by a positive constant, pulls it back and adds the new exceptional divisor, so the divisor stays effective and exceptional. This proves (26.125). Each exceptional divisor maps into the image of its center, which has dimension at most $n-2$. $\square$

Lemma 26.19.7. Fix Hermitian forms $h_ X,h_ Y$ for a modification $\pi :Y\to X$. There is a constant $K>0$ such that

26.126
\begin{equation} \int _ Y h_ Y\wedge \pi ^*\Psi \leq K\int _ X h_ X\wedge \Psi \end{equation}

for every smooth strictly positive $(n-1,n-1)$-form $\Psi $ on $X$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Psi =0$.

Proof. Use the factorization $p=\pi \circ F$ above and lemma 26.19.6. By compactness there is $a>0$ with $h_ Z\geq aF^*h_ Y$. Pulling back the semipositive form $\Psi $ and integrating, we obtain

\[ a\int _ Yh_ Y\wedge \pi ^*\Psi \leq \int _ Zh_ Z\wedge p^*\Psi =C_0\int _ Xh_ X\wedge \Psi -\int _ Dp^*\Psi . \]

Here the changes of variables use that $F$ and $p$ have degree one, and the Poincaré–Lelong formula, together with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p^*\Psi =0$, replaces the pairing with $\eta _ D$ by the integral over $D$. This last integral vanishes: on the regular locus of each component of $D$, the restriction of $p$ has rank at most $n-2$, so the restriction of the $(n-1,n-1)$-form $p^*\Psi $ is zero. Taking $K=C_0/a$ proves the assertion. $\square$

Proposition 26.19.8. Let $\pi :Y\to X$ be a modification of connected compact complex manifolds of dimension $n\geq 2$, and let $\theta $ be a smooth nef background on $X$. Then $\pi ^*\theta $ is nef. For fixed Hermitian forms $h_ X,h_ Y$ and smooth positive probability forms $\mu _ X,\mu _ Y$, there are constants $C,K>0$, independent of $\epsilon $, such that

26.127
\begin{equation} \mathcal V_{\mu _ Y}(\pi ^*\theta +\epsilon h_ Y) \leq C\mathcal V_{\mu _ X}(\theta +K\epsilon h_ X) \qquad (\epsilon >0). \end{equation}

Consequently

\[ \underline a(\pi ^*\theta )\geq \underline a(\theta ),\qquad \overline a(\pi ^*\theta )\geq \overline a(\theta ). \]

In dimension one a modification is an isomorphism and preserves both orders.

Proof. Step 1: nefness. Choose $C'$ with $\pi ^*h_ X\leq C'h_ Y$. For $\epsilon >0$, pull back a smooth representative $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\tau \geq -\tau h_ X$ with $\tau <\epsilon /C'$; this gives a positive representative of $\pi ^*\theta +\epsilon h_ Y$. Thus $\pi ^*\theta $ is nef, and both volumes in (26.127) are positive.

Step 2: regularized tests. The ratio $\pi ^*\mu _ X/\mu _ Y$ is smooth, nonnegative and bounded on the compact manifold $Y$, so there is $c>0$ with $\mu _ Y\geq c\pi ^*\mu _ X$. Fix a smooth Gauduchon metric $\gamma _0$ on $Y$, given by theorem 4.1.2. For an arbitrary Gauduchon metric $\gamma $ on $X$, write $\Psi =\gamma ^{n-1}$, and for $\delta >0$ define $\Gamma _\delta $ by

\[ \Gamma _\delta ^{n-1}=\pi ^*\Psi +\delta \gamma _0^{n-1}. \]

The right side is a strictly positive $(n-1,n-1)$-form, and such a form has a unique smooth positive $(n-1)$st root, obtained by solving for the Hermitian matrix of the root from the positive cofactor matrix. Since the right side is $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-closed, $\Gamma _\delta $ is a smooth Gauduchon metric on $Y$.

Step 3: the numerator. For $\epsilon >0$, lemma 26.19.7 gives

26.128
\begin{equation} \lim _{\delta \downarrow 0}\int _ Y(\pi ^*\theta +\epsilon h_ Y) \wedge \Gamma _\delta ^{n-1} =\int _ X\theta \wedge \Psi +\epsilon \int _ Yh_ Y\wedge \pi ^*\Psi \leq \int _ X(\theta +K\epsilon h_ X)\wedge \Psi . \end{equation}

The first term in the middle is nonnegative because $\theta $ is nef, and the second is positive because $\pi ^*\Psi $ is positive on the set where $\pi $ is biholomorphic, so the limit is positive.

Step 4: the denominator. We claim that

26.129
\begin{equation} \lim _{\delta \downarrow 0}D_{\mu _ Y}(\Gamma _\delta ) =\int _ Y(\pi ^*\gamma ^ n)^{(n-1)/n}\mu _ Y^{1/n} \geq c^{1/n}D_{\mu _ X}(\gamma )>0. \end{equation}

To justify the limit on the exceptional locus, represent $\Gamma _\delta ^{n-1}$ by its positive cofactor matrix. The density of $(\Gamma _\delta ^ n)^{(n-1)/n}$ is a fixed dimensional constant times the $1/n$th power of the determinant of that matrix, and this expression extends continuously to positive semidefinite matrices. Since $Y$ is compact, dominated convergence applies. The inequality follows from $\mu _ Y\geq c\pi ^*\mu _ X$ and the change of variables for the degree-one map $\pi $.

Step 5: conclusion. Test the infimum defining $\lambda _{\mu _ Y}(\pi ^*\theta +\epsilon h_ Y)$ with $\Gamma _\delta $, use (26.128) and (26.129), and let $\delta \downarrow 0$. For every Gauduchon metric $\gamma $ on $X$ this gives

\[ \lambda _{\mu _ Y}(\pi ^*\theta +\epsilon h_ Y) \leq c^{-1/n} \frac{\int _ X(\theta +K\epsilon h_ X)\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}}{D_{\mu _ X}(\gamma )}. \]

Taking the infimum over $\gamma $ and then the $n$th power proves (26.127) with $C=c^{-1}$.

Dividing the logarithm of (26.127) by $\log \epsilon <0$ reverses the inequality. The bounded error $\log C$ and the rescaling $\epsilon \mapsto K\epsilon $ do not affect lower and upper limits, as in the proof of proposition 26.19.1. This gives $\underline a(\pi ^*\theta )\geq \underline a(\theta )$ and $\overline a(\pi ^*\theta )\geq \overline a(\theta )$. Finally, a proper holomorphic map of degree one between compact Riemann surfaces is an isomorphism, which settles the case $n=1$. $\square$

26.19.3 Strict changes under one blowup

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The inequalities of proposition 26.19.8 can be strict. A blowup changes how much smooth volume lies near the zero set of a coefficient. Near a point of $\mathbb P^ m$, the Fubini–Study volume of the ball $\{ |z|^2<s\} $ is of order $s^ m$; after the point is blown up, the corresponding neighborhood of the exceptional divisor has volume of order $s$ for a Kähler form upstairs. For product backgrounds whose fiber coefficient vanishes at the blown-up point, the smooth equation can be solved explicitly, and this change in the distribution of volume produces a strict increase of the order.

Throughout this subsection let $B=\mathbb P^ m$ with $m\geq 2$, and let $H$ be the Fubini–Study form normalized by $\int _ BH^ m=1$. Let $\pi :\widetilde B\to B$ be the blowup of the origin of the affine chart $\{ Z_0\neq 0\} $. The projection $\rho :\widetilde B\to \mathbb P^{m-1}$ from the incidence description gives the form $L=\rho ^*\omega _{\rm FS}$, with $\omega _{\rm FS}$ normalized in the same way; we also write $H$ for $\pi ^*H$. The incidence embedding of $\widetilde B$ in $\mathbb P^ m\times \mathbb P^{m-1}$ makes $\kappa =H+L$ a Kähler form.

Proposition 26.19.9. For all integers $m\geq 2$ and $p,k\geq 1$, there are a single blowup, along a smooth center, of a projective manifold of dimension $m+p$ and a smooth semipositive nonclosed background on it such that the background and its pullback both have common orders, namely

\[ a_{\rm down}=\max \{ p-m/k,0\} ,\qquad a_{\rm up}=p-1/k. \]

Every positive integer occurs as the increase $a_{\rm up}-a_{\rm down}$, for a suitable dimension. In the same family, a background of positive volume can pull back to a background of volume zero, with both orders equal to zero.

Proof. Step 1: the backgrounds. Put $W_0=\mathbb P^ p$, with Fubini–Study form $\omega $ normalized by $\int _{W_0}\omega ^ p=1$. Write $s=|z|^2$ on the affine chart of $B$. Choose a smooth function $Q(s)\geq 0$ that equals $s^ k$ near zero, is positive for $s>0$, and is constant for large $s$; it defines a smooth function $q$ on $B$. Put $n=m+p$, choose $b>0$, and define

\[ \theta =bH+q\omega \quad \text{on }B\times W_0,\qquad \widetilde\theta =bH+(q\circ \pi )\omega \quad \text{on }\widetilde B\times W_0. \]

The map $\Pi =\pi \times \mathrm{id}$ is the blowup of the smooth center $\{ 0\} \times W_0$, and $\widetilde\theta =\Pi ^*\theta $. Fix the Kähler perturbation directions $h=H+\omega $ and $\widetilde h=\kappa +\omega $, and the smooth positive probability densities $\mu =H^ m\wedge \omega ^ p$ and $\widetilde\mu =\kappa ^ m\wedge \omega ^ p/(2^ m-1)$. Both backgrounds are smooth and semipositive, and they are not closed, since $\mathrm d\theta =\mathrm dq\wedge \omega \neq 0$.

Step 2: explicit solutions. Write $\nu _{\rm down}=H^ m$ and $\nu _{\rm up}=\kappa ^ m/(2^ m-1)$ for the base forms; we show below that both are probability forms. For either product, write the perturbed background as $\xi =A+g\omega $, where downstairs $A=(b+\epsilon )H$, upstairs $A=(b+\epsilon )H+\epsilon L$, and in both cases $g=q+\epsilon $, with pullback understood upstairs. The form $A$ is Kähler on the relevant base. Let $\nu $ be the corresponding base probability form, and put $M=\int A^ m$ and $J=\int g^{-p}\nu $. The smooth equation of theorem 9.1.1, together with closed Stokes, gives a smooth $u$ on the base with

\[ \alpha =A+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u>0,\qquad \alpha ^ m=(M/J)g^{-p}\nu . \]

Let $\Xi (s)$ denote the mass of $\nu $ on the set $\{ |z|^2<s\} $. These cumulative masses are

26.130
\begin{equation} \begin{split} \Xi _{\rm down}(s)& =\left(\frac{s}{1+s}\right)^ m,\\ \Xi _{\rm up}(s)& =\frac{\left(1+\frac{s}{1+s}\right)^ m-1}{2^ m-1}. \end{split} \end{equation}

To check these formulas and to compute $M$, write $t=\log |z|^2$ on the punctured affine chart. For a radial potential $F(t)$, polar integration of its complex Hessian gives

\[ \int _{s_1<|z|^2<s_2}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F(\log |z|^2))^ m =F'(\log s_2)^ m-F'(\log s_1)^ m. \]

The potentials of $H$ and $L$ are $\log (1+\mathrm{e}^ t)$ and $t$. Thus the potential of $xH+yL$ has slopes $y$ and $x+y$ at the two ends, and the total mass of $xH+yL$ is $(x+y)^ m-y^ m$. The cases $(x,y)=(1,0)$ and $(1,1)$ give (26.130); the exceptional divisor and the hyperplane at infinity have zero smooth volume. In particular, $M=(b+\epsilon )^ m$ downstairs and $M=(b+2\epsilon )^ m-\epsilon ^ m$ upstairs. The form

\[ \Omega =\alpha +g\omega =\xi +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u>0,\qquad \Omega ^ n=\binom {n}{m}\frac{M}{J}\nu \wedge \omega ^ p \]

solves the normalized equation, since $\nu \wedge \omega ^ p$ is the chosen probability density. The Gauduchon metric conformal to it is

\[ \gamma _*=g^{-p/(n-1)}\Omega . \]

Indeed, its $(n-1)$st power is

26.133
\begin{equation} \gamma _*^{n-1} =\binom {n-1}{m}g^{-1}\alpha ^ m\wedge \omega ^{p-1} +\binom {n-1}{m-1}\alpha ^{m-1}\wedge \omega ^ p. \end{equation}

In the first term a function on the base multiplies a top-degree form on the base, and every factor in the second term is closed; hence both terms are closed, and $\gamma _*$ is balanced, in particular Gauduchon. Theorem 26.1.3 gives the exact identities

26.134
\begin{equation} \mathcal V_\mu (\theta +\epsilon h) =\binom {n}{m}\frac{(b+\epsilon )^ m}{J_{\rm down}}, \qquad \mathcal V_{\widetilde\mu }(\widetilde\theta +\epsilon \widetilde h) =\binom {n}{m} \frac{(b+2\epsilon )^ m-\epsilon ^ m}{J_{\rm up}}, \end{equation}

where $J_{\rm down}=\int _ B(q+\epsilon )^{-p}\nu _{\rm down}$ and $J_{\rm up}=\int _{\widetilde B}(q\circ \pi +\epsilon )^{-p}\nu _{\rm up}$, and shows that $\gamma _*$ attains the infimum.

Step 3: asymptotics of the denominators. Near zero, differentiation of (26.130) gives

26.135
\begin{equation} \mathrm{d}\Xi _{\rm down}=(m s^{m-1}+O(s^ m))\, \mathrm{d} s,\qquad \mathrm{d}\Xi _{\rm up}=\left(\frac{m}{2^ m-1}+O(s)\right)\mathrm{d} s. \end{equation}

For a density $(a s^{d-1}+O(s^ d))\, \mathrm{d} s$ near zero and $q=s^ k$, the integral of $(q+\epsilon )^{-p}$ over a fixed small interval $[0,s_0]$ has the following leading behavior as $\epsilon \downarrow 0$:

26.136
\begin{equation} \begin{cases} \displaystyle \frac{a}{k}\mathrm B(d/k,p-d/k)\epsilon ^{d/k-p} & kp>d,\\[4pt] \displaystyle \frac{a}{k}\log (1/\epsilon )& kp=d,\\[4pt] \text{a finite positive limit}& kp<d. \end{cases} \end{equation}

Here $\mathrm B(u,v)=\int _0^\infty t^{u-1}(1+t)^{-u-v}\, \mathrm{d} t$ is the Beta function. In the first case substitute $s=\epsilon ^{1/k}v$ and then $t=v^ k$. In the second case the integrand is asymptotic to $a/s$ on the range $\epsilon ^{1/k}\ll s\ll 1$. The error term $O(s^ d)$ is negligible: on a sufficiently small fixed interval its relative size is arbitrarily small, while the integral over the complementary interval stays bounded. For $kp<d$, monotone convergence applies, since the limiting density is integrable. The numerators in (26.134) tend to $\binom {n}{m}b^ m>0$, and the integrals over the complement of a neighborhood of the origin stay bounded. Hence (26.135) and (26.136), with $d=m$ downstairs and $d=1$ upstairs, give the stated orders.

Step 4: examples. For $m=k=2$ and $p=1$, the denominators satisfy $J_{\rm down}\sim \log (1/\epsilon )$ and $J_{\rm up}\sim (\pi /3)\epsilon ^{-1/2}$, so the volumes are asymptotic to $3b^2/\log (1/\epsilon )$ and $(9b^2/\pi )\epsilon ^{1/2}$, respectively. The order changes from $0$ to $1/2$. For $m=2$ and $k=p=1$, the downstairs denominator has a finite positive limit, while $J_{\rm up}\sim (2/3)\log (1/\epsilon )$. Thus a background of positive volume downstairs pulls back to one whose perturbed volumes are asymptotic to $9b^2/[2\log (1/\epsilon )]$, and both orders are zero. Finally, for an integer $j\geq 1$, choose $m=j+2$, $p=j+1$ and $k=1$. Then $kp<m$ gives a positive limit of the volumes downstairs, while the orders are $0$ and $j$. $\square$

26.19.4 Positive-current subtraction and divisorial residuals

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Another way to change the order of degeneration is to subtract a positive current from the background. If $\theta -\beta $ differs from a positive current by a $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-exact term, then every Gauduchon pairing of $\theta $ dominates the corresponding pairing of $\beta $. When the residual $\beta $ is still nef, its perturbed volumes are therefore smaller, and its orders are at least those of $\theta $. The main example is the subtraction of the curvature form of an effective divisor. Such a subtraction can preserve the order, raise it by an integer or by a fraction, or produce unequal lower and upper orders; and the residual need not be nef.

Proposition 26.19.10. Let $\theta ,\beta $ be smooth nef real $(1,1)$-forms on a compact complex manifold $X$ of dimension $n$, and suppose that

\[ S=(\theta -\beta )+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq 0 \]

is a positive $(1,1)$-current for some integrable function $\varphi $. For every Hermitian form $h$, every smooth strictly positive probability form $\mu $ and every $\epsilon >0$,

26.140
\begin{equation} \mathcal V_\mu (\beta +\epsilon h)\leq \mathcal V_\mu (\theta +\epsilon h). \end{equation}

Consequently $\underline a(\beta )\geq \underline a(\theta )$ and $\overline a(\beta )\geq \overline a(\theta )$.

Proof. For every Gauduchon metric $\gamma $, distributional integration by parts gives

\[ \int _ X(\theta -\beta )\wedge \gamma ^{n-1} =\langle S,\gamma ^{n-1}\rangle \geq 0. \]

Hence, for each test metric, the quotient for $\theta +\epsilon h$ is at least the quotient for $\beta +\epsilon h$, and taking infima gives $\lambda _\mu (\beta +\epsilon h)\leq \lambda _\mu (\theta +\epsilon h)$. Both sides are positive because $\beta $ and $\theta $ are nef, so raising to the $n$th power gives (26.140). Dividing the logarithms by the negative number $\log \epsilon $ reverses the inequality and proves both comparisons of orders. $\square$

For an effective real divisor $D$ with curvature form $\eta _ D$, the Poincaré–Lelong formula gives $[D]=\eta _ D+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ for a quasi-plurisubharmonic function $\varphi $. Thus proposition 26.19.10 applies to $\beta =\theta -\eta _ D$, with $S=[D]$, whenever $\beta $ is nef. For a convergent countable positive combination of divisors, the same argument applies only when its curvature representative is smooth and a global potential realizes the current difference. If $T=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ is a positive current with $T-S\geq 0$, then $T-S=\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u-\varphi )$, so $\beta $ is the background of the positive current that remains after $S$ is removed from $T$.

Proposition 26.19.11. Subtracting the curvature form of an effective divisor from a smooth nef background, with a smooth nef residual, can preserve the common order, raise it by any integer between $1$ and the dimension or by a fraction, or produce a residual whose lower and upper orders differ. The residual can also fail to be nef.

Proof. Integer jumps. On $E^ n$, where $E$ is an elliptic curve, let $\omega _ i$ be the pullback of a flat area form of integral one from the $i$th factor, and set $h=\sum _ i\omega _ i$ and $\mu =\omega _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \omega _ n$. The divisor $F_ i$ obtained by fixing a point in the $i$th factor has $\omega _ i$ as a smooth curvature form: on that elliptic curve, any curvature form of the corresponding line bundle differs from $\omega _ i$ by $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ of a solution of a zero-mean Poisson equation, so the metric can be adjusted, and then pulled back. For $\theta =h$ and $D=\sum _{i=1}^ kF_ i$, the residual $\beta =\sum _{i=k+1}^ n\omega _ i$ is smooth and nef. The perturbed forms are closed, positive and of constant density with respect to $\mu $, so the zero potential solves their equations, and theorem 26.1.3 gives

26.141
\begin{equation} \mathcal V_\mu (\theta +\epsilon h)=n!(1+\epsilon )^ n,\qquad \mathcal V_\mu (\beta +\epsilon h)=n!\epsilon ^ k(1+\epsilon )^{n-k}. \end{equation}

For $n=1$ the same formula follows from $\lambda _\mu (\xi )=\int _ X\xi $. These examples realize each integer jump $1,\ldots ,n$. A nonzero divisor with jump zero is given by $\theta =2h$ and $D=F_1$, since then the residual is still strictly positive.

A fractional jump. Use the background $\beta =b\omega _1+q\omega _2$ of proposition 26.19.4, where $q=|\zeta |^{2m}$ near its unique zero and $m>1$. Choose $c>0$ and put $\theta =\beta +c\omega _2>0$ and $D=c(E\times \{ w_0\} )$, whose smooth curvature form is $c\omega _2$. The original background is strictly positive, so both its orders are zero, whereas the residual has common order $1-1/m$.

Unequal orders. Let $\beta _{\rm res}$ be the oscillatory rank-one background of proposition 26.19.5, and put

\[ \theta =\beta _{\rm res}+ch>0,\qquad D=c(\{ z_0\} \times E)+c(E\times \{ w_0\} ),\qquad \eta _ D=ch. \]

The background $\theta $ has both orders zero: since $\theta \geq ch$, every test quotient is bounded below by a positive constant, while one fixed test gives a finite upper bound. Its smooth nef residual is exactly $\beta _{\rm res}$, whose lower and upper orders differ. In each of these examples, the residual plus $[D]$ is a positive current in the smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-class of $\theta $, and its divisorial part is exactly $[D]$ because the residual is smooth. We do not need this current to have minimal singularities; that case is treated in corollary 26.19.12.

A residual that is not nef. Blow up $\mathbb P^2$ at two distinct points. Let $H$ be the pullback of the class of the normalized Fubini–Study form, let $E_1,E_2$ be the exceptional curves, and let $L$ be the strict transform of the line through the two points. The pullback $\theta $ of the Fubini–Study form is smooth, semipositive and nef. Subtract curvature forms of $D=E_1+E_2$. The residual $\beta $ has class $H-E_1-E_2$, which is the class of $L$, and the Poincaré–Lelong formula for the total transform of the line shows that it contains the positive current $[L]$. The intersection numbers $H^2=1$, $H\cdot E_ i=0$, $E_ i^2=-1$ and $E_1\cdot E_2=0$ give

\[ \int _ L\beta =(H-E_1-E_2)^2=-1. \]

If $\beta $ were nef, smooth representatives $\beta +\epsilon h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\epsilon >0$ would give $-1+\epsilon \int _ Lh\geq 0$ for all $\epsilon >0$, which is impossible. $\square$

For a prime divisor $D$ and $\delta >0$, consider the infimum of the generic Lelong numbers along $D$ of the positive currents $\theta +\delta h+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$; its limit as $\delta \downarrow 0$ is the minimal multiplicity of $\theta $ along $D$. For a nef background all minimal multiplicities vanish, since every $\theta +\delta h$ has a smooth positive representative. Removing the divisor defined by these multiplicities therefore leaves a nef background unchanged. Removing the divisorial part of a particular positive current in the class of $\theta $ is a different operation, even when that current has minimal singularities.

Corollary 26.19.12. On the ruled surface $X$ of lemma 25.5.1, keep its nef curvature form $\theta $, its section $D$ at infinity and its Kähler form $\kappa =\theta +\pi ^*\sigma _0$. For every smooth positive probability form $\mu $,

\[ \mathcal V_\mu (\theta +\epsilon \kappa )=2\epsilon (1+\epsilon ), \qquad \mathcal V_\mu (\epsilon \kappa )=2\epsilon ^2. \]

The only positive current of the form $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ is $[D]$. It has minimal singularities, and removing its whole divisor leaves the zero background and changes the common order from one to two.

Proof. Lemma 25.5.1 gives the uniqueness of $[D]$ and the nefness of $\theta $, and the calculation in the proof of proposition 25.5.3 gives $\int _ X\theta ^2=0$. Put $B=\pi ^*\sigma _0$. Then $B^2=0$, and

\[ \int _ X\theta \wedge B=\int _ D B=1, \]

by the Poincaré–Lelong formula, Stokes' theorem and the fact that the section $D$ maps isomorphically onto the elliptic base. The closed nef formula following theorem 26.10.1 now computes both volumes by expanding the squares, for every $\mu $. Every $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function $u$ satisfies $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=[D]$, so any two such functions differ by a pluriharmonic function, hence by a constant; in particular each has minimal singularities. Since $\theta $ is itself a curvature form of $\mathcal O_ X(D)$, removing the whole divisor leaves the zero background. The two volume polynomials give the orders one and two. $\square$

26.19.5 Integer orders in fixed projective cones

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

In the examples of section 26.19.1 the order is not an integer because a coefficient of the background vanishes at a point. We now consider backgrounds that are combinations, with strictly positive coefficient functions, of finitely many fixed closed semipositive generators on products of projective spaces and their linear blowups. Such backgrounds have integral orders even when they are not closed. The tool is a comparison with a closed semipositive form $B$ of constant rank.
