If $\theta $ is bounded above by a multiple of $B$ and, after the addition of a possibly singular potential, below by another multiple, then the Gauduchon quotients of $\theta +\epsilon h$ and of the closed form $B+\epsilon \Omega $ are comparable, and the latter have an explicit order. On a blown-up factor the pulled-back hyperplane form loses rank along the exceptional divisor, and the logarithmic potential of that divisor supplies the lower bound.

Lemma 26.19.13. Let $X$ be a compact Kähler manifold of dimension $n$, let $\Omega $ be a Kähler form, and let $B$ be a smooth closed semipositive $(1,1)$-form. Suppose a smooth nef background $\theta $ satisfies

\[ \theta \leq CB,\qquad \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq cB \]

as currents, for constants $c,C>0$ and a real function $\varphi \in L^1(X)$. For every Hermitian form $h$ and smooth strictly positive probability form $\mu $, there are constants $k,K>0$ such that, for $0<\epsilon \leq 1$,

26.142
\begin{equation} k\lambda _\mu (B+\epsilon \Omega ) \leq \lambda _\mu (\theta +\epsilon h) \leq K\lambda _\mu (B+\epsilon \Omega ). \end{equation}

If $B$ has constant rank $R$, there are moreover constants $L,U>0$ with

\[ L\epsilon ^{n-R}\leq \mathcal V_\mu (\theta +\epsilon h) \leq U\epsilon ^{n-R},\qquad 0<\epsilon \leq 1. \]

In particular, $\theta $ then has common order $n-R$.

Proof. Choose $c_ h,C_ h>0$ with $c_ h\Omega \leq h\leq C_ h\Omega $. For every Gauduchon metric $\gamma $, distributional Stokes gives

\[ \int _ X\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \wedge \gamma ^{n-1} =\langle \varphi ,\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\gamma ^{n-1}\rangle =0. \]

The lower bound for $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ therefore gives $\int _ X(\theta +\epsilon h)\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}\geq k\int _ X(B+\epsilon \Omega )\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}$ with $k=\min (c,c_ h)$, and the upper bound for $\theta $ gives the reverse inequality with $K=\max (C,C_ h)$. Dividing by $D_\mu (\gamma )>0$ and taking infima over all Gauduchon metrics proves (26.142); no rank hypothesis has been used.

Now suppose that $B$ has constant rank $R$. Its $R$ positive eigenvalues relative to $\Omega $ are then bounded above and away from zero, uniformly on $X$. With $A_\epsilon =B+\epsilon \Omega $, compactness gives constants $m_0,M_0>0$ with

26.143
\begin{equation} m_0\epsilon ^{n-R}\mu \leq A_\epsilon ^ n \leq M_0\epsilon ^{n-R}\mu ,\qquad 0<\epsilon \leq 1. \end{equation}

For every Gauduchon metric $\gamma $, the pointwise arithmetic–geometric mean inequality gives

\[ A_\epsilon \wedge \gamma ^{n-1} \geq \left(\frac{A_\epsilon ^ n}{\gamma ^ n}\right)^{1/n}\gamma ^ n, \]

and hence $\lambda _\mu (A_\epsilon )\geq m_0^{1/n}\epsilon ^{(n-R)/n}$. In the other direction, the positive closed form $\gamma =A_\epsilon $ is itself an admissible test. Writing $A_\epsilon ^ n=f_\epsilon \mu $, we have

\[ \begin{aligned} D_\mu (A_\epsilon )& =\int _ X f_\epsilon ^{(n-1)/n}\mu \geq m_0^{(n-1)/n}\epsilon ^{(n-R)(n-1)/n},\\ \int _ X A_\epsilon ^ n& \leq M_0\epsilon ^{n-R}, \end{aligned} \]

so the quotient of this test is at most $M_0m_0^{-(n-1)/n}\epsilon ^{(n-R)/n}$. Raising both bounds to the $n$th power and using (26.142) gives the two-sided power bounds. The argument includes $R=0$; when $n=1$, $D_\mu =1$. Fixed positive factors do not affect the order, since their logarithms are divided by $\log \epsilon $. $\square$

Linear blowups and the fixed generators

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The manifolds are products of factors of two kinds. A plain factor is $Z_ i=\mathbb P^{m_ i}$ with $m_ i\geq 1$; let $H_ i$ be its Fubini–Study form normalized to have integral one on lines, and set $\kappa _ i=H_ i$. A blown-up factor is

\[ Z_ i=\operatorname {Bl}_{\mathbb P^{\ell _ i}}\mathbb P^{m_ i}, \qquad m_ i\geq 2,\quad 0\leq \ell _ i\leq m_ i-2, \qquad r_ i=m_ i-\ell _ i-1, \]

the blowup of $\mathbb P^{m_ i}$ along a linear subspace of dimension $\ell _ i$. We describe one such factor and suppress the index for the moment. In homogeneous coordinates $[z_0:\cdots :z_\ell :w_0:\cdots :w_ r]$ on $\mathbb P^ m$, with the center $\{ w=0\} $, the blowup has the incidence model

\[ Z=\{ (x,[t])\in \mathbb P^ m\times \mathbb P^ r: w_ at_ b=w_ bt_ a\text{ for all }a,b\} . \]

Let $p:Z\to \mathbb P^ m$ and $q:Z\to \mathbb P^ r$ be the projections. In the chart $t_ j=1$, the equations $w_ a=t_ aw_ j$ are the usual charts of the blowup along $\{ w_0=\cdots =w_ r=0\} $. Writing $w=\lambda t$ shows that $q$ is locally a $\mathbb P^{\ell +1}$-bundle, with fiber coordinates $[z:\lambda ]$. Thus $Z$ is smooth, $q$ is a submersion, and the incidence inclusion is an embedding. With $\omega _ m$ and $\omega _ r$ the normalized Fubini–Study forms on $\mathbb P^ m$ and $\mathbb P^ r$, set

\[ H=p^*\omega _ m,\qquad Q=q^*\omega _ r,\qquad \kappa =H+Q. \]

The form $\kappa $ is Kähler, as the restriction of a product Kähler form, whereas $Q$ is semipositive of constant rank $r$.

Let $E=\{ w=0\} $ be the exceptional divisor. The coordinate section $w_ j$ of $p^*\mathcal O(1)$ vanishes exactly on $E+q^{-1}\{ t_ j=0\} $, so $\mathcal O(E)=p^*\mathcal O(1)\otimes q^*\mathcal O(-1)$. Give this line bundle the product metric, whose curvature is $H-Q$. For its canonical section $s_ E$, the function $\psi _ E=\log |s_ E|^2$ is locally integrable, and the Poincaré–Lelong formula, as in section 26.21, gives

26.146
\begin{equation} \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi _ E=[E]-H+Q,\qquad H+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\psi _ E/2)=\tfrac 12(\kappa +[E])\geq \kappa /2. \end{equation}

On $X=\prod _ i Z_ i$, put $n=\sum _ i m_ i$, and pull back all forms from the factors. Fix smooth strictly positive functions $f_ i$ on $X$, and, for each blown-up factor, a smooth strictly positive function $g_ i$ on $X$; these functions may depend on all coordinates of the product. The fixed cone consists of the backgrounds

26.147
\begin{equation} \theta =\sum _ i\bigl ((a_ i+b_ if_ i)H_ i+(c_ i+d_ ig_ i)Q_ i\bigr ), \qquad a_ i,b_ i,c_ i,d_ i\geq 0, \end{equation}

where the terms involving $Q_ i$, and the parameters $c_ i,d_ i$, are omitted for plain factors. All coefficient functions and generators are fixed, and only the parameters vary. Every member of the cone is smooth and semipositive, hence nef. We say that $H_ i$ occurs in $\theta $ if $a_ i+b_ i>0$, and that $Q_ i$ occurs if $c_ i+d_ i>0$. Define

26.148
\begin{equation} R_ i(\theta )= \begin{cases} m_ i,& \text{if }H_ i\text{ occurs},\\ r_ i,& \text{if }H_ i\text{ does not occur and }Q_ i\text{ occurs},\\ 0,& \text{if neither occurs}, \end{cases} \qquad R(\theta )=\sum _ i R_ i(\theta ). \end{equation}

The middle case can only occur on blown-up factors.

Theorem 26.19.14. Every background $\theta $ in the fixed cone (26.147) has common order $n-R(\theta )$. More precisely, for every smooth positive probability form $\mu $ and Hermitian form $h$ there are constants $L,U>0$, depending on $\theta ,\mu ,h$, such that

26.152
\begin{equation} L\epsilon ^{n-R(\theta )} \leq \mathcal V_\mu (\theta +\epsilon h) \leq U\epsilon ^{n-R(\theta )},\qquad 0<\epsilon \leq 1. \end{equation}

Proof. Let $B_\theta $ be the sum of the forms $\kappa _ i$ over the factors in which $H_ i$ occurs and of the forms $Q_ i$ over the factors in which only $Q_ i$ occurs. It is closed and semipositive of constant rank $R(\theta )$. Since the coefficients are bounded and $H_ i,Q_ i\leq \kappa _ i$, we have $\theta \leq CB_\theta $ for some $C>0$.

For the lower bound, let $s_ i=\min _ X(a_ i+b_ if_ i)>0$ for each blown-up factor in which $H_ i$ occurs, and put

\[ \varphi =\sum _{\substack {i\text{ blown up}\\ H_ i\text{ occurs}}} (s_ i/2)\psi _{E_ i}. \]

By (26.146), $s_ iH_ i+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(s_ i\psi _{E_ i}/2)=(s_ i/2)(\kappa _ i+[E_ i])\geq (s_ i/2)\kappa _ i$. The remaining terms $(a_ i+b_ if_ i-s_ i)H_ i$ and all terms involving $Q_ i$ are semipositive. On plain factors in which $H_ i$ occurs and on factors in which only $Q_ i$ occurs, the relevant coefficient is bounded below by a positive constant. Taking the minimum of these constants gives $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq cB_\theta $ for some $c>0$. For $\theta =0$ take $\varphi =0$ and any $c>0$. Now apply lemma 26.19.13 with $\Omega =\sum _ i\kappa _ i$. $\square$

Members of the fixed cone need not be closed: by construction

\[ \mathrm d\theta =\sum _ i b_ i\, \mathrm df_ i\wedge H_ i +\sum _{i\text{ blown up}}d_ i\, \mathrm dg_ i\wedge Q_ i. \]

If only one of these terms has a nonzero coefficient and the corresponding form, say $\mathrm df_ i\wedge H_ i$, is nonzero, then $\theta $ is not closed. On a blown-up factor, for instance, if $g$ is a small positive perturbation of a constant that varies along a fiber of $q$, then $\mathrm dg\wedge Q\ne 0$, since $r\geq 1$. On $\mathbb P^ p\times \mathbb P^ q$ with $p,q\geq 1$, a positive function $f$ that varies on the second factor gives the nonclosed background $fH_1$ of order $q$. Adding $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ of a smooth function does not change the exterior derivative.

Equality of orders under modifications

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

By proposition 26.19.8, pulling back by a modification can only increase the orders. For the fixed cone, the orders are preserved.

Proposition 26.19.15. Replacing some plain factors by linear blowups as above preserves the order of every background in the fixed cone. More generally, for every modification $\pi :Y\to X$ with $Y$ a compact Kähler manifold, the pullback $\pi ^*\theta $ has common order $n-R(\theta )$, and satisfies two-sided power bounds with this exponent for every smooth positive probability form and every Hermitian perturbation on $Y$.

Proof. For a product of linear blowups, pull back the coefficient functions and take the new coefficients of the forms $Q_ i$ to be zero. A form $H_ i$ that occurs on a plain factor becomes a form $H_ i$ that occurs on a blown-up factor, with the same rank $m_ i$, and factors in which nothing occurs remain so. Hence theorem 26.19.14, applied on both products, gives the same order.

For a general compact Kähler manifold $Y$, pull back the semipositive smooth remainders and the divisor identities used in the proof above. Each nonzero holomorphic section remains nonzero under the surjective map $\pi $, and the logarithm of the squared norm of its pullback is locally integrable, with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ equal to the current of its effective zero divisor minus the pulled-back curvature. Hence

\[ \pi ^*\theta \leq C\pi ^*B_\theta ,\qquad \pi ^*\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\varphi \circ \pi )\geq c\pi ^*B_\theta . \]

Fix a Kähler form $\Omega _ Y$ on $Y$, and apply the first part of lemma 26.19.13, which needs no rank hypothesis; the pulled-back background is semipositive and hence nef. Put $R=R(\theta )$. Pullback does not increase rank, so $(\pi ^*B_\theta )^{R+1}=0$, while

\[ \int _ Y(\pi ^*B_\theta )^ R\wedge \Omega _ Y^{n-R}>0: \]

for $R>0$ the integrand is positive on the nonempty set where $\pi $ is biholomorphic, and for $R=0$ the integral is the Kähler volume. For any smooth positive probability form $\mu _ Y$, the closed nef formula following theorem 26.10.1 gives

\[ \mathcal V_{\mu _ Y}(\pi ^*B_\theta +\epsilon \Omega _ Y) =\sum _{k=0}^{R}\binom nk\epsilon ^{n-k} \int _ Y(\pi ^*B_\theta )^ k\wedge \Omega _ Y^{n-k}. \]

All coefficients are nonnegative and the coefficient for $k=R$ is positive, so this polynomial is bounded above and below by positive multiples of $\epsilon ^{n-R}$ for $0<\epsilon \leq 1$. The comparison of quotients gives the same bounds for $\pi ^*\theta +\epsilon h_ Y$. In particular, the order upstairs equals the order downstairs. $\square$

Subtracting effective divisors

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now subtract effective divisors from members of the fixed cone. Let $\mathcal O(H_ i)$ and $\mathcal O(Q_ i)$ be the pullbacks of $\mathcal O(1)$ under the projections defining $H_ i$ and $Q_ i$, with their Fubini–Study metrics, whose curvature forms are $H_ i$ and $Q_ i$. Consider line bundles that are tensor products of integer powers of these bundles, negative powers included, and finite nonnegative real combinations of zero divisors of their nonzero holomorphic sections $s_\nu $:

\[ \begin{gathered} D=\sum _\nu t_\nu \operatorname {div}(s_\nu ),\quad t_\nu \geq 0,\\ \eta _ D=\sum _ i(\delta _ iH_ i+\rho _ iQ_ i),\qquad \Phi _ D=\sum _\nu t_\nu \log |s_\nu |^2. \end{gathered} \]

Here $\eta _ D$ is the corresponding combination of the curvature forms of the fixed metrics, so that $[D]=\eta _ D+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Phi _ D$. The terms involving $Q_ i$ are omitted for plain factors.

Lemma 26.19.16. The coefficients $(\delta _ i,\rho _ i)$ that occur for divisors $D$ of this form are exactly those satisfying

\[ \delta _ i\geq 0\text{ on plain factors};\qquad \delta _ i\geq 0,\quad \delta _ i+\rho _ i\geq 0 \text{ on blown-up factors}. \]

Proof. It suffices to treat one nonzero holomorphic section $s$, since nonnegative real combinations preserve the inequalities. Choose a point at which $s$ is nonzero and which lies outside all exceptional divisors, and fix the coordinates of the other factors. A projective line through that point inside a fiber of $q_ i$ has $H_ i$-degree one and $Q_ i$-degree zero. The restriction of $s$ to it is a nonzero section, so the exponent of $\mathcal O(H_ i)$ is nonnegative. On a plain factor, a line through the point gives the same inequality.

On a blown-up factor, write the chosen point as $([z:w],[w])$ with $w\ne 0$, and choose a linear map $A$ with $Aw=z$. The section of $q_ i$ given by $[t]\mapsto ([At:t],[t])$ passes through the point, and both $H_ i$ and $Q_ i$ have degree one on each projective line in its image. Such a line through the point exists because $r_ i\geq 1$, and $s$ restricts nontrivially to it. Its degree shows that the sum of the two exponents is nonnegative.

Conversely, let $F_ i$ be the pullback under $q_ i$ of a hyperplane. An admissible pair $(\delta _ i,\rho _ i)$ is realized by $\delta _ iE_ i+(\delta _ i+\rho _ i)F_ i$, since $E_ i$ has curvature $H_ i-Q_ i$, and coefficients on plain factors are realized by hyperplanes. These are allowed nonnegative combinations and give every coefficient tuple in the stated range. $\square$

Theorem 26.19.17. Let $\theta $ be in the fixed cone, with parameters $a_ i,b_ i,c_ i,d_ i$, and let $D$ be a divisor of the above form. Assume

\[ a_ i\geq \delta _ i\text{ for all }i,\qquad c_ i\geq \rho _ i\text{ for every blown-up factor}. \]

Then the residual $\beta =\theta -\eta _ D$ lies in the fixed cone, and the common orders $a(\beta )$ and $a(\theta )$ satisfy

\[ a(\beta )-a(\theta )=\sum _ i\bigl (R_ i(\theta )-R_ i(\beta )\bigr )\geq 0. \]

Equality holds if and only if, for every factor,

\begin{align*} a_ i-\delta _ i+b_ i& >0& & \text{whenever }a_ i+b_ i>0,\\ c_ i-\rho _ i+d_ i& >0& & \text{whenever }a_ i=b_ i=0\text{ and }c_ i+d_ i>0; \end{align*}

the second condition concerns only blown-up factors.

Proof. The residual parameters $(a_ i-\delta _ i,b_ i,c_ i-\rho _ i,d_ i)$ are nonnegative, so $\beta $ lies in the fixed cone; in particular it is smooth, semipositive and nef. Also

\[ (\theta -\beta )+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Phi _ D=[D]\geq 0, \]

which is the positive-current comparison of proposition 26.19.10. Apply theorem 26.19.14 to $\theta $ and to $\beta $. If $H_ i$ occurs in $\theta $ but not in $\beta $, the rank $R_ i$ drops from $m_ i$ to $r_ i<m_ i$ or to zero. If $H_ i$ does not occur in $\theta $, then $a_ i=b_ i=0$, and the range of lemma 26.19.16 together with $a_ i\geq \delta _ i$ forces $\delta _ i=0$ and hence $\rho _ i\geq 0$; thus $Q_ i$ cannot appear in $\beta $ if it was absent from $\theta $. Therefore no rank $R_ i$ increases, each difference $R_ i(\theta )-R_ i(\beta )$ is nonnegative, and the sum vanishes exactly when the generator that determines $R_ i(\theta )$ still occurs in $\beta $ for every $i$. This is the stated pair of conditions. $\square$

The parameter inequalities guarantee that the residual stays in the fixed cone; they do not describe all divisors whose residual is nef.

Example 26.19.18. On $Z=\operatorname {Bl}_{\mathbb P^\ell }\mathbb P^ m$, choose a smooth function $g>0$ with $\mathrm dg\wedge Q\ne 0$. For $t>0$, the nonclosed positive background $\theta _ t=tH+gQ$ has order zero. Removing $sE$, with its fixed curvature form $s(H-Q)$ and $0\leq s\leq t$, gives

\[ \beta _ s=(t-s)H+(g+s)Q,\qquad \theta _ t+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(s\psi _ E)=s[E]+\beta _ s. \]

Every residual $\beta _ s$ is nef and not closed. Its order equals that of $\theta _ t$ exactly for $s<t$, and at $s=t$ the order jumps by $\ell +1$. These examples realize each integer jump $1,\ldots ,m-1$.

Proof. The lower bound $\theta _ t\geq \min (t,\min g)\kappa >0$ shows that $\theta _ t$ is positive. For $s<t$ the form $H$ occurs in $\beta _ s$ and the rank is $m$; at $s=t$ only $Q$ occurs, with rank $r=m-\ell -1$. The order formula gives zero and $m-r=\ell +1$, respectively. The exterior derivative of every residual is $\mathrm dg\wedge Q\ne 0$. Finally, let $\ell $ range from zero to $m-2$. $\square$

At the endpoint $s=t$, the current $t[E]+\beta _ t$ in the class of $\theta _ t$ has a divisorial part, although the minimal multiplicities of $\theta _ t$ vanish because $\theta _ t$ is nef, as explained before corollary 26.19.12. The construction uses three hypotheses in an essential way: the coefficient functions are strictly positive, the subtracted divisor keeps the residual in the fixed cone, and, for general modifications, the source manifold is Kähler. Without such hypotheses the question in 26.19.49 is open.

26.19.6 A maximum-rank comparison criterion

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The constant-rank hypothesis in lemma 26.19.13 can be removed. For a closed semipositive form $B$, the intersection polynomial $\int _ X(B+\epsilon \Omega )^ n$ has a leading term of order $\epsilon ^{n-R}$, where $R$ is the largest rank that $B$ attains anywhere on $X$. Combined with the comparison of quotients, this gives the order $n-R$ for every background that is comparable to $B$. The criterion applies to backgrounds that vanish on an open set, and to positive variable multiples of arbitrary closed semipositive generators.

Proposition 26.19.19. Let $X$ be a compact Kähler manifold of dimension $n$, let $B$ be a smooth closed semipositive $(1,1)$-form, and put $R=\max _ X\operatorname {rank}B$. Suppose a smooth nef background $\theta $ satisfies

\[ \theta \leq CB,\qquad \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq cB \]

as currents, for constants $c,C>0$ and a real function $\varphi \in L^1(X)$. For every smooth positive probability form $\mu $ and Hermitian form $h$, there are constants $L,U>0$ such that

\[ L\epsilon ^{n-R}\leq \mathcal V_\mu (\theta +\epsilon h) \leq U\epsilon ^{n-R},\qquad 0<\epsilon \leq 1. \]

Thus $\theta $ has common order $n-R$, without any constant-rank hypothesis.

Proof. Fix a Kähler form $\Omega $. The first part of lemma 26.19.13, which does not use the rank, shows that $\lambda _\mu (\theta +\epsilon h)$ and $\lambda _\mu (B+\epsilon \Omega )$ differ by bounded positive factors. Since $B^{R+1}=0$, the closed nef formula following theorem 26.10.1 gives

\[ \mathcal V_\mu (B+\epsilon \Omega ) =\sum _{k=0}^{R}\binom nk\epsilon ^{n-k} \int _ X B^ k\wedge \Omega ^{n-k}. \]

All coefficients are nonnegative. If $R>0$, then at a point where $B$ has rank $R$, some $R\times R$ minor of its coefficient matrix is positive and stays positive nearby, so $B^ R\wedge \Omega ^{n-R}$ has positive integral. If $R=0$, this integral is the Kähler volume. Hence the coefficient of $\epsilon ^{n-R}$ is positive. For $0<\epsilon \leq 1$, every other power of $\epsilon $ that occurs is at most $\epsilon ^{n-R}$, so the polynomial is bounded below by its leading term and above by the sum of its coefficients times $\epsilon ^{n-R}$. Raising the comparison of quotients to the $n$th power gives the asserted bounds, and taking logarithms gives the order. $\square$

Corollary 26.19.20. On a compact Kähler manifold $X$ of dimension $n$, fix finitely many smooth closed semipositive $(1,1)$-forms $B_ j$ and smooth strictly positive functions $f_ j$. For $t_ j\geq 0$, put

\[ \theta =\sum _ jt_ jf_ jB_ j,\qquad B=\sum _{j:t_ j>0}B_ j,\qquad R=\max _ X\operatorname {rank}B. \]

The background $\theta $ is nef, has common order $n-R$, and satisfies the two-sided power bounds of proposition 26.19.19. For every modification $\pi :Y\to X$ with $Y$ a compact Kähler manifold, the pullback $\pi ^*\theta $ has the same order and power bounds with the same exponent, for every smooth positive probability form and Hermitian perturbation on $Y$.

Proof. Semipositivity gives nefness. If some $t_ j$ is positive, compactness and strict positivity of the functions $f_ j$ give

\[ \begin{gathered} cB\leq \theta \leq CB,\qquad c=\min _{j:t_ j>0}\bigl (t_ j\min _ X f_ j\bigr )>0,\\ C=\max _{j:t_ j>0}\bigl (t_ j\max _ X f_ j\bigr ), \end{gathered} \]

and proposition 26.19.19 applies with $\varphi =0$. If all parameters vanish, then $B=\theta =0$, $R=0$, any $c,C>0$ may be used, and the order is $n$.

These inequalities between smooth forms are preserved by pullback, and the rank of $\pi ^*B$ is at most $R$. If $R>0$, the set where $B$ has rank $R$ is nonempty and open, so it meets the dense open set where $\pi $ is biholomorphic; hence the maximal rank of $\pi ^*B$ is $R$. This is immediate if $R=0$. Since $\dim Y=n$, the same proposition applies on $Y$, with any choice of data there. No singular potential needs to be pulled back. $\square$

The constants depend on the parameters $t_ j$ and need not stay bounded as a positive parameter tends to zero. The rank of a sum records how the generators overlap, and it need not equal the sum of their ranks. Members of the family are not closed when $\sum _ jt_ j\, \mathrm df_ j\wedge B_ j\ne 0$.

Example 26.19.21. Let $X=\mathbb P^1\times \mathbb P^ q$, $q\geq 1$, with projections $p_1,p_2$. Choose a smooth nonnegative function $b\not\equiv 0$ on $\mathbb P^1$ that vanishes on a nonempty open set, and a smooth strictly positive nonconstant function $f$ on $\mathbb P^ q$. With $\omega _1$ the normalized Fubini–Study form on $\mathbb P^1$, set

\[ B=p_1^*(b\omega _1),\qquad \theta =(f\circ p_2)B. \]

The smooth nef background $\theta $ is not closed, vanishes on a nonempty open set, and has common order $q$, with two-sided bounds by multiples of $\epsilon ^ q$.

Proof. The form $b\omega _1$ is closed, because the first factor is a curve, and semipositive. Its pullback has rank one where $b>0$ and rank zero where $b=0$, so its maximal rank is one. Since $n=q+1$, corollary 26.19.20 gives the order $q$. A nonconstant smooth function $f$ on the connected second factor has $\mathrm df\ne 0$ somewhere. At a point of the product where $b>0$ and $\mathrm df\neq 0$, the horizontal two-form $B$ wedges nontrivially with the vertical one-form $p_2^*\mathrm df$. Thus $\mathrm d\theta =p_2^*\mathrm df\wedge B\ne 0$. $\square$

These are geometric sufficient conditions for an integral common order, in the direction of 26.19.49. They do not cover backgrounds without a closed semipositive comparison form, coefficient functions that are allowed to vanish, or the comparison of orders under residuals or non-Kähler modifications in general.

26.19.7 Flat anticanonical curves

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The maximum-rank criterion needs a closed semipositive comparison form. On a compact Kähler surface, such a form of rank at most one arises from a smooth divisor $D$ whose line bundle $\mathcal O_ S(D)$ is unitary flat near $D$, that is, admits a Hermitian metric of zero curvature on a neighborhood of $D$. Cutting off the logarithm of the flat norm of the defining section gives a semipositive metric on $\mathcal O_ S(D)$ whose curvature $B$ vanishes near $D$ and has rank at most one; every positive variable multiple $fB$ then has order exactly one. This holds whether or not some multiple of $D$ moves in a linear system, so the order of degeneration does not detect semiampleness. We first show that a smooth anticanonical curve of self-intersection zero always has a unitary flat neighborhood, and then apply this to blowups of the plane at nine points of a cubic.

The construction of the flat metric rests on an estimate on an elliptic curve that is uniform in the holonomy of the twisting line bundle.

Lemma 26.19.22. Let $D$ be an elliptic curve with a flat Hermitian metric, let $\vartheta $ be a parallel $(1,0)$-form of unit length, and let $v$ be its dual vector field. Let $L$ be a unitary flat line bundle on $D$, with flat connection $\nabla =\partial _ L+\bar\partial _ L$. For every smooth $L$-valued $(0,1)$-form $u$, there is a unique smooth section $f$ of $L$, orthogonal to the parallel sections, such that

\[ \bar\partial _ L f=\iota _ v\partial _ Lu. \]

If $\Pi _ L^{0,1}$ denotes the orthogonal projection onto the parallel $L$-valued $(0,1)$-forms, then, for every Sobolev norm $H^ k$ defined by the flat structure,

\[ \| f\| _{H^ k}=\| (I-\Pi _ L^{0,1})u\| _{H^ k} \leq \| u\| _{H^ k}. \]

In particular, the bound does not depend on the holonomy of $L$.

Proof. Put $P=\iota _ v\partial _ L$. Since the metric and $L$ are flat, $P=\nabla _ v$ and $P^*=-\nabla _{\bar v}$, and the Laplacian $\Delta _ L$ satisfies $\Delta _ L=P^*P=PP^*$ on sections and on $(0,1)$-forms. In degree $q\in \{ 0,1\} $, let $G_ q$ be the inverse of $\Delta _ L$ on the orthogonal complement of its kernel, extended by zero on the kernel. The operators $P$ and $P^*$ commute with $G_ q$. Set

\[ f=\bar\partial _ L^*G_1Pu. \]

The form $Pu$ is orthogonal to the parallel forms, so $\bar\partial _ Lf=Pu$; also $f$ is orthogonal to the parallel sections. Uniqueness follows because the kernel of $\bar\partial _ L$ on sections consists of parallel sections. Moreover,

\[ \| f\| _{L^2}^2 =\langle Pu,G_1Pu\rangle =\langle u,(I-\Pi _ L^{0,1})u\rangle . \]

Covariant differentiation along a global parallel real frame commutes with all these operators, so applying the identity to every derivative proves the $H^ k$ assertion. The argument never divides by a lower bound for a nonzero eigenvalue of $\Delta _ L$: the derivative in $Pu$ compensates for small eigenvalues. $\square$

For the neighborhood argument, let $S$ be a complex surface, not necessarily compact, and let $D\subset S$ be a smooth compact connected anticanonical divisor with $D^2=0$; thus $D$ is the zero divisor of a holomorphic section $s_ D$ of the anticanonical bundle $K_ S^{-1}$. By adjunction, $K_ D=(K_ S\otimes \mathcal O_ S(D))|_ D$ is trivial, so $D$ is an elliptic curve. Its normal bundle $N=\mathcal O_ S(D)|_ D$ has degree zero, and therefore admits a unitary flat metric. Let $\pi :N\to D$ be the projection, $N^\times $ the complement of the zero section, and $J_ N$ the complex structure of $N$. The reciprocal $s_ D^{-1}$ is a meromorphic two-form $\Omega _ S$, nowhere zero on $S\setminus D$, with a simple pole along $D$; we denote its residue, a nowhere vanishing holomorphic one-form on $D$, by $\vartheta _ D$.

Choose parallel unitary local frames of $N$, with fiber coordinate $w$. On $N^\times $ put

\[ \xi =w\partial _ w,\qquad \Theta =\frac{\mathrm dw}{w},\qquad \vartheta =\pi ^*\vartheta _ D,\qquad z=-\log |w|^2,\qquad \Omega _0=\Theta \wedge \vartheta . \]

These objects do not depend on the frame, since parallel unitary frames differ by constant factors of modulus one. Give $D$ the flat metric for which $\vartheta _ D$ has unit length, and let $v$ be the parallel vector field dual to $\vartheta _ D$. Equip $N^\times $ with the flat cylindrical metric $g$ for which the coframe $\vartheta ,\Theta $ is unitary, and let $\nabla $ be its connection. The end $z\to +\infty $ of this cylinder is the end near the zero section.

Lemma 26.19.23. There is a diffeomorphism $\Upsilon $ from a neighborhood of the zero section in $N$ onto a neighborhood of $D$ in $S$, equal to the identity on $D$, such that $\Upsilon $ maps each fiber disk of $N$ to a holomorphic curve in $S$, and

\[ |\nabla ^ k(\Upsilon ^*\Omega _ S-\Omega _0)|_ g =O(\mathrm e^{-z/2})\qquad (k\geq 0). \]

Proof. Step 1: a first identification. Start with the normal exponential map of a smooth Hermitian metric near $D$. Its differential along $D$ is complex linear and induces the identity on the normal quotient. In a parallel local frame and a local coordinate $z_0$ on $D$ with $\vartheta _ D=\mathrm dz_0$, choose holomorphic coordinates $Z,W$ on $S$ with $D=\{ W=0\} $. Their pullbacks under the first map satisfy

\[ Z=z_0+b(z_0,\bar z_0)w+O(|w|^2),\qquad W=w+O(|w|^2). \]

The residue normalization gives $\Omega _ S=(1+Wa(Z,W))\, \mathrm dW/W\wedge \mathrm dZ$ with $a$ holomorphic. Substituting, and noting that $\Theta \wedge \mathrm dw=w\Theta \wedge \Theta =0$, we obtain $\Omega _ S-\Omega _0=O(|w|)$ in the coframe $\mathrm dz_0,\mathrm d\bar z_0,\Theta ,\bar\Theta $. Every derivative by $\partial _{z_0},\partial _{\bar z_0},w\partial _ w,\bar w\partial _{\bar w}$ preserves this bound. Since $|w|=\mathrm e^{-z/2}$, this proves the cylindrical estimate for the first map.

Step 2: holomorphic fiber disks. It remains to modify the first map so that the fiber disks become holomorphic, without changing its first jet along $D$. On a fiber disk $\Delta _{p,\rho }\subset N_ p$ of radius $\rho $, perturb the map by $\Phi _ Y(q)=\exp _ q(Y_ q)$, where $Y$ is a vector field along the image of the disk. Identify the target tangent spaces with one fixed complex vector space over $p$, by Chern parallel transport along the radial paths and the short exponential paths. In these identifications, the antiholomorphic part of the differential of the perturbed disk is

\[ \mathcal F_{p,\rho }(Y) =\tfrac 12\bigl (\mathrm d\Phi _ Y+J_ S\mathrm d\Phi _ YJ_ N\bigr ) |_{T\Delta _{p,\rho }}. \]

Fix a Hölder exponent $0<\nu <1$, and use $C^{k,\nu }$ norms on the disks rescaled to unit size. The Cauchy–Green operator $T_\rho $ of the disk gives the normalized right inverse

\[ R_\rho a=T_\rho a-T_\rho a(0) -w(\partial _ wT_\rho a)(0) \]

of $\bar\partial $. We restrict its domain to $(0,1)$-forms vanishing at the center; its image consists of fields whose value and first jet vanish at the center, and on this image $\bar\partial $ and $R_\rho $ are inverse to each other. The differential of $\mathcal F_{p,\rho }$ at zero has the form $\bar\partial +K_{p,\rho }$, with

\[ \| K_{p,\rho }R_\rho \| \leq C\rho ,\qquad \| \mathcal F_{p,\rho }(0)\| \leq C\rho ^2. \]

Indeed, the two complex structures agree along the zero section, and rescaling by $w\mapsto \rho w$ contributes an additional factor $\rho $ to a pulled-back one-form. For small $\rho $, the operator $R_\rho (I+K_{p,\rho }R_\rho )^{-1}$ is a right inverse of the differential, and the quantitative implicit function theorem gives a correction $Y=O(|w|^2)$ with vanishing first jet that makes the disk holomorphic.

Step 3: global smoothness. Compactness of $D$ makes the choice of $\rho $ uniform in $p$. The normalized Cauchy operator is equivariant under unitary changes of the fiber frame, so the intrinsic parallel transports and the uniqueness of the small solution make the corrections agree on overlaps. The parametric implicit function theorem gives smooth dependence on $p$, and interior elliptic regularity on slightly smaller fiber disks gives smoothness along the fibers. Since the first jet is unchanged, the correction changes the map by $O(|w|^2)$ together with its cylindrical derivatives. Composing the first map with this correction therefore preserves the estimate and proves the lemma. $\square$

Theorem 26.19.24. Let $S$ be a complex surface and $D\subset S$ a smooth compact connected anticanonical divisor with $D^2=0$. Then the line bundle $\mathcal O_ S(D)$ is unitary flat on a neighborhood of $D$.

Proof. We look for a fiber-preserving change of the identification of lemma 26.19.23 after which the coordinate $z$ becomes pluriharmonic. The logarithm of the flat norm of the defining section is then $-z$, up to a pluriharmonic correction.

Step 1: horizontal forms. Use the identification $\Upsilon $ of lemma 26.19.23, and omit the pullback notation. A form on $N^\times $ is horizontal if its contractions with $\xi $ and $\bar\xi $ vanish. For a horizontal form $\eta $ define

\[ \mathrm d_ H\eta =\mathrm d\eta -\Theta \wedge \mathcal L_\xi \eta -\bar\Theta \wedge \mathcal L_{\bar\xi }\eta . \]

A form $\eta _ m$ on $D$ with values in $N^{-m}$ defines the horizontal form $w^ m\eta _ m$, homogeneous of degree $m$ in $w$, and direct differentiation shows that $\mathrm d_ H$ acts on it as the flat covariant exterior derivative. Since the fibers are holomorphic,

\[ \iota _{\bar\xi }\Omega _ S=0,\qquad \Omega _ S=\Theta \wedge \alpha +\beta , \qquad \alpha =\iota _\xi \Omega _ S, \]

with $\alpha ,\beta $ horizontal. Closedness gives $\mathcal L_{\bar\xi }\alpha =\mathcal L_{\bar\xi }\beta =0$ and $\mathcal L_\xi \beta =\mathrm d_ H\alpha $.

Step 2: a horizontal inverse. Fix $0<\delta <1/2$, and let $U_ T=\{ z\geq T\} $. For tensors define the weighted norms

\[ \| \eta \| _{k,\delta ,T}^2 =\sum _{j=0}^ k\int _{U_ T}\mathrm e^{2\delta (z-T)} |\nabla ^ j\eta |_ g^2\, \mathrm dV_ g. \]

Let $\mathcal H^{k+1}_{\delta ,T}$ be the space of functions $f$ on $U_ T$ with $\| f\| _{k+1,\delta ,T}<\infty $ and $\mathcal L_{\bar\xi }f=0$, and let $\mathcal D^ k_{\delta ,T}$ be the space of complex two-forms $\gamma $ on $U_ T$ with $\| \gamma \| _{k,\delta ,T}<\infty $, $\mathrm d\gamma =0$ and $\iota _{\bar\xi }\gamma =0$. We construct an operator

\[ Q_ T:\mathcal D^ k_{\delta ,T}\longrightarrow \mathcal H^{k+1}_{\delta ,T}, \qquad \| Q_ T\gamma \| _{k+1,\delta ,T}\leq C_{k,\delta }\| \gamma \| _{k,\delta ,T}, \]

with constant independent of $T$, such that

26.153
\begin{equation} \mathrm d_ H(Q_ T\gamma \, \vartheta ) =\gamma -\Theta \wedge \iota _\xi \gamma , \qquad Q_ T(\mathrm df\wedge \vartheta )=f. \end{equation}

Write $\gamma =\Theta \wedge \alpha +\beta $, with $\alpha ,\beta $ horizontal. The conditions on $\gamma $ give $\mathcal L_{\bar\xi }\gamma =0$ and, in the expansion in homogeneous components along the fibers, $m\beta _ m=\mathrm d_ H\alpha _ m$. Only components with $m\geq 1$ occur, because the weighted norm is finite and $\delta >0$ excludes the Laurent components of degree $m\leq 0$. Decompose

\[ \alpha _ m=-u_ m\vartheta +v_ m, \qquad u_ m\in \Omega ^0(D,N^{-m}),\quad v_ m\in \Omega ^{0,1}(D,N^{-m}). \]

Lemma 26.19.22 gives sections $e_ m$ with $\bar\partial e_ m=\iota _ v\partial v_ m$ and $\| e_ m\| _{H^ k}\leq \| v_ m\| _{H^ k}$. Put $f_ m=-(u_ m+e_ m)/m$. Then

\[ \mathrm d_ H(f_ m\vartheta )=\beta _ m,\qquad \| mf_ m\| _{H^ k}\leq C\| \alpha _ m\| _{H^ k}. \]

Also $\| \bar\partial f_ m\| _{H^ k}\leq C\| \beta _ m\| _{H^ k}$. Since $D$ is flat, integration by parts gives the same bound for $\partial f_ m$, which controls the horizontal derivatives of order $k+1$. A vertical derivative of a homogeneous component multiplies it by $m$, so every derivative involving a vertical direction is controlled by the bound for $mf_ m$. The components are orthogonal on the circles $\{ |w|=\text{constant}\} $, and the squared radial factor of the $m$th component is $\mathrm e^{-mz}$ with $m-2\delta >0$; integrating the weight gives the required estimate for $f=\sum _{m\geq 1}f_ m$, where each $f_ m$ is identified with its homogeneous horizontal lift, and we put $Q_ T\gamma =f$.

For forms with Sobolev coefficients, regularize each component by the heat operator of the flat base, which commutes with the differential equations above. The uniform estimates and orthogonality make the finite partial sums converge in the target space, and passing to the limit proves the first identity in (26.153). If $\gamma =\mathrm df\wedge \vartheta $, then $\alpha _ m=mf_ m\vartheta $, so $v_ m$ and the normalized $e_ m$ vanish, and the construction returns $f_ m$. This proves the second identity.

Step 3: a fixed point. Put $\eta =\Omega _ S-\Omega _0$, and choose an integer $k\geq 4$. By the cylindrical estimate, the weighted $H^{k+2}$ norm of $\eta $ on $U_{T-1}$ and its first $k+2$ derivatives are bounded by a number $a_ T$ with $a_ T\to 0$. For small $f\in \mathcal H^{k+1}_{\delta ,T}$, the map $\Phi _ f(\zeta )=\mathrm e^{f(\zeta )}\zeta $, which rescales each fiber point $\zeta $, is a $C^2$ diffeomorphism onto its image and maps $U_ T$ into $U_{T-1}$. Its restriction to every fiber is holomorphic, so $\Phi _ f^*\Omega _ S-\Omega _0$ lies in the domain of $Q_ T$. We look for $f$ such that $\Phi _ f^*\Omega _ S$ has no horizontal component. Since

\[ \Phi _ f^*\Omega _0-\Omega _0=\mathrm df\wedge \vartheta , \]

(26.153) reduces this to the equation

\[ Q_ T(\Phi _ f^*\Omega _ S-\Omega _0)=f+E_ T(f)=0, \qquad E_ T(f)=Q_ T(\Phi _ f^*\eta ). \]

On a fixed sufficiently small ball, multiplication and composition estimates in weighted Sobolev spaces on the flat cylinder give

\[ \| E_ T(f)\| _{k+1,\delta ,T}\leq Ca_ T,\qquad \| E_ T(f)-E_ T(g)\| _{k+1,\delta ,T} \leq Ca_ T\| f-g\| _{k+1,\delta ,T}. \]

The constants are independent of $T$, because translation in the cylindrical direction is an isometry, the weight is normalized at $T$, and the shifts of $z$ are uniformly bounded. Differentiating the pullback along the segment from $f$ to $g$ involves one Lie derivative of the fixed form $\eta $ and at most one derivative of $f-g$, and the gain of one derivative in $Q_ T$ restores the norm. For $T$ sufficiently large, $-E_ T$ is therefore a contraction of the closed ball of radius $2Ca_ T$ into itself. Let $f$ be its fixed point.

Step 4: a pluriharmonic function. By the first identity in (26.153), the horizontal component of $\Phi _ f^*\Omega _ S$ vanishes, so $\Theta \wedge \Phi _ f^*\Omega _ S=0$. The pulled-back two-form is nowhere zero and of type $(2,0)$ for $\Phi _ f^*J_ S$, so $\Theta $ is of type $(1,0)$ for this complex structure. Since $\mathrm dz=-(\Theta +\bar\Theta )$ and $\mathrm d\Theta =0$, the function $z$ is pluriharmonic for $\Phi _ f^*J_ S$.

Step 5: the flat metric. Return to $S$ through $A=\Upsilon \circ \Phi _ f$. On each fiber, $f$ is bounded and holomorphic on a punctured disk, so it extends holomorphically across the center, and its expansion in positive powers of $w$ gives $f(0)=0$. Since $f$ is uniformly small, Rouché's theorem, comparing $w\mathrm e^{f(w)}-c$ with $w-c$ on a fixed smaller circle, shows that every sufficiently small value $c$ is attained. Hence the image of $A$ contains a punctured neighborhood of $D$. The function $r=z\circ A^{-1}$ is $C^2$ and pluriharmonic there, hence smooth by elliptic regularity. If $w_ S$ is a local holomorphic defining function for $D$, the unchanged first normal jet of $\Upsilon $ and the bounded factor $\mathrm e^ f$ give

\[ C^{-1}|w|\leq |w_ S\circ A|\leq C|w|. \]

Thus $r+\log |w_ S|^2$ is bounded and pluriharmonic off $D$. Both this function and its negative are bounded plurisubharmonic functions off $D$, so they extend plurisubharmonically across $D$; the sum of the extensions vanishes almost everywhere, hence everywhere, so the extension is pluriharmonic. It follows that $F=-r$, which has a logarithmic pole along $D$, satisfies $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F=[D]$ near $D$. Locally, the metric for which the squared norm of the defining section equals $\mathrm e^{F}$ is smooth and flat, since $F-\log |w_ S|^2$ is smooth and pluriharmonic. These local metrics agree on overlaps and give the required unitary flat metric. $\square$

Proposition 26.19.25. Let $D$ be a smooth nonempty compact divisor in a complex surface $S$, and suppose $\mathcal O_ S(D)$ is unitary flat near $D$. Then $\mathcal O_ S(D)$ has a smooth semipositive metric on $S$ whose curvature $B$ has rank at most one and vanishes both near $D$ and outside a relatively compact neighborhood of $D$. If $S$ is compact Kähler, then $\max _ S\operatorname {rank}B=1$.

Proof. Let $s_ D$ be the defining section and $h_0$ a flat metric on a neighborhood $U$ of $D$. Put $F=\log |s_ D|_{h_0}^2$, which is pluriharmonic on $U\setminus D$. Choose a relatively compact tubular neighborhood $U_0\Subset U$ of $D$. Since $F$ tends uniformly to $-\infty $ along $D$ and is bounded on $\partial U_0$, after adding a constant to $F$ we may assume $F>0$ near $\partial U_0$. Choose a smooth concave function $\chi $ and a constant $c$ with $\chi (t)=t+c$ for $t\leq -1$ and $\chi (t)=0$ for $t\geq 0$. Define $\widetilde F=\chi (F)$ on $U_0$, extended by zero outside $U_0$, and define a metric $h$ on $S\setminus D$ by

\[ |s_ D|_ h^2=\mathrm e^{\widetilde F}. \]

Near $D$ this gives $h=\mathrm e^ c h_0$, so $h$ extends smoothly across $D$; it is also smooth near $\partial U_0$, where $\widetilde F=0$. By the Poincaré–Lelong formula its curvature is $B=[D]-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widetilde F$, and off $D$, since $F$ is pluriharmonic,

\[ B=-\chi ''(F)\, \mathrm dF\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}F\geq 0. \]

This smooth closed form has rank at most one and vanishes where asserted. On a compact Kähler surface with Kähler form $\kappa $,

\[ \int _ SB\wedge \kappa =\int _ D\kappa >0, \]

so $B$ is not identically zero and its maximal rank is one. $\square$

Corollary 26.19.26. Let $S$ be a compact Kähler surface and $D$ a smooth nonempty divisor whose line bundle is unitary flat near $D$, and let $B$ be the curvature form of proposition 26.19.25. For every smooth function $f>0$, every Hermitian form $h$ and every smooth positive probability form $\mu $, there are constants $L,U>0$ such that

\[ L\epsilon \leq \mathcal V_\mu (fB+\epsilon h) \leq U\epsilon ,\qquad 0<\epsilon \leq 1. \]

For a Kähler form $\kappa $, the closed background $B$ itself satisfies

\[ \mathcal V_\mu (B+\epsilon \kappa ) =2\epsilon \int _ D\kappa +\epsilon ^2\int _ S\kappa ^2. \]

For suitable $f$, the background $fB$ is not closed.

Proof. The inequalities $(\min f)B\leq fB\leq (\max f)B$ and proposition 26.19.19 give the two-sided bounds. The polynomial follows from the closed nef formula and $B^2=0$. At a point where $B$ has rank one, choose a smooth positive $f$ whose differential has a nonzero component along the kernel of $B$. Then $\mathrm d(fB)=\mathrm df\wedge B\ne 0$ there. $\square$

Recall that a line bundle is semiample if some positive power of it is generated by global sections, and that $|kD|$ denotes the linear system of effective divisors linearly equivalent to $kD$. A line bundle on a curve is torsion if some positive power is trivial; the least such power is its exact order.

Proposition 26.19.27. Let $S$ be obtained from $\mathbb P^2$ by nine successive point blowups, each centered on the strict transform of a fixed smooth cubic. The final strict transform $D$ of the cubic is a smooth elliptic anticanonical curve with $D^2=0$, and the anticanonical bundle has a smooth semipositive metric whose curvature has rank at most one, as in proposition 26.19.25. The anticanonical bundle is semiample exactly when $N=\mathcal O_ D(D)$ is torsion. If $N$ has exact order $m$, then $|mD|$ is basepoint-free and $|kD|=\{ kD\} $ for $1\leq k<m$. If $N$ is not torsion, then $|kD|=\{ kD\} $ for every $k\geq 1$.

Proof. A blowup at a point of the cubic replaces the class of the cubic by its pullback minus the exceptional curve, exactly as it does for the anticanonical class. The strict transform remains isomorphic to the cubic, and its self-intersection decreases by one. After nine steps, $D$ is anticanonical and $D^2=9-9=0$. Theorem 26.19.24, Proposition 26.19.25 give the metric.

For $k\geq 1$ we use the restriction sequences

\[ 0\longrightarrow \mathcal O_ S((k-1)D) \longrightarrow \mathcal O_ S(kD) \longrightarrow N^ k\longrightarrow 0. \]

A nontrivial line bundle of degree zero on an elliptic curve has no nonzero section, since such a section would have a zero divisor of degree zero and would trivialize the bundle. If $N$ is not torsion, induction on $k$ gives $h^0(S,\mathcal O_ S(kD))=h^0(S,\mathcal O_ S)=1$. This proves that every $|kD|$ is the single divisor $kD$, and in particular that the anticanonical bundle is not semiample.

Suppose now that $N$ has exact order $m$. For $1\leq k<m$ we have $H^0(D,N^ k)=0$, and $H^1(D,N^ k)=0$ by Serre duality on the elliptic curve. Since $S$ is rational, $H^1(S,\mathcal O_ S)=0$. The same sequences give $h^0(S,\mathcal O_ S(kD))=1$ and $H^1(S,\mathcal O_ S(kD))=0$ for $0\leq k<m$. For $k=m$ the restriction map to $H^0(D,N^ m)$ is therefore surjective, and the constant section of $N^ m\simeq \mathcal O_ D$ lifts to a section of $\mathcal O_ S(mD)$ that does not vanish on $D$. Together with $s_ D^ m$, this section generates $\mathcal O_ S(mD)$ everywhere. Thus $|mD|$ is basepoint-free, while no smaller positive multiple of $D$ moves.

Finally, non-torsion configurations exist, with nine distinct centers. Fix the first eight points on the cubic and vary the ninth. Under the identification of $D$ with the cubic, the normal bundle is $\mathcal O_ D(3)\otimes \mathcal O_ D(-p_1-\cdots -p_9)$, where $\mathcal O_ D(3)$ is the restriction of $\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^2}(3)$. As $p_9$ varies, these bundles run through the whole group of line bundles of degree zero, by the Abel map of the elliptic curve. The torsion subgroup is countable, whereas the curve is uncountable, so even after excluding the first eight points there are non-torsion choices. For them, corollary 26.19.26 gives nonclosed backgrounds of order one, although the anticanonical bundle is not semiample. $\square$

26.19.8 Flat divisors and Hartogs extension

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

All the nine-point blowups of proposition 26.19.27 carry backgrounds of order one, so the order of degeneration does not distinguish the torsion case, in which a multiple of $D$ moves in a basepoint-free pencil, from the non-torsion case, in which no multiple of $D$ moves. The difference is visible in the complex geometry near $D$. On the tube where the curvature form of proposition 26.19.25 vanishes, the logarithm of the flat norm of the defining section is pluriharmonic. Its regular level sets are therefore Levi-flat, that is, real hypersurfaces foliated by complex hypersurfaces. These complex leaves are governed by the unitary holonomy of the normal bundle: finite holonomy gives compact fibers of a local holomorphic function, whereas infinite holonomy makes every leaf dense and forces every holomorphic function on the punctured tube to be constant. In both cases the complement of $D$ is not Stein.

In this subsection $S$ is a complex manifold of dimension $n\geq 2$ and $D\subset S$ is a smooth compact connected divisor. Suppose that $L=\mathcal O_ S(D)$ has a unitary flat metric $h$ on a neighborhood of $D$. Write $s_ D$ for its defining section and $N=L|_ D$ for the normal bundle, with the induced metric and flat connection, and let $\chi :\pi _1(D)\to \mathrm U(1)$ be the holonomy character of $N$, which describes parallel transport around closed loops. In parallel unit frames, $s_ D$ is represented by holomorphic functions $W_ j$ satisfying $W_ j=t_{jk}W_ k$ with locally constant $t_{jk}\in \mathrm U(1)$. The flat connection defines on each circle bundle $\{ v\in N:|v|=r\} $ a foliation whose leaves are given locally, in parallel frames, by $\{ w=\text{constant}\} $; we call it the flat horizontal foliation.

Lemma 26.19.28. There are $R>0$, a neighborhood $U$ of $D$ and a smooth diffeomorphism

\[ \Upsilon :\{ v\in N:|v|<R\} \longrightarrow U \]

that is the identity on the zero section and preserves the defining function: in each parallel normal frame, $W_ j(\Upsilon (p,w))=w$. In particular, $|s_ D|_ h\circ \Upsilon =|w|$. For $0<r<R$, the set

\[ M_ r=\{ x\in U:|s_ D(x)|_ h=r\} \]

is a compact connected Levi-flat hypersurface, and $\Upsilon $ maps the leaves of the flat horizontal foliation of $\{ |w|=r\} $ onto the complex leaves of $M_ r$.

Proof. Choose a smooth normal exponential map $\Upsilon _0$ whose first normal derivative is complex linear; the derivative of $s_ D$ identifies the normal bundle with $L|_ D$, and we use this identification to fix that first derivative. Parallel transport along the normal rays identifies $\Upsilon _0^*L$ with the pullback of $N$. In compatible parallel frames one then has

\[ W_ j(\Upsilon _0(p,a))=a+O(|a|^2). \]

For each $p$, this is a smooth map between real two-dimensional disks whose derivative at zero is the identity. The inverse function theorem with parameters, uniformly over the compact base $D$, gives a unique small $a=a(p,w)$ with $W_ j(\Upsilon _0(p,a))=w$. It depends smoothly on $(p,w)$, and its first derivative in $w$ at $w=0$ is the identity. On an overlap of frames, $a$ and $w$ change by the same constant unitary factor, so uniqueness makes these corrections agree. After decreasing $R$, the map $(p,w)\mapsto (p,a(p,w))$ is a diffeomorphism of a tube onto its image, and its composition with $\Upsilon _0$ is the required map $\Upsilon $.

Off $D$, the function $F=\log |s_ D|_ h^2$ is pluriharmonic, and its radial derivative in the tube is nonzero. Hence every $M_ r$ is a smooth hypersurface; it is compact and connected, being a circle bundle over $D$. Locally its complex tangent space is $\ker \mathrm dW_ j$, so it is Levi-flat, with complex leaves the hypersurfaces $\{ W_ j=\text{constant}\} $. The identity $W_ j\circ \Upsilon =w$ pulls this distribution back to $\{ \mathrm dw=0\} $ in parallel frames, which is the flat horizontal foliation of the circle bundle. The map $\Upsilon $ is only a smooth tubular identification; its base projection need not be holomorphic. $\square$

Proposition 26.19.29. In this setting, $N$ is torsion as a holomorphic line bundle if and only if its unitary holonomy has finite image. If $N$ has exact order $m$, then a sufficiently small tube $U$ admits a proper surjective holomorphic map

\[ \phi :U\longrightarrow \{ z\in \mathbb C:|z|<R^ m\} \]

with connected fibers and $\operatorname {div}(\phi )=mD$. Its fibers over nonzero values are smooth compact complex hypersurfaces.

Proof. If $\chi ^ m=1$, a parallel nowhere-zero section trivializes $N^ m$ holomorphically. Conversely, if a holomorphic section $e$ trivializes $N^ m$, then $\log |e|^2$ is pluriharmonic on the compact connected manifold $D$, hence constant by the maximum principle. In parallel holomorphic frames the coefficient of $e$ is a holomorphic function of constant modulus, hence constant. Therefore $e$ is parallel and $\chi ^ m=1$. Consequently the least such $m$ is both the order of $N$ as a torsion line bundle and the order of the finite holonomy group.

By lemma 26.19.28, the tube $U$ retracts onto $D$. Hence the flat bundle $L^ m$ on $U$ has trivial holonomy and a global parallel section $e_ U$ of unit length. Define $\phi =s_ D^ m/e_ U$. Then $\phi $ is holomorphic, $\operatorname {div}(\phi )=mD$ and $|\phi |=|s_ D|_ h^ m$. The inverse image of a compact subset of the target lies in a smaller closed normal disk bundle and is closed there, which proves properness, and the tubular model shows that every value in the disk is attained.

Choose local parallel unit frames $e_ j$ of $L$ with $e_ j^ m=e_ U$, so that $\phi =W_ j^ m$. In the tubular model, the fiber over a nonzero value consists, over each point of $D$, of the $m$ roots of that value. Parallel transport acts on these roots through $\chi $, whose image has exact order $m$ and therefore acts transitively. Thus this fiber is a connected covering of $D$, as a real manifold. Since $\mathrm d\phi =mW_ j^{m-1}\mathrm dW_ j$, it is a smooth complex hypersurface, and it is compact because $\phi $ is proper. The fiber over zero has connected support $D$, which completes the proof that all fibers are connected. $\square$

Theorem 26.19.30. Suppose $N$ is not torsion. Then every complex leaf of $M_ r$ is dense in $M_ r$, and every holomorphic function on $U\setminus D$ is constant:

\[ H^0(U\setminus D,\mathcal O_ S)=\mathbb C. \]

Suppose moreover that $S$ is connected. For every compact set $K\subset S\setminus D$ such that $(S\setminus D)\setminus K$ is connected, every holomorphic function on $(S\setminus D)\setminus K$ is constant, and in particular extends holomorphically to $S\setminus D$. Similarly, for every connected open neighborhood $V$ of $D$, every holomorphic function on $V\setminus D$ is constant and extends to $S\setminus D$.

Proof. By the preceding proposition, $\chi (\pi _1(D))$ is an infinite subgroup of $\mathrm U(1)$. Such a subgroup is dense, since a proper closed subgroup of the circle is finite, as one sees by lifting it to a closed subgroup of $\mathbb R$ containing $2\pi \mathbb Z$. The circle bundle with its flat horizontal foliation is the suspension of $\chi $, that is, the quotient of $\widetilde D\times S^1$ by the diagonal action of $\pi _1(D)$, acting on $S^1$ through $\chi $. A leaf projects onto $D$, and its intersection with each fiber contains the holonomy orbit of any one of its points, which is dense in the fiber. In a local trivialization, horizontal transport maps the orbits in nearby fibers to each other by a fixed rotation, so the leaf is dense in the whole circle bundle. By lemma 26.19.28, every complex leaf is dense in $M_ r$.

Let $f$ be holomorphic on $U\setminus D$. Its modulus attains a maximum on the compact set $M_ r$. On the complex leaf through a maximum point, $|f|$ is bounded by this maximum and attains it, so the maximum modulus principle makes $f$ constant on the connected leaf. By density and continuity, $f$ equals the same constant $c$ on $M_ r$. Near any point of $M_ r$, choose holomorphic coordinates $(z,W_ j)$ with $z\in \mathbb C^{n-1}$. For each fixed $z$, the holomorphic function $W_ j\mapsto f(z,W_ j)-c$ vanishes on an arc of the circle $|W_ j|=r$, so it vanishes on the coordinate neighborhood by the one-variable identity theorem. Analytic continuation on the connected punctured tube gives $f\equiv c$.

A compact set $K\subset S\setminus D$ is disjoint from a sufficiently small tube around the compact divisor $D$. A holomorphic function on $(S\setminus D)\setminus K$ is therefore constant on this punctured tube, and by the identity theorem on the connected set $(S\setminus D)\setminus K$ it is constant everywhere; the constant function is the required extension. Similarly, every open neighborhood $V$ of $D$ contains a full tube, and $V\setminus D$ is connected, because paths in the connected manifold $V$ can be perturbed off the real codimension-two submanifold $D$. The identity theorem gives the assertion for $V$. The same path argument shows that $S\setminus D$ is connected. $\square$

Recall that a complex manifold is holomorphically separable if its holomorphic functions separate points, and that a Stein manifold is holomorphically separable and holomorphically convex (see the next subsection for the latter notion).

Corollary 26.19.31. For any holonomy of $N$, the punctured tube $U\setminus D$ admits no smooth strictly plurisubharmonic function, and $S\setminus D$ is not Stein. In the non-torsion nine-point blowups of proposition 26.19.27, every holomorphic function on the complement of $D$ is constant, and the Hartogs extension of theorem 26.19.30 across compact holes holds.

Proof. The restriction of a smooth strictly plurisubharmonic function on $U\setminus D$ to a complex leaf of $M_ r$ is strictly plurisubharmonic. At a maximum point on the compact set $M_ r$, this restriction has a local maximum on the leaf, which has positive dimension because $n\geq 2$; this contradicts the maximum principle.

In the finite-holonomy case, each fiber of the map of proposition 26.19.29 over a nonzero value is a connected compact complex hypersurface of positive dimension. By the maximum modulus principle, every holomorphic function on $S\setminus D$ is constant on it, so two distinct points of the fiber cannot be separated. In the infinite-holonomy case, theorem 26.19.30 makes every holomorphic function constant on the punctured tube, which again contradicts holomorphic separability. In both cases $S\setminus D$ is not Stein. Finally, theorem 26.19.24 gives the flat neighborhood for the nine-point blowups, whose normal bundle is non-torsion by assumption, and theorem 26.19.30, also with $K=\varnothing $, gives the last assertion. $\square$

26.19.9 Punctured line bundles and elliptic quotients

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The nine-point blowups show that a background with a well-defined order of degeneration can have very different holomorphic geometry near its null locus. The remaining subsections give a second instance of this contrast, for Kähler classes on a ruled surface over an elliptic curve. A Kähler class determines a holomorphic extension of the tangent bundle by the trivial line bundle and an affine bundle modeled on the cotangent bundle, its cotangent torsor. The tautological line bundle of the extension is big, but its section algebra is not finitely generated, and the torsor is never Stein. In this subsection we construct the relevant quotients and show that they are not Stein, although their universal covers are. The obstruction now comes from fiberwise Laurent expansions on the punctured total space of a line bundle of nonzero degree, rather than from the flat circle levels used in corollary 26.19.31.

For a compact subset $K$ of a complex manifold $Y$, its holomorphic hull is

\[ \widehat K_ Y=\{ y\in Y:|f(y)|\leq \max _ K|f|\text{ for every }f\in \mathcal O(Y)\} . \]

The manifold $Y$ is holomorphically convex if every such hull is compact.

Lemma 26.19.32. Let $C$ be a compact connected Riemann surface, and let $A\to C$ be a holomorphic line bundle of positive degree. Write $A^\times $ for its total space with the zero section removed. Then $A^\times $ is not holomorphically convex. More precisely, put $B=A^*$ and choose a smooth Hermitian norm on $B$. Every holomorphic function on $B^\times $ extends holomorphically to $B$, and the holomorphic hull in $B^\times $ of

\[ K_ r=\{ v\in B:|v|=r\} ,\qquad r>0, \]

contains $\{ v:0<|v|\leq r\} $ and is not compact.

Proof. In a local holomorphic frame of $B$, a function $f\in \mathcal O(B^\times )$ has a fiberwise Laurent expansion

\[ f(z,t)=\sum _{k\in \mathbb Z}a_ k(z)t^ k. \]

By the Cauchy integral formula, each coefficient is holomorphic in $z$, and under a change of frame the coefficients $a_ k$ transform as the components of global sections of $B^{-k}$. For $k<0$ this line bundle has negative degree, so it has no nonzero section: the zero divisor of a nonzero holomorphic section has nonnegative degree. Thus all negative coefficients vanish. On a smaller base chart, Cauchy estimates on a fixed fiber circle give locally uniform convergence of the remaining power series at $t=0$. These extensions agree on overlaps and define a holomorphic function on all of $B$.

The maximum modulus principle on the disk in each fiber gives

\[ |f(v)|\leq \max _{K_ r}|f|\qquad (0<|v|\leq r). \]

The circle bundle $K_ r$ is compact because $C$ is compact. A sequence in one fiber whose norms tend to zero lies in the hull and has no convergent subsequence in $B^\times $, so the hull is not compact. Finally, fiberwise inversion identifies $A^\times $ biholomorphically with $B^\times $: a nonzero $v\in A$ corresponds to the unique $\ell \in A^*$ with $\ell (v)=1$. This proves the assertion for $A^\times $. $\square$

The following quotient isolates this obstruction in an affine-bundle model.

Let $\Lambda =\mathbb Z+\mathbb Z\tau \subset \mathbb C$, with $\operatorname {Im}\tau >0$, and put $E=\mathbb C/\Lambda $. A factor of automorphy is a family of nowhere-zero holomorphic functions $b_\lambda (z)$, $\lambda \in \Lambda $, satisfying

\[ b_{\lambda +\gamma }(z)=b_\lambda (z+\gamma )b_\gamma (z). \]

This cocycle identity says that $\lambda \cdot (z,t)=(z+\lambda ,b_\lambda (z)t)$ is an action of $\Lambda $ on $\mathbb C_ z\times \mathbb C_ t$, and the quotient

\[ A=(\mathbb C_ z\times \mathbb C_ t)/\Lambda \]

is a holomorphic line bundle over $E$. Suppose that $A$ has positive degree.

Example 26.19.33. The factors

\[ b_{m\tau +n}(z)=\exp \bigl (-\pi \mathrm{i}(m^2\tau +2mz)\bigr ) \]

define a line bundle of degree one on $E$.

Proof. For $\gamma =r\tau +s$, the cocycle identity follows from

\[ \begin{aligned} b_{m\tau +n}(z+\gamma )b_{r\tau +s}(z) & =b_{(m+r)\tau +n+s}(z)\exp (-2\pi \mathrm{i}ms)\\ & =b_{(m+r)\tau +n+s}(z). \end{aligned} \]

Put $v=\operatorname {Im}\tau $. The squared norm

\[ |t|^2\exp \bigl (-2\pi (\operatorname {Im}z)^2/v\bigr ) \]

is invariant under the action, because $\operatorname {Im}(z+m\tau +n)=\operatorname {Im}z+mv$ and

\[ |b_{m\tau +n}(z)|^2=\exp \bigl (2\pi (m^2v+2m\operatorname {Im}z)\bigr ). \]

Its first Chern form is

\[ \frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\pi }\partial \bar\partial \left(\frac{2\pi (\operatorname {Im}z)^2}{v}\right) =\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2v}\, \mathrm{d}z\wedge \mathrm{d}\bar z. \]

This positive form integrates to one over a fundamental parallelogram, which has area $v$. Thus the degree is one. $\square$

On

\[ Q=\{ ([x_0:x_1],[w_0:w_1])\in \mathbb P^1\times \mathbb P^1: \Delta =x_0w_1-x_1w_0\ne 0\} , \]

the complement of the diagonal, define the holomorphic function $\sigma =x_1w_0/\Delta $.

Proposition 26.19.34. Fix $\kappa \in \mathbb C^*$ and $K\in \mathbb C$. For $\lambda =m\tau +n\in \Lambda $, the formula

\[ \begin{aligned} \lambda \cdot (z,u,[x],[w])={}& \bigl (z+\lambda ,u+m(K+\kappa \sigma ),\\ & [x_0:b_\lambda (z)x_1],[w_0:b_\lambda (z)w_1]\bigr ) \end{aligned} \]

defines a free and properly discontinuous holomorphic action of $\Lambda $ on $\mathbb C^2\times Q$. The quotient $Z$ is not Stein, and $\sigma $ descends to a holomorphic function on $Z$. If $K\notin \{ 0,\kappa \} $, the fiber

\[ \sigma ^{-1}(-K/\kappa )\simeq \mathbb C\times A^\times \]

is a closed submanifold of $Z$ that is not holomorphically convex. If $K=0$ or $K=\kappa $, the quotient contains a compact elliptic curve. In every case the universal cover of $Z$ is the Stein manifold $\mathbb C^2\times Q$.

Proof. The action. Multiplying $x_1$ and $w_1$ simultaneously by $b_\lambda (z)$ multiplies $\Delta $ and $x_1w_0$ by the same factor, so $\sigma $ is invariant. This invariance and the cocycle identity for $b$ give the group law, including the additivity of the integer $m$ in the $u$-coordinate. Translation in the $z$-coordinate makes the action free and properly discontinuous: only finitely many lattice translates of the projection of one compact set meet another fixed compact set. Hence the quotient $Z$ is a complex manifold, and $\sigma $ descends to a holomorphic function on $Z$.

The fiber of $\sigma $. Set $a=-K/\kappa $. If $a\notin \{ 0,-1\} $, every point of $\{ \sigma =a\} \subset Q$ has $x_0x_1\ne 0$, since $\sigma =0$ where $x_1=0$ and $\sigma =-1$ where $x_0=0$. In the chart

\[ (t,y)\longmapsto ([1:t],[y:ty+1]), \]

we have $\Delta =1$, and the equation $\sigma =a$ becomes $ty=a$. Hence this fiber of $\sigma $ on $Q$ is $\mathbb C^*$, with parameter $t$, and the group action on its preimage is

\[ \lambda \cdot (z,u,t)=(z+\lambda ,u,b_\lambda (z)t). \]

This gives the asserted biholomorphism with $\mathbb C\times A^\times $. The submanifold $\{ u=0\} $ of this fiber is closed in the fiber, and therefore closed in $Z$; under fiberwise inversion it is $B^\times $, with $B=A^*$ as in lemma 26.19.32. Let $K_ r$ be the compact circle bundle of that lemma. Every holomorphic function on $Z$ restricts to a holomorphic function on $B^\times $, so the hull of $K_ r$ in $Z$ contains the same sequence with fiber norms tending to zero. This sequence has no convergent subsequence in the closed submanifold, and hence none in $Z$. Thus neither the fiber nor $Z$ is holomorphically convex.

The exceptional parameters. For $K=0$, take $q_+=([1:0],[0:1])$, where $\sigma =0$. The invariant submanifold $\{ u=0,\ q=q_+\} $ descends to a copy of $E$. For $K=\kappa $, use $q_-=([0:1],[1:0])$, where $\sigma =-1$, with the same conclusion. Every holomorphic function on $Z$ is constant on this compact curve by the maximum modulus principle, so $Z$ is not holomorphically separable, and again not Stein.

The universal cover. The map

\[ Q\longrightarrow \{ (s,c,d)\in \mathbb C^3:cd=s(s-1)\} , \qquad ([x],[w])\longmapsto \left(\frac{x_0w_1}{\Delta },\frac{x_0w_0}{\Delta },\frac{x_1w_1}{\Delta }\right) \]

is an isomorphism. Its inverse recovers $[x]$ and $[w]$ as the column space and the row space of the rank-one matrix

\[ \begin{pmatrix} c & s \\ s-1 & d \end{pmatrix}. \]

This matrix never vanishes, and the hypersurface is smooth: simultaneous vanishing of the three partial derivatives of $cd-s(s-1)$ would give $c=d=0$ and $s=1/2$, contradicting its equation. Thus $Q$ is an affine variety and hence Stein. Projection to $[x]\in \mathbb P^1$ makes $Q$ a locally trivial bundle of affine lines. The chart above and the chart over $\{ x_1\ne 0\} $, given by $(v,y_\infty )\mapsto ([v:1],[vy_\infty +1:y_\infty ])$, are related by

\[ v=1/t,\qquad y_\infty =-t^2y-t. \]

Since the fibers are contractible, $\pi _1(Q)=\pi _1(\mathbb P^1)=0$. Therefore $\mathbb C^2\times Q$ is simply connected and Stein, and it is the universal cover of $Z$. $\square$

26.19.10 Cotangent torsors on split elliptic ruled surfaces

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now show that the quotients of proposition 26.19.34 are the cotangent torsors of a family of extensions on the ruled surface $M=\mathbb P_{\mathrm{lines}}(\mathcal O_ E\oplus A)$, a family that contains the canonical extensions of all Kähler classes. An extension of the trivial line bundle by the cotangent bundle is determined, up to isomorphism, by a class in $H^1(M,\Omega ^1_ M)$. This group is two-dimensional: one component comes from the elliptic base and one from the ruling fibers. In the quotient, the base component appears as the constant $K$ and the ruling component as the coefficient $\kappa $ of $\sigma $.

Keep $E=\mathbb C/\Lambda $ with $\Lambda =\mathbb Z+\mathbb Z\tau $, and let $\vartheta _ E$ be the holomorphic one-form on $E$ whose pullback to $\mathbb C$ is $\mathrm{d}z$. On a complex manifold $Y$, a holomorphic extension of vector bundles

\[ 0\longrightarrow \Omega _ Y^1\longrightarrow W \xrightarrow {p_ W}\mathcal O_ Y\longrightarrow 0 \]

defines the affine bundle $T_ W=p_ W^{-1}(1)$, modeled on $\Omega _ Y^1$, which we call its cotangent torsor. Choosing local holomorphic sections $s_ i$ of $W$ with $p_ W(s_ i)=1$, the differences $s_ j-s_ i$ form a Čech cocycle with values in $\Omega ^1_ Y$; its class in $H^1(Y,\Omega _ Y^1)$ is the class of the extension, and it determines the extension up to isomorphism. Dually, a class $\alpha \in H^1(Y,\Omega ^1_ Y)$ determines an extension $0\to \mathcal O_ Y\to V\to T_ Y\to 0$; when $\alpha $ is the class of a Kähler form, $V$ is the canonical extension of that Kähler class.

Lemma 26.19.35. Choose local inverses $z_ i:U_ i\to \mathbb C$ of the projection $\mathbb C\to E$, and on each component of an overlap write

\[ z_ i-z_ j=m_{ij}\tau +n_{ij},\qquad m_{ij},n_{ij}\in \mathbb Z. \]

The cocycle $\{ m_{ij}\vartheta _ E\} $ defines a nonzero class $\eta \in H^1(E,\Omega _ E^1)$, which spans this one-dimensional space.

Proof. Consider the vector bundle obtained from $\mathbb C_ z\times \mathbb C_ a\times \mathbb C_ b$ by the action

\[ (m\tau +n)\cdot (z,a,b)=(z+m\tau +n,a+mb,b). \]

The coordinate $b$ gives a map to $\mathcal O_ E$, which defines an extension of $\mathcal O_ E$ by $\Omega _ E^1$, the kernel coordinate $a$ corresponding to $a\vartheta _ E$. The local lifts $s_ i=(z_ i,0,1)$ satisfy $s_ j-s_ i=m_{ij}\vartheta _ E$, as one sees by expressing $s_ i$ in the coordinates over $z_ j$. Thus the extension has class $\eta $.

If it split, the pullback of a holomorphic lift of $1$ to $\mathbb C$ would have the form $(z,g(z),1)$ with $g$ entire and

\[ g(z+1)=g(z),\qquad g(z+\tau )=g(z)+1. \]

The derivative $g'$ would be lattice periodic, hence constant by the maximum principle on the compact torus. The first identity then forces this constant to be zero, contradicting the second. Hence $\eta \ne 0$. Serre duality on the compact connected curve gives $H^1(E,\Omega _ E^1)\simeq H^0(E,\mathcal O_ E)^*\simeq \mathbb C$, which proves the spanning assertion. $\square$

Lemma 26.19.36. Every holomorphic line bundle $A$ of positive degree $d$ on $E$ is defined by factors of automorphy of the form

\[ b_{m\tau +n}(z)=\chi _{m\tau +n} \exp \bigl (-\pi \mathrm{i}d(m^2\tau +2mz)\bigr ), \]

where $\chi :\Lambda \to \mathrm U(1)$ is a character. In particular,

\[ \frac{b_{m\tau +n}'(z)}{b_{m\tau +n}(z)}=-2\pi \mathrm{i}dm. \]

Proof. Let $A_ d$ be the $d$th tensor power of the degree-one bundle of example 26.19.33. Its factors are the stated exponential factors, and its degree is $d$. The bundle $F=A\otimes A_ d^*$ has degree zero. Start with a smooth Hermitian metric on $F$; its Chern curvature has integral zero. Solving the scalar Poisson equation on the flat compact torus gives a smooth real function whose addition to the weight of the metric makes the curvature vanish. The resulting Chern connection is flat and unitary.

On the simply connected cover $\mathbb C$, parallel transport gives a global parallel frame of unit length of the pullback of $F$. It is holomorphic, because the $(0,1)$-part of the Chern connection is the $\bar\partial $-operator of $F$. Each lattice element changes this frame by a constant unitary factor, and these factors form a character. Tensoring with the given frame of $A_ d$ gives factors of the stated form for $A$, and differentiation gives the last formula. $\square$

Let $M=\mathbb P_{\mathrm{lines}}(\mathcal O_ E\oplus A)$ be the bundle of lines in $\mathcal O_ E\oplus A$, and let $f:M\to E$ be its projection. Let $C_\infty =\mathbb P_{\mathrm{lines}}(A)$ be the section given by the lines in $A$. Over $U_ i$ the line spanned by $(1,t_ ie_ i)$, where $e_ i$ is the local frame of $A$, defines an affine coordinate $t_ i$ away from $C_\infty $, and $v_ i=1/t_ i$ is a coordinate away from the other section $\mathbb P_{\mathrm{lines}}(\mathcal O_ E)$. Over $U_ i\cap U_ j$,

\[ t_ i=b_{ij}t_ j,\quad v_ i=b_{ij}^{-1}v_ j, \qquad b_{ij}=b_{m_{ij}\tau +n_{ij}}(z_ j). \]

Define $\xi \in H^1(M,\Omega _ M^1)$ as the logarithmic cocycle of $\mathcal O_ M(-C_\infty )$. More precisely, use the cover by the charts with indices $(i,0)$ and $(i,\infty )$, the local defining functions $q_{i,0}=1$ and $q_{i,\infty }=v_ i$ of $C_\infty $, and the cocycle $\mathrm{d}\log (q_{j,b}/q_{i,a})$. This convention contains no factor $1/(2\pi \mathrm{i})$.

Lemma 26.19.37. There is a direct sum decomposition

\[ H^1(M,\Omega _ M^1)=\mathbb C\xi \oplus \mathbb C f^*\eta . \]

The restriction of a class $c=\rho \xi +Kf^*\eta $ to any ruling fiber is nonzero if and only if $\rho \ne 0$.

Proof. The relative cotangent sequence is

\[ 0\longrightarrow f^*\Omega _ E^1\longrightarrow \Omega _ M^1 \longrightarrow \Omega _{M/E}^1\longrightarrow 0. \]

The two affine charts of a projective line give the direct images

\[ f_*\mathcal O_ M=\mathcal O_ E,\quad R^1f_*\mathcal O_ M=0, \qquad f_*\Omega _{M/E}^1=0,\quad R^1f_*\Omega _{M/E}^1=\mathcal O_ E. \]

For the last identity, a Laurent cocycle $g(z,t)\, \mathrm{d}t$ can be reduced, by subtracting forms holomorphic on the two charts, to its coefficient of $\mathrm{d}t/t$. Its residue is unchanged by $t_ i=b_{ij}t_ j$, with differentials taken relative to the base, so the local generators glue to the trivial line bundle. For functions, the two halves of a Laurent series exhaust the cocycle, which gives $R^1f_*\mathcal O_ M=0$. The direct images in degree zero follow because $\mathbb P^1$ has no nonconstant holomorphic functions and no nonzero holomorphic one-forms.

The Leray spectral sequences and the fact that the base is a curve now give

\[ H^1(M,f^*\Omega _ E^1)\simeq H^1(E,\Omega _ E^1),\quad H^2(M,f^*\Omega _ E^1)=0,\quad H^1(M,\Omega _{M/E}^1)\simeq \mathbb C, \]

together with $H^0(M,\Omega ^1_{M/E})=0$. The cohomology sequence of the relative cotangent sequence is therefore the exact sequence

\[ 0\longrightarrow \mathbb C f^*\eta \longrightarrow H^1(M,\Omega _ M^1)\longrightarrow \mathbb C\longrightarrow 0. \]

On a fiber, the class $\xi $ is represented on the two affine charts by $\mathrm{d}\log v=-\mathrm{d}t/t$, whose residue is nonzero, so $\xi $ splits the sequence. The pulled-back class $f^*\eta $ restricts to zero on every fiber. This proves both assertions. $\square$

Theorem 26.19.38. Let $W$ be a holomorphic extension of $\mathcal O_ M$ by $\Omega _ M^1$ with class $c=\rho \xi +Kf^*\eta $, where $\rho \ne 0$. Then $T_ W$ is biholomorphic to the quotient of proposition 26.19.34, with the factors of lemma 26.19.36 and

\[ \kappa =2\pi \mathrm{i}d\rho . \]

In particular, $T_ W$ is not Stein, while its universal cover is Stein. This applies to the cotangent torsor of the canonical extension of every Kähler class on $M$.

Proof. Step 1: a model torsor. Over $\mathbb C_ z\times \mathbb P^1$, glue two affine cotangent charts by

\[ v=1/t,\qquad u_\infty =u,\qquad y_\infty =-t^2y-\rho t. \]

Here $(u,y)$ are the coefficients of the covector $u\, \mathrm{d}z+y\, \mathrm{d}t$, and the linear part of the gluing is the change of these coefficients under $v=1/t$. If $s_0$ and $s_\infty $ are the zero sections of the two charts, the affine term says that

\[ s_\infty -s_0=\rho \, \mathrm{d}v/v. \]

For $\lambda =m\tau +n$, give the first chart the lattice action

\[ (z,t,u,y)\longmapsto \bigl (z+\lambda ,b_\lambda (z)t, u+Km-(b_\lambda '/b_\lambda )ty,y/b_\lambda (z)\bigr ). \]

Its linear part is the transport of covectors under $(z,t)\mapsto (z+\lambda ,b_\lambda (z)t)$, and the cocycle identity for the factors and the invariance of $ty$ give the group law. In the other chart the action becomes

\[ (z,v,u_\infty ,y_\infty )\longmapsto \bigl (z+\lambda ,b_\lambda ^{-1}v, u_\infty +Km+(b_\lambda '/b_\lambda )(v y_\infty +\rho ), b_\lambda y_\infty \bigr ), \]

which is holomorphic also at $v=0$, and the two formulas agree on the overlap. Translation in $z$ makes the action free and properly discontinuous.

Step 2: its class. We compute the class of the quotient torsor using the local lifts $z_ i$. On the two types of overlaps over the base, the zero sections satisfy

\[ \begin{aligned} s_{j,0}-s_{i,0}& =Km_{ij}f^*\vartheta _ E,\\ s_{j,\infty }-s_{i,\infty } & =Km_{ij}f^*\vartheta _ E+\rho \, \mathrm{d}\log (v_ j/v_ i). \end{aligned} \]

For a mixed overlap, these formulas and $s_{j,\infty }-s_{j,0}=\rho \, \mathrm{d}v_ j/v_ j$ give

\[ s_{j,\infty }-s_{i,0}=Km_{ij}f^*\vartheta _ E+\rho \, \mathrm{d}v_ j/v_ j. \]

These are exactly the cocycles defining $Kf^*\eta +\rho \xi $. Two extensions with the same class have splitting cocycles that differ by a coboundary, and changing their local lifts by that coboundary identifies their inverse images of $1$. Thus the quotient torsor is $T_ W$.

Step 3: identification with the quotient. Since $\rho \ne 0$, set $Y=y/\rho $ and $Y_\infty =y_\infty /\rho $. Their transition is $Y_\infty =-t^2Y-t$. The maps

\[ (t,Y)\longmapsto ([1:t],[Y:tY+1]),\qquad (v,Y_\infty )\longmapsto ([v:1],[vY_\infty +1:Y_\infty ]) \]

agree on overlaps and identify the glued charts with $Q$. In the first chart $\sigma =tY=ty/\rho $. Substituting $b_\lambda '/b_\lambda =-2\pi \mathrm{i}dm$ turns the lattice action into the action of proposition 26.19.34 with $\kappa =2\pi \mathrm{i}d\rho $. That proposition gives the assertions on the Stein property.

Step 4: Kähler classes. The restriction of a Kähler class to a ruling fiber is nonzero, because its integral over the fiber is positive. For the canonical extension $0\to \mathcal O_ M\to V\to T_ M\to 0$ of a Kähler class, the dual extension of $\mathcal O_ M$ by $\Omega ^1_ M$ has the negative of that class, which still has nonzero restriction to the fibers; by lemma 26.19.37 its ruling component $\rho $ is nonzero. Finally, we use the quotient convention for projectivization: $\mathbb P(V)$ parametrizes the one-dimensional quotients of the fibers of $V$, that is, the lines in $V^*$. The complement $\mathbb P(V)\setminus \mathbb P(T_ M)$ consists of the lines in $V^*$ not contained in $\Omega ^1_ M$, and it identifies with $p_ W^{-1}(1)\subset V^*$ by normalizing a nonzero functional to take the value one on the distinguished copy of $\mathcal O_ M$. This proves the final assertion. $\square$

26.19.11 Symmetric tensors on split elliptic ruled surfaces

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

To compute the section algebra of a canonical extension, we first compute that of the tangent bundle of $M$. A holomorphic section of $\operatorname {Sym}^ rT_ M$ is the same as a holomorphic function on the total space of the cotangent bundle that is a homogeneous polynomial of degree $r$ on each fiber. The $\mathbb C^*$-action on the rulings lifts to the cotangent bundle and decomposes these polynomials into weights. Each weight space is the space of sections of an explicit vector bundle on $E$, built from powers of $A$ and symmetric powers of a nonsplit bundle $U$ of rank two. Riemann–Roch then gives exact dimensions: the tautological line bundle on $\mathbb P(T_ M)$ is big, while a square-zero quotient shows that the section algebra is not finitely generated.

Keep $E$, the line bundle $A$ of degree $d>0$, the surface $M$ and the normalized factors $b_\lambda $ of lemma 26.19.36, and put

\[ q_\lambda =-b_\lambda '/b_\lambda =2\pi \mathrm{i}dm, \qquad \lambda =m\tau +n. \]

Write $u\, \mathrm{d}z+y\, \mathrm{d}t$ for a covector in the finite ruling chart. Evaluating covectors on the Euler field $t\partial /\partial t$ of the rulings gives the global fiberwise linear function $h=ty$ on the cotangent bundle. The dimensions of the weight spaces measure how many symmetric tensors there are, and their products detect the failure of finite generation.

Let $U$ be the rank-two vector bundle on $E$ whose sections, pulled back to $\mathbb C$, are the linear polynomials $F(z,h,u)$ in formal variables $h,u$ with entire coefficients satisfying

\[ F(z+\lambda ,h,u)=F(z,h,u-q_\lambda h). \]

The invariant polynomial $h$ and the coefficient of $u$ give an exact sequence $0\to \mathcal O_ Eh\to U\to \mathcal O_ E\to 0$.

Lemma 26.19.39. For every integer $r\geq 0$, the space $H^0(M,\operatorname {Sym}^ rT_ M)$ is the direct sum of finitely many weight spaces, with weights $-r\leq \ell \leq r$. The weight-zero space is $H^0(E,\operatorname {Sym}^ rU)$. For $1\leq k\leq r$, the space of weight $-k$ is

\[ H^0(E,A^ k\otimes \operatorname {Sym}^{r-k}U), \]

and the space of weight $k$ is $H^0(E,A^{-k}\otimes \operatorname {Sym}^{r-k}U)$. Multiplication adds tensor degrees and weights.

Proof. On the cover $\mathbb C\times \mathbb P^1$, the cotangent coordinates of the two ruling charts are related by

\[ v=1/t,\qquad u_\infty =u,\qquad y_\infty =-t^2y, \]

and the lattice acts by

\[ (z,t,u,y)\longmapsto (z+\lambda ,b_\lambda t,u+q_\lambda ty,y/b_\lambda ); \]

both formulas follow by preserving the one-form $u\, \mathrm{d}z+y\, \mathrm{d}t$. In the other ruling chart the action is

\[ (z,v,u_\infty ,y_\infty )\longmapsto (z+\lambda ,b_\lambda ^{-1}v, u_\infty -q_\lambda v y_\infty ,b_\lambda y_\infty ), \]

which is holomorphic at $v=0$ and agrees with the formula in the finite chart. Both formulas preserve $h=ty=-v y_\infty $.
