A function that is homogeneous of degree $r$ on the fibers is a sum of terms $g_ b(z,t)y^ bu^{r-b}$, $0\leq b\leq r$, with $g_ b$ holomorphic on $\mathbb C^2$. Since $y=-v^2y_\infty $, holomorphy at $v=0$ says that $v^{2b}g_ b(z,1/v)$ is holomorphic there; there is no cancellation between different $b$, because the monomials in $u_\infty ,y_\infty $ are linearly independent. Expanding in $t$ therefore gives

\[ g_ b(z,t)=\sum _{a=0}^{2b}c_{a,b}(z)t^ a, \]

with entire coefficients.

Give $t$ weight $1$, $y$ weight $-1$ and $u$ weight $0$, so that $t^ ay^ bu^{r-b}$ has weight $\ell =a-b$. The chart changes and the lattice action preserve this grading; equivalently, it is the grading by the cotangent lift of the global action $t\mapsto ct$, $c\in \mathbb C^*$. The bounds $0\leq a\leq 2b$ and $b\leq r$ give $-r\leq \ell \leq r$.

For $\ell =-k<0$, a homogeneous function of weight $\ell $ can be written uniquely as $y^ kF(z,h,u)$ with $F$ homogeneous of degree $r-k$. Its invariance is equivalent to

\[ F(z+\lambda ,h,u) =b_\lambda (z)^ kF(z,h,u-q_\lambda h), \]

which is the transformation rule for sections of $A^ k\otimes \operatorname {Sym}^{r-k}U$. Conversely, each such polynomial defines a function that is holomorphic in both ruling charts, because its exponents satisfy $0\leq a\leq 2b$. For $\ell =k>0$, the condition $a\leq 2b$ shows that a function of weight $\ell $ can be written uniquely as $t^ kh^ kF(z,h,u)$, with $F$ of the same remaining degree $r-k$. Its invariance involves the factor $b_\lambda ^{-k}$, which gives $A^{-k}\otimes \operatorname {Sym}^{r-k}U$. Weight zero consists exactly of the homogeneous polynomials $F(z,h,u)$ with the transformation rule of $\operatorname {Sym}^ rU$. Products of polynomial functions add degrees and weights, which proves the last assertion. $\square$

Lemma 26.19.40. The extension $U$ does not split. For every $s\geq 0$,

\[ H^0(E,\operatorname {Sym}^ sU)=\mathbb C h^ s. \]

For $k\geq 1$ and $s\geq 0$,

\[ \begin{aligned} H^0(E,A^{-k}\otimes \operatorname {Sym}^ sU)& =0,\\ H^1(E,A^ k\otimes \operatorname {Sym}^ sU)& =0,\\ h^0(E,A^ k\otimes \operatorname {Sym}^ sU)& =kd(s+1), \end{aligned} \]

where $h^0=\dim H^0$. For $s\geq 1$, taking the coefficient of $u^ s$ defines a surjection

\[ H^0(E,A\otimes \operatorname {Sym}^ sU)\longrightarrow H^0(E,A), \]

whose kernel is $hH^0(E,A\otimes \operatorname {Sym}^{s-1}U)$.

Proof. A splitting of $U$ would give an invariant section $u+a(z)h$ lifting $1$, with $a$ entire and

\[ a(z+1)=a(z),\qquad a(z+\tau )=a(z)-2\pi \mathrm{i}d. \]

The derivative $a'$ would then be lattice periodic, hence constant on the compact torus. The first identity forces this constant to vanish, contradicting the second. Thus the extension does not split.

More generally, write an invariant polynomial of degree $s$ as

\[ F(z,h,u)=\sum _{j=0}^ s a_ j(z)h^{s-j}u^ j. \]

If its largest nonzero $u$-degree is $j>0$, then $a_ j$ is lattice periodic, hence a nonzero constant. Comparing the next coefficients gives

\[ a_{j-1}(z+1)=a_{j-1}(z),\qquad a_{j-1}(z+\tau )=a_{j-1}(z)-j(2\pi \mathrm{i}d)a_ j, \]

and the same derivative argument gives a contradiction. Hence $F=a_0h^ s$ with $a_0$ constant; this also covers $s=0$.

The powers of the invariant line $\mathcal O_ Eh$ give a filtration of $\operatorname {Sym}^ sU$ whose $s+1$ successive quotients are trivial line bundles. A line bundle of negative degree on a compact curve has no nonzero section, since the zero divisor of a nonzero section has nonnegative degree. Hence the filtration twisted by $A^{-k}$ has no global sections. Serre duality and $\Omega _ E^1\simeq \mathcal O_ E$ give $H^1(E,A^ k)=0$, and the cohomology sequences of the filtration twisted by $A^ k$ then give $H^1(E,A^ k\otimes \operatorname {Sym}^ sU)=0$. Riemann–Roch on the curve of genus one, for a bundle of rank $s+1$ and degree $kd(s+1)$, gives the dimension formula.

Finally, the leading coefficient gives the exact sequence of bundles

\[ 0\longrightarrow A\otimes \operatorname {Sym}^{s-1}U \xrightarrow {\ h\ }A\otimes \operatorname {Sym}^ sU \longrightarrow A\longrightarrow 0. \]

Indeed, translating $u$ by a multiple of $h$ does not change the leading coefficient, and a polynomial with zero leading coefficient is divisible by $h$. The vanishing of $H^1(E,A\otimes \operatorname {Sym}^{s-1}U)$ just proved gives the asserted surjectivity and kernel on global sections. $\square$

For a line bundle $\mathcal L$ on a projective manifold $Y$ of dimension $N$, recall that its volume is $\operatorname {vol}(\mathcal L)=\limsup _{r\to \infty }N!\, h^0(Y,\mathcal L^ r)/r^ N$, and that $\mathcal L$ is big if and only if $\operatorname {vol}(\mathcal L)>0$. For a vector bundle $F$ we use the quotient convention introduced in the proof of theorem 26.19.38, in which the projection $\pi :\mathbb P(F)\to Y$ satisfies $\pi _*\mathcal O_{\mathbb P(F)}(r)=\operatorname {Sym}^ rF$.

Theorem 26.19.41. For every integer $r\geq 0$,

\[ h^0(M,\operatorname {Sym}^ rT_ M) =1+\frac{d\, r(r+1)(r+2)}6. \]

The tautological line bundle $\mathcal O_{\mathbb P(T_ M)}(1)$ is big and has volume $d$.

Proof. By lemma 26.19.39, lemma 26.19.40, the positive weights contribute nothing, the weight zero contributes one dimension, and the weight $-k$ contributes $kd(r-k+1)$ dimensions. Thus

\[ h^0(M,\operatorname {Sym}^ rT_ M) =1+d\sum _{k=1}^ r k(r-k+1) =1+\frac{d\, r(r+1)(r+2)}6. \]

The elliptic curve and its holomorphic line bundle $A$ are algebraic, so $M$ and $\mathbb P(T_ M)$ are projective. The identity $\pi _*\mathcal O_{\mathbb P(T_ M)}(r)=\operatorname {Sym}^ rT_ M$ identifies the section spaces. Since $\mathbb P(T_ M)$ has dimension three,

\[ \operatorname {vol}\bigl (\mathcal O_{\mathbb P(T_ M)}(1)\bigr ) =\limsup _{r\to \infty }\frac{6h^0(M,\operatorname {Sym}^ rT_ M)}{r^3}=d>0, \]

so the tautological line bundle is big. $\square$

Theorem 26.19.42. The complex algebra

\[ R=\bigoplus _{r\geq 0}H^0(M,\operatorname {Sym}^ rT_ M) \]

is not finitely generated.

Proof. Write $R_{r,k}$ for the component of tensor degree $r$ and weight $-k$. By the preceding results only $k\geq 0$ occurs, multiplication adds both indices, and the subalgebra of weight zero is $\mathbb C[h]$. Let $I$ be the ideal formed by all components with $k>0$, and put

\[ S=R/(hR+I^2). \]

Its component of weight zero is $\mathbb C$, and its ideal $J$ of positive weight index satisfies $J^2=0$, so $S=\mathbb C\oplus J$ as a vector space.

For every $r\geq 1$, products in $I^2$ have weight index at least two. Consequently the component of $S$ of degree $r$ and weight index one is

\[ R_{r,1}/hR_{r-1,1}\simeq H^0(E,A). \]

For $r=1$ this holds because $R_{0,1}=0$, and for $r\geq 2$ it is the last assertion of lemma 26.19.40 with $s=r-1$. Each of these components has dimension $d>0$, and they lie in distinct degrees, so $J$ is infinite dimensional.

If $R$ were finitely generated, its quotient $S$ would be finitely generated too. Write a finite set of algebra generators of $S$ as $c_ i+j_ i$, $1\leq i\leq N$, with $c_ i\in \mathbb C$ and $j_ i\in J$. Because $J^2=0$, every polynomial in these generators lies in the finite-dimensional span of $1,j_1,\ldots ,j_ N$. This contradicts the infinite dimension of $J$. $\square$

26.19.12 Section algebras of cotangent extensions

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

We now pass from the tangent bundle to the dual $V$ of an extension $W$ of $\mathcal O_ M$ by $\Omega ^1_ M$ with nonzero ruling component, for instance the canonical extension of a Kähler class. Sections of $\operatorname {Sym}^ rV$ are fiber-homogeneous polynomial functions on $W$. Compared with the cotangent bundle there is one more fiber coordinate $w$, the coordinate of the quotient $\mathcal O_ M$, and the affine term of the torsor becomes a quadratic relation among the fiber-polynomial functions over the ruling. The weight decomposition of the preceding subsection still applies and shows that the section algebra of $V$ is a polynomial ring in one variable over the tangent algebra. In particular, $\mathcal O_{\mathbb P(V)}(1)$ is big and the section algebra of $V$ is not finitely generated.

Keep the notation of the two preceding subsections. Let $W$ be an extension of $\mathcal O_ M$ by $\Omega _ M^1$ with class $\rho \xi +Kf^*\eta $, where $\rho \ne 0$, and put $V=W^*$. Adding the coordinate $w$ of the quotient $\mathcal O_ M$ to the charts in the proof of theorem 26.19.38, the fiber coordinates over the elliptic cover change by

\[ v=1/t,\qquad u_\infty =u,\qquad y_\infty =-t^2y-\rho t w,\qquad w_\infty =w. \]

For $\lambda =m\tau +n$, the lattice acts by

\[ (z,t,u,y,w)\longmapsto (z+\lambda ,b_\lambda t,u+m(Kw+q_0ty),y/b_\lambda ,w), \qquad q_0=2\pi \mathrm{i}d, \]

with the normalized factors $b_\lambda $. Fiber-homogeneous polynomial functions on $W$ represent symmetric tensors of $V$. In the ruling coordinates they satisfy a quadratic relation coming from the affine term of the torsor, while the elliptic monodromy determines which weights descend to $M$.

Lemma 26.19.43. Over $\mathbb C\times \mathbb P^1$, the graded algebra of fiber-homogeneous polynomial functions on the pullback of $W$ is

\[ \mathcal O(\mathbb C) [c_-,c_+,h,w,u]/\bigl (c_-c_+-h(h+\rho w)\bigr ), \]

where all the variables have degree one and, in the finite ruling chart,

\[ c_-=y,\qquad c_+=t(ty+\rho w),\qquad h=ty. \]

The lattice fixes $h$ and $w$, multiplies $c_-$ by $b_\lambda ^{-1}$ and $c_+$ by $b_\lambda $, and sends $u$ to $u+m(q_0h+Kw)$.

Proof. Set $s_1=y$ and $s_2=ty+\rho w$ in the finite chart. Since $\rho \ne 0$, these are linear coordinates that can replace $y,w$, with $w=(s_2-ts_1)/\rho $. In the infinite chart put

\[ (s_{1,\infty },s_{2,\infty }) =(-v y_\infty -\rho w,-y_\infty ). \]

The transition is $(s_{1,\infty },s_{2,\infty })=t(s_1,s_2)$, so $s_1,s_2$ are the fiber coordinates of $\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^1}(-1)^{\oplus 2}$, while $u$ is the coordinate of a trivial line bundle.

By holomorphy in the infinite chart, a polynomial that is homogeneous of degree $j$ in $s_1,s_2$ has coefficients of degree at most $j$ in $t$. Distributing the exponent of $t$ among the $j$ fiber factors, every admissible monomial is a product of $s_1,s_2,ts_1,ts_2$. These four generators satisfy the determinant relation

\[ s_1(ts_2)-(ts_1)s_2=0, \]

and there are no further relations. Indeed, counting the $j+1$ monomials in $s_1,s_2$ and the $j+1$ admissible powers of $t$, the space of functions of degree $j$ has dimension $(j+1)^2$. The polynomial algebra in four variables modulo this nonzero quadratic relation has the same dimension

\[ \binom {j+3}{3}-\binom {j+1}{3}=(j+1)^2 \]

in degree $j$, where the second term is zero for $j<2$. The natural surjective graded map is therefore an isomorphism in every degree. Including $u$ adjoins a polynomial variable. The argument is uniform in $z$, and all coefficient functions are entire.

Identify the four generators as $c_-=s_1$, $h=ts_1$, $h+\rho w=s_2$ and $c_+=ts_2$; the determinant relation becomes the stated quadratic relation. These functions are polynomial also in the infinite chart, where

\[ c_-=-v^2y_\infty -\rho v w,\qquad c_+=-y_\infty ,\qquad h=-v y_\infty -\rho w. \]

The lattice formulas follow by substitution in the finite chart. $\square$

Theorem 26.19.44. There is a degree-preserving isomorphism of complex algebras

\[ \bigoplus _{r\geq 0}H^0(M,\operatorname {Sym}^ rV) \simeq \left(\bigoplus _{r\geq 0}H^0(M,\operatorname {Sym}^ rT_ M)\right)[w], \]

where the variable $w$ on the right has degree one.

Proof. Step 1: weights. Give $c_-$ weight $-1$, $c_+$ weight $1$, and $h,w,u$ weight zero. These weights come from the ruling action and are compatible with the quadratic relation and the lattice action. Using the relation to eliminate products $c_-c_+$, a polynomial of weight $-k$, $k\geq 1$, can be written as $c_-^ kF(z,h,w,u)$, and a polynomial of weight $k$ as $c_+^ kF(z,h,w,u)$, where $F$ has degree $r-k$ in tensor degree $r$; weight zero consists of the polynomials $F(z,h,w,u)$. These expressions are unique. Indeed, the substitution $c_+\mapsto h(h+\rho w)/c_-$ embeds the algebra of lemma 26.19.43 into

\[ \mathcal O(\mathbb C)[h,w,u,c_-,c_-^{-1}], \]

the reduced expressions involve distinct powers of $c_-$, and their coefficients vanish only if the original coefficients vanish, because $h(h+\rho w)$ is a nonzero polynomial. This proves that the weight decomposition is a direct sum.

Step 2: coefficient bundles. Let $U_3$ be the rank-three bundle on $E$ of linear polynomials in $h,w,u$ with the transformation rule

\[ F(z+\lambda ,h,w,u) =F(z,h,w,u-m(q_0h+Kw)). \]

By the transformation of $c_-$ and $c_+$ in lemma 26.19.43, the block of weight $-k$ is $H^0(E,A^ k\otimes \operatorname {Sym}^{r-k}U_3)$, and the block of weight $k$ is $H^0(E,A^{-k}\otimes \operatorname {Sym}^{r-k}U_3)$; the inverse translation of $u$ appears when invariance is written out. The signs of the twists agree with those in lemma 26.19.39.

Step 3: reduction to the tangent bundle. Since $q_0\ne 0$, put $H=h+(K/q_0)w$. Then the translation of $u$ is $u\mapsto u-mq_0H$, and consequently

\[ U_3\simeq U\oplus \mathcal O_ Ew, \]

where the variable $h$ of the bundle $U$ defined above is replaced by $H$. The symmetric powers of $U_3$ are the direct sums of the bundles $w^ j\operatorname {Sym}^{s-j}U$, $0\leq j\leq s$. The vanishing for negative twists in lemma 26.19.40 eliminates every positive weight, and the same lemma identifies the algebra of sections of weight zero with $\mathbb C[H,w]$.

In each negative weight, expanding in powers of $w$ identifies the remaining spaces with the corresponding blocks for the tangent bundle in lemma 26.19.39, with a polynomial variable $w$ adjoined. Sending the Euler variable $h$ of those blocks to $H$ and their leading factor $y^ k$ to $c_-^ k$ gives bijections in every tensor degree and weight. Products of surviving blocks multiply their coefficients and add their powers of $c_-$, and the eliminated positive weights contribute no products. The bijections therefore preserve multiplication and give the asserted isomorphism of graded algebras. $\square$

Corollary 26.19.45. For every integer $r\geq 0$,

\[ h^0(M,\operatorname {Sym}^ rV) =r+1+d\binom {r+3}{4}. \]

The tautological line bundle on $\mathbb P(V)$ is big, with volume $d$, and the section algebra of $V$ is not finitely generated. These conclusions hold for the canonical extension of every Kähler class on $M$.

Proof. The isomorphism of theorem 26.19.44 and theorem 26.19.41 give

\[ h^0(M,\operatorname {Sym}^ rV) =\sum _{j=0}^ r h^0(M,\operatorname {Sym}^ jT_ M) =r+1+d\sum _{j=0}^ r\binom {j+2}{3} =r+1+d\binom {r+3}{4}, \]

where $\binom {2}{3}=0$. The surface $M$ is projective, and by coherent GAGA the holomorphic bundle $V$ is algebraic. Thus $\mathbb P(V)$ is a projective fourfold, and the volume of its tautological line bundle is the limit of $24h^0(M,\operatorname {Sym}^ rV)/r^4$, which equals $d>0$. Hence this line bundle is big. If the section algebra of $V$ were finitely generated, so would be its quotient by $w$, contradicting theorem 26.19.42.

For the canonical extension $0\to \mathcal O_ M\to V\to T_ M\to 0$ of a Kähler class, the dual extension of $\mathcal O_ M$ by $\Omega ^1_ M$ has nonzero ruling component, as shown in the proof of theorem 26.19.38. Its class therefore has $\rho \ne 0$ in the conventions used here, and the same argument applies. $\square$

26.19.13 Cotangent extensions with zero ruling component

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Every Kähler class has a nonzero ruling component, but the extensions with $\rho =0$, that is, with class $Kf^*\eta $, can be treated in the same way. Their section algebras are the same as for $\rho \neq 0$, but their torsors behave differently: they contain compact rational curves, and their universal covers are not Stein.

Keep the notation of the preceding subsections, with the class $\eta $ and the form $\vartheta _ E$ of lemma 26.19.35. Let $W$ be an extension of $\mathcal O_ M$ by $\Omega _ M^1$ with class $Kf^*\eta $, and put $V=W^*$. The homogeneous charts used above, now with zero ruling component, have transitions

\[ v=1/t,\qquad u_\infty =u,\qquad y_\infty =-t^2y,\qquad w_\infty =w. \]

For $\lambda =m\tau +n$, the lattice acts by

\[ (z,t,u,y,w)\longmapsto (z+\lambda ,b_\lambda t,u+m(q_0ty+Kw),y/b_\lambda ,w), \qquad q_0=2\pi \mathrm{i}d. \]

For the differences $z_ i-z_ j=m_{ij}\tau +n_{ij}$ of local lifts, the zero sections of these charts differ by $Km_{ij}f^*\vartheta _ E$ on overlaps over the base and agree on the two ruling charts, so they give the class $Kf^*\eta $. The linear parts are the cotangent transitions, and in the infinite ruling chart the lattice acts holomorphically by $u_\infty \mapsto u_\infty +m(Kw-q_0v y_\infty )$. Thus the same cocycle computation constructs $W$ also when its ruling component is zero. The fiberwise change of coordinates used for $\rho \ne 0$ is not available, but the polynomial functions on the cotangent bundle give the homogeneous algebra directly.

Lemma 26.19.46. Over $\mathbb C\times \mathbb P^1$, the graded algebra of fiber-homogeneous polynomial functions on the pullback of $W$ is

\[ \mathcal O(\mathbb C)[c_-,c_+,h,u,w]/(c_-c_+-h^2), \qquad c_-=y,\quad c_+=t^2y,\quad h=ty, \]

where all five variables have degree one. The lattice fixes $h$ and $w$, sends $c_-$ to $b_\lambda ^{-1}c_-$ and $c_+$ to $b_\lambda c_+$, and translates $u$ by $m(q_0h+Kw)$.

Proof. As in the proof of lemma 26.19.39, holomorphy in the infinite ruling chart bounds the degree in $t$ of the coefficient of $y^ j$ by $2j$; the variable $w$ does not change between the charts. Distributing the exponent $a$ among $j$ integers in $\{ 0,1,2\} $, every monomial $t^ ay^ j$ with $0\leq a\leq 2j$ is a product of $y$, $ty$ and $t^2y$. These generators satisfy $y(t^2y)=(ty)^2$.

In degree $j$, the space of functions of $y$ and $t$ has dimension $2j+1$. The polynomial algebra in three variables modulo the nonzero quadratic relation has the same dimension

\[ \binom {j+2}{2}-\binom {j}{2}=2j+1, \]

where the second term is zero for $j<2$, so the natural surjective graded map is an isomorphism. Adjoining the two trivial fiber coordinates $u,w$ gives the stated algebra, with entire coefficients in $z$. In the other chart,

\[ c_-=-v^2y_\infty ,\qquad c_+=-y_\infty , \qquad h=-v y_\infty , \]

so all generators are holomorphic there. Substitution gives their lattice transformations. $\square$

Proposition 26.19.47. There is a degree-preserving isomorphism of algebras

\[ \bigoplus _{r\geq 0}H^0(M,\operatorname {Sym}^ rV) \simeq \left(\bigoplus _{r\geq 0}H^0(M,\operatorname {Sym}^ rT_ M)\right)[w], \qquad \deg w=1. \]

Consequently the dimension formula, the bigness and volume of the tautological line bundle, and the failure of finite generation in corollary 26.19.45 hold also for this extension.

Proof. Give $c_-,c_+$ weights $-1,1$, and $h,u,w$ weight zero. Using $c_-c_+=h^2$, the blocks of negative weight have the form $c_-^ kF(z,h,u,w)$, those of positive weight the form $c_+^ kF(z,h,u,w)$, and the block of weight zero consists of the polynomials $F$. These expressions are unique: the substitution $c_+=h^2/c_-$ embeds the algebra into $\mathcal O(\mathbb C)[h,u,w,c_-,c_-^{-1}]$, and distinct weights correspond to distinct powers of $c_-$ with nonzero polynomial coefficients. In tensor degree $r$, the remaining polynomial degree is $r-k$.

The coefficient bundle $U_3$ now has the inverse translation $u\mapsto u-m(q_0h+Kw)$. Thus the block of weight $-k$ is $H^0(E,A^ k\otimes \operatorname {Sym}^{r-k}U_3)$, the block of weight $k$ has $A^{-k}$ in place of $A^ k$, and the block of weight zero is $H^0(E,\operatorname {Sym}^ rU_3)$. Put $H=h+(K/q_0)w$. Since $q_0\ne 0$, this identifies $U_3$ with the bundle $U$ defined above, with $H$ in place of its Euler variable, plus the trivial line bundle generated by $w$.

By lemma 26.19.40, all positive weights vanish, and the algebra of weight zero is $\mathbb C[H,w]$. Expanding the negative blocks in powers of $w$ identifies them with the blocks for the tangent bundle in lemma 26.19.39, and the substitutions $h\mapsto H$ and $y^ k\mapsto c_-^ k$ give bijections in every degree and surviving weight. Products multiply the coefficient polynomials and add the powers of $c_-$, so these bijections preserve multiplication. This proves the isomorphism. Summing the dimensions for the tangent bundle and taking the quotient by $w$ gives the remaining assertions, exactly as in the proof of corollary 26.19.45. $\square$

Proposition 26.19.48. The cotangent torsor $T_ W$ is not Stein. Its universal cover is

\[ \mathbb C^2\times \operatorname {Tot} (\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^1}(-2)), \]

where $\operatorname {Tot}$ denotes the total space of a line bundle; this cover is not Stein either.

Proof. Set $w=1$. The subset $\{ y=0\} $ of the finite ruling chart agrees with $\{ y_\infty =0\} $ in the other chart, and the lattice acts on it by $u\mapsto u+Km$. It therefore descends to a submanifold of $T_ W$ that is a $\mathbb P^1$-bundle over

\[ B_ K=\mathbb C^2_{z,u}/\Lambda ,\qquad (m\tau +n)\cdot (z,u)=(z+m\tau +n,u+Km); \]

this quotient is a complex manifold because translation in $z$ gives a free and properly discontinuous action. Every fiber of this bundle is a compact connected curve in $T_ W$, on which holomorphic functions are constant. Thus $T_ W$ is not holomorphically separable, and hence not Stein.

Before the lattice quotient, the affine charts have no affine term in the ruling direction, and they glue to $\mathbb C^2_{z,u}\times \operatorname {Tot}(\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^1}(-2))$, since $\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^1}(-2)$ is the cotangent bundle of $\mathbb P^1$. This space is simply connected: contracting the fibers of the line bundle retracts the last factor onto $\mathbb P^1$, and $\mathbb C^2$ is contractible. The lattice acts freely and properly discontinuously, so this space is the universal cover. Its zero section contains a compact projective line, which again obstructs holomorphic separability and hence the Stein property. $\square$

Thus the classes with zero and with nonzero ruling component have isomorphic graded section algebras, although theorem 26.19.38 gives a Stein universal cover only when the ruling component is nonzero.

Problem 26.19.49. Which geometric conditions on a nonclosed nef background force a common degeneration order, or an integral one? When is equality attained in the modification and residual order comparisons?

26.20 Current tests for smooth positivity

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Lamari's duality, theorem 4.3.1, characterizes pseudoeffective backgrounds by their pairings with Gauduchon metrics. Smooth positivity, and hence nefness, is characterized in the same way by pairing with all positive $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-closed $(n-1,n-1)$-currents. By the Hahn–Banach theorem, the minimum of these pairings over normalized currents is exactly the largest $a$ such that $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u>ah$ for a smooth function $u$, and the nef boundary is where this minimum vanishes. The full set of currents is needed here: the closure of the smooth tests used in Lamari's duality detects pseudoeffectivity and bigness instead. The minimizing currents at the nef boundary appear again at the maximal time of the Chern–Ricci flow, and positive variational volume forces their determinant-weighted measures to vanish.

Let $X$ again be a connected compact complex manifold of dimension $n\geq 1$. Fix a Hermitian metric $h$, and let $\mathscr G_ h$ be the set of normalized Gauduchon currents of section 4.4, that is, positive $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-closed $(n-1,n-1)$-currents $T$ with $\int _ Xh\wedge T=1$. This set is weakly compact for $n\geq 2$. For $n=1$, elliptic regularity and the maximum principle give $\mathscr G_ h=\{ 1/\int _ Xh\} $, so compactness holds in this case as well. For a smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\theta $, define

\[ m_ h(\theta )=\min _{T\in \mathscr G_ h}\int _ X\theta \wedge T. \]

The minimum exists by weak compactness, and it is finite since $-Ch\leq \theta \leq Ch$ for some constant $C$.

Theorem 26.20.1. The background $\theta $ has a smooth positive representative if and only if $m_ h(\theta )>0$. Moreover, $\theta $ is nef if and only if $m_ h(\theta )\geq 0$, and

\[ m_ h(\theta )=\sup \{ a\in \mathbb R:\ \exists u\in C^\infty (X,\mathbb R), \ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u>ah\} . \]

Proof. If $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq ah$ with $a>0$ and $u$ smooth, then integration by parts gives $\int _ X\theta \wedge T\geq a$ for every $T\in \mathscr G_ h$. Since a smooth positive representative satisfies such a bound for some $a>0$ by compactness, this proves one implication.

For the converse, work in the real Fréchet space of smooth real $(1,1)$-forms. Let $\mathcal K$ be its open convex cone of strictly positive forms, and let $\mathcal L=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}C^\infty (X,\mathbb R)$. If the affine subspace $\theta +\mathcal L$ is disjoint from $\mathcal K$, the Hahn–Banach separation theorem for an open convex set and a disjoint affine subspace gives a nonzero continuous real linear functional $T$ with

\[ T(\theta +\ell )\leq T(\eta )\qquad (\ell \in \mathcal L,\ \eta \in \mathcal K). \]

Replacing $\ell $ by arbitrary real multiples shows that $T(\mathcal L)=0$. Scaling a fixed $\eta \in \mathcal K$ to zero shows that $T(\theta )\leq 0$, while scaling it to infinity shows that $T(\eta )\geq 0$. Since every smooth semipositive form is a limit of strictly positive forms, $T$ is a positive current, and positivity makes it a current of order zero. The equality $T(\mathcal L)=0$ says exactly that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}T=0$. Since $T\ne 0$ and $h>0$, the trace $T(h)$ is positive, and normalizing $T$ gives an element of $\mathscr G_ h$ with nonpositive pairing against $\theta $. This contradicts $m_ h(\theta )>0$ and proves the converse.

Applying the equivalence just proved to $\theta -ah$, and using $m_ h(\theta -ah)=m_ h(\theta )-a$, shows that the set in the supremum is exactly $(-\infty ,m_ h(\theta ))$. Finally, $\theta $ is nef if and only if $\theta +\varepsilon h$ has a smooth positive representative for every $\varepsilon >0$, that is, if and only if $m_ h(\theta )+\varepsilon >0$ for all such $\varepsilon $, or equivalently $m_ h(\theta )\geq 0$. $\square$

For $n\geq 2$, let $\mathscr C_ h\subseteq \mathscr G_ h$ be the weak closure of the smooth strictly positive normalized Gauduchon forms, and let $\kappa _ h(\theta )$ be the linear positivity threshold of corollary 4.3.2, which is the minimum of $\int _ X\theta \wedge T$ over $T\in \mathscr C_ h$, by section 4.4. The inclusion $\mathscr C_ h\subseteq \mathscr G_ h$ gives $m_ h(\theta )\leq \kappa _ h(\theta )$. The larger test set detects smooth positivity and nefness, whereas the weak closure of the smooth tests detects the existence of positive and of strictly positive currents in the class, that is, pseudoeffectivity and bigness. The inequality can be strict, as example 28.8.2 shows.

Proposition 26.20.2. Let $\theta $ be nef with $\lambda _\mu (\theta )>0$. If $T\in \mathscr G_ h$ satisfies $\int _ X\theta \wedge T=0$, then the determinant-weighted measure of $T$, computed as in (26.39) with reference metric $h$ and with $\mu $ in place of $\mathrm e^ F\mathrm dV$, vanishes.

Proof. In dimension one the hypotheses cannot hold, since $\int _ X\theta \wedge T=\lambda _\mu (\theta )/\int _ Xh>0$. Suppose $n\geq 2$, and write $D_\mu (T)$ for the total mass of this determinant-weighted measure; for $T=\gamma ^{n-1}$ it is the denominator $D_\mu (\gamma )$ of definition 26.1.1. If $\eta \in \mathscr P$, its solution form $\Omega $ satisfies $\Omega ^ n=\lambda _\mu (\eta )^ n\mu $, so $\Omega /\lambda _\mu (\eta )$ is an admissible test in the infimum of lemma 26.6.1. Together with Stokes' theorem, this gives

\[ \int _ X\eta \wedge T=\int _ X\Omega \wedge T \geq \lambda _\mu (\eta )D_\mu (T). \]

Apply this to $\eta =\theta +\varepsilon h$. The left side equals $\varepsilon $, while theorem 26.10.1 gives $\lambda _\mu (\eta )\to \lambda _\mu (\theta )>0$. Hence $D_\mu (T)=0$. The determinant-weighted measure is positive and has total mass zero, so it vanishes. $\square$

Thus, at a nef background of positive volume, every annihilating current in $\mathscr G_ h$ has a coefficient matrix of deficient rank almost everywhere with respect to its trace measure. This alone does not force the trace measure to be supported on an analytic subset.

26.20.1 Maximal time of the Chern–Ricci flow

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

The Chern–Ricci flow deforms a Hermitian metric by $\partial _ t\omega =-\operatorname {Ric}(\omega )$. Since the Chern–Ricci forms of any two Hermitian metrics differ by a $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-exact form, a solution starting at $\omega _0$ stays in the affine family $\omega _0-t\operatorname {Ric}(\omega _0)$ modulo $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ of smooth functions. It can therefore exist only as long as this family has smooth positive representatives. Conversely, a positive representative at a later time provides a uniformly positive reference path, and parabolic estimates exclude an earlier singularity. The current criterion of theorem 26.20.1 then computes the lifetime.

Let $\omega _0$ be the initial Hermitian metric. For a Hermitian form $\omega $ on $X$ we use the Chern–Ricci form

\[ \operatorname {Ric}(\omega )=-\mathrm{i}\partial \bar\partial \log \omega ^ n =-2\pi \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log \omega ^ n, \]

where $\omega ^ n$ is identified with its coefficient in local holomorphic coordinates; a change of coordinates changes $\log \omega ^ n$ by a pluriharmonic function and does not affect the form. This normalization fixes the time scale of the flow $\partial _ t\omega =-\operatorname {Ric}(\omega )$. Put $\chi =\operatorname {Ric}(\omega _0)$ and $\theta _ t=\omega _0-t\chi $.

Theorem 26.20.3. The Chern–Ricci flow starting from $\omega _0$ has a unique maximal smooth solution on $[0,\tau )$, where

\[ \tau =\sup \{ t\geq 0:\ \theta _ t\text{ has a smooth positive representative}\} . \]

Proof. Call a time $t\geq 0$ admissible if $\theta _ t$ has a smooth positive representative. The admissible times form an interval containing zero, since convex combinations of positive representatives at two times are positive representatives at the intermediate times, and the set is open because positivity is an open condition. We show that the flow exists on $[0,t_1)$ for every admissible $t_1>0$ and that it cannot exist beyond $\tau $.

Step 1: the scalar equation. Fix an admissible $t_1>0$, and choose a smooth real function $a$ with $\theta _{t_1}+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a>0$. The reference forms

\[ \gamma _ t=\theta _ t+\frac t{t_1}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a =\left(1-\frac t{t_1}\right)\omega _0 +\frac t{t_1}(\theta _{t_1}+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a),\qquad 0\leq t\leq t_1, \]

are uniformly positive, with all derivatives uniformly bounded. Put

\[ \Omega =\mathrm{e}^{a/(2\pi t_1)}\omega _0^ n. \]

Then $\dot\gamma _ t=-\operatorname {Ric}(\Omega )$, where the Ricci form of a volume form is defined by the same local expression. Consider the scalar equation

26.163
\begin{equation} \dot u=2\pi \log \frac{(\gamma _ t+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n}{\Omega }, \qquad \gamma _ t+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u>0,\qquad u(0)=0. \end{equation}

Applying $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ shows that $\omega (t)=\gamma _ t+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u(t)$ solves the metric flow. Conversely, for a solution $\omega (t)$ of the metric flow, the function $u(t)=2\pi \int _0^ t\log (\omega (s)^ n/\Omega )\, \mathrm ds$ satisfies $\omega (t)=\gamma _ t+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u(t)$ and solves (26.163). The linearization of (26.163) is $\partial _ t-2\pi \operatorname {tr}_{\omega (t)}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$, a strictly parabolic operator, so the classical short-time existence theory for smooth strictly parabolic equations gives a solution. The difference of two solutions satisfies a linear parabolic equation whose principal coefficients, the averages of the inverse metrics along the segment between the two solutions, are positive; the maximum principle therefore proves uniqueness.

Suppose that the maximal time $t_*$ of this solution satisfies $t_*<t_1$. We derive estimates independent of $t<t_*$ that allow us to continue the solution.

Step 2: bounds for $u$ and $\dot u$. For a large constant $C$, the functions $Ct$ and $-Ct$ are a supersolution and a subsolution of (26.163), respectively: at a spatial extremum of $u$ the complex Hessian has the appropriate sign, and $\gamma _ t^ n/\Omega $ is bounded above and away from zero. The maximum principle gives $|u|\leq Ct_1$.

Set $v=\dot u$ and $\mathcal L=2\pi \operatorname {tr}_{\omega (t)}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$. Differentiation gives

\[ (\partial _ t-\mathcal L)v=2\pi \operatorname {tr}_{\omega (t)}\dot\gamma _ t, \qquad (\partial _ t-\mathcal L)u=v-2\pi n+2\pi \operatorname {tr}_{\omega (t)}\gamma _ t. \]

Since $\gamma _ t$ is affine in time, these identities imply

\begin{align*} (\partial _ t-\mathcal L)(t v-u-2\pi nt) & =-2\pi \operatorname {tr}_{\omega (t)}\omega _0<0,\\ (\partial _ t-\mathcal L)((t_1-t)v+u+2\pi nt) & =2\pi \operatorname {tr}_{\omega (t)}\gamma _{t_1}>0. \end{align*}

By the maximum principle, the first expression is bounded above by its initial value zero, and the second is bounded below by $\min _ Xt_1v(0)$. The bound for $u$ and $t_1-t\geq t_1-t_*>0$ give a lower bound for $v$. The first inequality gives an upper bound for $v$ on $[t_*/2,t_*)$, and the smooth solution bounds it on $[0,t_*/2]$. In particular, $\omega (t)^ n/\Omega =\mathrm{e}^{v/(2\pi )}$ is bounded above and away from zero.

Step 3: a parabolic trace inequality. We next bound the trace, keeping track of the time derivative of the reference metric. In the coordinates of lemma 9.4.1, write $g(t)$ for the matrix of $\gamma _ t$ and, as in that lemma, $h=g+(u_{i\bar j})$ for the matrix of $\omega (t)$. Put

\[ S=\operatorname {tr}_ g h,\qquad B=\operatorname {tr}_ h g, \qquad F=\log \frac{\Omega }{\gamma _ t^ n}, \]

so that $\log (\det h/\det g)=v/(2\pi )+F$. The family $g(t)$ has uniformly bounded Chern torsion and curvature, so at each fixed time lemma 9.4.1 applies with uniform constants. Direct differentiation of $S$ gives

\[ \partial _ t\log S =\frac{\Delta _ g v+\operatorname {tr}_ g\dot g}{S} -\frac{\operatorname {tr}(g^{-1}\dot g\, g^{-1}h)}{S}. \]

In $(\partial _ t-\mathcal L)\log S$, the term $\Delta _ gv/S$ cancels the corresponding term in the lower bound for $\mathcal L\log S$ given by the lemma. With $\tau _ k=\sum _ iT_{ik}^ i$ the torsion trace of $g(t)$, as in the lemma, we obtain on $\{ S\geq 1\} $

26.164
\begin{equation} (\partial _ t-\mathcal L)\log S \leq -\frac{4\pi }{S^2}\operatorname {Re}(h^{k\bar q}\tau _ k S_{\bar q})+CB+C. \end{equation}

Indeed, $\Delta _ gF$ and $\operatorname {tr}_ g\dot g$ are uniformly bounded, $S^{-1}\leq B/n^2$, and $-Cg\leq \dot g\leq Cg$ bounds the last quotient above. These bounds account for all the terms coming from the time dependence of the reference metric.

Step 4: the trace bound. Choose a constant $C_0$ such that $1\leq s=u+C_0\leq D$ on the whole time interval, and maximize

\[ Q=\log S-Au+s^{-1} \]

on $X\times [0,t_0]$, where $t_0<t_*$ and the constant $A\geq 1$ will be fixed independently of $t_0$. If the maximum is attained at time zero, the initial data bound $Q$. At a maximum with positive time, $\partial \log S=(A+s^{-2})\partial u$. Since $|\tau |_ h^2\leq CB$, Young's inequality bounds the absolute value of the first term on the right of (26.164) by

\[ 2\pi s^{-3}|\partial u|_ h^2 +\frac{C(A+1)^2s^3}{S^2}B. \]

If $S^2\leq (A+1)^2D^3$, the trace is already bounded at the maximum. Otherwise the last coefficient is at most $C$, and

\[ \begin{split} 0\leq (\partial _ t-\mathcal L)Q & \leq CB+C-(A+s^{-2})(v-2\pi n+2\pi B) -2\pi s^{-3}|\partial u|_ h^2\\ & \leq (C-2\pi A)B+C(A+1). \end{split} \]

Choosing $A$ with $2\pi A>C+1$ bounds $B$ at the maximum. In terms of the eigenvalues, $S\leq n(\det h/\det g)B^{n-1}$ for $n\geq 2$, and $S=\det h/\det g$ for $n=1$, so the determinant bound of Step 2 bounds $S$ at the maximum in every case. The bound for $u$ transfers this estimate from $Q$ to $S$ everywhere, uniformly in $t_0$, and the lower bound for the determinant then bounds the smallest eigenvalue away from zero. Thus $\omega (t)$ and $\gamma _ t$ are uniformly equivalent for $t<t_*$.

Step 5: higher estimates and continuation. Uniform ellipticity now gives the higher estimates by classical local parabolic regularity. In a coordinate ball, write the logarithmic determinant as a concave function of the complex part of the real Hessian. On the compact set of positive matrices just obtained, its supporting affine functions have uniformly positive and bounded slopes, and their infimum extends the operator to a concave, uniformly elliptic function of all real symmetric matrices. The dependence on space and time enters only through the smooth family $g(t)$ and the fixed density $\Omega $. The parabolic Evans–Krylov estimate gives uniform $C^{2+\alpha ,1+\alpha /2}$ bounds away from the initial time, and the smooth solution covers a fixed initial interval. Differentiating the equation and applying parabolic Schauder estimates then bounds all spatial and time derivatives uniformly. This is the parabolic counterpart of the local argument used in corollary 9.4.4.

The bounds for the time derivatives show that $u(t)$ converges in every spatial $C^ k$ norm as $t\uparrow t_*$. By uniform ellipticity, the smooth limit still has a strictly positive solution form, and short-time existence with this limit as initial datum extends the solution beyond $t_*$, a contradiction. Hence the flow exists up to every admissible time $t_1$, and by uniqueness these solutions patch together on $[0,\tau )$.

Step 6: maximality. Integrating the metric flow gives

\[ \omega (t)=\theta _ t+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\left(2\pi \int _0^ t \log \frac{\omega (s)^ n}{\omega _0^ n}\, \mathrm{d}s\right). \]

Hence every time at which a smooth positive solution exists is admissible, which proves maximality. $\square$

Combining the continuation theorem with the current criterion gives a numerical description of the lifetime.

Corollary 26.20.4. Let

\[ b=\max _{T\in \mathscr G_{\omega _0}}\int _ X\chi \wedge T. \]

Then the maximal smooth existence time is

\[ \tau =\begin{cases} 1/b,& b>0,\\ +\infty ,& b\leq 0.\end{cases} \]

If $\tau <\infty $, the limiting background $\theta _\tau $ is nef and there is a current $T_*\in \mathscr G_{\omega _0}$ with $\int _ X\theta _\tau \wedge T_*=0$. If moreover $\lambda _\mu (\theta _\tau )>0$, every such annihilating current has zero determinant-weighted measure.

Proof. For $t\geq 0$, the normalization of the test currents gives the identity

\[ m_{\omega _0}(\theta _ t)=1-tb. \]

Theorem 26.20.1, Theorem 26.20.3 give the formula for $\tau $. When $b>0$, the maximum defining $b$ is attained by weak compactness, and a maximizer $T_*$ annihilates $\theta _\tau $. The same identity gives $m_{\omega _0}(\theta _\tau )=0$, hence nefness. The last assertion is proposition 26.20.2. $\square$

Along the flow, $\omega (t)=\theta _ t+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ t$ with a smooth potential $\varphi _ t$. Consequently, for every fixed $T\in \mathscr G_{\omega _0}$,

\[ \int _ X\omega (t)\wedge T =1-t\int _ X\chi \wedge T. \]

These traces describe the obstruction at the maximal time without any bound on the total Monge–Ampère mass. They do not locate the singularities of the flow, nor do they give smooth convergence away from an analytic set; such geometric questions require local estimates in addition to these scalar pairings.

26.20.2 A positive-volume endpoint that is not big

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Backgrounds reached at the maximal time of a Chern–Ricci flow are not better behaved, as far as the relation between positive volume and bigness is concerned. The radial background of section 26.13 occurs, up to a smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ change, as the limiting background of a Chern–Ricci flow at its maximal time. The normalized currents annihilating it can also be described explicitly.

We use the notation of section 26.13: $n=m+1\geq 3$, $t=(m-1)/m$, $a=x/t$, and

\[ X=\mathbb P^ m\times \mathbb P^1,\qquad \theta =\omega +a w,\qquad \mu =\omega ^ m\wedge w, \qquad Z=\{ p\} \times \mathbb P^1, \]

where $\omega $ and $w$ are the normalized Fubini–Study forms pulled back from the two factors, so that $\mu $ is a probability form. Start the flow from the Hermitian metric

26.169
\begin{equation} \omega _0=(n+1)\omega +(a+2)w. \end{equation}

Theorem 26.20.5. With the normalization of the Ricci form in 26.20.1, the maximal smooth existence time of the Chern–Ricci flow starting from $\omega _0$ is $\tau =1/(2\pi )$. The limiting background $\alpha _\tau =\omega _0-\tau \operatorname {Ric}(\omega _0)$ is nef and not big, and

\[ \alpha _\tau =\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log (a+2),\qquad \mathcal V_\mu (\alpha _\tau )=n. \]

Its variational volume is positive for every smooth strictly positive probability form on $X$.

Proof. The initial metric is strictly positive, and

\[ \omega _0^ n=n(n+1)^ m(a+2)\mu . \]

With our normalization, the Ricci forms of the normalized Fubini–Study metrics are $2\pi n\omega $ and $4\pi w$. Consequently

\[ \operatorname {Ric}(\omega _0) =2\pi \bigl (n\omega +2w-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log (a+2)\bigr ). \]

Write $s$ for the flow time and put $r=2\pi s$. The affine background is

26.170
\begin{equation} \alpha _ s=(n+1-nr)\omega +(a+2-2r)w +r\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log (a+2). \end{equation}

For $0\leq r<1$, adding $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ of the smooth function $-r\log (a+2)$ gives a strictly positive representative: the horizontal coefficient is at least one and the vertical coefficient is at least $2(1-r)>0$. At $r=1$ we obtain the identity $\alpha _\tau =\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log (a+2)$. Since the logarithm is a smooth function on $X$, the bijection $u\mapsto u-\log (a+2)$ between the potentials of the two backgrounds preserves nefness and bigness, and Stokes' theorem for Gauduchon metrics shows that the variational volume is unchanged. Theorem 26.13.2 therefore shows that the endpoint background is nef, not big, and of volume $n$.

For $r>1$, choose a nonzero smooth nonnegative function $f$ on the base supported in $\{ a+2-2r<0\} $, a neighborhood of $p$. Its pullback $S=f\omega ^ m$ is a closed semipositive $(n-1,n-1)$-form. For every quasi-plurisubharmonic function $v$, distributional Stokes gives

\[ \int _ X(\alpha _ s+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)\wedge S =\int _{\mathbb P^ m}f(a+2-2r)\omega ^ m<0, \]

so $\alpha _ s$ is not even pseudoeffective for $r>1$. At $r=1$, a smooth positive representative would contradict nonbigness. Thus $r=1$ is the supremum of the admissible times, and theorem 26.20.3 gives the smooth flow on $[0,\tau )$ with exactly this lifetime; the reference representatives used above need not solve the flow equation. Finally, proposition 26.14.1 gives positivity of the volume for every smooth positive probability form. $\square$

Proposition 26.20.6. The current $[Z]/2$ belongs to $\mathscr G_{\omega _0}$ and annihilates $\alpha _\tau $. Every $R\in \mathscr G_{\omega _0}$ with $\int _ X\alpha _\tau \wedge R=0$ has its trace probability $\sigma _ R=\omega _0\wedge R$ supported on $Z$. In the coefficient-matrix convention of section 26.6, its matrix is

\[ P_ R=n\Pi _{\mathrm{vert}}\qquad \sigma _ R\text{-almost everywhere}, \]

where $\Pi _{\mathrm{vert}}$ is the $\omega _0$-orthogonal projection onto the tangent line of the fiber $\{ p\} \times \mathbb P^1$. In particular, $P_ R$ has rank one, and the determinant-weighted measure of $R$ vanishes for every smooth positive reference density.

Proof. Only smooth forms need to be restricted to $Z$, and their restrictions satisfy $\omega _0|_ Z=2w$ and $\alpha _\tau |_ Z=0$. This proves that $[Z]/2$ is normalized and annihilates $\alpha _\tau $.

Let $R$ be a general annihilator. Since $\alpha _\tau $ and $\theta $ differ by $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ of a smooth function, Stokes' theorem gives $\int _ X\theta \wedge R=0$. The measure $\theta \wedge R$ is nonnegative, so it vanishes. On every compact set $K\subset X\setminus Z$, the explicit form $\theta $ satisfies $\theta \geq c_ K\omega _0$ for some $c_ K>0$. Thus $\sigma _ R(K)=0$, and a countable exhaustion by compact sets shows that $\sigma _ R$ is supported on $Z$.

Relative to $\omega _0$, the matrix of $R$ is characterized, for smooth test forms $\beta $ with matrix $B_\beta $, by

\[ \beta \wedge R=\frac1n\operatorname {tr}(B_\beta P_ R)\sigma _ R, \qquad P_ R\geq 0,\qquad \operatorname {tr}P_ R=n. \]

At points of $Z$, the form $\theta $ is positive on the horizontal $m$-plane and vanishes on the vertical line. The vanishing of $\theta \wedge R$ therefore forces all horizontal diagonal entries of $P_ R$ to vanish, and the inequality $|(P_ R)_{ij}|^2\leq (P_ R)_{ii}(P_ R)_{jj}$ for positive matrices removes their rows and columns. The remaining vertical entry is $n$, which proves the formula and the vanishing of the determinant. $\square$

For each such annihilator $R$, the trace identity at the end of 26.20.1 becomes

\[ \int _ X\omega (s)\wedge R=1-2\pi s, \qquad 0\leq s<1/(2\pi ). \]

This is a statement about averaged traces. The support statement uses the explicit semipositive representative $\theta $ of the endpoint class; the vanishing of the determinant alone would not give it. Neither statement concerns pointwise collapse of the metrics, their local convergence, or uniqueness of the trace probability.

26.20.3 Twisting and distance bounds

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

A twisted Chern–Ricci flow is the equation $\partial _ t\omega =-\operatorname {Ric}(\omega )+\zeta $, where the twist $\zeta $ is a fixed smooth closed real $(1,1)$-form. Diameter bounds along such flows cannot depend only on the maximal time and on the initial Monge–Ampère density: the twist and the length of the time interval enter the geometry, even for flat Kähler metrics.

Example 26.20.7. Let $E=\mathbb C/(\mathbb Z+\mathrm i\mathbb Z)$, let $X=E^ n$ with $n\geq 2$, and let $\eta _ j=(\mathrm i/2)\mathrm dz_ j\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ j$ be the flat forms pulled back from the factors. Put $\omega _0=\sum _{j=1}^ n\eta _ j$. All the flows below start at $\omega _0$, with potential zero and initial density one relative to $\omega _0^ n$. In particular, hypotheses on the initial data expressed through a decomposition $\mathrm e^{\psi ^+-\psi ^-}$ of the density, with quasi-plurisubharmonic functions $\psi ^\pm $ subject to fixed $L^ p$ bounds, hold uniformly for all these flows, with $\psi ^+=\psi ^-=0$.

For each $M>0$, the twist $\zeta _ M=M\eta _1-\eta _2$ gives the explicit solution

\[ \omega _ M(t)=(1+Mt)\eta _1+(1-t)\eta _2+ \sum _{j=3}^ n\eta _ j, \qquad 0\leq t<1, \]

of $\partial _ t\omega =-\operatorname {Ric}(\omega )+\zeta _ M$. Its maximal smooth existence time is exactly one, while

\[ \operatorname {diam}(X,\omega _ M(1/2)) \geq \sqrt{1+M/2}\, \operatorname {diam}(E,\eta _1). \]

For the single fixed twist $\zeta =\omega _0$, the solution is $(1+t)\omega _0$ for all $t\geq 0$, and its diameter grows as $\sqrt{1+t}\, \operatorname {diam}(X,\omega _0)$.

Proof. Each of these metrics has constant coefficients, so its Chern–Ricci form vanishes; differentiation in $t$ proves the flow equations, and positivity of the coefficients gives the stated existence intervals. In the first family, pair a hypothetical positive representative at a time $t\geq 1$ with the closed positive form $\eta _1\wedge \eta _3\wedge \cdots \wedge \eta _ n$, where the empty product is $1$ when $n=2$. The potential term integrates to zero, and the pairing is a positive fixed multiple of $1-t$, so it cannot be strictly positive. Integrating the flow equation shows that every smooth solution differs from the affine background by $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ of a smooth function, so no smooth positive continuation beyond $t=1$ is possible.

Projection to the first elliptic factor does not increase lengths once the metric of that factor is scaled by $1+Mt$. Taking two points that differ only in the first factor proves the lower bound for the diameter. For the second flow, every path length is multiplied by $\sqrt{1+t}$, which proves the exact formula for the diameter. $\square$

On a fixed finite time interval, a common Dini modulus for the potentials and a uniform bound for the affine backgrounds do give control of distances and diameters, by corollary 1.18.4. The example shows why such bounds must be imposed when the twist varies or the time interval grows. It does not concern the limit metric at the maximal time of an untwisted flow.

26.21 Divisorial constraints and residual volume

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

Suppose we require a $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function to have generic Lelong numbers at least $a_ j$ along prescribed prime divisors $D_ j$. Removing the divisor $D=\sum _ ja_ jD_ j$ leaves the residual background $\theta -\eta _ D$, where $\eta _ D$ is a curvature form of $D$, and in each Gauduchon quotient this removal subtracts the Gauduchon degree of $D$ from the numerator. Lamari's duality therefore decides when such functions exist, and the volume of the residual background measures what remains. By the correspondence of theorem 15.9.1, equations in the ordinary singularity type of the logarithmic potential of $D$ reduce to equations in the residual background. When the residual background has a smooth positive representative, the smooth maximizer of the dual problem forces every subsolution with the prescribed Lelong numbers and the residual volume as scalar to be the logarithmic potential of $D$ plus a smooth function, up to a constant.

Fix a smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\theta $ on $X$. Let $D=\sum _{j=1}^ N a_ jD_ j$ be an effective real divisor, with distinct prime divisors $D_ j$ and coefficients $a_ j>0$. Choose a smooth Hermitian metric on each line bundle $\mathcal O_ X(D_ j)$, write $s_ j$ for its canonical section, and let $\eta _ j$ be its Chern curvature form. Set

\[ \sigma _ D=\sum _ j a_ j\log |s_ j|^2, \qquad \eta _ D=\sum _ j a_ j\eta _ j, \qquad \beta _ D=\theta -\eta _ D. \]

The Poincaré–Lelong formula reads

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\sigma _ D=[D]-\eta _ D. \]

For a Gauduchon metric $\gamma $ on $X$, define the Gauduchon degree

\[ \deg _\gamma D=\int _ X[D]\wedge \gamma ^{n-1} =\sum _ j a_ j\int _{D_ j}\gamma ^{n-1}, \]

where integration over a singular divisor means integration over its regular locus, or equivalently against its current of integration. This degree is finite, positive for $D\ne 0$, and equal to $\int _ X\eta _ D\wedge \gamma ^{n-1}$ by distributional Stokes. We write $\nu (u,D_ j)$ for the generic Lelong number of $u$ along $D_ j$, as in section 1.13.

Proposition 26.21.1. The following are equivalent:

  1. there is $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $\nu (u,D_ j)\geq a_ j$ for every $j$;

  2. the residual background $\beta _ D$ is pseudoeffective;

  3. for every Gauduchon metric $\gamma $,

    \[ \int _ X\theta \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}\geq \deg _\gamma D. \]

The first condition does not require $u-\sigma _ D$ to be bounded, nor the Lelong numbers of $u$ to equal the prescribed ones.

Proof. Suppose first that $u$ satisfies the first condition. By lemma 1.17.1, these Lelong numbers can be subtracted: $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u-[D]\geq 0$. Consequently

\[ \beta _ D+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u-\sigma _ D)=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u-[D]\geq 0, \]

where the difference $u-\sigma _ D$ is first taken in $L^1$ and then replaced by its quasi-plurisubharmonic representative. This proves the second condition. Conversely, if $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\beta _ D)$, then $u=\sigma _ D+v$ satisfies

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=[D]+\beta _ D+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\geq 0, \]

so its generic Lelong numbers along the $D_ j$ are at least $a_ j$. For $n\geq 2$, Lamari's duality, theorem 4.3.1, says that a smooth background is pseudoeffective exactly when all its Gauduchon integrals are nonnegative; applied to $\beta _ D$, together with the degree identity, it gives the equivalence of the second and third conditions. For $n=1$ the same criterion reads $\int _ X\beta _ D\geq 0$: necessity follows from Stokes' theorem, and for sufficiency one solves $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f=(\int _ X\beta _ D)\mu -\beta _ D$ for a smooth real function $f$ with the scalar Green operator. $\square$

For a nonzero effective real divisor $D$ and a pseudoeffective $\theta $, the exact threshold is therefore

26.171
\begin{equation} \tau _\theta (D)= \inf _{\gamma \text{ Gauduchon}} \frac{\int _ X\theta \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}}{\deg _\gamma D}. \end{equation}

It is finite and nonnegative, and a function $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $\nu (u,D_ j)\geq ta_ j$ for all $j$ exists if and only if $0\leq t\leq \tau _\theta (D)$. The endpoint is included, because the non-strict Gauduchon inequalities still hold there and duality gives a positive current. This is a threshold for the existence of a positive current, not for the existence of a bounded residual potential.

Whenever $\beta _ D$ is pseudoeffective, define the residual volume

\[ \mathcal V_\mu (\theta ;D)=\mathcal V_\mu (\beta _ D). \]

The definition and the degree identity give the exact formula

26.172
\begin{equation} \mathcal V_\mu (\theta ;D)^{1/n} =\inf _{\gamma \text{ Gauduchon}} \frac{\int _ X\theta \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}-\deg _\gamma D}{D_\mu (\gamma )}, \end{equation}

and the residual volume is finite and nonnegative. Changing the auxiliary metrics on the line bundles changes $\eta _ D$ by a smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-exact term, so the residual volume does not depend on these metrics, nor on a smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ change of $\theta $. It depends on the prescribed divisor and on the smooth probability measure $\mu $.

Here $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta $ denotes the extended non-pluripolar Monge–Ampère measure of theorem 6.1.2.

Theorem 26.21.2. Suppose that $\beta _ D$ has a smooth positive representative, and put $c=\mathcal V_\mu (\theta ;D)$. There is a $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function $u_0=\sigma _ D+v$, with $v$ smooth, satisfying

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u_0)=c\mu . \]

Every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ such that

\[ \nu (u,D_ j)\geq a_ j\quad (1\leq j\leq N),\qquad \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u)\geq c\mu \]

differs from $u_0$ by a constant. In particular, a solution of a normalized equation $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u)=c'\mu $ in the ordinary singularity type of $\sigma _ D$ has scalar $c'=c$, has a smooth remainder $u-\sigma _ D$, and is unique up to constants.

Proof. Existence. The smooth equation and theorem 26.1.3 give a smooth $v$ with

\[ \Omega =\beta _ D+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v>0,\qquad \Omega ^ n=c\mu . \]

Then $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\sigma _ D+v)=[D]+\Omega \geq 0$. Off $D$, the extended measure of $u_0=\sigma _ D+v$ is $\Omega ^ n$, and both measures give zero mass to $D$. This proves existence.

Reduction to the residual background. Let $u$ satisfy the two inequalities. Removing the divisor as in the proof of proposition 26.21.1, we obtain

\[ w=u-\sigma _ D-v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\Omega ),\qquad \Omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u-[D]\geq 0, \]

where the difference is first taken in $L^1$ and then replaced by its canonical representative. On $X\setminus D$, the two currents differ only by smooth changes in the decomposition of their local potentials, so their extended measures agree there, and both measures vanish on $D$. Hence

\[ \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\Omega (w) =\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u)\geq c\mu . \]

Rigidity. Take $\mathrm{d}V=\mu $. The zero function solves the smooth equation $\Omega ^ n=c\mu $ in the background $\Omega $, so its subsolution threshold is $b_\Omega (1)=c$, by proposition 14.27.4. For $n\geq 2$, the normalized $(n-1)$st power of the Gauduchon metric conformal to $\Omega $ is a smooth strictly positive maximizer of the dual problem. By corollary 26.9.6, any two threshold subsolutions differ by a constant; comparing $w$ with zero shows that $w$ is constant. For $n=1$, the positive current $\Omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w$ dominates its extended measure, hence dominates $c\mu =\Omega $, while both have total mass $\int _ X\Omega =c$ by Stokes' theorem. Thus $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w=0$, and $w$ is again constant.

Solutions in the ordinary type. Finally, let $u$ be in the ordinary singularity type of $\sigma _ D$ with $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u)=c'\mu $ for some $c'\geq 0$. Then $\nu (u,D_ j)=a_ j$, and the same reduction gives a bounded $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\Omega )$ with $\mathrm{MA}_\Omega (w)=c'\mu $. Suppose $c'<c$. For every constant $C$, the bounded functions $w$ and $C$ satisfy $\mathrm{MA}_\Omega (w)=(c'/c)\mathrm{MA}_\Omega (C)$, so theorem 10.2.2 gives $w\geq C$, which is impossible for a bounded $w$. The case $c'>c$ is symmetric, with the roles of $w$ and the constants exchanged. Therefore $c'=c$, and the rigidity just proved gives $u=u_0+\mathrm{constant}$. $\square$

When $\theta $ is Hermitian, write $\mu =f\, \mathrm{d}V$ relative to a smooth strictly positive probability volume $\mathrm{d}V$. For every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq [D]$ and $\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u)\geq cf\, \mathrm{d}V$, in particular for the potential $u_0$ of theorem 26.21.2 with $c=\mathcal V_\mu (\theta ;D)$, proposition 28.8.1 bounds the coefficient $c$ in terms of the subsolution threshold $b_\theta (f)$ of theorem 14.27.2 and the linear threshold of the class of the divisor. The residual problem prescribes the divisor, whereas the threshold $b_\theta (f)$ allows all $\theta $-plurisubharmonic functions.

Along a family $D_ t=tD$, $t\geq 0$, in the feasible interval, the root of (26.172) is concave and nonincreasing in $t$: every test quotient is affine in $t$ with nonpositive slope, and both properties pass to the infimum. On a subinterval where $\theta -t\eta _ D$ has a smooth positive representative, choose a minimizing Gauduchon metric $\gamma _ t$ for that background. Proposition 26.2.2 then gives the sharper formula

26.173
\begin{equation} \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathcal V_\mu (\theta ;tD)^{1/n} =-\frac{\deg _{\gamma _ t}D}{D_\mu (\gamma _ t)}<0. \end{equation}

In dimension one the derivative is $-\deg D$, since the denominator is one. If the residual background at an endpoint of the interval is nef, theorem 26.10.1 also expresses its volume as the limit of the scalars of the smooth normalized equations for the perturbations by $\epsilon h$, with $h$ a fixed Hermitian form and $\epsilon \downarrow 0$.

26.21.1 Varying divisorial coefficients

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

For a fixed support, the residual equation depends smoothly on the coefficients as long as the residual background stays in $\mathscr P$. The smooth remainders then give convergence in capacity of the solutions, and they determine all generic Lelong numbers of the limit on every modification. Keep $D_ j$, $s_ j$ and $\eta _ j$ as above, and put $\sigma _ j=\log |s_ j|^2$.

For $a=(a_1,\ldots ,a_ N)\in \mathbb R_{\geq 0}^ N$ put

\[ D_ a=\sum _ j a_ jD_ j,\qquad \sigma _ a=\sum _{a_ j>0}a_ j\sigma _ j,\qquad \beta _ a=\theta -\sum _ j a_ j\eta _ j, \]

where zero coefficients are omitted from the logarithmic sum. Let $\mathcal A$ be the set of such $a$ for which $\beta _ a$ has a smooth positive representative.

Proposition 26.21.3. There are unique smooth functions $v_ a$, $a\in \mathcal A$, such that

\[ \int _ Xv_ a\, \mathrm{d}\mu =0,\qquad \Omega _ a=\beta _ a+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ a>0,\qquad \Omega _ a^ n=c(a)\mu , \quad c(a)=\mathcal V_\mu (\beta _ a)>0. \]

The maps $a\mapsto v_ a$ and $a\mapsto c(a)$ are smooth, and every smooth norm of $v_ a$ is bounded on compact subsets of $\mathcal A$. Here smoothness at a parameter with a zero coefficient means that the family is the restriction of a smooth family defined on an open subset of $\mathbb R^ N$.

Set $u_ a=\sigma _ a+v_ a$. If $a^{(k)}\in \mathcal A$ and $a^{(k)}\to a\in \mathcal A$, then $u_{a^{(k)}}\to u_ a$ in $L^1$ and in capacity, and smoothly on compact subsets of $X\setminus \bigcup _ jD_ j$. Moreover,

\[ \bigl \| \mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u_{a^{(k)}}) -\mathrm{MA}^{\mathrm{ext}}_\theta (u_ a)\bigr \| _{\mathrm{TV}} =|c(a^{(k)})-c(a)|\longrightarrow 0, \]

where $\| \cdot \| _{\mathrm{TV}}$ is the total variation norm. For every modification $\pi :Y\to X$ with $Y$ smooth and every prime divisor $E\subset Y$,

\[ \nu (u_ a\circ \pi ,E)=\sum _ j a_ j\operatorname {ord}_ E(\pi ^*D_ j), \]

where $\operatorname {ord}_ E(\pi ^*D_ j)$ is the vanishing order along $E$ of the pullback of a local equation of $D_ j$. In particular, the limit $u_ a$ has exactly the ordinary singularity type of $\sigma _ a$, and no further singularity appears in this finite family.

Proof. The set of parameters in $\mathbb R^ N$ for which $\beta _ a$ has a smooth positive representative is open, and the smooth equation gives $v_ a$ and $c(a)$ on it. The argument in the proof of proposition 26.2.2 gives smooth dependence of both quantities on the parameter, with the fixed mean normalization; uniqueness makes the local families agree. Covering a compact set of parameters by finitely many such neighborhoods gives the asserted bounds.

The Poincaré–Lelong formula gives

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ a=[D_ a]+\Omega _ a, \]

so $u_ a\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. Its extended measure equals $\Omega _ a^ n=c(a)\mu $ off the union of the divisors, and both measures vanish on that union; this also covers the case $D_ a=0$. Each fixed $\sigma _ j$ is locally integrable, so the convergence of the coefficients and the smooth convergence of $v_ a$ give convergence in $L^1$. Off the union of the divisors every $\sigma _ j$ is smooth, which gives smooth convergence there. For convergence in capacity, remove a neighborhood of this analytic set of arbitrarily small Hermitian capacity; on its compact complement the convergence is uniform. Since the union of the divisors has capacity zero, this proves convergence in capacity. The identity for the total variation distances follows from the equations and $\mu (X)=1$.

Finally, after pullback the logarithmic potential $\sigma _ j\circ \pi $ has generic Lelong number $\operatorname {ord}_ E(\pi ^*D_ j)$ along $E$, whereas $v_ a\circ \pi $ is smooth. Additivity of Lelong numbers for the finite sum proves the formula on every modification. Since $v_ a$ is smooth also at the limiting parameter, $u_ a$ has the ordinary singularity type of $\sigma _ a$. $\square$

The smooth bounds above use that the limiting parameter lies in $\mathcal A$. When the sections and weights move, theorem 15.9.2 gives $L^1$ convergence of the potentials normalized by $\sup _ X=0$; when the residual background is fixed, it also allows uniformly bounded measurable right-hand sides converging in $L^1$. On a modification, the pullback of a positive probability density can vanish along the exceptional divisor, so the smooth equation with positive density no longer applies directly. For a minimal exceptional pole, proposition 28.11.4 gives a vanishing order of the density that is necessary for a smooth residual, but this order alone does not ensure smoothness.

Multiplier ideals need not stabilize in this family. At a smooth point of $D_ j$ outside the other divisors, take a local coordinate $z_1$ defining $D_ j$. There $u_ a=a_ j\log |z_1|^2+O(1)$, so

\[ \mathcal I(u_ a)=(z_1^{\lfloor a_ j\rfloor }) \]

by the one-variable integral in example 1.12.5 and Fubini's theorem. Thus, if the parameter stays in $\mathcal A$ while $a_ j$ increases to an integer from below, this local ideal jumps at the limit. Convergence of the Lelong numbers to their limiting values does not imply that the multiplier ideals are eventually equal to those of the limit.

26.21.2 A ruled residual and its subtraction cost

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

On the ruled surface of section 19.10, the Gauduchon variational volume has a different dependence on the data from the two volumes of an ordinary type. Keep $Y=\mathbb P^1\times \mathbb P^1$, $\theta =cw_1+aw_2$, $0\leq b<c$ and $\beta =(c-b)w_1+aw_2$. Let $m>0$ be smooth on the first curve, with $\int mw=1$, and put

\[ \mu =mw_1\wedge w_2,\qquad k=c-b,\qquad H=\int _{\mathbb P^1}\frac{m}{a}w. \]

In dimension two the Gauduchon tests are all smooth positive $(1,1)$-forms $T$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}T=0$, and $D_\mu (T)=\int _ Y\sqrt{T^2/\mu }\, \mu $.

Proposition 26.21.4. The all-test infimum is attained and

26.174
\begin{equation} \mathcal V_\mu (\theta ;bD)=\mathcal V_\mu (\beta )=\frac{2(c-b)}{H}, \qquad \mathcal V_\mu (\theta )=\frac{2c}{H}. \end{equation}

Writing $V=2k/H$ and $g=Vm/(2a)$, a minimizing test is the closed positive form

\[ T=\frac ga w_1+w_2. \]

Proof. Since $\int gw=k$, solve $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f=(g-k)w$ on the first sphere. Then $\Omega =\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f=gw_1+aw_2>0$ and $\Omega ^2=V\mu $. For every Gauduchon test, Stokes and the matrix arithmetic–geometric mean inequality give

\[ \int _ Y\beta \wedge T=\int _ Y\Omega \wedge T \geq \sqrt V\, D_\mu (T). \]

The displayed $T$ is closed, since $g/a$ depends only on the first factor, and $T=a^{-1}\Omega $, so equality holds pointwise. Explicitly,

\[ \int _ Y\beta \wedge T=2k, \qquad D_\mu (T)=\sqrt V\int \frac ma w=\sqrt V H. \]

This proves the infimum over all tests. The curvature of $D$ is $w_1$, so the divisorial residual is indeed $\beta $. Replacing $k$ by $c$ gives the formula for $\theta $. $\square$

Fix a real constant $d$ and a smooth real first-factor function $e$. On an interval where $c_ s=c-sd>b$ and $a_ s=a-se>0$, keep $b,m$ fixed and put

\[ k_ s=c_ s-b,\qquad H_ s=\int \frac{m}{a_ s}w, \qquad V_ s=\frac{2k_ s}{H_ s},\qquad T_ s=\frac{V_ sm}{2a_ s^2}w_1+w_2. \]

Corollary 26.21.5. For the residual background $\beta _ s=k_ sw_1+a_ sw_2$,

26.175
\begin{equation} \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm ds}\sqrt{V_ s} =-\frac{\int _ Y(d w_1+e w_2)\wedge T_ s}{D_\mu (T_ s)}. \end{equation}

Consequently, for every compact segment $[s_0,s_1]$ in that interval,

\[ \sqrt{V_{s_0}}-\sqrt{V_{s_1}} =\int _{s_0}^{s_1} \frac{\int _ Y(d w_1+e w_2)\wedge T_ s}{D_\mu (T_ s)}\, \mathrm ds. \]

Proof. We have $H_ s'=\int em/a_ s^2\, w$, $D_\mu (T_ s)=\sqrt{V_ s}H_ s$ and

\[ \int _ Y(d w_1+e w_2)\wedge T_ s=d+\frac{V_ s}{2}H_ s'. \]

Differentiating $V_ s=2k_ s/H_ s$ gives (26.175); integration proves the second identity. This is the explicit first variation of proposition 26.2.2 along the residual family. $\square$

For $c=1$, $b=1/4$, $a=2+\tau $ and $m=1$, the height $\tau $ has distribution $\mathrm d\tau /2$ under $w$. Hence $H=(\log 3)/2$, and the residual and original variational volumes are $3/\log 3$ and $4/\log 3$. The ordinary-type volumes from (19.26) are instead $3/2$ and $9/2$, independent of $m$. The subtraction identity differentiates the density-dependent variational volume. Pure-pullback invariance of the ordinary-type masses does not identify an arbitrary smooth positive probability density upstairs with the degenerate pullback of $\mu $.

The distinction persists along a segment ending at a nef background. The following surface example combines the subtraction cost with an imposed nonanalytic ordinary type, including the range in which its closed first moment is infinite.

Example 26.21.6. Let $\mu =w_1\wedge w_2$, let $\tau (z)=(|z|^2-1)/(|z|^2+1)$ be the height on the first sphere, and set

\[ \theta _ s=w_1+(2+\tau -s)w_2,\qquad 0\leq s<1. \]

Fix the ordinary type of $U=1-(1-G_ p)^\alpha $ with $1/2\leq \alpha <1$, and write $\sigma _-(s),\sigma _+(s)$ for its lower and upper volumes in $\theta _ s$. Then

26.176
\begin{equation} V_ s:=\mathcal V_\mu (\theta _ s)=\frac4{\log ((3-s)/(1-s))},\qquad \sigma _-(s)=2(1-s),\qquad \sigma _+(s)=2(3-s). \end{equation}

The improper subtraction cost is finite and equals $2/\sqrt{\log 3}$, while $\sigma _+(s)\to 4$. The endpoint $\theta _1=w_1+(1+\tau )w_2$ is nef, has zero variational volume, and is not big.

Proof. Take $c=1$, $b=0$, $a_ s=2+\tau -s$ and $m=1$ in proposition 26.21.4. Radial integration on the first sphere gives the law $\mathrm d\tau /2$ on $[-1,1]$, so

\[ H_ s=\frac12\int _{-1}^1\frac{\mathrm dt}{2+t-s} =\frac12\log \frac{3-s}{1-s}. \]

Thus $V_ s=2/H_ s$. Since $b=0<c$, theorem 19.10.2 gives all rough representatives of the fixed type finite positive mass, with the extrema in (26.176). The reference has zero Lelong numbers and trivial multiplier ideals but no analytic representative, by proposition 19.10.3. Its infinite closed first moment in this range of $\alpha $ was computed after (19.27); no such moment is needed for either extremum.

For the minimizing tests $T_ s=V_ s(2+\tau -s)^{-2}w_1/2+w_2$, corollary 26.21.5 gives

\[ \frac{\int _ Yw_2\wedge T_ s}{D_\mu (T_ s)} =-\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm ds}\sqrt{V_ s} =\frac{2}{(1-s)(3-s)[\log ((3-s)/(1-s))]^{3/2}}. \]

Integrating to $r<1$ gives $\sqrt{V_0}-\sqrt{V_ r}$. Since $V_ r\to 0$, the improper integral is $\sqrt{V_0}=2/\sqrt{\log 3}$. By theorem 26.1.3, every minimizing test is a constant multiple of $T_ s$, so the cost is independent of this choice.

The endpoint is smooth semipositive, hence nef. Monotonicity gives

\[ 0\leq \lambda _\mu (\theta _1)\leq \lambda _\mu (\theta _ s) \longrightarrow 0, \]

so its variational volume vanishes. To exclude bigness, suppose that $\theta _1+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q\geq \epsilon (w_1+w_2)$ for a quasi-plurisubharmonic $q$ and $\epsilon >0$. Choose a nonzero smooth nonnegative $(1,1)$-form $\zeta $ on the first sphere supported where $1+\tau <\epsilon $. Its pullback is closed, so distributional Stokes gives

\[ 0\leq \int _ Y(\theta _1+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q-\epsilon (w_1+w_2))\wedge \zeta =\int _{\mathbb P^1}(1+\tau -\epsilon )\zeta <0, \]

a contradiction. Thus the positive segment ends outside the big cone, although its upper ordinary-type volume has a positive limit. $\square$

26.22 A divisorial threshold on the Hopf threefold

From Singularities in Hermitian pluripotential theory

On the Hopf threefold, the pullback of a hyperplane has an explicit divisorial threshold. The residual metric solves its normalized equation with zero potential, so the residual volume can be computed directly, also at the nef endpoint.

In this section $X$ is the Hopf threefold of chapter 8, with its projection $\pi :X\to \mathbb P^2$, and we write $\zeta $ for its vertical form, so that $\omega =\alpha +\zeta $. These forms satisfy

\[ \alpha ^3=0,\quad \zeta ^2=0,\quad \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\zeta =-\alpha ^2,\quad \int _ X\alpha ^2\wedge \zeta =2. \]

Let $H$ be the inverse image under $\pi $ of a projective line in $\mathbb P^2$. The pulled-back Fubini–Study metric gives $\eta _ H=\alpha $ and a logarithmic potential $\sigma _ H$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\sigma _ H=[H]-\alpha $.

Proposition 26.22.1. The divisorial threshold is $\tau _\omega (H)=1$. For $\mu _0=\omega ^3/6$ and $0\leq a\leq 1$,

\[ \mathcal V_{\mu _0}(\omega ;aH)=6(1-a)^2. \]

For $a<1$ this is the scalar of the equation solved, as in theorem 26.21.2, by $a\sigma _ H$ plus a smooth function. At $a=1$ the residual background is nef, and the logarithmic potential $\sigma _ H$ has zero non-pluripolar Monge–Ampère measure. For $a<1$, every residual background has infinite upper mass.

Proof. The residual background is

\[ R_ a=(1-a)\alpha +\zeta . \]

It is positive for $0\leq a<1$ and semipositive for $a=1$, so every coefficient $a\leq 1$ is feasible. For the reverse inequality, consider the metrics $\gamma _ t=\alpha +t\zeta $, $t>0$. They are Gauduchon, since

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\gamma _ t^2)=-2t\alpha ^3=0. \]

Using $\zeta ^2=0$, we obtain

\[ \frac{\int _ X\omega \wedge \gamma _ t^2}{\deg _{\gamma _ t}H} =\frac{1+2t}{2t}\longrightarrow 1\qquad (t\to \infty ), \]

and the threshold formula gives $\tau _\omega (H)=1$.

For $a<1$,

\[ R_ a^3=3(1-a)^2\alpha ^2\wedge \zeta =(1-a)^2\omega ^3=6(1-a)^2\mu _0. \]

Hence the zero potential solves the residual smooth equation, and uniqueness of the scalar gives the volume. At $a=1$, the form $R_1=\zeta $ is nef, because $\zeta +\epsilon \omega >0$ for $\epsilon >0$, and the same computation gives

\[ \mathcal V_{\mu _0}(\zeta +\epsilon \omega ) =6\epsilon ^2(1+\epsilon )\longrightarrow 0. \]

By theorem 26.10.1, the residual volume at $a=1$ is zero. Moreover $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\sigma _ H=[H]+\zeta $, so the non-pluripolar measure of $\sigma _ H$ is $\zeta ^3=0$.

Finally, fix $a<1$ and choose $0<\delta \leq \min \{ 1-a,1\} $, so that $R_ a-\delta \omega \geq 0$. For the smooth functions $V_ j$ of theorem 8.0.1,

\[ R_ a+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\delta V_ j) =(R_ a-\delta \omega )+\delta (\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V_ j)>0. \]

Monotonicity of the determinant on positive matrices bounds the mass of this form below by $\delta ^3\int _ X(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V_ j)^3$, which tends to infinity. Thus a finite residual volume and an explicit divisorial threshold coexist with unbounded upper mass. $\square$
